The Flywheel Ledger

Names worth holding if the trade doesn't work. Companion to the Asset Atlas — nothing here repeats it.

The flywheel sleeve is a two-month hold aiming for a 5–15% move. The Atlas already covers the obvious candidates; this guide covers the second tier of boring — established businesses with real earnings, cash return, and no existential question mark. The test for inclusion: if the exit never comes, would you be comfortable owning it for a year?

Each entry has the Atlas structure plus two additions built for the flywheel. Watch gives the reporting cadence and the two or three numbers that actually move the stock, and names the peers that report first and set the tone. How to use it says what tends to produce a two-month move in that name, how large the typical earnings-day reaction runs (a historical tendency, not a promise), and what being stuck costs you — yield, drawdown character, and how long "stuck" tends to last.

Boring Tech

Hardware assemblers, payroll processors, connectors, storage, payments plumbing, government IT, data vendors, contract manufacturers, and the software duopolies nobody talks about at parties.

Hardware & enterprise IT

DELL Dell Technologies

Sector: PCs / Servers / Storage · Market cap: ~$100B · P/E: ~17x · Yield: ~1.5%

Dell sells roughly $110B a year of hardware across two segments. Client Solutions Group (~$50B) is PCs, workstations, and monitors — a mature, low-margin business (operating margin around 6–7%) that lives on corporate refresh cycles. Infrastructure Solutions Group (~$55B and growing fast) is servers, storage, and networking, and is where the story is: Dell assembles and ships full AI server racks built on Nvidia GPUs to hyperscalers, neoclouds like CoreWeave, xAI, and large enterprises, with an AI backlog that has run into the tens of billions. Storage (PowerStore, PowerScale, PowerMax) is the high-margin piece of ISG and the part that makes AI deployments profitable for Dell rather than just large.

Michael Dell controls the vote, and the capital allocation reflects an owner: buybacks that shrink the count several percent a year, a dividend growing ~20% annually, and no interest in growth at any cost. The stock has been re-rated from "PC company" to "AI infrastructure at a hardware multiple" — it went from the low $40s in early 2023 to the $130–170 range, with two 30%-plus drawdowns along the way when AI margins disappointed.

Shines when: AI server backlog converts to shipments, enterprise refresh cycles hit (Windows end-of-support pushing PC upgrades), storage attach rates on AI deployments rise, and ISG margin expands.

Dangers: AI servers carry gross margins in the low-to-mid teens because the GPU is most of the bill of materials — revenue can double while profit grows far less. Customer concentration: a handful of AI buyers drive ISG lumpiness. Consumer PCs are structurally weak; commercial PCs are cyclical. DRAM and NAND price spikes crush client margins with a lag. Tariffs on Asian assembly.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. AI server orders, shipments, and backlog; ISG operating margin; storage growth; IDC quarterly PC shipments. Nvidia and Super Micro report a week or two earlier and set the mood.

How to use it: The two-month move almost always comes from one of its own earnings or from Nvidia's. Typical earnings reactions run ±8–15%, so half the two-month target can arrive or vanish overnight — it's the highest-beta name in this section despite the boring products. If stuck: mid-teens multiple, growing dividend, buybacks — you're fine unless you bought at the top of an AI hype leg, in which case the drift down can last two quarters.

HPQ HP Inc.

Sector: PCs / Printing · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~8x · Yield: ~4%

HP Inc. is what remained after the 2015 split sent servers and services to HPE: about $54B of annual revenue, of which Personal Systems (PCs, workstations, displays) is roughly $37B and Printing roughly $17B. The economics are inverted from how they look. PCs are 70% of sales but a low-single-digit-margin fight with Lenovo and Dell for the number-two spot globally. Printing is 30% of sales but the majority of operating profit, because HP sells printers cheaply and ink and toner expensively — the razor-and-blade model — and its installed base of office and home printers still consumes supplies.

The problem is that the installed base shrinks a little every year as offices print less, and HP's answer has been cost cuts, buybacks that retire 3–5% of shares annually, and a dividend near 4%. It is the definition of a cash-return story with no growth narrative: the multiple sits in the high single digits and has for years. Management periodically promises an "AI PC" upgrade cycle and a return to print stability; the market periodically believes it for a quarter.

Shines when: A commercial PC refresh cycle hits (end-of-support deadlines, AI PCs), print supplies revenue stabilizes, and the buyback keeps EPS rising on flat sales.

Dangers: Structural print decline. PC commoditization, Chinese competition, and tariffs on hardware built in Asia. Memory-cost inflation flows straight into PC gross margin. Guidance cuts have produced 10–15% single-day drops several times.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends October). Personal Systems units and margin, print supplies revenue trend, free cash flow against the $3B-plus target, IDC PC shipments.

How to use it: A value rental. The two-month setup is buying after a guidance-cut drop when the yield spikes toward 5% and selling into the next "PC refresh is here" narrative. Earnings reactions ±5–12%. If stuck, the dividend and buyback keep you paid, but the ceiling is low — this is not a name that quietly compounds while you wait.

HPE Hewlett Packard Enterprise

Sector: Enterprise IT / Networking · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~11x · Yield: ~2%

The enterprise half of the HP split, with roughly $35B of run-rate revenue after closing the Juniper Networks acquisition in mid-2025. The segments: Server (the largest, including AI systems built on the Cray supercomputing lineage), Networking (Aruba plus Juniper — now the number-two enterprise networking vendor behind Cisco, with real share in data-center switching and AI back-end networks), Hybrid Cloud (storage, GreenLake as-a-service), and Financial Services. GreenLake, its subscription platform, is the recurring-revenue story management has told for years with mixed credibility.

The Juniper deal was the big swing: it roughly doubled networking, which carries far better margins than servers, and the thesis is that HPE's earnings mix shifts toward the good stuff. Activist Elliott arrived in 2025 with a large stake, pushed for cost cuts and margin targets, and got board representation. The stock has traded at a persistent discount to Cisco and Dell because execution has been uneven for a decade.

Shines when: Networking growth and margin come through post-Juniper, AI server margins improve from the low teens, enterprise refresh spending returns, and Elliott's cost program shows up in operating margin.

Dangers: Integration risk on a deal this size. Server margins are thin and lumpy — one bad AI-server quarter in 2025 dropped the stock ~15%. Debt rose to fund Juniper. Cisco and Arista are formidable in exactly the segment HPE just bought into.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends October). Networking revenue and margin, AI systems backlog and gross margin, free cash flow, synergy milestones. Cisco reports a couple of weeks earlier.

How to use it: A re-rating candidate — it trades at roughly half Cisco's multiple, so if Juniper delivers the gap closes. Two-month moves come on earnings (±8–15%) and on Cisco's results. If stuck, the yield helps and the balance sheet is investment grade, but cheap-and-stays-cheap is HPE's historical pattern.

IBM International Business Machines

Sector: Enterprise Software / Consulting / Infrastructure · Market cap: ~$260B · P/E: ~24x GAAP, ~20x adjusted · Yield: ~2.5–3%

IBM generates about $64B of revenue in three segments. Software (~$28B, 45%) is Red Hat (Linux, OpenShift, Ansible), automation, data and AI (Db2, watsonx), transaction processing, and since 2025 HashiCorp (Terraform). Consulting (~$21B) is systems integration and business transformation. Infrastructure (~$14B) is the z-series mainframe — which still processes the bulk of the world's card transactions and bank core ledgers — plus Power servers and storage. The 2021 Kyndryl spin removed the low-margin managed-infrastructure outsourcing that had dragged results for a decade.

The investment case is free cash flow: $13–14B a year, guided higher, funding a dividend paid without interruption since 1916 and continuous tuck-in software acquisitions. Software growth in the high single digits is what the stock is priced on; when it dips toward mid-singles the market reacts badly. Mainframe cycles (a new generation every ~3 years, z17 launched 2025) swing infrastructure revenue by double digits and add a cyclical layer under the software story.

Shines when: Software growth accelerates, mainframe-cycle quarters lift infrastructure, consulting signings pick up (especially generative-AI bookings), and FCF guidance rises.

Dangers: Consulting is the first thing enterprises cut. Software growth is modest enough that a one-point miss triggers double-digit drops — July 2025 saw ~8% down on a software miss. Acquisition-fueled growth adds debt (~$60B gross). The multiple has re-rated from 12x to 20x-plus; the margin for error shrank with it.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Software organic growth, Red Hat growth, consulting signings and book-to-bill, FCF guidance, mainframe cycle timing.

How to use it: Most quarters IBM moves like a utility with a tech ticker; on earnings day it moves like a growth stock (±6–10%). The two-month setup is a pre-earnings drift into a mainframe-cycle quarter, or a post-earnings dip on a minor software miss. If stuck, this is close to the ideal stuck outcome: a growing dividend, rising FCF, and a business that has survived everything for a century.

NTAP NetApp

Sector: Enterprise Storage · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~17x · Yield: ~2%

NetApp is the largest pure-play enterprise storage company, at ~$6.6B of revenue. It sells all-flash arrays running ONTAP, its data-management operating system, and increasingly sells ONTAP as a first-party cloud service inside AWS, Azure, and Google Cloud — which lets enterprises keep the same storage layer on-prem and in the cloud. Hybrid cloud (hardware plus software) is ~90% of revenue; public cloud services are the smaller, higher-growth piece. The AI pitch is that training data, vector databases, and inference pipelines all need high-performance enterprise storage, and NetApp is the incumbent in many of the largest data estates.

The company generates ~$1.5B of free cash flow and returns essentially all of it through buybacks and a dividend. Growth is modest — mid-single digits in good years — so the stock trades on a value multiple and moves on quarterly execution.

Shines when: All-flash array growth accelerates, AI-related storage deals convert, cloud services scale, and NAND costs fall.

Dangers: Dell, Pure Storage, HPE, and the hyperscalers themselves compete for the same budgets. NAND price swings hit gross margin. Enterprise IT budgets. Modest growth caps the multiple.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends April). All-flash array annualized revenue run rate, product gross margin, public cloud services growth, AI deal commentary.

How to use it: Cheap tech value with an AI-adjacent story; earnings reactions ±8–15% — outsized for a boring company. The two-month setup is a post-earnings drop on a guidance nitpick with the next report as the catalyst. If stuck, cash-rich and returning capital, but the stock can go nowhere for a year at a time.

STX Seagate Technology

Sector: Data Storage (HDD) · Market cap: ~$50B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~1.5%

Seagate is one of two remaining makers of hard disk drives, generating ~$9–10B of revenue in a duopoly with Western Digital (Toshiba is a distant third). Hard drives were supposed to die to flash; instead hyperscalers buy exabytes of high-capacity nearline drives every quarter for data that must be stored but not accessed constantly, because per terabyte a hard drive costs a fraction of an SSD. Mass-capacity drives (cloud and enterprise) are now ~85% of revenue; the legacy PC and consumer drive business is a rounding error.

Seagate's technology bet is HAMR — heat-assisted magnetic recording, which pushes capacity past 30–40 terabytes per drive and lowers cost per terabyte for the cloud. After years of delays HAMR qualified at major hyperscalers in 2025, and with both makers having cut capacity in the 2023 downturn, supply is tight and pricing firm. The stock roughly tripled in 2025 on this. Irish-domiciled; dividend plus buybacks when the cycle allows.

Shines when: Cloud capex grows, HAMR ramps with better margins, supply stays tight, and the AI data explosion keeps nearline demand rising.

Dangers: Deeply cyclical — 2022–23 saw revenue fall by roughly half and the stock drop 60%. Flash could eventually displace nearline drives if NAND costs fall far enough. Debt from prior downturns. After a triple, expectations are high.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends June). Exabytes shipped, average capacity per drive, nearline pricing, gross margin, HAMR mix, hyperscaler capex guidance from Microsoft, Meta, Alphabet, and Amazon.

How to use it: A cyclical riding a supercycle; earnings reactions ±10–15% and two-month moves of 20–40% are routine. The most un-boring stock behavior in this section attached to the most boring product. If stuck at a cycle peak, the drawdowns are 40–60% and take a year or more to recover — size accordingly.

WDC Western Digital

Sector: Data Storage (HDD) · Market cap: ~$50B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~0.5%

The other half of the HDD duopoly, at ~$10B of revenue. Western Digital spun off its flash memory business as Sandisk in February 2025, which turned a messy two-business conglomerate into a pure hard-drive company with a cleaner balance sheet, higher margins, and a dividend reinstated after years without one. The product story mirrors Seagate's: high-capacity nearline drives for hyperscalers, using ePMR and UltraSMR technology now and its own HAMR transition later.

The post-spin WDC has been a straightforward story — same tight supply, same cloud demand, same pricing power — and the stock has tracked Seagate closely with occasional divergence on quarterly execution.

Shines when: Cloud storage demand grows, supply stays tight, capacity-per-drive gains hold pricing, and the HAMR transition goes smoothly.

Dangers: Identical cycle risk to Seagate. Technology-transition execution risk — a late HAMR ramp cedes share. Concentrated hyperscaler customers negotiate hard.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends June). Exabytes shipped, gross margin, nearline mix, capacity per drive.

How to use it: Pair-trade material with STX — the two move together and diverge on execution, so a relative-value spread is a cleaner bet than either alone. If stuck, the post-spin balance sheet is the cleaner of the two, but the cyclicality is identical.

CDW CDW Corporation

Sector: IT Reseller / Solutions · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~17x · Yield: ~1.5%

CDW is a value-added reseller — it sells about $21B a year of hardware, software, cloud subscriptions, and services from more than a thousand vendors (Dell, HP, Cisco, Microsoft, Apple, Lenovo) to corporate, small-business, government, education, and healthcare customers in the U.S., UK, and Canada. The business is a large, technically trained sales force plus engineers who design and integrate solutions; CDW makes money on the spread and on services attached to the hardware.

Capital-light, high return on invested capital, a dividend raised annually, and buybacks. Revenue tracks enterprise and public-sector IT budgets closely — it fell in 2023–24 when spending paused after the pandemic surge, and recovers when device refresh and cloud projects resume. Federal and education budget uncertainty in 2025 was a headwind.

Shines when: Corporate IT spending recovers, PC and device refresh cycles hit, public-sector budgets stabilize, and higher-margin services and cloud grow their share.

Dangers: Tied directly to IT budgets. Federal and education spending cuts. Thin gross margins (high teens). Vendors going direct or shifting to consumption models that bypass resellers.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Net sales growth by customer segment, gross profit growth, hardware vs. software/services mix.

How to use it: A bellwether for enterprise IT spending; earnings reactions ±5–10%. It tends to move with Dell and HP results. If stuck, a capital-light, cash-generative distributor — comfortable.

MSI Motorola Solutions

Sector: Public Safety Communications · Market cap: ~$75B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~1%

Not the phone company — that went to Lenovo in 2014. Motorola Solutions is the land-mobile-radio business, ~$11B of revenue, and the dominant supplier of two-way radios and dispatch systems to police, fire, EMS, and utilities across North America, with roughly 70% share in the P25 public-safety standard. Products and Systems Integration (~60%) is radios, infrastructure, and the multi-year contracts to build statewide networks. Software and Services (~40%) is command-center software (911 call handling, records management), video security and access control (Avigilon, Pelco), and maintenance contracts — the recurring, higher-margin piece that grows faster.

Government customers, decade-long contracts, mission-critical products with no acceptable substitute — the combination produces raised guidance nearly every quarter, mid-20s operating margins, and a stock that compounded relentlessly for a decade. Capital return is a small dividend and steady buybacks; acquisitions are tuck-ins in video and software.

Shines when: Federal and state public-safety budgets grow, video and software attach rates rise, international orders (the UK, Australia, the Middle East) expand, and the shift to broadband-capable radios drives a refresh.

Dangers: A premium valuation. Government procurement cycles and budget delays. Competition in video from Axon (body cameras) and Chinese vendors abroad. Tariff exposure on hardware. Very little that goes wrong — which is itself the risk, because the multiple assumes it stays that way.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Backlog, Software & Services growth, operating margin, video and command-center bookings.

How to use it: One of the lowest-drama compounders in tech — earnings reactions ±3–6% and a stock that grinds rather than jumps. A poor fit for a 5–15% two-month target unless bought on a rare 10%-plus pullback. As a stuck-bucket holding it is superb.

GRMN Garmin

Sector: Consumer Electronics / Navigation · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~24x · Yield: ~2%

Garmin makes GPS devices for five markets: Fitness (running and cycling watches, bike computers — now the largest segment), Outdoor (adventure and dive watches, handhelds, satellite communicators), Aviation (integrated cockpits for general aviation, avionics retrofits), Marine (chartplotters, sonar, autopilots), and Auto OEM (infotainment for BMW and others). About $6.5–7B of revenue. Swiss-domiciled, vertically integrated with its own factories in Taiwan, and debt-free with billions in cash.

It spent years beating expectations because premium wearables kept growing despite the Apple Watch, and because aviation and marine are high-margin, replacement-driven businesses with loyal customers. Dividend raised annually; occasional buybacks. Founder-family culture, low turnover, product-led rather than marketing-led.

Shines when: Fitness and outdoor demand holds, new watch launches land, aviation and marine grow, and Auto OEM turns profitable at scale.

Dangers: Consumer discretionary — a recession hits wearables first. Apple and Chinese competition in fitness. Tariffs on Taiwan-made goods. Currency swings on a global revenue base. A valuation that assumes the beat-and-raise streak continues.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Fitness segment growth, gross margin, Auto OEM profitability, full-year guidance revisions.

How to use it: A consumer-tech cyclical with a pristine balance sheet; earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is buying a tariff or consumer-fear pullback ahead of a product-launch quarter. If stuck, no debt, cash pile, dividend — very safe.

IT services & government IT

CTSH Cognizant

Sector: IT Services · Market cap: ~$38B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: ~1.5%

Cognizant is a ~$20B-revenue IT services company headquartered in New Jersey with the majority of its ~350,000 employees in India — American client relationships, Indian delivery economics. Health Sciences is the largest vertical (~30%: payer and provider systems, pharma), followed by Financial Services (~28%), Products and Resources, and Communications, Media, and Technology. It does application development and maintenance, cloud migration, data engineering, and, through the 2024 Belcan acquisition, engineering and R&D services for aerospace and industrials.

The company lost a step to Accenture and Infosys in the late 2010s, then spent 2023–25 fixing attrition, winning large deals, and buying back stock. It's a cheaper, slower version of Accenture: mid-teens multiple, a dividend, and buybacks that retire ~2–3% of shares a year. The generative-AI question hangs over the whole sector — clients want automation to reduce the hours they pay for.

Shines when: Discretionary IT budgets recover, large deal bookings convert to revenue, healthcare clients modernize claims and clinical systems, and AI services bookings ramp.

Dangers: AI deflation — same work for fewer billable hours. H-1B visa restrictions and fees; rupee appreciation. Competition from Infosys, TCS, Wipro, and Accenture. Pricing pressure and attrition in a tight Indian labor market.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Bookings, organic constant-currency growth, healthcare vertical trend, attrition. Accenture (mid Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec) and Infosys (mid Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct) report first and set the tone.

How to use it: A cheap sector cyclical; earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is buying after Accenture spooks the sector and holding into Cognizant's own report. If stuck, the balance sheet is clean and the buyback continues, but the AI overhang can keep the multiple compressed indefinitely.

KD Kyndryl

Sector: IT Infrastructure Services · Market cap: ~$8B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: none

Kyndryl is the managed-infrastructure services business IBM spun off in 2021 — ~$15B of revenue from running mainframes, data centers, networks, and cloud environments for large enterprises (banks, insurers, airlines, retailers) under multi-year contracts. It came out of IBM with a book of unprofitable legacy contracts, a shrinking revenue base, and a stock that fell by half. Since then it has re-priced or exited the bad contracts, signed partnerships with the hyperscalers it was once forbidden to work with, and built a consulting layer.

The turnaround has shown up in profit before revenue: adjusted pretax income and free cash flow have risen for several years while revenue only recently stopped declining. Buybacks started in 2025. It is the cheapest large IT services company by a wide margin, for reasons that are partly legitimate.

Shines when: Revenue turns positive, signings grow, hyperscaler-partnership work scales, and margin targets are met.

Dangers: Structural decline in legacy infrastructure outsourcing. Low margins. Pension and restructuring costs. Competition from Accenture, DXC, and Indian firms. A turnaround that could stall at "less bad."

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Signings, revenue growth, adjusted pretax margin, free cash flow.

How to use it: A turnaround at a value multiple; earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is a revenue-inflection quarter. If stuck, cash flow supports the buyback, but this is the most fragile name in the section — a stalled turnaround goes nowhere for years.

LDOS Leidos

Sector: Government IT / Defense Services · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~16x · Yield: ~1%

Leidos is the largest IT and technology services contractor to the U.S. federal government — ~$17B of revenue across four segments: National Security and Digital (intelligence-agency IT, cyber, software), Health and Civil (its largest single contract is running disability medical exams for the VA; plus airport security screening equipment), Commercial and International, and Defense Systems (hypersonics, missile defense, naval systems, the Dynetics subsidiary). It was born from SAIC's 2013 split and absorbed Lockheed Martin's IT business in 2016.

The economics are contractor economics: mid-teens EBITDA margins on cost-plus and fixed-price work, a backlog around $45B (over two years of revenue), steady free cash flow, buybacks, and a modest dividend. It is boring in the best sense — the government pays on time, contracts run five to ten years, and recompete win rates are high.

Shines when: Federal IT modernization and defense budgets grow, VA exam volume stays high, hypersonics and space programs ramp, and margin improvement continues.

Dangers: Federal budget cuts and efficiency initiatives — 2025's contract scrutiny hit the sector hard. Continuing resolutions and shutdowns delay awards. Protest and recompete losses on large contracts. Contractor labor-cost inflation.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Backlog and book-to-bill, adjusted EBITDA margin, VA exam volumes, free cash flow, federal budget headlines.

How to use it: A defensive name that trades on budget politics as much as results; earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is buying a budget-scare selloff — the sector regularly drops 15–25% on Washington headlines and recovers — ahead of a clean quarter. If stuck, the backlog and cash flow make it an easy hold.

SAIC Science Applications International

Sector: Government IT Services · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: ~13x · Yield: ~1.5%

SAIC is a ~$7.5B-revenue government technology integrator — IT modernization, cloud migration, engineering, and mission support for the Department of Defense, intelligence agencies, and civilian departments. It is the smaller, purer remnant of the 2013 split that created Leidos. Lower margins than Leidos (high-single-digit EBITDA), more concentrated in cost-plus IT work, and a stock that has drifted as growth stalled.

Capital return is steady: buybacks plus a dividend, and management has been retooling the portfolio toward higher-margin engineering and software. A cheap, small, unloved way to own federal spending.

Shines when: Federal IT modernization budgets grow, new awards outpace recompete losses, and margin mix improves.

Dangers: Budget cuts and contract scrutiny. Recompete losses — a few large contracts matter. Low margins leave little cushion. Small-cap liquidity.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends January). Book-to-bill, backlog, organic growth, free cash flow.

How to use it: A small-cap version of the LDOS trade with more volatility (earnings reactions ±8–15%); moves with the sector on budget headlines. If stuck, the dividend and buyback carry you, but growth has been elusive.

CACI CACI International

Sector: Government IT / Defense Technology · Market cap: ~$11B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: none

CACI is a ~$8.5B-revenue provider of technology and expertise to national-security customers — the intelligence community, DoD, and Homeland Security. Roughly half is "expertise" (mission support, analysts, engineers) and half is "technology" (software-defined electronic warfare, signals intelligence, cyber, space ground systems, network modernization). Its technology work is more differentiated than most peers', which supports better margins and a premium multiple.

No dividend; capital goes to buybacks and acquisitions (Azure Summit for electronic warfare, among others). Backlog is around $30B, and the company has grown organically in the high single digits for years — unusual in the sector.

Shines when: Intelligence and electronic-warfare budgets grow, technology-heavy contracts win, and organic growth stays ahead of peers.

Dangers: The same federal budget and continuing-resolution risk as the sector. Acquisition integration. A premium multiple that compresses when the group sells off.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends June). Organic growth, book-to-bill, technology vs. expertise mix, backlog.

How to use it: The quality name in government services; earnings reactions ±5–10%. It sells off with the sector on budget headlines and recovers faster. If stuck, no yield, but consistent growth and buybacks.

BAH Booz Allen Hamilton

Sector: Government Consulting · Market cap: ~$13B · P/E: ~14x · Yield: ~2%

Booz Allen is a ~$12B-revenue management and technology consultancy that works almost exclusively for the U.S. government — defense, intelligence, and civil agencies — on AI, cyber, digital transformation, and engineering. It is the most "consulting" of the federal contractors, with high headcount and premium billing rates, and had been the sector's growth leader for years.

2025 was brutal: the administration's efficiency push targeted consulting contracts specifically, Booz Allen was named in the cuts, civil-agency revenue fell, the company laid off thousands, and the stock fell by roughly half from its peak. Defense and intelligence work held up. It now trades at a value multiple with a dividend and buybacks, and the question is whether civil comes back or the company is permanently smaller.

Shines when: Defense and intelligence budgets grow, civil-agency spending stabilizes, AI and cyber work expands, and margins recover from the reset.

Dangers: Political targeting of consulting spend. Civil-agency revenue decline. A headcount cost structure that can't flex fast enough. Contract protests and recompetes.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends March). Civil vs. defense/intel revenue trend, backlog, headcount, adjusted EBITDA margin.

How to use it: A fallen-angel value setup; earnings reactions ±10–15% since the reset. The two-month setup is a defense-budget catalyst or a civil-stabilization quarter. If stuck, the dividend and buyback are intact, but this is a name that needs Washington to change its mind.

Payroll, back office & financial data

ADP Automatic Data Processing

Sector: Payroll / HR Software · Market cap: ~$120B · P/E: ~28x · Yield: ~2%

ADP processes payroll for roughly one in six American private-sector workers — about 1.1 million client companies, 40 million workers, in 140 countries — and generates ~$21B of revenue. Employer Services (payroll, HR, benefits, tax filing) is the core; the PEO business (ADP TotalSource) co-employs workers for small businesses and passes through their benefits costs, which inflates revenue and depresses margin. Retention runs above 90%; switching payroll providers is a nightmare, so clients don't.

The quiet second engine is client funds interest. ADP collects payroll taxes and wages from employers days before it pays them out, holding an average float in the tens of billions. It invests that in a laddered bond portfolio and earns well over $1B a year in interest — so the rate cycle shows up directly in EPS. Fifty consecutive years of dividend increases; buybacks retire a percent or two of shares annually. The valuation is rarely below 25x.

Shines when: U.S. employment grows (pays-per-control, ADP's own client headcount metric), rates stay elevated, new bookings accelerate, and PEO worksite employees grow.

Dangers: A recession shrinks client headcount and bookings. Falling rates reduce float income with a lag as the bond ladder rolls. Competition from Paycom, Paylocity, Rippling, and Gusto in small and mid-market. The stock underperformed in 2025 as bookings decelerated and the compounder multiple compressed.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends June). New business bookings growth, pays-per-control, client funds interest yield, PEO worksite employee growth. The monthly ADP National Employment Report is a free preview of its own headcount trend.

How to use it: Low beta — earnings reactions ±3–6% — so a 5–15% two-month move needs a catalyst like a rate-expectation shift or a rare de-rating drawdown to buy into. Better suited to the stuck bucket than the move bucket. If stuck, it's among the best holds in this guide.

PAYX Paychex

Sector: Payroll / HR Services · Market cap: ~$50B · P/E: ~28x · Yield: ~3%

Paychex serves about 800,000 clients, most with fewer than 100 employees, generating ~$6B of revenue after the 2025 Paycor acquisition (~$4.1B) added a mid-market, modern-interface platform. Same model as ADP: recurring payroll and HR fees, a PEO and insurance-agency business, and interest on client funds held between collection and payout. Paychex historically pays out most of its earnings as dividends and has a lower-growth, higher-yield profile than ADP.

The Paycor deal was a change of character — Paychex took on real debt for the first time in decades and bought growth it wasn't generating organically. The integration is the story for the next few years: cross-selling Paycor into the base and moving upmarket without losing the small-business focus that made it.

Shines when: Small-business hiring and formation are strong, rates stay elevated, Paycor cross-selling delivers revenue synergies, and the PEO grows.

Dangers: Small businesses cut headcount first in a downturn. Paycor integration and leverage. PEO health-insurance cost inflation. A premium multiple for mid-single-digit organic growth.

Watch: Reports late Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends May). Client count, revenue per client, float interest, Paycor synergy commentary, small-business employment data.

How to use it: Same rate-and-jobs catalysts as ADP with more small-business sensitivity, more yield, and slightly more earnings-day volatility (±4–8%). If stuck, the 3% yield plus float income make it a comfortable, if unexciting, hold.

BR Broadridge Financial Solutions

Sector: Investor Communications / Fintech Back Office · Market cap: ~$28B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~1.5%

Broadridge runs plumbing almost nobody outside finance has heard of: it processes the overwhelming majority of proxy votes and shareholder communications in North America (every annual report, proxy statement, and prospectus mailed or emailed to a brokerage customer), and it provides post-trade processing, wealth-management platforms, and data for broker-dealers and asset managers. About $7B of revenue, ~95% recurring, spun out of ADP in 2007 and run with the same culture.

Recurring revenue grows high single digits, margins expand slowly, the dividend has been raised every year since the spin, and the stock compounds without drama. It is a toll road on the securities industry's regulatory obligations — as long as companies have shareholders, Broadridge gets paid.

Shines when: Equity and fund position counts grow (more accounts, more communications), capital-markets technology sales close, and fee-based recurring revenue outgrows the low-margin distribution pass-through.

Dangers: Regulatory changes to proxy delivery or fees. Broker consolidation. Event-driven revenue (mutual-fund proxies) is lumpy. A premium multiple for high-single-digit growth.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). Recurring revenue growth, closed sales, event-driven revenue, adjusted operating margin.

How to use it: Low beta; earnings reactions ±4–8%. A poor two-month mover on its own, but it periodically gets dragged down by fintech or software selloffs it has nothing to do with — that's the entry. If stuck, an ideal hold.

SSNC SS&C Technologies

Sector: Financial Services Software · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: ~1%

SS&C provides fund administration, portfolio accounting, and back-office software and services to asset managers, hedge funds, private equity, insurers, and healthcare payers — ~$6.2B of revenue, ~95% recurring. Its products (Geneva, Advent, Eze, Black Diamond, plus the DST healthcare and retirement-recordkeeping businesses) run the accounting for a large share of the world's alternative assets. Founder Bill Stone still runs it and has built it through dozens of acquisitions financed with debt that is then paid down.

The model is buy, integrate, cut costs, delever, repeat. Organic growth is mid-single digits, margins are high (high-30s EBITDA), free cash flow is strong, and the stock trades at a mid-teens multiple because acquisition-driven companies with leverage don't get premium valuations. Dividend plus buybacks.

Shines when: Alternative-asset flows grow (more funds to administer), organic growth accelerates, acquisitions land, and leverage falls.

Dangers: Leverage. Acquisition integration. Fund-industry fee compression flowing down to service providers. Key-person risk on the founder-CEO. Slow organic growth.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic revenue growth, adjusted EBITDA margin, net leverage, acquisition announcements.

How to use it: A value compounder; earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a deal announcement or an organic-growth acceleration. If stuck, the cash flow and delevering work in your favor.

JKHY Jack Henry & Associates

Sector: Bank Technology · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~28x · Yield: ~1.5%

Jack Henry provides core banking systems, digital banking, and payment processing to community and regional banks and credit unions — ~$2.4B of revenue, ~90% recurring, with a customer base of thousands of small institutions that stay for decades. It is the third core-banking vendor alongside Fiserv and FIS, with a reputation for better service and technology, and is in the middle of a multi-year migration of its cores to a cloud-native platform.

Slow, steady, unleveraged: high-single-digit growth, a dividend raised for over thirty years, and a premium multiple for the quality. The main risk is its customers' consolidation — every time a small bank is acquired by a large one, Jack Henry may lose a core.

Shines when: Community-bank technology spending grows, cloud-core migrations drive higher revenue per client, payments (cards, bill pay, Zelle) grow, and consolidation slows.

Dangers: Bank M&A removing clients. A premium multiple. Competition from newer cloud cores and digital-banking platforms (Q2, Alkami, nCino). Slow growth.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). Non-GAAP revenue growth, core wins and losses, cloud migration count, operating margin.

How to use it: Low beta, low drama; earnings reactions ±4–8%. Not a natural two-month mover; better as a stuck-bucket holding bought during bank-sector selloffs, which drag it down without changing its business.

FDS FactSet

Sector: Financial Data · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.5%

FactSet sells financial data and analytics software to asset managers, banks, hedge funds, and wealth managers — ~$2.3B of revenue, ~95% recurring, with client retention in the high 90s. It competes with Bloomberg and LSEG at the terminal level and with S&P and MSCI on data, and has spent a decade broadening from a research platform into portfolio analytics, wealth management, and data feeds. Dividend raised for over twenty consecutive years; consistent buybacks.

The stock spent years as a quiet compounder, then de-rated in 2025 as the market decided AI would either replace financial analysts (FactSet's users) or let firms build their own data tools — the trade that hit Gartner, Roper, and the rest. A CEO transition in 2025 added uncertainty. It now trades at a multiple it hasn't seen in a decade.

Shines when: Organic ASV (annual subscription value) growth accelerates, wealth and buy-side seat counts recover, AI features drive upsell rather than displacement, and margins hold.

Dangers: Asset-manager consolidation and cost cutting reduce seats. AI disruption of analyst workflows. Competition from Bloomberg and the exchanges' data arms. New leadership.

Watch: Reports late Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends August). Organic ASV growth, client and user counts, adjusted operating margin, retention.

How to use it: A de-rated compounder; earnings reactions ±6–12% since 2025. The two-month setup is buying after a sector AI-scare and holding into an ASV-growth report. If stuck, a long dividend record and a subscription base — comfortable, though the narrative may take years to clear.

VRSK Verisk Analytics

Sector: Insurance Data & Analytics · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~35x · Yield: ~0.7%

Verisk sells data and analytics to the property-and-casualty insurance industry — ~$3B of revenue, nearly all subscription. Its ISO unit maintains the standardized policy forms, loss-cost data, and rating information that most U.S. insurers use to price policies; its claims business (Xactimate) is the software adjusters use to estimate repair costs on nearly every property claim; catastrophe modeling, underwriting data, and anti-fraud products round it out. Insurers contribute their data to Verisk and buy back the aggregated analytics — a network effect no competitor can replicate.

Having sold its energy and financial-services businesses, Verisk is a pure insurance play with 50%-plus EBITDA margins, mid-to-high single-digit organic growth, and steady buybacks. The multiple is high because the business is close to a regulated utility for the insurance industry, with pricing power and near-zero churn.

Shines when: Insurance premium growth and claims volume rise (catastrophe seasons, inflation in repair costs), new analytics products land, and pricing escalators kick in.

Dangers: A premium valuation. Insurer consolidation reduces the customer count. Regulatory scrutiny of ISO's role. AI enabling insurers to build their own analytics — the same long-tail risk as every data vendor.

Watch: Reports late Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic constant-currency growth, subscription growth, EBITDA margin, buyback pace.

How to use it: Low beta, low drama; earnings reactions ±4–7%. The two-month setup is a data-vendor selloff — when Gartner or FactSet report badly the whole group drops — dragging down a business with no exposure to the problem. If stuck, an excellent hold.

EFX Equifax

Sector: Credit Bureau / Workforce Data · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~0.7%

Equifax is one of the three U.S. credit bureaus, but its most valuable business is something else: Workforce Solutions, built on The Work Number — a database of payroll records from millions of employers that lets lenders, government agencies, and employers verify income and employment instantly. It has become the largest and highest-margin segment (~45% of ~$6B revenue, 50%-plus margins) and grows with mortgage volume, government benefits verification, and hiring. U.S. Information Solutions (the credit bureau) and International make up the rest.

After the 2017 data breach, Equifax spent years and billions rebuilding its technology on the cloud; the result is a leaner cost base and faster product velocity. The stock is a leveraged bet on mortgage activity — credit inquiries and income verifications spike when rates fall and refinancing picks up — layered on a data monopoly with structural growth.

Shines when: Mortgage volumes recover as rates fall, Workforce Solutions adds employer records and government contracts, and cloud-transition cost savings hit margins.

Dangers: Mortgage volume sensitivity — the stock fell sharply when rates spiked. Regulatory scrutiny of bureaus and verification pricing. Data security. Competition from Experian, TransUnion, and payroll-provider verification alternatives.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Workforce Solutions growth, mortgage-related revenue, non-mortgage growth, adjusted EBITDA margin, mortgage-rate trend.

How to use it: The most rate-sensitive name in this section — it moves on mortgage-rate expectations as much as its own results (earnings reactions ±6–12%). The two-month setup is a rate-cut cycle. If stuck, a data monopoly with structural growth; the only cost is waiting for the mortgage cycle.

TRU TransUnion

Sector: Credit Bureau · Market cap: ~$18B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~0.5%

TransUnion is the second of the three credit bureaus — ~$4.3B of revenue, majority U.S. Markets (credit reporting to lenders, marketing data, insurance underwriting data, and the Neustar identity business acquired in 2021), plus a sizable International segment led by India, the UK, and Canada. Its credit data is the same oligopoly as Equifax's; its differentiators are identity resolution and a faster-growing emerging-markets footprint.

More leverage than Equifax after the Neustar and Sontiq deals, which pressured the stock when rates rose; the company has been paying down debt and running a cost program. Growth is mid-to-high single digits with margin expansion.

Shines when: Consumer lending volumes rise, mortgage and auto inquiries recover, India and international grow double digits, and deleveraging progresses.

Dangers: The consumer credit cycle — fewer originations means fewer credit pulls. Regulatory pressure on bureaus. Leverage. Neustar integration and a history of overpaying for deals.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. U.S. Markets organic growth, financial-services vertical, international growth, net leverage, free cash flow.

How to use it: A more leveraged, more volatile version of EFX (earnings reactions ±7–12%); moves with consumer credit data and rates. If stuck, the business is durable, but leverage makes the drawdowns deeper.

FICO Fair Isaac

Sector: Credit Scoring / Analytics Software · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~55x · Yield: none

Fair Isaac owns the FICO Score — the credit score used in the overwhelming majority of U.S. consumer lending decisions, including nearly all mortgages sold to Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac. The Scores segment (~55% of ~$2B revenue, 90%-plus margins) licenses the score to the credit bureaus and lenders and has raised its price aggressively for years — mortgage score pricing rose several-fold over five years — with almost no volume loss. The Software segment sells decisioning and fraud-management platforms to banks.

It is one of the purest pricing-power stories in the market, and the stock has traded accordingly at 50–70x earnings. The risk that comes with that: the FHFA and the mortgage industry have pushed back on score pricing, VantageScore (owned by the bureaus) has been approved for mortgage use, and headlines about competition have produced 10–20% single-day drops. Massive buybacks, no dividend.

Shines when: Score price increases hold, mortgage origination volumes recover, software bookings grow, and regulatory noise fades.

Dangers: Regulatory and political intervention on score pricing. VantageScore adoption in mortgages. Mortgage-volume sensitivity. A valuation that assumes pricing power lasts forever.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends September). Scores revenue growth (mortgage vs. non-mortgage), software ARR, FHFA and Fannie/Freddie announcements.

How to use it: A monopoly at a monopoly price — the boring business with the least boring stock: earnings and regulatory reactions ±10–20%. The two-month setup is buying a regulatory-headline drop when the actual pricing is intact. If stuck, the business is extraordinary; the multiple can fall 40% without the business changing.

IT Gartner

Sector: Research & Advisory · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: none

Gartner sells research subscriptions, advisory, and conferences to technology and business executives — ~$6.5B of revenue, over 75% from Research (the Magic Quadrant reports, analyst inquiry, benchmarking) sold to CIOs and, through its GBS unit, to HR, finance, legal, and sales leaders. Contract value is the key metric; retention has historically been in the high 80s to low 90s, and the business compounded for two decades on price increases and seat growth.

Then in 2025 the stock fell by roughly half: contract-value growth slowed, federal government cuts hit its public-sector business, and the market decided generative AI could answer the questions executives pay Gartner to answer. The company responded with buybacks — it retired a large chunk of its shares at the lows — and the multiple went from 35x to the mid-teens. No dividend.

Shines when: Contract-value growth reaccelerates, the federal drag laps, GBS grows double digits, and AI is shown to increase demand for research rather than replace it.

Dangers: AI disruption of research and advisory — the core bear case. Federal and enterprise budget cuts. Sales-force productivity. A deep de-rating that could go further if contract value keeps decelerating.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Contract-value growth (Research, GBS), retention rates, sales headcount, buyback pace.

How to use it: A fallen compounder; earnings reactions ±10–20% since the de-rating. The two-month setup is a contract-value stabilization quarter. If stuck, the buyback is aggressive and the business is still very profitable, but this is one where the market may be right about structural change — hold it as a bet, not a certainty.

Payments

FI Fiserv

Sector: Payments / Bank Technology · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~11x · Yield: none

Fiserv is the plumbing on both sides of a card transaction. Its Financial Solutions segment sells core banking systems, digital banking, and card-issuer processing to thousands of banks and credit unions. Its Merchant Solutions segment (from the 2019 First Data merger) acquires payments for millions of merchants, anchored by Clover — a point-of-sale hardware-and-software platform for small and mid-sized businesses that grew 20–30% a year and was the growth engine the whole stock was priced on. About $21B of revenue.

For a decade Fiserv was a metronome: double-digit adjusted EPS growth, buybacks, a steadily rising stock. Then in July 2025 Clover growth slowed and the stock fell ~14%; in October 2025 a new CEO cut guidance sharply, the stock fell roughly 44% in a day — the worst in its history — and the market concluded the old EPS growth had been partly manufactured. Now it trades at a value multiple with a credibility problem and a reset base.

Shines when: Clover growth stabilizes, merchant volumes hold, the reset guidance is beaten rather than met, and buybacks resume at scale.

Dangers: The trust deficit — investors won't pay up again until several clean quarters print. Merchant competition from Toast, Square, Shift4, and Stripe. Bank consolidation shrinks the core-processing client list. Leverage from the First Data deal.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Clover revenue growth and gross payment volume, merchant organic growth, Financial Solutions organic growth, free cash flow.

How to use it: A recovery setup — cheap on every metric, waiting for proof. Earnings reactions are now ±10–20%, so a two-month position is essentially an earnings bet. If stuck, the cash flows are real and the buyback will eventually do its work; the question is whether the multiple recovers in one year or five.

FIS Fidelity National Information Services

Sector: Bank Technology · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~14x · Yield: ~2%

FIS sells core banking software (the systems that run deposits, loans, and general ledgers), digital banking, and card-issuer processing to financial institutions worldwide — ~$10B of revenue, most of it recurring. In 2019 it bought Worldpay for $43B, one of the worst large-cap deals of the era; in 2024 it sold a majority of Worldpay to GTCR, and in 2025 sold the remaining stake to Global Payments while taking GPN's issuer-processing business in return. The result is a pure bank-and-issuer technology company with no merchant acquiring.

Growth is low — mid-single digits — but the revenue is sticky (banks replace core systems roughly once a generation) and the cash flow supports a dividend and buybacks. The stock spent years in the penalty box for the Worldpay debacle; the Global Payments swap was an attempt to make it simple again.

Shines when: Bank IT spending is stable, issuer-processing integration delivers synergies, recurring revenue growth ticks toward high-single digits, and leverage comes down.

Dangers: A slow-growth business with a history of execution disappointments. Bank consolidation removes clients. Debt. Competition from Fiserv and Jack Henry in cores.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Banking Solutions organic growth, issuer integration milestones, free cash flow conversion, leverage ratio.

How to use it: A restructuring story at a value multiple; earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a clean quarter after the issuer business lands. If stuck, dividend plus buybacks, but the growth ceiling is low and the stock can sit.

GPN Global Payments

Sector: Merchant Acquiring · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~8x · Yield: ~1%

Global Payments processes card transactions for merchants — and after buying Worldpay in a ~$24B deal from FIS and GTCR, it is one of the largest merchant acquirers on earth, processing on the order of $4 trillion of annual volume across small businesses, enterprises, and e-commerce. In the same transaction it handed its issuer-processing business to FIS, making it a merchant pure-play. Roughly $13B of pro forma revenue.

The stock trades at a single-digit multiple because the market doubts three things: that the Worldpay integration works, that legacy acquirers can hold share against software-led competitors like Adyen, Stripe, and Toast, and that the debt taken on for the deal gets paid down before the next downturn. It is the deepest value in payments.

Shines when: Worldpay synergies materialize on schedule, merchant volumes grow, e-commerce and enterprise wins offset small-business share loss, and leverage falls.

Dangers: Large-deal integration risk. Structural share loss to modern acquirers. Leverage. Sentiment so skeptical that even good quarters get sold.

Watch: Reports late Feb/early May/Aug/Nov. Merchant organic growth, synergy realization, net leverage, free cash flow.

How to use it: The deep-value cousin of Fiserv — high beta for a supposedly boring processor, with earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is an integration-milestone quarter. If stuck, cheap with real cash flow, but this is the archetype of a value stock that stays cheap for years.

CPAY Corpay

Sector: Corporate Payments · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: none

Corpay (formerly FleetCor) is a ~$4.3B-revenue business-payments company built on three franchises: Vehicle Payments (fuel cards for fleets in the U.S., Brazil, and the UK, plus tolls in Brazil), Corporate Payments (accounts-payable automation, virtual cards, and cross-border FX payments — the fastest-growing piece), and Lodging (hotel payments for workforce travel). It earns interchange, transaction fees, and float, and has compounded EPS at mid-teens rates for a decade through acquisitions and pricing.

The stock trades at a discount to the payments peer group because the fuel-card business is perceived as legacy and Brazil adds currency noise; management rebranded to Corpay to emphasize corporate payments. Heavy buybacks, no dividend, moderate leverage.

Shines when: Corporate-payments and cross-border volumes grow, fuel prices are stable (revenue has some fuel-price sensitivity), Brazil is calm, and acquisitions land.

Dangers: Fuel-price and fleet-volume sensitivity. Brazil currency and politics. Regulatory scrutiny (the FTC sued over fee practices). Leverage. A sprawling acquired portfolio.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Organic revenue growth by segment, corporate-payments growth, retention, fuel-price impact, buyback pace.

How to use it: A cheap payments compounder; earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a clean corporate-payments quarter or a Brazil-scare pullback. If stuck, the buyback works in your favor and the business is durable.

WEX WEX

Sector: Fleet & Benefits Payments · Market cap: ~$6B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: none

WEX is Corpay's smaller rival: ~$2.6B of revenue from fleet fuel cards (Mobility), health savings account and benefits administration (Benefits — a large HSA custodian earning float on account balances), and corporate and travel payments (virtual cards for online travel agencies). The Benefits segment gives it a rate-sensitivity angle: HSA balances earn interest that flows to WEX.

The stock de-rated hard in 2024–25 as fuel prices fell, a large travel customer moved volume in-house, and growth stalled; management responded with a large tender-offer buyback at the lows. It now trades at a single-digit multiple with a business that still generates substantial cash.

Shines when: Fuel prices firm (revenue rises with fuel), travel-payments volume recovers, HSA balances grow with rates elevated, and the buyback shrinks the float.

Dangers: Fuel-price sensitivity. Customer concentration in travel. Falling rates reduce HSA float income. Leverage from the tender. Small-cap liquidity.

Watch: Reports early Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Segment revenue growth, fuel-price impact, HSA account growth and custodial balances, adjusted EPS, leverage.

How to use it: A deep-value cyclical; earnings reactions ±10–15%. The two-month setup is a fuel-price rally or a travel-volume recovery. If stuck, cheap with cash flow, but customer concentration and fuel exposure make it the riskier of the two fleet-card names.

Components, connectors & contract manufacturing

GLW Corning

Sector: Specialty Glass / Optical · Market cap: ~$60B · P/E: ~28x · Yield: ~2%

Corning is a 170-year-old materials science company with about $15B of core sales across five segments. Optical Communications (now the largest, ~40%, and growing fastest) makes fiber-optic cable and connectivity for telecom carriers and, critically, for AI data centers, where GPU clusters need orders of magnitude more fiber between racks than conventional servers. Display Technologies makes the glass substrates in most TVs and monitors. Specialty Materials is Gorilla Glass for phones and Corning's optical-glass work. Environmental Technologies makes ceramic substrates and filters for vehicle emissions. Life Sciences sells lab glassware and consumables. It also owns Hemlock Semiconductor, a U.S. polysilicon producer feeding domestic solar and semiconductor wafers.

The AI build-out changed Corning's identity from sleepy display supplier to optical growth name: multi-year supply agreements with hyperscalers and carriers, a "Springboard" plan targeting billions in incremental sales, and margin expansion as optical scales. The stock roughly doubled in 2025. Berkshire-style patience runs the place — heavy R&D, patient capital, a dividend raised most years.

Shines when: Data-center fiber orders ramp, carrier fiber builds (federal broadband funding, AT&T's fiber expansion) accelerate, domestic solar content rules favor Hemlock, and display pricing holds.

Dangers: Display is mature and deflationary. The business is capital-intensive and cyclical across nearly every end market — autos, TVs, phones, telecom capex. Fiber demand is lumpy; a hyperscaler capex pause would hit hard. After doubling, the stock prices in the optical story.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Optical Communications growth split enterprise vs. carrier, core operating margin, Springboard progress, hyperscaler capex guidance.

How to use it: A cyclical with a secular kicker — moves on hyperscaler capex signals and its own optical numbers (earnings reactions ±6–12%). If stuck, the yield and diversified industrial base cushion you, but a de-rating from AI-story multiples back to industrial multiples is a real drawdown path.

TEL TE Connectivity

Sector: Connectors / Sensors · Market cap: ~$60B · P/E: ~24x · Yield: ~1.7%

TE Connectivity makes ~$17B a year of connectors, sensors, relays, and harness components — the parts that carry power and signal between everything else. Transportation Solutions (~55%: automotive, commercial vehicles, sensors) rides global light-vehicle production but grows faster than it because content per vehicle keeps rising: an EV or hybrid uses substantially more high-voltage connectors and sensors than a combustion car. Industrial Solutions (~45%: factory automation, aerospace and defense, energy, medical, and data center) is the part that has become interesting — AI servers need high-speed and high-power connectors, and grid modernization needs TE's energy components.

Swiss-domiciled, run conservatively: steady buybacks, a dividend raised annually, and a cost structure that flexed well through the 2023–24 auto and industrial softness. Margins expanded through the downturn, which is the tell of a well-managed parts supplier.

Shines when: Auto production is stable to rising, EV and hybrid mix increases content, data-center and grid connector orders accelerate, and industrial destocking ends.

Dangers: Half the revenue rides auto cycles; a production cut in Europe or China shows up immediately. Tariffs and China exposure (a large manufacturing and sales footprint). Currency. Copper and gold input costs.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends September). Organic growth by segment, industrial orders, content-per-vehicle commentary, book-to-bill. Global light-vehicle production forecasts from S&P Global Mobility.

How to use it: Moves with auto production data and AI capex; earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is buying into an industrial destocking trough or after a European auto scare. If stuck, a wide-moat parts supplier with steady cash return — very comfortable.

APH Amphenol

Sector: Connectors / Interconnect · Market cap: ~$140B · P/E: ~38x · Yield: ~0.6%

Amphenol is TE's main rival and the best operator in the category — $20B-plus of revenue growing at rates no parts supplier should. Its structure is the moat: well over a hundred autonomous business units, each with its own P&L and general manager, coordinated by a small headquarters that allocates capital and buys companies. Products span high-speed and power connectors, cable assemblies, antennas, sensors, and fiber, sold into IT and datacom (now the largest end market and the fastest-growing, on AI servers and switches), mobile devices, automotive, industrial, aerospace, defense, and broadband.

It has bought its way into scale — CommScope's connectivity business, Carlisle Interconnect, and dozens of smaller deals — and integrates them without missing a beat. Margins expand nearly every year; the stock split 2-for-1 in 2024 and has compounded at rates that embarrass most software companies. The valuation reflects it: a high-30s earnings multiple for an industrial.

Shines when: AI data-center interconnect demand surges, acquired businesses integrate and re-rate, and operating margins set new records.

Dangers: A premium valuation with no room for error. IT and datacom has become a large enough slice that a hyperscaler capex pause would hit hard. Mobile devices is cyclical. Continuous dealmaking carries integration risk.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic growth by end market, IT datacom growth rate, operating margin, book-to-bill, acquisition announcements.

How to use it: The quality-at-a-price version of TEL. Earnings reactions ±6–12% — sharp for an industrial. The two-month setup is a pullback on sector-wide capex fear rather than a company problem. If stuck, you own the best operator in industrial tech, but the multiple can compress 30% without anything going wrong.

KEYS Keysight Technologies

Sector: Test & Measurement · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: none

Keysight is the old Hewlett-Packard test-and-measurement business, spun out of Agilent in 2014. About $5.5–6B of revenue, with Spirent (acquired 2025) adding network and security testing. Communications Solutions (~70%) sells oscilloscopes, signal analyzers, and network test systems to wireless, wireline, and aerospace/defense customers — every 5G and future 6G radio, every high-speed data-center link, is designed and validated on this equipment. Electronic Industrial Solutions (~30%) serves semiconductor, automotive, and energy customers. Software and services are ~40% of revenue and rising.

Boring on the surface, essential in every technology cycle: nobody ships a chip or a radio without measuring it. Mid-20s operating margins, strong free cash flow, and buybacks rather than a dividend. The business tracks R&D spending, which is more stable than production spending but still cyclical.

Shines when: Wireless standards transition (6G research, satellite comms), semiconductor design activity and AI data-center network testing ramp, defense electronics budgets grow, and Spirent cross-selling delivers.

Dangers: Tied to R&D budgets, which get cut in downturns. China exposure and export controls. Lumpy large orders. Spirent integration.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends October). Orders and book-to-bill, software and services mix, China revenue, semiconductor segment trend.

How to use it: Picks-and-shovels for technology R&D; earnings reactions ±5–10%. It often bottoms before the broad semiconductor cycle because orders lead production. If stuck, no yield, but the cash flow and buyback make it a long-term compounder.

JBL Jabil

Sector: Electronics Manufacturing Services · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~0.2%

Jabil is a ~$29B-revenue contract manufacturer — it builds other companies' products: AI data-center racks and liquid-cooling systems for hyperscalers, networking equipment, medical devices, automotive electronics, retail and payment hardware, and industrial systems. It exited most of its Apple-related mobility work in 2024 (selling that business to BYD Electronics), which cut revenue but improved margins and removed customer concentration. Segments now: Regulated Industries (healthcare, auto, energy), Intelligent Infrastructure (cloud, data center, networking — the growth engine), and Connected Living and Digital Commerce.

EMS is a thin-margin business (mid-single-digit operating margin), but Jabil has been re-rated because AI infrastructure gave it a growth story, it buys back stock aggressively, and it has more U.S. manufacturing capacity than peers as tariffs push reshoring.

Shines when: AI data-center rack and cooling demand grows, U.S. reshoring drives new programs, healthcare and auto electronics hold, and margins expand from mix.

Dangers: Thin margins with no cushion. Hyperscaler capex cyclicality. Customer concentration in cloud. Tariff whiplash on global manufacturing. Working-capital intensity.

Watch: Reports mid Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends August). Intelligent Infrastructure growth, core operating margin, free cash flow, AI-related revenue commentary.

How to use it: An AI-capex cyclical with an industrial multiple; earnings reactions ±8–15%. It moves with Dell and Super Micro. If stuck, the buyback and diversified end markets help, but it is not a name to hold through a capex downturn.

FLEX Flex

Sector: Electronics Manufacturing Services · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: none

Flex is Jabil's closest peer — ~$27B of revenue from contract manufacturing for data centers (server and storage racks, and, through acquisitions like Anord Mardix and JetCool, the power distribution and liquid cooling that AI facilities need), automotive, healthcare, industrial, and consumer devices. Singapore-domiciled, spun off its Nextracker solar-tracker stake in 2024, and has repositioned toward higher-margin "value-added" manufacturing and power and cooling products.

Same thin-margin economics as Jabil, same AI-driven re-rating, same buyback story. The differentiation is Flex's power and cooling product business, which gives it some proprietary-product margin on top of contract manufacturing.

Shines when: Data-center power and cooling demand grows, AI rack integration scales, auto and healthcare hold, and margins expand.

Dangers: Same as Jabil — thin margins, hyperscaler capex cycles, tariffs, working capital. Automotive weakness in Europe.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends March). Data-center revenue growth, adjusted operating margin, free cash flow, power-products commentary.

How to use it: A pair with JBL — they move together on AI-capex sentiment and diverge on execution. If stuck, the same caveat: not a hold through a capex downturn.

ARW Arrow Electronics

Sector: Electronic Components Distribution · Market cap: ~$7B · P/E: ~12x · Yield: none

Arrow distributes semiconductors and electronic components from hundreds of suppliers to hundreds of thousands of industrial, automotive, and electronics customers — ~$30B of revenue on razor-thin margins (low-single-digit operating margin) — plus an enterprise computing solutions business that resells servers, storage, and software. It is the plumbing between chipmakers and the companies that solder their chips onto boards, and its results are a real-time read on the component cycle.

Distribution is a working-capital business: Arrow's earnings swing with inventory cycles and pricing, it has almost no pricing power of its own, and it trades at a single-digit to low-teens multiple. It buys back stock steadily and has no dividend. The 2023–25 component downturn hit it hard; a recovery in industrial and automotive demand is the setup.

Shines when: The component inventory cycle turns (book-to-bill above one), industrial and auto demand recovers, and enterprise computing spending picks up.

Dangers: Deeply cyclical, thin margins, working-capital intensity. Suppliers going direct to large customers. Tariffs on components. Low margin means small revenue moves swing EPS a lot.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Components book-to-bill by region, inventory days, operating margin, enterprise computing growth.

How to use it: A cycle-turn cyclical; earnings reactions ±8–12%. The two-month setup is buying when book-to-bill crosses above one. If stuck, cheap and buying back stock, but it can drift for years in a component downturn.

AVT Avnet

Sector: Electronic Components Distribution · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: ~2.5%

Avnet is Arrow's smaller rival — ~$23B of revenue distributing semiconductors and components, plus Farnell, a catalog distributor of small-quantity parts to engineers. Same thin-margin, working-capital-intensive economics, same cycle sensitivity, with more exposure to Asia and a dividend that Arrow doesn't pay.

Trades at a low multiple, buys back stock, and moves with the same component-cycle data as Arrow. Farnell had a difficult few years and is the turnaround piece.

Shines when: The component cycle turns, Asia demand recovers, Farnell margins recover, and inventory normalizes.

Dangers: Same as Arrow, plus a weaker margin profile and small-cap liquidity.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends June). Book-to-bill by region, inventory, Farnell operating margin.

How to use it: A cheaper, smaller, higher-yielding ARW — pair-trade material. If stuck, the dividend helps, but the cycle governs everything.

Semiconductors & chip design

MCHP Microchip Technology

Sector: Microcontrollers / Analog · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~40x trough, ~20x normalized · Yield: ~3%

Microchip sells microcontrollers, analog and mixed-signal chips, FPGAs, and timing products — the low-cost brains and interfaces in cars, industrial equipment, appliances, medical devices, and data-center power systems. Over 100,000 customers, none dominant; tens of thousands of part numbers; a franchise built on long product lifecycles and sticky design wins. Peak revenue was ~$8.4B in fiscal 2023.

Then came one of the worst inventory corrections in analog history: customers had double-ordered through the shortage, and revenue collapsed roughly 40% peak to trough by fiscal 2025. Founder Steve Sanghi returned as CEO in late 2024, cut costs, idled a fab, and worked down inventory. The dividend — grown for years — briefly exceeded free cash flow at the trough. Gross margins that touched 68% fell into the 50s and are climbing back.

Shines when: The analog and MCU inventory cycle turns up, bookings and book-to-bill improve, industrial and auto demand recover, and gross margin climbs back toward the 60s.

Dangers: Deeply cyclical — the boring-but-cyclical semi. Competition from Texas Instruments, NXP, STMicro, Renesas, and Infineon. Auto demand and Chinese local competition. Leverage taken on for past acquisitions (Microsemi).

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Bookings, book-to-bill, inventory days, gross margin trajectory, distributor sell-through.

How to use it: A cycle-turn stock; earnings reactions ±8–12%. Bought early in a recovery it delivers 30–50% over a year; bought at a peak, the drawdown is the same size in reverse. If stuck, the 3% yield helps, but a stuck MCHP position at the wrong point in the cycle is a two-year wait.

NXPI NXP Semiconductors

Sector: Automotive / Industrial Semis · Market cap: ~$55B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~2%

NXP is the largest automotive semiconductor supplier — ~$12B of revenue, roughly half from autos: radar chips for driver assistance, battery management for EVs, secure car access, infotainment and vehicle-network processors. Industrial and IoT is the second segment (factory automation, edge processors), then Mobile (the NFC and secure-element chips behind tap-to-pay on most Android phones), then Communications Infrastructure. Dutch-domiciled, formed from Philips Semiconductors and Freescale.

The long-term story is content growth — the average car carries several hundred dollars of semiconductors and rising, and NXP is positioned in the fastest-growing pieces (radar, software-defined-vehicle processors). The short-term story is the auto and industrial inventory cycle, which has been correcting since 2023 and kept recovering later than management hoped. Dividend plus consistent buybacks.

Shines when: Auto production grows, content per vehicle rises, the industrial cycle turns, and Chinese auto demand holds.

Dangers: The auto cycle. Chinese EV makers shifting to local chip suppliers. Inventory corrections that drag on. Tariffs and trade tension with China, a large market. A recovery that keeps arriving next quarter.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Auto segment growth, distributor inventory, gross margin, China revenue. Texas Instruments, STMicro, and ON report around the same time and move it.

How to use it: The cleanest way to own boring auto semis; earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a post-earnings dip on "recovery delayed" ahead of improving auto production data. If stuck, dividend and buybacks, but it can trade sideways for years in an auto slowdown.

CDNS Cadence Design Systems

Sector: EDA Software · Market cap: ~$95B · P/E: ~55x · Yield: none

Cadence is one of two companies (with Synopsys) that make the software every chip is designed with — a duopoly with roughly 85%-plus recurring revenue, multi-year contracts, and customers (Nvidia, Apple, TSMC, Broadcom, the hyperscalers designing custom silicon) who cannot switch. About $5.2B of revenue: core EDA (digital and custom design, verification), IP (licensed design blocks), hardware emulation and prototyping systems (Palladium, Protium — a large and growing piece as chips get too complex to verify in software), and system design and analysis (thermal, electromagnetic, and computational fluid dynamics for boards, packages, and whole systems).

Every advance in chip complexity — 3-nanometer transistors, chiplets, AI accelerators — requires more design software, so Cadence grows double digits through every cycle with 40%-plus operating margins. The valuation has reflected it for years: north of 50x earnings, which means a quarter of modest guidance produces a 10% drop.

Shines when: Semiconductor design activity expands (AI accelerators, custom hyperscaler silicon, automotive), hardware emulation cycles ramp, China restrictions ease, and system-analysis products grow.

Dangers: A premium valuation with no room for error. Export controls on China, a meaningful market. Slowing design starts in a semiconductor downturn. Customer concentration. AI could compress design cycles in a way that reduces seat demand — the long-tail bear case.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Backlog and bookings, hardware emulation revenue, China revenue, operating margin, full-year guidance revisions.

How to use it: The expensive, high-quality end of boring tech; earnings reactions ±8–12%. The two-month setup is a sector-wide semiconductor selloff dragging down a company whose revenue doesn't fall in semiconductor downturns. If stuck, the business is bulletproof — the multiple isn't, and a de-rating from 55x to 35x is a 35% drawdown with nothing wrong.

SNPS Synopsys

Sector: EDA / Simulation Software · Market cap: ~$90B · P/E: ~35x adjusted · Yield: none

Synopsys is the larger of the two EDA companies, with ~$7B of revenue growing toward $9B pro forma after closing the $35B acquisition of Ansys in July 2025. Design Automation (core EDA: synthesis, place-and-route, verification, plus hardware emulation) is the anchor. Design IP licenses interface and processor blocks (USB, PCIe, DDR, ARC processors) that chip designers buy rather than build — the second-largest IP vendor after Arm. Ansys brought engineering simulation software (structural, fluid, electromagnetic, optical) used by every aerospace, auto, and industrial company on earth, on the thesis that chip design and physical-system simulation converge.

Two things went wrong in 2025. The IP business, which had grown steadily, stumbled as customers delayed and China restrictions bit; and in September 2025 a guidance cut sent the stock down roughly 35% in a day. Meanwhile the Ansys deal added debt and integration risk to a company that had been debt-free. The core EDA franchise is untouched — it's the wrapper that got complicated.

Shines when: Ansys cross-selling delivers, the IP business recovers, China restrictions ease, AI chip design keeps demand for design tools high, and debt is paid down.

Dangers: Post-Ansys leverage and integration. IP business volatility — a lumpy, project-driven revenue line. China export exposure. Credibility rebuilt slowly after the 2025 shock. Same long-tail AI-compresses-design-cycles risk as Cadence.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends October). Design Automation growth, IP growth, Ansys integration milestones and synergy targets, China revenue, leverage.

How to use it: A recovery setup on a duopoly business, cheaper than Cadence on a relative basis; earnings reactions ±10–20% since the 2025 shock. The two-month setup is a clean quarter that restores credibility. If stuck, you hold an irreplaceable software franchise working through a big integration — the downside is time, not permanence.

Internet infrastructure & software

VRSN VeriSign

Sector: Internet Infrastructure · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~1%

VeriSign runs the .com and .net registries — about $1.6B of revenue, 65%-plus operating margins, and one of the cleanest business models in existence. Every .com registration or renewal (~160 million domains) pays VeriSign a wholesale fee under an exclusive contract with ICANN that includes pre-approved price increases of up to 7% in four of every six years.
