It also operates two of the thirteen root DNS servers that make the internet resolve names. Capital spending is negligible; nearly all cash goes to buybacks, which have roughly halved the share count in fifteen years. Berkshire Hathaway has been a top holder for over a decade. A dividend was initiated in 2025.

The only debate is the domain base: it declined for several years as Chinese speculative registrations lapsed, and price increases carried revenue while volume shrank. The bull case is that the base stabilizes and pricing plus buybacks compound EPS at high single digits indefinitely; the bear case is regulatory pushback on pricing when the .com contract renews in 2030.

Shines when: The domain base grows or stops shrinking, contractual price increases kick in, buybacks accelerate, and the ICANN relationship stays quiet.

Dangers: Domain base decline. Regulatory scrutiny of monopoly pricing. Contract renewal risk in 2030. Alternative top-level domains and the slow decline of the domain as an identity layer.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Domain base net adds, renewal rate, price-increase timing, buyback pace.

How to use it: A near-monopoly toll road with almost no volatility — earnings reactions ±2–5%, and 5%-plus two-month moves are rare. Better as ballast than as a mover. The stuck bucket's stuck bucket.

ROP Roper Technologies

Sector: Vertical Software · Market cap: ~$50B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~0.7%

Roper Technologies is a holding company for niche vertical-market software — ~$7.5B of revenue from dozens of businesses that each dominate a small market: Deltek (government-contractor ERP), Aderant (law-firm software), Vertafore (insurance-agency software), Procare (childcare management), Frontline (K-12 HR), ConstructConnect, Strata (healthcare finance), and more, plus a shrinking legacy of test-and-measurement and industrial products. It started as an industrial pump company and, over twenty years, sold the industrials and bought software.

The model is compounding cash flow: buy software businesses with high retention and pricing power, leave management in place, harvest the cash, repeat. High free-cash-flow conversion, low capex, consistent tuck-in acquisitions, and a small dividend raised annually. The stock fell in 2025 as organic growth slowed toward mid-single digits and the market decided AI would disrupt niche software — the trade that hit everything that sells seats.

Shines when: Organic growth reaccelerates, acquisitions land accretively, the AI-disruption narrative fades or Roper shows its products benefit from AI, and leverage is deployed into good deals.

Dangers: AI disruption of vertical software is the bear case — if each niche can be replaced by a general model plus a prompt, the moat erodes. Growth depends on continuous acquisition. Leverage rises around deals. Multiple compression continues if growth doesn't return.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic growth, application-software segment growth, net retention, acquisition announcements, leverage.

How to use it: A quality compounder on sale after a de-rating; earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a sector-wide software selloff that drags Roper down with businesses it doesn't resemble. If stuck, one of the most durable cash-flow profiles in software.

FFIV F5

Sector: Application Delivery / Security · Market cap: ~$17B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: none

F5 sells the BIG-IP product line — application delivery controllers (load balancers) that sit in front of applications in nearly every large enterprise and manage traffic, availability, security, and access — plus NGINX (the open-source web server it acquired), distributed cloud services, and API security. About $3B of revenue, roughly half systems (hardware) and half software and services. Growth is low, but gross margins are above 80% and free cash flow is large, mostly returned through buybacks.

The company has spent years transitioning from hardware boxes to software subscriptions and SaaS, with mixed results: hardware refresh cycles still drive the biggest swings, and AI application traffic recently drove a capacity upgrade wave. In October 2025 F5 disclosed that a nation-state actor had breached its systems and exfiltrated BIG-IP source code and vulnerability data — a serious event for a security vendor, and one the stock absorbed reasonably well.

Shines when: Hardware refresh cycles hit (AI workloads driving capacity upgrades), software subscriptions grow, margins expand, and the breach fades from the narrative.

Dangers: Cloud providers offer native load balancing that shrinks F5's addressable market. Security incidents. Low growth caps the multiple. Lumpy hardware demand.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends September). Systems revenue growth, software subscription growth, gross margin, buyback pace.

How to use it: Cheap infrastructure with periodic refresh-driven upside; earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a refresh-cycle quarter. If stuck, cash-rich and buying back stock; low drama.

AKAM Akamai

Sector: CDN / Cloud Security · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~13x · Yield: none

Akamai invented the content delivery network in 1998 and still runs one of the world's largest, but the business has been rebuilt around it. Of ~$4B of revenue, Security (bot management, web application firewall, zero-trust access, API protection, DDoS mitigation) is now the largest segment at over half and growing high single to low double digits. Cloud Compute (built on the 2022 Linode acquisition) is the smallest but fastest-growing, an attempt to sell distributed compute to customers who already run traffic through Akamai. Delivery (the original CDN business) shrinks every year as the largest customers — Netflix, Meta, Apple — build their own networks.

The stock trades on cash flow, not growth: consistent buybacks, no dividend, and a low-teens multiple because the market sees a melting ice cube with a security business bolted on rather than a security company with legacy drag.

Shines when: Security and compute growth outpaces delivery decline, compute reaches scale and margin, and the market re-rates it as a security company.

Dangers: Delivery decline is structural and accelerating. The compute pivot competes with hyperscalers on their home turf. Cloudflare and Fastly in delivery and security. Low growth; the transition has been "next year" for a while.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Security growth, compute growth, delivery decline rate, operating margin.

How to use it: A value name in transition; earnings reactions ±8–12%. The two-month setup is buying after a delivery-driven miss when security growth is intact. If stuck, cheap on cash flow, but the melting-ice-cube label keeps the multiple pinned.

EBAY eBay

Sector: E-commerce Marketplace · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: ~1.5%

The original online marketplace, now a mature platform with ~$10.5B of revenue on ~$75B of gross merchandise volume. eBay stopped trying to be Amazon a decade ago and focused on "enthusiast" categories where a marketplace of individual sellers beats a warehouse: collectibles (trading cards, coins, stamps — with authentication), auto parts and accessories (the largest category), refurbished electronics, luxury watches and handbags (authenticated), and pre-owned fashion. It sold PayPal, StubHub, and its classifieds businesses over the years and returned the proceeds.

GMV is flat to slightly up; profit growth comes from raising the take rate (managed payments, promoted-listings advertising — now over $2B a year), cost control, and enormous buybacks that have cut the share count by roughly 40% in a decade. A dividend since 2019. The stock is a low-growth cash machine that periodically re-rates when a category (trading cards in 2020–21, collectibles again in 2025) catches a wave.

Shines when: Focus categories grow, advertising revenue expands, buybacks compound EPS on flat sales, and international (Germany, UK) stabilizes.

Dangers: GMV stagnation. Amazon, Temu, and social commerce. Tariff and de minimis rule changes hitting cross-border trade. Take-rate increases that push sellers to alternatives. Currency.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. GMV growth, focus-category growth, take rate, advertising revenue, buyback pace.

How to use it: A low-growth cash machine; earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a GMV inflection in a focus category. If stuck, buybacks and dividend — very comfortable.

GDDY GoDaddy

Sector: Domains / Small-Business Software · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: none

GoDaddy is the world's largest domain registrar (~85 million domains under management) and sells the things small businesses need next: website builders, hosting, email, and payments and commerce tools, bundled into its Airo platform. About $5B of revenue: Applications and Commerce (the higher-growth, higher-margin half) and Core Platform (domains and hosting). It earns high retention from customers who would rather not migrate their websites, and it has been raising prices and attaching more products per customer.

Enormous buybacks — the share count has fallen steadily for years — and a stock that ran hard in 2024 on margin expansion, then fell in 2025 as growth slowed and AI website builders raised competitive fears. Trades on free cash flow per share, which management guides to and beats.

Shines when: Applications-and-commerce growth accelerates, ARPU rises on bundling, free-cash-flow-per-share targets are hit, and the buyback continues.

Dangers: Slowing domain growth. AI-native website builders and Squarespace/Wix competition. Price-increase fatigue. Moderate leverage.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Total bookings, Applications & Commerce growth, ARPU, normalized EBITDA margin, free cash flow per share.

How to use it: A cash-flow compounder that trades like a growth stock on earnings day (±8–15%). The two-month setup is a post-earnings drop on a bookings nitpick. If stuck, the buyback is relentless — comfortable.

Enterprise software — second pass

ADSK Autodesk

Sector: Design & Engineering Software · Market cap: ~$65B · P/E: ~35x · Yield: none

AutoCAD, Revit (the standard for building information modeling in architecture and construction), Fusion (manufacturing CAD/CAM), Maya and 3ds Max (film and game animation), and a growing construction-management cloud — ~$7B of revenue, nearly all subscription, with a customer base of architects, engineers, and contractors who cannot switch because their files, standards, and licenses are built around it. Roughly 40% of revenue is from outside North America.

Autodesk has been a steady 10–15% grower with expanding margins, but 2023–25 brought an accounting-timing investigation (free-cash-flow presentation), a CFO change, and activist Starboard pushing for cost cuts and buybacks; the company responded with layoffs and a margin target. A 2025 report that it was exploring a bid for PTC was quickly walked back. No dividend; buybacks.

Shines when: Construction and manufacturing software adoption grows, the new transaction model (selling direct rather than through resellers) lifts revenue recognition, margins expand toward 40%, and AI features drive upsell.

Dangers: AI disruption of design software (the same long-tail fear as every seat-based vendor). Construction and manufacturing cyclicality. A premium multiple. Governance scars from the investigation.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends January). Billings growth, revenue by product family, non-GAAP operating margin, free cash flow, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a software-sector AI-scare selloff that hits a company with a physical-world moat. If stuck, a franchise every architect uses — comfortable, at a premium.

PTC PTC Inc.

Sector: Industrial Software · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: none

Creo (3D CAD for mechanical engineers), Windchill (product-lifecycle management — the system of record for how a product is designed, changed, and manufactured), Onshape (cloud CAD), ServiceMax (field service), and Codebeamer (software-defined product engineering) — ~$2.6B of revenue, essentially all subscription, sold to manufacturers in aerospace, autos, industrials, and medical devices. Boston-based; a new CEO (Neil Barua) since 2024.

PTC compounds annual recurring revenue at 10–12% with high retention and expanding free cash flow, and buys back stock. It is the sleepier cousin of Autodesk: less well known, more industrial, and occasionally the subject of takeover speculation (Autodesk in 2025).

Shines when: ARR growth holds double digits, PLM adoption expands as manufacturers digitize, Windchill and Codebeamer win regulated-industry deals, and free cash flow grows.

Dangers: Manufacturing capex cyclicality (deal timing slips). AI disruption of CAD. Competition from Siemens, Dassault, and Autodesk. Currency.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends September). Constant-currency ARR growth, free cash flow, net new ARR by product, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is an ARR beat or takeover speculation. If stuck, a mission-critical industrial software franchise — comfortable.

TYL Tyler Technologies

Sector: Public-Sector Software · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~45x · Yield: none

The dominant software vendor to state and local governments — court and justice systems, property-tax and appraisal, ERP and financials for cities and counties, public-safety records, and payment processing for everything from parking tickets to hunting licenses (a business that grew with the 2021 NIC acquisition, which brought most states' online-service portals). Revenue ~$2.4B, over 80% recurring. Governments almost never switch software; Tyler's retention is near 99%.

The story is the transition from on-premise licenses to SaaS, which depressed reported growth for years and is now lifting it as subscriptions compound; margins are expanding as the cloud migration matures. No dividend; modest buybacks. The multiple is high because there is no comparable public-sector software franchise.

Shines when: SaaS conversions accelerate, transaction revenue grows with digital government services, margins expand, and state and local budgets stay healthy (they run on property and sales taxes, which have been strong).

Dangers: A 45x multiple. Government budget stress in a recession. Long sales cycles. AI disruption of government services. Competition from Oracle and Workday in ERP.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Recurring revenue growth, SaaS revenue growth, transaction revenue, non-GAAP operating margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. Not a natural mover. If stuck, one of the stickiest customer bases in software — comfortable, at a price.

OTEX OpenText Corporation

Sector: Enterprise Information Management · Market cap: ~$8B · P/E: ~8x · Yield: ~3.5%

A Canadian software conglomerate — content management, business-network EDI, cybersecurity, application modernization, and analytics — built by acquiring mature software businesses (Documentum, GXS, Carbonite, and the 2023 purchase of Micro Focus for ~$6B) and running them for cash. Revenue ~$5.2B, mostly recurring, with modest growth and heavy debt from the Micro Focus deal, which it has been paying down by selling pieces (the mainframe-modernization business to Rocket Software for ~$2.3B in 2024).

The long-time CEO stepped down in 2025 amid slow growth and investor frustration; the stock trades at a single-digit multiple with a 3.5% yield and buybacks. It is the archetype of the "software value stock": real cash flow, no growth, a market that doubts management can change either.

Shines when: Cloud bookings grow, leverage falls, cost cuts lift margins, a new CEO articulates a credible strategy, and the multiple re-rates even modestly.

Dangers: Organic revenue decline. Leverage. Integration of a sprawling portfolio. AI disruption of content management. Leadership transition.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). Cloud revenue growth, adjusted EBITDA margin, free cash flow, net leverage, enterprise cloud bookings.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a new-CEO catalyst or a value rotation. If stuck, the yield is covered by cash flow — tolerable, but this has been cheap for years.

DOX Amdocs

Sector: Telecom Software & Services · Market cap: ~$10B · P/E: ~13x · Yield: ~2.3%

The billing, customer-management, and operations software that runs inside most large telecom carriers — AT&T, T-Mobile, and Vodafone are among its largest customers — delivered as multi-year managed-services contracts with a workforce concentrated in Israel, India, and Cyprus. Revenue ~$4.5B, over 60% from managed services, with a backlog of over $4B. Guernsey-domiciled; a dividend raised annually for over a decade and steady buybacks that retire a few percent of shares a year.

Growth is low (low-to-mid single digits) because carriers grow slowly, but the revenue is sticky and the cash conversion is high. It exited low-margin non-core businesses in 2024 to lift margins. A textbook boring tech stock at a value multiple.

Shines when: Carrier cloud migrations and 5G monetization projects drive bookings, managed-services renewals extend, margins expand, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Telecom capex and consolidation. Customer concentration. Israel exposure. AI reducing services hours. Slow growth capping the multiple.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends September). 12-month backlog, managed-services revenue, non-GAAP operating margin, free cash flow, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a backlog-driven guidance raise. If stuck, cash-generative and returning capital — comfortable.

CHKP Check Point Software Technologies

Sector: Cybersecurity · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~24x · Yield: none

The original firewall company (founded in Israel in 1993) — network security appliances and software, cloud security, endpoint, email security, and a growing security-operations platform — ~$2.7B of revenue with the highest margins in cybersecurity (operating margins above 40%), no debt, a cash pile of billions, and a buyback program that has retired a large share of the stock. It grows mid-single digits, far slower than Palo Alto, CrowdStrike, or Fortinet, because it prioritized profit over growth for two decades.

Founder Gil Shwed handed the CEO role to Nadav Zafrir (a former Israeli cyber-intelligence commander) in late 2024 with a mandate to accelerate growth through acquisitions and platform sales. The stock re-rated modestly on the change.

Shines when: Growth accelerates toward high single digits under the new CEO, firewall refresh cycles hit, cloud and email security grow double digits, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Share loss to faster-growing platforms. Israel exposure. Firewall commoditization. A growth acceleration that never arrives.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Revenue growth, billings, subscription growth, operating margin, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a growth-inflection quarter. If stuck, the most conservative balance sheet in cybersecurity — comfortable.

GEN Gen Digital

Sector: Consumer Cybersecurity / Fintech · Market cap: ~$18B · P/E: ~13x · Yield: ~1.8%

Norton, LifeLock, Avast, AVG, and CCleaner — the consumer antivirus and identity-protection subscriptions that ~40 million people pay for every year — plus, since the 2025 acquisition of MoneyLion (~$1B), a consumer-finance app with lending and banking products. Revenue ~$4.9B, nearly all subscription, with 60%-plus operating margins. Formed by NortonLifeLock's 2022 merger with Avast; Arizona-based with a Czech engineering base.

Consumer security is a mature, cash-generative category; Gen's strategy is bundling (identity, privacy, finance) to raise revenue per user and reduce churn. It pays down merger debt, buys back stock, and pays a dividend. Cheap for a subscription business because the category isn't growing.

Shines when: Direct-customer count stabilizes and ARPU rises on bundling, MoneyLion cross-sell works, leverage falls, and cybersecurity scares drive sign-ups.

Dangers: Category decline (free built-in security from Microsoft and Apple). Churn. Leverage. MoneyLion credit and regulatory risk. Consumer weakness.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Direct customer count, ARPU, bookings growth, operating margin, net leverage.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a bookings beat at a low multiple. If stuck, a subscription cash machine — comfortable.

Semiconductors, instruments & components — second pass

TER Teradyne

Sector: Semiconductor Test / Robotics · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~35x · Yield: ~0.4%

The largest maker of automated test equipment — the machines that test every chip after it's made — with roughly half the market alongside Advantest, plus a robotics business (Universal Robots collaborative arms, Mobile Industrial Robots) that has been slower than hoped. Revenue ~$3B. Test demand follows chip volumes and complexity: AI accelerators, high-bandwidth memory, and custom silicon all need more test time per unit, which has pulled Teradyne into the AI trade after years of dependence on Apple's iPhone cycle.

Cyclical — test equipment orders swing with fab utilization — but with a net cash balance sheet, buybacks, and a small dividend. It trades at a premium to its history on the AI compute-test story.

Shines when: AI accelerator and memory test demand grows, mobile recovers, robotics turns profitable, and gross margin holds.

Dangers: Semiconductor cycle. Customer concentration (Apple and its suppliers, Nvidia's ecosystem). Advantest competition in AI test. Robotics losses. Tariffs and China restrictions.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Semiconductor test revenue by end market, robotics revenue, gross margin, quarterly guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is a compute-test order surge. If stuck, a duopoly with a clean balance sheet — comfortable at mid-cycle.

MPWR Monolithic Power Systems

Sector: Power Management Semiconductors · Market cap: ~$45B · P/E: ~45x · Yield: ~0.6%

Designs the power-management chips — voltage regulators and power modules — that feed clean electricity to processors in data centers, cars, industrial equipment, and consumer devices, using a proprietary process technology at outsourced foundries. Revenue ~$2.5B, growing 20–30% a year, with 55% gross margins and a founder (Michael Hsing) who has run it since 1997. It became an AI stock as its power modules were designed into Nvidia's GPU boards, then fell 30% in late 2024 on reports that Nvidia was diversifying suppliers — and recovered as enterprise data, automotive, and industrial grew.

Dividend raised annually; no debt. One of the best-run analog companies, at a multiple that reflects it.

Shines when: Data-center power content grows (higher-power GPUs need more of its parts), automotive and industrial recover, new product lines (battery management, audio) ramp, and share holds at Nvidia and AMD.

Dangers: Customer concentration in AI. Competition from Infineon, TI, and ADI in data-center power. A 45x multiple. Tariffs and China exposure.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Revenue by end market (enterprise data, automotive, industrial), gross margin, quarterly guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is a post-customer-scare recovery. If stuck, a debt-free compounder — comfortable, though the multiple leaves little room.

ENTG Entegris

Sector: Semiconductor Materials & Filtration · Market cap: ~$13B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~0.3%

Sells the consumables and containers that keep chip fabs clean: filters and purifiers for the gases and chemicals used in fabrication, specialty chemicals and slurries for polishing wafers (from the 2022 CMC Materials acquisition), and the wafer carriers and reticle pods that move silicon between tools without contamination. Revenue ~$3.3B, roughly 80% recurring consumables that scale with wafer starts rather than with tool purchases. As chips shrink, contamination tolerances fall, and Entegris's content per wafer rises.

The CMC deal added leverage that is being paid down; growth follows fab utilization with a lag. A small dividend; buybacks resumed as leverage fell.

Shines when: Wafer starts grow (AI, memory recovery), leading-edge node transitions increase content, new fabs ramp (especially in the U.S.), and leverage falls.

Dangers: Semiconductor cycle. China export restrictions. Leverage. Competition from Pall and local Asian suppliers. Fab construction delays.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Revenue vs. wafer-start growth, Materials Solutions and Advanced Purity Solutions growth, EBITDA margin, net leverage.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a wafer-start inflection. If stuck, a consumables business with rising content per wafer — comfortable.

AMKR Amkor Technology

Sector: Semiconductor Packaging & Test · Market cap: ~$6B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.3%

The largest U.S.-headquartered outsourced semiconductor assembly and test company (OSAT) — it takes finished wafers and packages the chips into the modules that go on circuit boards, and tests them — with factories in Korea, Taiwan, the Philippines, Japan, Vietnam, and a new advanced-packaging plant in Arizona (Peoria) supported by CHIPS Act funding and an Apple commitment. Revenue ~$6.5B. Advanced packaging (chiplets, 2.5D for AI chips) is where the growth and margin are; commodity packaging for phones is the volume.

Amkor is a low-margin, capital-intensive, cyclical business — but it is also one of only two large non-Chinese OSATs (ASE is the other), which makes it strategically important as the U.S. tries to onshore packaging. Small dividend; the founding Kim family controls the vote.

Shines when: Advanced packaging for AI ramps, the Arizona plant wins customers, smartphone volumes recover, and auto and industrial turn up.

Dangers: Cyclicality and thin margins. Customer concentration (Apple). Competition from TSMC's own packaging and ASE. Capex intensity. Geopolitical exposure across Asia.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Revenue by end market, gross margin, capex, Arizona progress, quarterly guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is an advanced-packaging win. If stuck, cheap and strategically protected — tolerable through a cycle.

LFUS Littelfuse

Sector: Circuit Protection · Market cap: ~$6B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~1.2%

Fuses — the original product, since 1927 — plus the broader family of circuit-protection, power-control, and sensing components (varistors, relays, switches, semiconductors for power control) that go into cars, industrial equipment, data-center power supplies, electronics, and the grid. Revenue ~$2.3B across Electronics, Transportation, and Industrial segments. Chicago-based, grown through dozens of acquisitions, and run conservatively with a dividend raised annually and buybacks.

Like TE and Amphenol at smaller scale: content-per-unit growth (an EV has more circuit protection than a combustion car; a data center has more than an office), through-cycle pricing, and a short-cycle exposure that makes it a sensitive read on industrial activity.

Shines when: Electronics and industrial destocking ends, EV and renewable content grows, data-center power demand rises, and acquisitions add.

Dangers: Short-cycle industrial exposure. Auto production. Tariffs and China. Small-cap liquidity. Acquisition integration.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic growth by segment, book-to-bill, adjusted operating margin, quarterly guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is an industrial-orders inflection. If stuck, a century-old component maker with a growing dividend — comfortable.

ST Sensata Technologies

Sector: Sensors & Controls · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: ~13x · Yield: ~1.5%

Pressure, temperature, position, and electrical sensors for cars (tire-pressure monitors, braking, emissions, EV battery management), heavy vehicles, aircraft, and industrial equipment — ~$3.8B of revenue, roughly 60% automotive. Spun out of Texas Instruments to private equity in 2006, public since 2010, UK-domiciled, based in Massachusetts. It spent years overpaying for acquisitions and lost investor trust; a new CEO in 2024 launched a portfolio pruning and cost program, and an activist (Elliott) is involved.

Cheap because growth has been zero and execution poor; the bull case is a self-help turnaround plus content growth in electrification. Dividend since 2022; buybacks.

Shines when: Auto production stabilizes, EV content grows, non-core divestitures complete, margins recover on cost cuts, and leverage falls.

Dangers: Auto cycle and Chinese competition. Execution history. Leverage. Tariffs. A turnaround that stalls.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic growth by segment, adjusted operating margin, free cash flow, divestiture progress.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a turnaround-progress quarter at a value multiple. If stuck, cheap with a dividend — tolerable, with execution as the risk.

TDY Teledyne Technologies

Sector: Instruments & Imaging · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: none

A serial acquirer of niche technology businesses: digital imaging (FLIR thermal cameras and sensors, acquired 2021 for $8B; machine-vision cameras; X-ray detectors), instrumentation (marine sonar and oceanographic instruments, environmental and gas monitoring, test equipment), aerospace and defense electronics, and engineered systems. Revenue ~$6B. Run for decades on a decentralized model — buy a good small technology company, leave management in place, push margins — and famous for never overpaying.

No dividend; the cash goes to acquisitions and, lately, buybacks. It grows mid-single digits organically plus deals, with steadily expanding margins. Defense and space exposure (unmanned systems, satellite imaging) is a tailwind.

Shines when: Defense and space demand grows, industrial imaging recovers, acquisitions add, and margin expansion continues.

Dangers: Industrial capex cyclicality. Acquisition-dependent growth. Defense budget timing. Currency.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic growth by segment, non-GAAP operating margin, acquisition announcements, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a deal announcement or a defense catalyst. If stuck, one of the best capital allocators in industrial tech — comfortable.

TRMB Trimble Inc.

Sector: Positioning & Construction Software · Market cap: ~$18B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: none

GPS positioning hardware and the software built on it — for construction (machine control for excavators and graders, surveying, estimating and project management through the AECO cloud), transportation and logistics (fleet routing, freight matching), and, until it sold most of the business into a joint venture with AGCO, agriculture. Revenue ~$3.7B, shifting from hardware to recurring software (ARR over $2B). Colorado-based; a 2024 audit issue delayed filings and dented the stock before being resolved.

The strategy is "connect and scale": sell the software that turns positioning data into productivity for contractors, with recurring revenue replacing hardware cycles. No dividend; buybacks.

Shines when: ARR growth stays double digits, construction software adoption grows, infrastructure spending lifts machine-control demand, and margins expand on mix.

Dangers: Construction and transportation cyclicality. Hardware exposure. Competition from Autodesk and Bentley in software, Hexagon and Topcon in hardware. Currency.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. ARR growth, AECO segment growth, EBITDA margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is an infrastructure catalyst or an ARR beat. If stuck, a recurring-revenue franchise in a physical-world niche — comfortable.

ZBRA Zebra Technologies

Sector: Enterprise Mobile Computing & Barcode · Market cap: ~$14B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: none

The barcode scanners, rugged handheld computers, label printers, and RFID readers that warehouse workers, retail associates, nurses, and delivery drivers carry — ~$5.2B of revenue, with roughly 40% share in enterprise mobile computing (the Motorola Solutions enterprise business it bought in 2014). Demand rides retail and logistics capex: it boomed in the e-commerce build-out, collapsed in 2023 as customers over-bought, and recovered in 2024–25.

Zebra is a cyclical with a technology moat (its devices run the workflows that retailers and shippers depend on) and a value multiple. No dividend; buybacks. Tariffs on Asian-made hardware were a 2025 headwind.

Shines when: Retail and logistics device refresh cycles hit, RFID adoption grows, services and software attach rise, and tariff costs are passed through.

Dangers: Capex cyclicality (2023 showed how sharp). Tariffs. Competition from Honeywell and consumer-device makers. Customer concentration in large retailers.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic growth by segment, gross margin, adjusted EBITDA margin, quarterly guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a refresh-cycle inflection. If stuck, a category leader at a mid-teens multiple — comfortable at mid-cycle.

LOGI Logitech International

Sector: Computer Peripherals · Market cap: ~$14B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.5%

Mice, keyboards, webcams, headsets, gaming peripherals, and video-conferencing hardware — ~$4.6B of revenue, Swiss-domiciled and Silicon Valley-run, with leading share in nearly every category it sells. It doubled during the pandemic work-from-home boom, gave back the surge in 2022–23, and stabilized as a steady mid-single-digit grower with 40%-plus gross margins, no debt, a dividend raised annually, and buybacks.

The boring truth is that everyone needs a mouse and Logitech makes the best one; the risk is that it sells discretionary hardware made in China and Southeast Asia into a tariff regime.

Shines when: PC refresh cycles lift peripherals, gaming and video-collaboration grow, tariff costs are absorbed through sourcing shifts, and margins hold.

Dangers: Tariffs on Asian production. Consumer and enterprise IT spending softness. Category commoditization. Currency (reports in dollars, costs in francs and yuan).

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends March). Sales growth by category, gross margin, tariff mitigation, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a tariff-relief headline. If stuck, debt-free with a dividend — comfortable.

CIEN Ciena Corporation

Sector: Optical Networking · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

The leading maker of optical transport systems — the equipment that sends data over fiber between cities and, increasingly, between data centers — with its WaveLogic coherent optics technology, plus routing and switching, and network software. Revenue ~$5B. For twenty years Ciena sold mostly to telecom carriers, a slow-growth customer base; the AI build-out changed that, as hyperscalers became its largest customers, needing data-center interconnect capacity at scale, and the stock rose severalfold in 2025.

Ciena also sells pluggable optics and is expanding into the inside-the-data-center market. No dividend; buybacks. Maryland-based.

Shines when: Hyperscaler data-center interconnect orders grow, carriers upgrade for AI traffic, pluggable optics ramp, and margins expand on mix.

Dangers: Hyperscaler capex cycles. Component supply constraints. Competition from Nokia, Cisco, and Huawei (outside the U.S.). A multiple that has re-rated from telecom-equipment levels to AI levels.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends October). Revenue by customer type (cloud vs. service provider), orders and backlog, gross margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±10–20%. The two-month setup is a hyperscaler-capex catalyst. If stuck, a technology leader in a duopoly-like niche — comfortable, though the AI premium can deflate.

FN Fabrinet

Sector: Optical Manufacturing Services · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: ~35x · Yield: none

A Thailand-based contract manufacturer that builds optical transceivers and modules for the companies that sell them — including a very large share of the 800-gigabit transceivers Nvidia ships with its AI systems — plus lasers, industrial optics, and automotive lidar and sensing. Revenue ~$3.5B, growing 20%-plus, with a Cayman domicile, U.S. listing, and manufacturing scale in Thailand that no competitor matches at the precision optics require.

Like Jabil and Flex, it is thin-margin contract manufacturing; unlike them, it dominates a niche (precision optical assembly) with customer concentration in the AI build-out. No dividend; net cash.

Shines when: AI transceiver volumes grow, 1.6-terabit programs ramp, new customers diversify from Nvidia, and Thai capacity expansions come online.

Dangers: Customer concentration (Nvidia is a large share). Thin margins. Technology transitions (co-packaged optics would change what gets outsourced). Thailand and tariff exposure.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). Datacom revenue growth, customer concentration, gross margin, capacity expansion.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±10–15%. The two-month setup is a transceiver-program ramp. If stuck, a debt-free niche manufacturer — tolerable, with concentration as the risk.

GFS GlobalFoundries

Sector: Semiconductor Foundry · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: none

The largest U.S.-headquartered contract chip manufacturer — the former AMD fabs in upstate New York, plus Dresden and Singapore — making mature-node chips (28 nanometers and above: RF for phones, automotive microcontrollers, power management, silicon photonics) rather than the leading-edge logic TSMC makes. Revenue ~$6.7B. Majority-owned by Abu Dhabi's Mubadala; a CHIPS Act grant funds New York expansion; a 2025 leadership change brought back founder-era executive Tim Breen as CEO.

GlobalFoundries is the "trusted foundry" — U.S. defense and auto customers pay for supply security — but mature-node pricing has been under pressure from Chinese foundries adding capacity. It trades on the reshoring thesis more than on growth.

Shines when: Automotive and industrial demand recovers, U.S. and European customers pay for onshore supply, utilization rises, and silicon photonics for AI ramps.

Dangers: Mature-node price competition from China. Utilization-driven margins. Customer concentration (Qualcomm, AMD). Capital intensity. Mubadala overhang.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Revenue by end market, wafer shipments and ASP, gross margin, utilization commentary, quarterly guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a reshoring or auto-recovery catalyst. If stuck, a strategically protected foundry — tolerable through a cycle.

STM STMicroelectronics (ADR)

Sector: Automotive & Industrial Semis · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~40x trough · Yield: ~1%

Europe's largest chipmaker — microcontrollers (the STM32 family, ubiquitous in industrial and consumer devices), automotive chips (a major Tesla supplier through silicon-carbide power devices), sensors, and analog — ~$12B of revenue, headquartered in Geneva with fabs in France and Italy, and the French and Italian governments as major shareholders. It went through the same 2023–25 inventory correction as Microchip and NXP, with revenue falling 20%-plus and margins compressing, and has been cutting costs and restructuring its manufacturing footprint.

The bull case is the recovery: silicon carbide for EVs, industrial reacceleration, and a fab consolidation that lifts margins. Dividend maintained through the trough; buybacks. The ADR trades on the NYSE.

Shines when: The industrial and auto inventory cycle turns, silicon-carbide demand grows, cost cuts hit, and utilization rises.

Dangers: Cyclicality. Chinese competition in silicon carbide and microcontrollers. Government shareholder influence. Tesla concentration. Euro reporting and tariffs.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Revenue by segment, gross margin, bookings and book-to-bill, quarterly guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a cycle-turn quarter. If stuck, a European champion at a trough — comfortable if the cycle turns within a year.

RMBS Rambus

Sector: Memory Interface Chips & IP · Market cap: ~$9B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: none

Once a patent-licensing company infamous for suing the memory industry, Rambus reinvented itself as a chip designer: the memory-interface chips (registering clock drivers, buffers) that sit on every DDR5 server memory module and manage the signal between processor and memory — a market that grows with server volumes and memory bandwidth, and one where Rambus holds leading share. Revenue ~$700M, with a still-large IP-licensing stream (interface and security IP) and 75%-plus gross margins.

AI servers use far more memory per system, and each new DDR generation adds more Rambus content; the stock rose severalfold on that story. No dividend; buybacks; net cash.

Shines when: Server memory-module volumes grow, DDR5 and future MRDIMM transitions add content, and licensing renews.

Dangers: Server-cycle sensitivity. Competition from Montage and Renesas. Licensing lumpiness. A small company at a premium multiple.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Product revenue growth, licensing revenue, gross margin, quarterly guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is a server-memory ramp. If stuck, debt-free with a duopoly niche — comfortable.

Defense & Industrials

Shipyards, bombers, hydraulics, compressors, trucks, HVAC, elevators, fasteners, railroads, garbage trucks, and uniforms. The Atlas has the headline names (LMT, RTX, GE, CAT, HON, DE, ETN, EMR, UNP, WM); this is everything one tier down — much of it run better than the headliners.

Defense

GD General Dynamics

Sector: Defense / Business Jets · Market cap: ~$85B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~2%

A ~$50B-revenue defense conglomerate with one non-defense business that drives the stock. Marine Systems (~$15B) is Electric Boat — the lead builder of Virginia-class attack submarines and the Columbia-class ballistic-missile submarines that will carry the nuclear deterrent for fifty years — plus Bath Iron Works (destroyers) and NASSCO. Combat Systems (~$9B) makes Abrams tanks, Stryker vehicles, and artillery munitions, with European demand surging. Technologies (~$13B) is GDIT, one of the largest federal IT contractors, plus Mission Systems. And Aerospace (~$12B) is Gulfstream, the leading large-cabin business-jet maker, whose new G700 and G800 deliveries set the tone for quarterly results.

Run with unusual discipline: no big acquisitions, steady margins, and a dividend raised for 30-plus consecutive years. The submarine backlog is measured in decades; the Gulfstream order book gives it a cyclical, high-margin kicker that pure defense names lack.

Shines when: Submarine funding and shipyard throughput improve, Gulfstream deliveries ramp on the G700/G800, munitions demand from Europe stays elevated, and GDIT wins federal IT awards.

Dangers: Shipyard labor shortages and supply-chain delays that slow submarine deliveries and compress Marine margins. Business-jet demand is cyclical. Defense budget fights and continuing resolutions. GDIT is exposed to the same federal-services scrutiny as Leidos and Booz Allen.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Gulfstream deliveries and Aerospace margin, Marine Systems margin, total backlog (well over $90B), munitions orders, submarine budget lines.

How to use it: Low drama; earnings reactions ±3–7%, mostly on Gulfstream delivery timing. The two-month setup is a defense-budget scare or a Gulfstream delivery slip that the next quarter corrects. If stuck, one of the most comfortable holds in the sector — a growing dividend and a fifty-year submarine program.

NOC Northrop Grumman

Sector: Defense · Market cap: ~$85B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.6%

A ~$42B-revenue defense prime built around the nuclear triad and space. Aeronautics makes the B-21 Raider stealth bomber (the Air Force's next 100-plus aircraft), the F-35 center fuselage, and Triton drones. Space Systems builds missile-warning and communications satellites, the Sentinel intercontinental ballistic missile replacing the Minuteman, and solid rocket motors. Mission Systems supplies radars, electronic warfare, and the sensors inside other companies' platforms. Defense Systems covers munitions and sustainment.

The tension is fixed-price development on programs of staggering size: the B-21's early production lots were bid at fixed prices and produced charges of over $1B; Sentinel breached its cost baseline so badly it triggered a formal Pentagon review and restructuring. Both programs survived and are now in the money-making phase, but the multiple reflects the scar tissue. Dividend raised for 20-plus years; steady buybacks.

Shines when: B-21 production ramps profitably, Sentinel is restructured on stable terms, space and missile-defense budgets (including Golden Dome) grow, and munitions demand rises.

Dangers: Fixed-price development charges — the B-21 is a reminder that even the best programs can produce billion-dollar surprises. Sentinel cost and schedule. Budget politics. Concentration in U.S. government contracts.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Segment margins (especially Aeronautics and Space), free cash flow guidance, B-21 and Sentinel program updates, backlog (over $90B).

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%, larger when a program charge appears. The two-month setup is buying after a charge-driven drop when the underlying programs are intact. If stuck, comfortable — the triad gets funded regardless of politics.

LHX L3Harris Technologies

Sector: Defense Electronics / Propulsion · Market cap: ~$50B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.8%

Formed by the 2019 merger of L3 and Harris, ~$21B of revenue, positioned as the "sixth prime" — a supplier of the electronics and propulsion that go inside the big five's platforms. Space and Airborne Systems (satellites, missile-tracking payloads, aircraft mission systems); Integrated Mission Systems (maritime, intelligence, surveillance); Communication Systems (the tactical radios most U.S. and allied soldiers carry, plus night vision); and Aerojet Rocketdyne, acquired in 2023, which makes the solid rocket motors and propulsion for Patriot, THAAD, Javelin, Standard Missile, and the Sentinel ICBM — the most supply-constrained component in the missile industrial base.

The Aerojet deal turned L3Harris into the beneficiary of every missile restocking headline: Ukraine, Israel, the Pacific, and Golden Dome all need motors. Management has been cutting costs and divesting non-core businesses; dividend raised annually, buybacks steady.

Shines when: Missile and munitions production ramps (rocket-motor capacity expansion), missile-defense and space budgets grow, tactical radio orders rise, and margins expand toward targets.

Dangers: Aerojet execution — expanding rocket-motor production is hard and the Pentagon watches closely. Leverage from the deal. Budget continuing resolutions. Some legacy programs are low-margin.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Backlog, Aerojet segment growth and margin, book-to-bill, free cash flow, missile-defense program awards.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%; it also moves on geopolitical headlines more than the primes. The two-month setup is a missile-demand catalyst (a supplemental appropriation, a Golden Dome award). If stuck, a solid dividend grower with a business that governments cannot do without.

HII Huntington Ingalls Industries

Sector: Shipbuilding · Market cap: ~$10B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~2%

America's largest military shipbuilder and the only builder of nuclear aircraft carriers, ~$12B of revenue. Newport News Shipbuilding (Virginia) builds and refuels carriers and is Electric Boat's partner on Virginia- and Columbia-class submarines. Ingalls Shipbuilding (Mississippi) builds destroyers, amphibious assault ships, and Coast Guard cutters. Mission Technologies is a smaller defense-services and unmanned-systems business. Spun out of Northrop Grumman in 2011.

A monopoly with a problem: shipyards are labor-intensive, and the post-pandemic workforce turnover crushed productivity — margins fell to the mid-single digits, deliveries slipped, and the stock lost a third of its value in 2024. The bull case is that the Navy's shipbuilding push (a bipartisan priority, with wage subsidies and capacity investment) fixes throughput and margins over several years. Dividend raised annually.

Shines when: Shipyard labor stabilizes and margins recover toward 8–10%, the Navy's shipbuilding budget and workforce funding grow, and carrier and submarine milestones hit on schedule.

Dangers: Labor — hiring and retaining welders is the whole story. Fixed-price contracts on ships that take years to build. Schedule slips that push milestone payments. Small-cap liquidity for a name this strategic.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Shipbuilding segment margin, headcount and attrition commentary, milestone deliveries, free cash flow, Navy budget lines.

How to use it: A margin-recovery story; earnings reactions ±8–15%, larger than the primes because of the operational leverage. The two-month setup is a quarter showing margin improvement. If stuck, you own the only carrier builder in the country — the business will not go away, but the recovery is measured in years.

TXT Textron

Sector: Aerospace / Defense · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: ~13x · Yield: ~0.1%

A ~$14B-revenue conglomerate whose pieces you know by other names. Bell makes helicopters and tiltrotors and won the Army's Future Long Range Assault Aircraft program — the V-280 Valor, the Black Hawk's replacement, a program that could be worth $70B over decades. Textron Aviation is Cessna and Beechcraft: Citation business jets, King Air turboprops, and piston trainers. Textron Systems makes drones, armored vehicles, and training systems. Industrial includes Kautex (automotive fuel systems) and specialized vehicles (E-Z-GO golf carts, Arctic Cat).

The stock trades at a conglomerate discount because the industrial pieces are mediocre and business jets are cyclical, but the Bell FLRAA award is a generational contract that the market has only partly priced. Buybacks rather than dividends.

Shines when: Citation deliveries and backlog grow, FLRAA moves from development into production, Bell military volume rises, and the industrial segment is fixed or sold.

Dangers: Business-jet cyclicality. Labor strikes (a 2024 Aviation strike hit deliveries). Industrial segment weakness. FLRAA development risk on a fixed schedule.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Aviation deliveries and backlog, Bell segment margin, FLRAA milestones, Industrial margin, buybacks.

How to use it: A cheap, lumpy name; earnings reactions ±6–12% on delivery timing. The two-month setup is a delivery-catch-up quarter or a FLRAA milestone. If stuck, the multiple is low enough that the downside is limited, but the conglomerate discount can persist for years.

Diversified industrials & machinery

ITW Illinois Tool Works

Sector: Diversified Industrial · Market cap: ~$75B · P/E: ~24x · Yield: ~2.3%

A ~$16B-revenue collection of niche industrial businesses run on a single operating philosophy — the "80/20" method, which focuses each business on the 20% of customers and products that generate 80% of profit and ruthlessly simplifies the rest. Seven segments: Automotive OEM (fasteners and components), Food Equipment (Hobart commercial kitchens), Test and Measurement and Electronics (Instron), Welding (Miller), Polymers and Fluids, Construction Products, and Specialty Products. No segment dominates; most hold number-one or number-two positions in small markets.

The result is operating margins around 26%, the highest of any diversified industrial, and a dividend raised for over 60 consecutive years. It grows slowly — organic growth of 1–3% is typical — and the stock trades as a quality compounder rather than a growth story. Buybacks are steady; acquisitions are rare and small.

Shines when: Industrial production and auto builds are stable to rising, food-equipment and construction recover, price outruns cost, and margin expansion continues on enterprise initiatives.

Dangers: Low growth — the stock needs margin expansion to work, and margins are already high. Auto cycle exposure. Currency. A premium multiple for a slow grower.

Watch: Reports early Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic growth by segment, operating margin, enterprise-initiative contribution, full-year EPS guidance revisions.

How to use it: Low beta; earnings reactions ±3–6%. Not a natural two-month mover — it goes up slowly and down slowly. As a stuck-bucket holding it is about as good as industrials get.

PH Parker-Hannifin

Sector: Motion & Control / Aerospace · Market cap: ~$95B · P/E: ~26x · Yield: ~1%

The world's largest maker of motion and control technologies — ~$20B of revenue from hydraulics, pneumatics, filtration, fluid connectors, seals, and climate-control components that go into construction equipment, factory machinery, trucks, aircraft, and increasingly data centers (cooling systems). The 2022 acquisition of Meggitt made Aerospace Systems roughly a third of revenue, with high-margin aftermarket content on nearly every commercial and military aircraft — braking systems, fuel systems, fire protection, sensors.

Parker has raised its dividend for nearly 70 consecutive years, one of the longest streaks in the market, and the "Win Strategy" operating system has pushed margins from the mid-teens toward the mid-20s over a decade. It is the industrial the other industrials benchmark against.

Shines when: Aerospace aftermarket grows (air traffic, engine shop visits), the industrial cycle turns up (orders positive), margins expand on mix, and deleveraging from Meggitt completes.

Dangers: Industrial cyclicality — North American and European orders were negative through much of 2024–25. Aerospace supply-chain issues. Premium valuation. Currency.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). Orders by segment (the leading indicator), Aerospace organic growth and margin, adjusted segment margin, free cash flow.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is buying when industrial orders turn positive after a negative stretch — Parker's order data is a leading indicator for the whole sector. If stuck, an ideal hold: the dividend streak is one of the safest in the market.

DOV Dover

Sector: Diversified Industrial · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.2%

An ~$8B-revenue collection of engineered products in five segments: Engineered Products (vehicle-service lifts, waste-handling equipment, aerospace components), Clean Energy and Fueling (fuel dispensers, natural-gas and hydrogen components), Imaging and Identification (Markem-Imaje product-coding printers), Pumps and Process Solutions (industrial pumps, biopharma components, and CPC — the quick-disconnect thermal connectors used in data-center liquid cooling, its fastest-growing product), and Climate and Sustainability Technologies (refrigeration cases, heat exchangers, beverage-can-making machinery).

Dover has raised its dividend for nearly 70 consecutive years and manages its portfolio actively — selling slower businesses and buying niche growth. The data-center liquid-cooling angle gave a boring name a real growth story in 2024–25.

Shines when: Data-center liquid-cooling demand grows (CPC), biopharma components recover, industrial orders turn, and portfolio actions add margin.

Dangers: Cyclical across most segments. Refrigeration and can-making are lumpy. Acquisition pricing. A mid-cap that can be overlooked.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Bookings and book-to-bill, Pumps and Process organic growth (thermal connectors), segment margins, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a data-center-cooling catalyst quarter or an industrial-orders inflection. If stuck, a dividend king with a clean balance sheet — comfortable.

IR Ingersoll Rand

Sector: Compressors / Pumps / Vacuum · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~0.1%

Not the old Ingersoll Rand (that became Trane): this is the 2020 merger of Gardner Denver with Ingersoll Rand's industrial segment, ~$7.5B of revenue from air compressors, vacuum pumps, blowers, and fluid-handling equipment sold into every factory, hospital, and process plant. Industrial Technologies and Services (~80%) is compressors and the recurring aftermarket that follows them; Precision and Science Technologies (~20%) is pumps for medical, life-science, and industrial applications.

The model is serial acquisition: dozens of bolt-on deals a year, integrated through the Ingersoll Rand Execution Excellence system, funded by strong free cash flow. Employees receive equity grants broadly — an ownership culture management credits for margin gains. Buybacks rather than a meaningful dividend.

Shines when: Industrial orders and book-to-bill turn positive, aftermarket revenue grows, acquisitions add accretively, and margins expand.

Dangers: Short-cycle industrial exposure — orders can turn negative quickly. Acquisition-driven growth carries integration risk. A premium multiple for a compressor company. Organic growth has been modest.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Orders growth, organic revenue growth, adjusted EBITDA margin, acquisition announcements, free cash flow.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is an orders-inflection quarter. If stuck, a well-run compounder with a clean balance sheet.

ROK Rockwell Automation

Sector: Factory Automation · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~1.5%

The largest pure-play industrial automation company in the U.S. — ~$8.5B of revenue from the programmable logic controllers (the Logix family), drives, motion control, sensors, and software (FactoryTalk) that run production lines in food, beverage, auto, pharma, and semiconductor plants. Intelligent Devices, Software and Control, and Lifecycle Services are the segments. It is the American champion against Siemens, Schneider, and ABB, and the obvious beneficiary of manufacturing reshoring.

Deeply cyclical for a "technology" company: the 2023 boom (customers double-ordered during shortages) turned into a 2024 bust as distributors worked down inventory, and orders fell sharply before recovering. Dividend raised annually; buybacks.

Shines when: Capital spending on new factories rises (reshoring, EV and battery plants, data-center construction), distributor destocking ends, software and services grow, and margins recover.

Dangers: Capex cyclicality. Inventory swings at distributors. Competition from Siemens and Schneider. A premium multiple for a cyclical.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends September). Orders and book-to-bill, organic growth by segment, segment margin, distributor inventory commentary, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a book-to-bill inflection — Rockwell leads the automation cycle. If stuck at a peak, the drawdowns are 30–40%; if stuck at a trough, patience is rewarded.

NDSN Nordson

Sector: Precision Dispensing / Industrial Technology · Market cap: ~$14B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~1.3%

A ~$2.8B-revenue maker of precision equipment for dispensing adhesives, coatings, sealants, and fluids — the machines that glue a cereal box, coat a phone screen, apply the adhesive on a diaper, or dispense the precise dot of material inside a semiconductor package. Industrial Precision Solutions (packaging, product assembly, industrial coatings), Medical and Fluid Solutions (components for medical devices, fluid management — the fastest-growing piece), and Advanced Technology Solutions (electronics dispensing, test and inspection).

Nordson has raised its dividend for over 60 consecutive years — a dividend king — and grows through bolt-on acquisitions (Atrion in 2024 for medical components) run through a common operating model. Margins are high (mid-20s EBITDA) because the equipment is specified into customers' processes and the consumables and parts recur.

Shines when: Industrial capex and packaging demand recover, medical device production grows, electronics and semiconductor capex turns, and acquisitions integrate.

Dangers: Capex cyclicality across most segments. Electronics exposure is volatile. Acquisition integration and leverage. A mid-cap that can trade thinly.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Dec (fiscal year ends October). Organic growth by segment, backlog, EBITDA margin, acquisition contribution.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is an industrial-orders inflection. If stuck, a dividend king with a niche-monopoly portfolio — comfortable.

CMI Cummins

Sector: Engines / Power Generation · Market cap: ~$55B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~2%

The world's largest independent maker of diesel and natural-gas engines — ~$34B of revenue — sold to truck makers (Paccar, Daimler, Navistar), construction and mining equipment makers, and marine and rail customers, plus the Components segment (turbochargers, filtration, emissions aftertreatment, axles from the Meritor acquisition), a Distribution segment (parts and service), and Power Systems, which makes the large generators that data centers use for backup power — a business that became the growth story as hyperscalers ordered generators by the hundred. Accelera is its zero-emissions unit (batteries, fuel cells, electrolyzers), losing money but shrinking.

Cummins spun off its filtration business as Atmus in 2023 and settled a large emissions-compliance case with the government in 2024. It has raised its dividend for decades, buys back stock, and is regarded as the best-managed company in the heavy-duty ecosystem.

Shines when: Data-center generator demand grows, the North American truck cycle turns up (pre-buy ahead of 2027 emissions rules), Distribution grows, and Accelera losses narrow.

Dangers: The truck cycle — engine volumes swing 20–30% between peak and trough. Emissions regulation shifts. Electrification eventually eroding the diesel franchise. China and India exposure.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Power Systems revenue and margin, North American heavy-duty truck production forecasts, Engine segment margin, Accelera losses, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a data-center-generator catalyst or a truck-cycle turn (watch ACT Research orders). If stuck, a dividend grower with a fortress balance sheet — comfortable.

PCAR Paccar

Sector: Heavy Trucks · Market cap: ~$50B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~1.5% plus a special dividend most years

Kenworth, Peterbilt, and DAF — ~$30B of revenue from heavy and medium-duty trucks sold in North America, Europe, and increasingly South America and Australia. Truck margins are the best in the industry because Paccar builds to order rather than to inventory, controls its own dealer network, and designs its own engines. The Parts segment (~$7B) is the profit stabilizer — high-margin aftermarket revenue that grows through every truck downturn. Financial Services finances the trucks it sells.

Family-influenced (the Pigott family has run it for a century), conservatively financed, and famous for paying a regular dividend plus a large special dividend in December that brings the total yield to 3–4% in good years. It has not lost money in over 80 years.

Shines when: Class 8 truck orders rise (a pre-buy ahead of 2027 emissions rules is the current setup), European truck demand recovers, Parts growth continues, and pricing holds.

Dangers: The truck cycle — Class 8 production swings 20–30% peak to trough, and freight recessions delay fleet replacement. Tariffs on cross-border truck components. EU emissions rules. A large 2025 antitrust settlement in Europe.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Truck deliveries and gross margin, ACT Research Class 8 orders (monthly), Parts revenue growth, special-dividend announcement in December.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%; the stock leads truck orders by a few months. The two-month setup is buying when Class 8 orders bottom or ahead of the December special dividend. If stuck, the Parts business and balance sheet make it the safest way to own the truck cycle.

Distribution & services

GWW W.W. Grainger

Sector: MRO Distribution · Market cap: ~$50B · P/E: ~26x · Yield: ~0.9%

The largest maintenance, repair, and operations distributor in North America — ~$18B of revenue selling two million products (safety gear, fasteners, motors, cleaning supplies, tools) to businesses and institutions that need them tomorrow. High-Touch Solutions (~80%) is the core Grainger business with sales reps, branches, and on-site inventory management for large customers. Endless Assortment (~20%) is Zoro in the U.S. and MonotaRO in Japan — online, small-customer, catalog-of-everything.

Dividend raised for over 50 consecutive years, consistent buybacks, and a track record of taking share from smaller distributors every year through better data, pricing, and delivery. It is a boring business that compounds because the fragmented competition is worse.

Shines when: Industrial production and employment grow, share gains continue in High-Touch, MonotaRO grows double digits, and gross margin holds on pricing.

Dangers: Industrial-activity sensitivity (a soft economy shows up in daily sales). Amazon Business as a competitor in the small-customer segment. Tariff-driven cost inflation that must be passed through. A premium multiple.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Daily sales growth, High-Touch organic growth vs. market, gross margin, operating margin, MonotaRO growth.

How to use it: Low beta; earnings reactions ±4–8%. Not a natural two-month mover. As a stuck-bucket holding it is excellent — a share gainer with a half-century dividend record.

FAST Fastenal

Sector: Industrial Distribution · Market cap: ~$50B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: ~1.9%

Started as a fastener store in Minnesota in 1967; now an ~$8B-revenue industrial distributor with a model nobody has replicated: rather than shipping from warehouses, Fastenal puts inventory inside customers' plants through industrial vending machines and "Onsite" branches staffed by Fastenal employees, so the customer never runs out of the small parts that stop a production line. Fasteners are now under a third of sales; safety products, tools, and general MRO are the rest.

Fastenal reports monthly daily sales numbers, so there are no surprises — only confirmations. Margins are the best in distribution (operating margins around 20%), the dividend is raised most years with occasional specials, and the balance sheet carries almost no debt. The stock split 2-for-1 in 2025. The multiple is the price of predictability.

Shines when: Manufacturing activity (ISM above 50) lifts daily sales, Onsite and vending installations accelerate, gross margin stabilizes, and pricing offsets tariffs.

Dangers: A 40x multiple for a distributor. Direct sensitivity to manufacturing activity — sales growth stalled during the 2023–24 industrial soft patch. Gross-margin pressure from mix shift to larger customers. Tariff cost inflation.

Watch: Reports mid Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct, plus monthly daily-sales releases. Daily sales growth, Onsite signings, vending installations, gross margin, ISM manufacturing PMI.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8% but rarely surprising given the monthly data. The two-month setup is buying a multiple-compression dip ahead of an ISM inflection. If stuck, you own one of the highest-quality businesses in the sector at a price that assumes it stays that way.

CTAS Cintas

Sector: Uniform Rental / Facility Services · Market cap: ~$85B · P/E: ~45x · Yield: ~0.8%

Uniform rental is a route business: Cintas picks up dirty uniforms, launders them, and delivers clean ones on the same truck that also drops off mats, mops, restroom supplies, first-aid cabinets, and fire-protection services — each additional product on an existing route is nearly pure margin. About $11B of revenue, over a million business customers, and a route density that no competitor (UniFirst, Aramark's former uniform unit Vestis) can match. Uniform Rental and Facility Services is ~78% of revenue; First Aid and Safety and Fire Protection are the faster-growing add-ons.

Over 40 consecutive years of dividend increases, consistent buybacks, and a stock that has compounded at nearly 20% a year for two decades. The result is one of the richest multiples in industrials — 45x earnings for a laundry — because the business grows high single digits with expanding margins in every economic environment.

Shines when: Employment grows (more workers means more uniforms), cross-selling of first aid and fire protection deepens routes, pricing holds, and margin expansion continues.

Dangers: The multiple — a de-rating from 45x to 30x is a 33% drawdown with nothing wrong. Employment sensitivity in a recession. Labor and fuel cost inflation. Acquisition of a large competitor (it attempted UniFirst) would add integration risk.

Watch: Reports late Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends May). Organic growth by segment, gross margin, operating margin, full-year guidance revisions, employment data.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%; the stock grinds higher between reports. Not a two-month mover. A superb stuck-bucket holding if you can accept the valuation risk.

URI United Rentals

Sector: Equipment Rental · Market cap: ~$55B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~0.8%

The largest equipment rental company in the world — ~$16B of revenue from renting aerial lifts, earthmovers, generators, and specialty equipment (trench safety, power and HVAC, fluid solutions, mobile storage) to contractors and industrial customers from over 1,500 locations. Built through acquisition (RSC, BlueLine, General Finance, Ahern, Yak) and consolidation of a fragmented industry where scale wins: bigger fleets mean better utilization, purchasing terms, and the ability to serve national accounts.

Rental penetration keeps rising as contractors prefer renting to owning, and mega-projects (semiconductor fabs, data centers, LNG plants, infrastructure) drive demand. Free cash flow is enormous; buybacks retire several percent of shares a year; a dividend started in 2023. Specialty rental (higher margin, faster growing) is the growth engine.

Shines when: Non-residential construction and mega-projects grow, rental rates rise, fleet utilization holds, specialty grows double digits, and acquisitions add accretively.

Dangers: Construction cyclicality — rental demand falls fast in a downturn and used-equipment values fall with it. Capital intensity (fleet purchases). Leverage. Rate and utilization pressure from competitors adding fleet.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Rental revenue growth, fleet productivity, time utilization, specialty growth, used-equipment pricing, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%; it also moves with construction and rate data. The two-month setup is a mega-project-driven raise or a rate-cut catalyst for construction. If stuck, the market leader in a consolidating industry with heavy buybacks — comfortable outside a construction recession.

HVAC, buildings, water & electrical

TT Trane Technologies

Sector: HVAC · Market cap: ~$95B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~0.9%

The former Ingersoll Rand, renamed after it spun off its industrial businesses in 2020 to become a pure climate company — ~$21B of revenue. Commercial HVAC (Trane chillers, rooftop units, building controls, and services) is the largest and most profitable piece, and became a growth story as data centers, semiconductor fabs, and EV battery plants needed enormous cooling capacity. Residential HVAC (Trane and American Standard) rides housing and replacement cycles. Transport refrigeration (Thermo King) cools trucks, trailers, and containers. Irish-domiciled.

Bookings have run ahead of revenue for years, building a backlog that gives visibility other industrials lack; margins have expanded steadily; the dividend is raised annually with buybacks. It has been the best-performing HVAC stock of the cycle.

Shines when: Commercial bookings (data centers, industrial facilities) stay strong, services grow, residential replacement recovers, and margin expansion continues.

Dangers: A premium multiple that prices continued double-digit growth. Data-center order lumpiness. Residential housing weakness. Transport refrigeration is cyclical with freight. Refrigerant transitions add cost.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Bookings growth and backlog, Americas commercial HVAC growth, adjusted operating margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a bookings-driven beat-and-raise quarter or a data-center-capex scare that hits it unfairly. If stuck, a well-run compounder — but bought at 30x, a stuck position can spend a year going sideways.

CARR Carrier Global

Sector: HVAC / Refrigeration · Market cap: ~$60B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.3%

Spun out of United Technologies in 2020, then completely rebuilt: it sold its fire and security businesses and commercial refrigeration, and bought Viessmann Climate Solutions (a German heat-pump and boiler leader) for ~$13B in 2024 to become a pure climate company with ~$22B of revenue. Climate Solutions Americas (residential and light-commercial HVAC — the largest piece), Climate Solutions Europe (Viessmann, heat pumps), Climate Solutions Asia-Pacific, Middle East, and Africa, and Climate Solutions Transportation (container and truck refrigeration).

Carrier trades at a discount to Trane because its mix leans residential and European, both of which have been weak — U.S. residential went through a refrigerant transition and inventory destocking in 2025, and European heat-pump demand collapsed when subsidies shifted. The bull case is that both normalize while data-center commercial HVAC grows. Dividend raised annually; heavy buybacks since the portfolio reshaping.

Shines when: U.S. residential replacement demand recovers after destocking, European heat-pump subsidies stabilize, data-center cooling orders grow, and Viessmann synergies hit.

Dangers: Residential housing and weather sensitivity. European policy on heat pumps. Refrigerant-transition inventory swings. Viessmann integration. Tariffs on components.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Americas residential organic growth and channel inventory, Europe orders, adjusted operating margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12% — more volatile than Trane because of the residential exposure. The two-month setup is a destocking-ends quarter. If stuck, cheaper than Trane with the same secular tailwinds; comfortable.

JCI Johnson Controls International

Sector: Building Systems · Market cap: ~$65B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~1.5%

Commercial building technology — HVAC equipment (York chillers, Silent-Aire data-center cooling), building controls and automation (Metasys, OpenBlue software), fire detection and suppression, and security — ~$23B of revenue after selling its residential and light-commercial HVAC business to Bosch in 2025 to become a pure commercial-buildings company. Roughly half of revenue is service and recurring. Irish-domiciled.

A long-time underperformer that changed CEOs in 2025 (Joakim Weidemanis from Danaher) and immediately began a Danaher-style operating overhaul: portfolio simplification, cost cuts, margin targets. The stock re-rated on the promise of catching up to Trane's margins. Dividend paid for over a century; buybacks.

Shines when: Data-center cooling orders grow (Silent-Aire), service revenue grows, the new CEO's margin program delivers, and commercial construction holds.

Dangers: Execution — the turnaround has been promised before. Commercial real-estate weakness. Fire and security are slow growers. Tariffs and currency. Legacy legal and environmental liabilities.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends September). Orders and backlog, service growth, segment margin, restructuring savings, data-center commentary.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a margin-progress quarter under the new CEO. If stuck, a century-old dividend payer in the middle of a credible turnaround.

LII Lennox International

Sector: Residential & Commercial HVAC · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~0.8%

A ~$5.5B-revenue HVAC pure play with an unusual model: Lennox sells directly to contractors through its own network of over 250 company-owned stores rather than through independent distributors, which gives it better pricing control and margins than Carrier or Trane's residential businesses. Home Comfort Solutions (residential — ~70%) and Building Climate Solutions (commercial rooftop units, refrigeration). Sold its European operations in 2023 to focus on North America.

Margins expanded sharply from 2022 to 2025 on pricing and mix, and the stock doubled. It is the cleanest way to own U.S. residential HVAC replacement demand — and the most exposed to its weakness. Dividend raised annually; buybacks.

Shines when: Replacement demand recovers after the refrigerant-transition destocking, hot summers drive emergency replacements, commercial rooftop demand holds, and pricing sticks.

Dangers: Residential housing and existing-home-sales sensitivity. Weather. Refrigerant-transition inventory swings (2025 was rough). Competition from Carrier, Trane, and Daikin. Tariffs.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Home Comfort organic growth and margin, channel inventory, commercial backlog, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–12% — the most volatile HVAC name. The two-month setup is a summer-demand beat or a destocking-ends quarter. If stuck, a well-run business, but residential cycles can keep it flat for a year.

OTIS Otis Worldwide

Sector: Elevators & Escalators · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~22x · Yield: ~1.6%

The world's largest elevator company, spun out of United Technologies in 2020 — ~$14B of revenue. The business that matters is Service (~60% of revenue and most of the profit): maintenance contracts on an installed base of over 2.4 million units, renewed year after year with pricing escalators, because a building cannot let its elevators go unmaintained. New Equipment (~40%) is cyclical and has been dragged down by China's property collapse, where Otis is a major player.

The model is to sell new equipment at low margin to build the service base, then harvest maintenance for decades. Dividend raised annually since the spin; steady buybacks. It is the closest thing to a subscription business in heavy industry.

Shines when: Service portfolio grows and pricing holds, modernization (upgrading old elevators) accelerates, China new-equipment stabilizes, and margins expand on mix.
