Dangers: China — new-equipment orders fell for years. Commercial real-estate weakness. Competition from Schindler, Kone, and TK Elevator. Leverage from the spin. Slow overall growth.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Service organic growth and margin, maintenance portfolio growth, modernization orders, New Equipment orders (China vs. rest of world).

How to use it: Low beta; earnings reactions ±4–8%, usually on China new-equipment. The two-month setup is a China-stabilization quarter. If stuck, the service annuity makes it a comfortable hold.

XYL Xylem

Sector: Water Technology · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~28x · Yield: ~1.1%

The largest pure-play water technology company — ~$8.8B of revenue from pumps and treatment for utilities (Water Infrastructure), smart water meters and networks (Measurement and Control Solutions — the Sensus metering business), pumps for buildings and industry (Applied Water), and the 2023 acquisition of Evoqua, which added water treatment services and equipment for industrial and municipal customers. Spun out of ITT in 2011.

Water is an infrastructure story with a decade of tailwinds: aging U.S. pipes, federal funding, drought and flood resilience, and the shift to smart metering. Dividend raised annually; moderate buybacks; a valuation that reflects scarcity — there are few ways to own water.

Shines when: Utility capital budgets and federal water funding grow, smart-meter deployments ramp, Evoqua synergies hit, and margins expand.

Dangers: A premium multiple. Municipal budget cycles and project timing. Industrial exposure through Evoqua. Metering is lumpy (large utility contracts). Currency.

Watch: Reports early Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Orders and backlog, Measurement and Control organic growth, adjusted EBITDA margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is an infrastructure-funding catalyst or a post-earnings dip on project timing. If stuck, a scarce asset with structural demand — comfortable.

PNR Pentair

Sector: Water / Pool Equipment · Market cap: ~$17B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1%

A ~$4.2B-revenue water company whose most important business is swimming pools: Pool (~40% of revenue, roughly half of profit) makes pumps, filters, heaters, and automation for residential pools, with a replacement-driven installed base of millions of U.S. pools. Water Solutions (commercial water and ice, residential filtration) and Flow (pumps for residential, commercial, and industrial use) round it out. Irish-domiciled.

The pool business boomed during the pandemic, busted in 2023 as new-pool construction fell and dealers destocked, and has been recovering on the replacement cycle ever since. Dividend raised for nearly 50 consecutive years; margin expansion through a transformation program has been the equity story.

Shines when: Pool replacement demand holds and new-pool construction recovers, transformation-program margin gains continue, and commercial water grows.

Dangers: Housing and discretionary sensitivity — a pool is the ultimate discretionary purchase. Weather. Tariffs on components. Dealer inventory swings.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Pool segment organic growth and margin, return on sales, full-year guidance, housing and existing-home-sales data.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–10%. The two-month setup is buying a housing-fear dip ahead of pool season (spring quarter). If stuck, a dividend aristocrat with a replacement-driven core — comfortable.

HUBB Hubbell

Sector: Electrical & Utility Products · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~1.2%

A ~$6B-revenue maker of the unglamorous hardware that holds the grid together. Utility Solutions (~65%) sells transmission and distribution components — insulators, connectors, arresters, enclosures, and grid automation and metering — to electric utilities, and became a growth story as utilities spend record sums hardening and expanding the grid for data centers, electrification, and wildfire resilience. Electrical Solutions (~35%) sells wiring devices, lighting controls, and connectors to industrial and commercial customers, including data centers.

Founded in 1888, Hubbell raised its dividend for over 15 consecutive years and has expanded margins through portfolio pruning (it sold residential lighting) and pricing. The stock re-rated as grid investment became a multi-decade theme.

Shines when: Utility capex on transmission and distribution grows, grid-automation orders rise, data-center electrical demand expands, and pricing holds.

Dangers: Utility ordering can be lumpy and subject to inventory digestion (a 2024 destocking hit). A premium multiple for an electrical-products company. Raw-material costs (copper, steel). Competition from Eaton and Schneider in electrical.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Utility Solutions organic growth, Electrical Solutions growth, adjusted operating margin, utility capex guidance from the large utilities.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a grid-capex catalyst or a destocking-ends quarter. If stuck, a picks-and-shovels play on a decade-long grid build — comfortable.

Tools

SWK Stanley Black & Decker

Sector: Tools / Industrial · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: ~4%

DeWalt, Craftsman, Stanley, Black+Decker, and Cub Cadet — ~$15B of revenue, about 85% from Tools and Outdoor sold through Home Depot, Lowe's, Amazon, and industrial distributors, plus an Industrial segment of engineered fastening for autos and aerospace. It is the largest tool company in the world, with the brands professionals buy.

And it has been a mess: after over-earning during the pandemic, it overbuilt inventory, margins collapsed in 2022, and the stock fell 60%. A multi-year transformation — supply-chain overhaul, SKU reduction, $2B of cost savings — has been rebuilding gross margin toward 35%, but tariffs on Chinese-sourced tools hit hard in 2025 and a new CEO took over. It has raised its dividend for over 55 consecutive years, a streak the market worries about because the payout is high.

Shines when: Gross margin recovers toward the mid-30s, housing turnover lifts DIY and pro demand, tariff costs are offset by pricing and sourcing shifts, and debt comes down.

Dangers: Tariffs — a large share of production was in China and Mexico. Housing weakness. The dividend consuming most of free cash flow. Execution on a long turnaround. Leverage.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Gross margin, Tools organic growth, free cash flow vs. dividend, inventory, tariff mitigation progress.

How to use it: A turnaround at a value multiple; earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is a gross-margin-progress quarter or a housing-rate-cut catalyst. If stuck, the 4% yield pays you to wait, but this is a name where the wait has already been three years.

SNA Snap-on

Sector: Professional Tools · Market cap: ~$17B · P/E: ~17x · Yield: ~2.7%

Premium hand tools, power tools, tool storage, and diagnostics sold to auto mechanics — mostly through a fleet of nearly 5,000 franchised vans that visit repair shops weekly, which is the moat: the mechanic buys on the spot, on credit that Snap-on's Financial Services arm extends. About $4.7B of revenue across Snap-on Tools (the van channel), Commercial and Industrial (aerospace, military, education), and Repair Systems and Information (diagnostic software and equipment sold to shops and dealerships). Gross margins above 50% — the highest of any tool company.

Slow, steady, and cash-rich: a dividend raised for 15-plus consecutive years, never cut since 1939, plus buybacks. The stock trades on mechanic confidence — when technicians feel good about their income they buy expensive tool boxes; when they don't, sales stall, which is what happened in 2024.

Shines when: Technician confidence and vehicle repair volumes rise (older car fleets mean more repair), diagnostic software subscriptions grow, and Financial Services credit stays clean.

Dangers: Consumer-like sensitivity in the van channel. Financial Services credit losses in a downturn. Tariffs on imported components (most production is in the U.S., which helps). Slow growth.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Snap-on Tools organic growth, Repair Systems growth, gross margin, Financial Services delinquencies.

How to use it: Low beta; earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a van-channel reacceleration. If stuck, a cash machine with a fortress balance sheet — comfortable.

Rail, waste & logistics

CSX CSX Corporation

Sector: Railroad · Market cap: ~$65B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.5%

One of the two eastern U.S. railroads (with Norfolk Southern), ~$14B of revenue hauling intermodal containers, coal, chemicals, autos, grain, and metals across 20,000 miles of track east of the Mississippi. The 2017 arrival of Hunter Harrison brought precision scheduled railroading and transformed CSX from the industry's laggard into its most efficient operator, with operating ratios in the low 60s.

The 2025 announcement that Union Pacific would acquire Norfolk Southern to create the first transcontinental railroad put CSX in play: either it partners with BNSF (Berkshire) to match, or it becomes a target itself, or it competes against a much larger rival. Meanwhile, coal (once a quarter of revenue) keeps declining, and intermodal competes with trucking. Dividend raised annually; heavy buybacks.

Shines when: Freight volumes recover, intermodal takes share from trucking, coal exports hold, operating ratio improves, and merger dynamics produce a premium.

Dangers: Freight recession sensitivity. Coal decline. Regulatory scrutiny of rail mergers (the Surface Transportation Board). Service disruptions and labor. If UNP–NSC closes without a CSX response, competitive positioning weakens.

Watch: Reports mid Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Volume by commodity, revenue per unit, operating ratio, weekly AAR carload data, merger headlines.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%, but merger headlines can produce 5–10% days. The two-month setup is a merger-catalyst or a freight-volume inflection. If stuck, a railroad — one of the most durable business models in existence; the dividend and buyback carry you.

WAB Westinghouse Air Brake (Wabtec)

Sector: Rail Equipment · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~0.5%

The dominant supplier to the rail industry: locomotives (it merged with GE Transportation in 2019 and is the largest locomotive maker in the world), braking systems, couplers, digital signaling, and a services business that maintains and modernizes a fleet of over 23,000 locomotives — ~$11B of revenue, with Freight (~75%) and Transit (~25%, passenger-rail equipment and services). Roughly half of revenue is aftermarket services and parts, which recur through every freight cycle.

Growth comes from locomotive modernizations (rebuilding old engines with new technology, cheaper than new units), international orders (Kazakhstan, India, Brazil), and digital products that improve railroad efficiency. Dividend raised annually; buybacks. Margins have expanded steadily since the GE merger integration.

Shines when: Railroads invest in fleet modernization, international locomotive orders land, services grow, and margins expand on integration synergies.

Dangers: Railroad capex cyclicality. Customer concentration (a handful of North American railroads). International project timing and currency. A premium multiple for an industrial.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Backlog (multi-year), Freight segment organic growth, adjusted operating margin, international orders, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a backlog-driven raise. If stuck, a near-monopoly with a large services annuity — comfortable.

RSG Republic Services

Sector: Waste Management · Market cap: ~$70B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~1%

The second-largest waste company in North America — ~$17B of revenue collecting, transferring, recycling, and landfilling trash for residential, commercial, and industrial customers, plus Environmental Solutions (hazardous waste, industrial cleaning, acquired with US Ecology in 2022). Landfills are the moat: nobody can permit a new one, so owning a network of them near population centers is a permanent toll road, and pricing rises above inflation every year.

Cascade Investment (Bill Gates's vehicle) has been the largest shareholder for over a decade. The company raises its dividend annually, buys back stock, and has expanded margins through pricing, route technology, and recycling automation. It is the boring business par excellence: trash volume grows with the economy, pricing grows faster, and customers rarely switch.

Shines when: Pricing exceeds cost inflation, volumes hold, recycled commodity prices rise, Environmental Solutions grows, and renewable natural gas from landfills adds revenue.

Dangers: A premium multiple. Volume softness in construction and industrial waste during slowdowns. Recycled commodity price swings. Labor and fuel costs. Regulatory pressure on landfills and emissions.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Core price, volume, adjusted EBITDA margin, Environmental Solutions growth, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Low beta; earnings reactions ±3–6%. Not a two-month mover. As a stuck-bucket holding it is nearly ideal — defensive, pricing power, and a network of assets that cannot be replicated.

WCN Waste Connections

Sector: Waste Management · Market cap: ~$45B · P/E: ~35x · Yield: ~0.7%

The third-largest North American waste company, ~$9.5B of revenue, with a deliberately different strategy: it operates in secondary and rural markets where it is often the only provider, and in exclusive-franchise markets (much of the U.S. West) where municipalities grant it sole rights, rather than competing in big cities against Waste Management and Republic. The result is the highest margins and pricing power in the industry. Canadian-domiciled after its 2016 merger with Progressive Waste.

Growth is acquisition-driven — it buys dozens of small private haulers a year — plus pricing above inflation and landfill development (including a large new site in the Northeast). Dividend raised annually since initiation; it trades at a premium even to its premium-priced peers.

Shines when: Pricing holds above cost inflation, acquisitions add revenue, exclusive-market density improves margins, and landfill gas projects contribute.

Dangers: The richest multiple in the sector. Acquisition dependence. Recycled commodity and renewable-energy-credit price swings. Canadian tax and currency considerations. Employee-turnover costs in a labor-intensive business.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Core price, adjusted EBITDA margin, acquisition revenue, employee turnover, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Low beta; earnings reactions ±3–6%. Same logic as Republic — a stuck-bucket name, not a mover.

EXPD Expeditors International

Sector: Freight Forwarding · Market cap: ~$16B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.3% (paid semiannually)

An asset-light logistics company — ~$11B of revenue arranging air and ocean freight, customs brokerage, and warehousing for shippers, without owning planes or ships. It buys capacity from carriers and sells it to customers, earning a spread that widens when freight markets are chaotic (2021–22 was a windfall) and narrows when they normalize. Founded in Seattle in 1979 and run by lifers; no debt, no acquisitions, semiannual dividends, and steady buybacks.

Expeditors is a model of conservatism: it never gives guidance, never borrows, and compensates every branch on its own profit. Net revenue (the spread) is the number that matters, not gross revenue, which swings with freight rates.

Shines when: Freight rates spike (supply disruptions, tariff-driven front-loading, Red Sea diversions), volumes grow, and customs brokerage benefits from trade complexity.

Dangers: Freight-rate normalization compresses the spread. Trade wars reduce volumes even as they increase complexity. Competition from DSV, Kuehne+Nagel, and DHL. No guidance means the stock reacts hard to surprises.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Airfreight and ocean tonnage and volumes, net revenue per shipment, operating margin, freight-rate indices (Drewry, Baltic).

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a freight-rate spike (they are frequent). If stuck, no debt and a cash pile — one of the safest balance sheets in the sector.

CHRW C.H. Robinson

Sector: Freight Brokerage · Market cap: ~$17B · P/E: ~22x · Yield: ~2%

The largest truckload freight broker in North America — ~$17B of revenue matching shippers with trucking capacity from a network of tens of thousands of carriers — plus Global Forwarding (air and ocean). Like Expeditors, it earns a spread; unlike Expeditors, its core is domestic trucking, a market that went through a three-year freight recession after the pandemic boom, with too many trucks chasing too little freight.

A new CEO in 2023 launched an operating overhaul — AI-driven quoting and load matching, headcount reduction, a "lean" operating model — that raised productivity and margins even as the freight market stayed weak. The stock re-rated on the self-help story. Dividend raised for over 25 consecutive years; buybacks.

Shines when: The truckload cycle turns (spot rates rise above contract), productivity gains continue, market share grows in a fragmented industry, and forwarding benefits from disruptions.

Dangers: Freight-cycle timing — brokers get squeezed when spot rates rise faster than contract pricing. Digital brokers and carrier-direct platforms. Execution risk on the operating model. Slow volume growth.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Adjusted gross profit per load, North American Surface Transportation volumes and margin, headcount productivity, spot vs. contract truckload rates (Cass, DAT indices).

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a freight-cycle turn or a productivity-driven beat. If stuck, a dividend grower with a clean balance sheet in the middle of a credible operating turnaround.

Consumer Staples

Diapers, bleach, chocolate, canned soup, coffee, spices, grocery, dollar stores, beer, bourbon, grain. The Atlas has PG, KO, PEP, CL, MDLZ, GIS, COST, WMT, PM, MO; this is the rest of the pantry — a sector that spent 2023–25 out of favor, which is where most of the value in this section comes from.

Household & personal careKMBCLXCHDPackaged foodHSYCAGCPBKHCSJMHRLMKCLWPOSTGrocery & discount retailKRACICASYBJSFMDGBeverages & alcoholKDPSTZBF.BTAPMNSTAgriculture & proteinADMBGTSN

Household & personal care

KMB Kimberly-Clark

Sector: Household & Personal Care · Market cap: ~$45B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~3.8%

Huggies, Kleenex, Kotex, Cottonelle, Scott, and Depend — ~$20B of revenue in three segments: North America (about half), International Personal Care, and International Family Care and Professional. Kimberly-Clark invented the disposable-diaper and facial-tissue categories and still holds number-one or number-two share in most of them, but it competes with Procter & Gamble on one side and private label on the other, and pulp is its largest input cost.

Then it made the biggest move in its history: in November 2025 it agreed to acquire Kenvue — Tylenol, Band-Aid, Listerine, Neutrogena, the former Johnson & Johnson consumer business — for roughly $48B, creating a company with ~$32B of revenue and a much larger health-and-wellness footprint. The stock fell double digits on the announcement, on leverage and integration worries and Kenvue's Tylenol litigation overhang. The deal is expected to close in the second half of 2026. Dividend raised for over 50 consecutive years.

Shines when: Pulp and commodity costs fall, the Kenvue integration delivers cost synergies, pricing holds without volume loss, and the dividend streak is maintained through the deal.

Dangers: Integration and leverage from a transformational deal. Kenvue's litigation and brand issues. Private-label share gains in tissue and diapers. Currency on a large international base. Birth-rate decline in diapers.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic growth (price vs. volume), gross margin, Kenvue closing and synergy updates, net leverage, pulp prices.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%; deal headlines can be larger. The two-month setup is a post-deal-close clean quarter or a staples rotation. If stuck, a half-century dividend record at a nearly 4% yield — but the leverage means "stuck" now carries more risk than it used to.

CLX Clorox

Sector: Household Products · Market cap: ~$16B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~3.8%

Clorox bleach and wipes, Pine-Sol, Glad bags, Kingsford charcoal, Hidden Valley ranch, Burt's Bees, Brita, and cat litter — ~$7B of revenue, mostly U.S., with leading share in most categories. It sold its Argentina business and its vitamins unit to focus the portfolio. A 2023 cyberattack disrupted order processing and cost it a quarter of sales; a 2025 ERP system transition caused another temporary distortion as retailers pre-bought inventory.

The stock is a classic staples bond proxy: a dividend raised for nearly 50 consecutive years, a yield near 4%, and a multiple that moves with rate expectations as much as with results. Margins recovered from the 2022 cost squeeze back toward the low-40s gross, and management's "IGNITE" strategy targets steady margin expansion rather than growth.

Shines when: Gross margin recovers to pre-pandemic levels, the ERP transition laps, rates fall (bond-proxy re-rating), and category volumes stabilize.

Dangers: Low growth — categories like bleach and charcoal don't grow. Private label. Commodity costs (resin, chlorine). Execution slips (cyberattack, ERP). Rate sensitivity in the multiple.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). Organic sales growth, gross margin, ERP-related shipment timing, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a rate-cut rotation into staples or a post-disruption normalization quarter. If stuck, the yield and dividend history make it an easy hold, though you will not be compensated with growth.

CHD Church & Dwight

Sector: Household & Personal Care · Market cap: ~$24B · P/E: ~26x · Yield: ~1.2%

Arm & Hammer (baking soda, laundry, cat litter, toothpaste), OxiClean, Batiste dry shampoo, Trojan, Waterpik, Hero (acne patches), TheraBreath, Zicam, and Vitafusion — ~$6.2B of revenue built by buying niche brands with growth and running them through a lean, low-overhead operation. About 80% of sales are domestic; roughly half come from "value" positioning, which helps when consumers trade down.

For twenty years the formula worked: acquire a brand with a growth story (Hero and TheraBreath are the recent successes), expand distribution, and let margins scale. Not every deal has worked — Vitafusion gummies and Flawless hair removal produced write-downs — and 2025 brought slower category growth and a rare guidance cut. Dividend raised for over 25 years; buybacks.

Shines when: Hero, TheraBreath, and Batiste grow double digits, trade-down favors value brands, a new acquisition lands, and gross margin expands on productivity.

Dangers: A premium multiple for a staples company. Acquisition misfires. Category slowdowns in personal care. Retailer inventory destocking. Tariffs on imported components.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic growth, gross margin, growth-brand performance (Hero, TheraBreath), acquisition announcements, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a post-guidance-cut dip when the growth brands are intact. If stuck, a well-run compounder — but bought at 26x, sideways is the likely stuck outcome.

Packaged food

HSY The Hershey Company

Sector: Confectionery / Snacks · Market cap: ~$38B · P/E: ~22x · Yield: ~3%

Hershey's, Reese's, Kit Kat (U.S. license), Kisses, Twizzlers, Jolly Rancher, plus salty snacks (SkinnyPop, Dot's Pretzels, Pirate's Booty) — ~$11.5B of revenue, roughly 90% North America, with nearly half of U.S. chocolate share. The Hershey Trust controls the voting stock, which has blocked takeover attempts (Mondelez approached in 2024) and keeps the company independent.

The 2024–25 cocoa price spike — cocoa tripled to record highs on West African crop failures — crushed gross margins and pushed the stock down 40% from its peak, and management responded with pricing, hedging, and a new CEO (Kirk Tanner, from Wendy's) in 2025. The bull case is simple: cocoa normalizes, margins recover, and a dominant franchise re-rates. The bear case is GLP-1 drugs reducing snacking and a consumer that trades down from premium candy.

Shines when: Cocoa prices fall and hedges roll off, pricing sticks without volume loss, salty snacks grow, and the new CEO's cost program delivers.

Dangers: Cocoa — the single largest driver of earnings. GLP-1 impact on candy consumption. Private label and trade-down. Tariffs on cocoa imports. Trust ownership limits strategic options.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Gross margin, cocoa futures (ICE), North America Confectionery volume vs. price, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%, and the stock also moves with cocoa prices day to day. The two-month setup is a cocoa-price decline ahead of a margin-recovery quarter. If stuck, a 3% yield and a franchise with pricing power — comfortable once cocoa normalizes.

CAG Conagra Brands

Sector: Packaged Food · Market cap: ~$9B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: ~7% (the market is pricing dividend risk)

Birds Eye, Healthy Choice, Marie Callender's, Slim Jim, Hunt's, Chef Boyardee, Duncan Hines, Orville Redenbacher's, Reddi-wip — ~$11.5B of revenue across Grocery and Snacks (~40%), Refrigerated and Frozen (~40%), International, and Foodservice. Conagra bet on frozen meals with the 2018 Pinnacle Foods acquisition, which loaded the balance sheet with debt and left it with a portfolio of mature brands.

The stock fell to multi-decade lows in 2025 as volumes declined, cost inflation and tariffs squeezed margins, and the market doubted the dividend was sustainable at a payout near or above free cash flow. It trades at a single-digit multiple with a yield that says "cut coming" — the classic packaged-food value trap or value opportunity, depending on execution.

Shines when: Volume trends stabilize, input-cost inflation eases, debt comes down, the dividend is maintained through the trough, and frozen and snacks regain growth.

Dangers: A dividend cut. Leverage. Volume decline in center-store categories. Private label. GLP-1 effects on frozen meals and snacks. A portfolio with few growth brands.

Watch: Reports early Apr/Jul/Oct/Dec (fiscal year ends May). Organic volume, adjusted gross margin, free cash flow vs. dividend, net leverage, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a dividend-affirmed clean quarter. If stuck, the yield is only comforting if it survives — treat this as the riskiest staple in the section.

CPB The Campbell's Company

Sector: Packaged Food · Market cap: ~$9B · P/E: ~11x · Yield: ~5%

Campbell's soup, Pepperidge Farm (Goldfish, Milano), Prego, Pace, Swanson, V8, Snyder's-Lance, Kettle Brand, Cape Cod, and — since the 2024 acquisition of Sovos Brands — Rao's, the premium pasta sauce that is the fastest-growing large brand in the center of the store. About $10B of revenue in two segments: Meals and Beverages (soup, sauces) and Snacks (Goldfish, pretzels, chips). Dropped "Soup" from the corporate name in 2024. The Dorrance family heirs hold a large stake.

Soup is a mature, shrinking category propped up by pricing; snacks were supposed to be the growth engine but stalled in 2024–25 as consumers pulled back. Rao's is growing, but the stock de-rated to an 11x multiple and a 5% yield as volumes declined and leverage from the Sovos deal weighed.

Shines when: Snacks volume recovers, Rao's keeps growing double digits, Sovos synergies hit, input costs ease, and debt is paid down.

Dangers: Soup decline. Snacks weakness and private-label pressure. Leverage. GLP-1 effects on snacking. Steel and tomato costs; tariffs on cans.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends July). Snacks organic growth, Rao's growth, adjusted gross margin, net leverage, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a snacks-stabilization quarter. If stuck, the 5% yield is better covered than Conagra's, and Rao's is a real asset — a tolerable hold.

KHC The Kraft Heinz Company

Sector: Packaged Food · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: ~6%

Heinz ketchup, Kraft mac and cheese, Philadelphia, Oscar Mayer, Lunchables, Velveeta, Jell-O, Kool-Aid, Maxwell House — ~$26B of revenue and one of the most instructive failures in consumer investing. The 2015 merger engineered by 3G Capital and Berkshire Hathaway cut costs relentlessly, starved the brands of investment, and produced a $15B write-down in 2019. Berkshire remains the largest holder and took its own impairment in 2025.

In September 2025 the company announced it would split in two: a "Global Taste Elevation" company (Heinz, Philadelphia, Kraft sauces — the growth brands) and a "North American Grocery" company (Oscar Mayer, Lunchables, Kraft mac and cheese — the mature cash generators), with completion targeted for the second half of 2026. The stock did not rally; the market sees two mediocre companies instead of one. It trades at 10x earnings with a 6% yield.

Shines when: The split unlocks a higher multiple for the sauces business, volumes stabilize, emerging-market growth continues, and the dividend is maintained through the separation.

Dangers: Volume decline across the portfolio. Private label. GLP-1 effects on processed food. The split adding cost and dis-synergies. Dividend allocation between the two companies. Berkshire eventually selling.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic volume/mix, adjusted gross margin, split timeline and capital structure, free cash flow, Berkshire filings.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%; split headlines can be larger. The two-month setup is a separation catalyst. If stuck, the yield is well covered and the brands are not going anywhere — but this has been a value trap for a decade, and the split may not change that.

SJM The J.M. Smucker Company

Sector: Packaged Food / Coffee · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~11x · Yield: ~4%

Folgers, Dunkin' (at-home coffee), Café Bustelo, Jif, Smucker's, Uncrustables (frozen PB&J — a billion-dollar brand growing double digits), Milk-Bone, Meow Mix, and since the 2023 Hostess acquisition, Twinkies and Ding Dongs — ~$9B of revenue. The Smucker family still runs it from Orrville, Ohio, and the dividend has been raised for over 25 consecutive years.

Two problems hit at once: coffee bean prices spiked to record highs in 2024–25, forcing repeated price increases that hurt volumes, and the $5.6B Hostess deal looked worse every quarter as sweet-baked-goods sales fell, producing impairments. The stock trades at 11x earnings with a 4% yield — cheap for a company with Uncrustables and the number-one at-home coffee position.

Shines when: Coffee costs fall and pricing holds, Uncrustables capacity expansion drives growth, Hostess stabilizes, and leverage comes down.

Dangers: Coffee commodity costs. Hostess — further impairments, secular decline in sweet baked goods. Leverage. Pet food competition. GLP-1 effects.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends April). Coffee segment volume and margin, Uncrustables growth, Sweet Baked Snacks trend, net leverage, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a coffee-price decline ahead of a margin quarter. If stuck, a family-run dividend grower at a value multiple — comfortable, with Hostess as the caveat.

HRL Hormel Foods

Sector: Packaged Food / Protein · Market cap: ~$14B · P/E: ~17x · Yield: ~4.5%

Spam, Jennie-O turkey, Planters (bought from Kraft Heinz in 2021), Skippy, Applegate, Hormel bacon and pepperoni, Justin's — ~$12B of revenue across Retail, Foodservice (a strength — Hormel is a major supplier to restaurants), and International. The Hormel Foundation controls nearly half the stock, which insulates it from activists and takeover. A dividend king: raised for nearly 60 consecutive years.

Growth stalled for several years — Planters underperformed, turkey went through avian-flu disruptions, and the company's cost structure lagged peers — and the stock fell to decade lows. A new CEO took over in 2025 with a "Transform and Modernize" program targeting cost savings and portfolio focus. The yield near 4.5% is the highest in its history.

Shines when: The cost program delivers, Planters and turkey recover, foodservice grows, pork and turkey input costs ease, and the dividend streak continues.

Dangers: Low growth in mature categories. Avian flu and protein commodity swings. Planters continued weakness. Foundation control limiting change. Tariffs on exports.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends October). Organic volume by segment, adjusted operating margin, cost-savings progress, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a cost-program-progress quarter or a staples rotation. If stuck, a dividend king with a fortress balance sheet — one of the safest yields in the section.

MKC McCormick & Company

Sector: Spices & Flavor · Market cap: ~$18B · P/E: ~22x · Yield: ~2.6%

The world's largest spice and seasoning company — McCormick, Lawry's, Old Bay, French's mustard, Frank's RedHot, Cholula, Zatarain's, Simply Asia — ~$6.8B of revenue in two segments: Consumer (~57%, retail spices and condiments) and Flavor Solutions (~43%, custom flavors and seasonings sold to packaged-food companies and restaurant chains, a business that grows with its customers' innovation). Founded in Baltimore in 1889; still headquartered in Maryland.

Spices are a structurally growing category (home cooking, global cuisines, health), and McCormick has raised its dividend for nearly 40 consecutive years. The business has been dealing with tariffs on imported spices (nearly all raw materials come from abroad) and Flavor Solutions softness as restaurant customers slowed, but it remains the highest-quality name in packaged food.

Shines when: Consumer volume grows, Flavor Solutions recovers with restaurant innovation, tariff costs are mitigated through pricing and sourcing, and gross margin expands.

Dangers: Tariffs on spice imports. Private-label spices. Flavor Solutions cyclicality with restaurant traffic. A premium multiple for staples. Leverage from past acquisitions (Cholula, FONA).

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic growth by segment (volume vs. price), gross margin, tariff mitigation, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a tariff-relief catalyst or a Flavor Solutions recovery quarter. If stuck, a category leader with a structural tailwind — comfortable.

LW Lamb Weston Holdings

Sector: Frozen Potatoes · Market cap: ~$8B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~2.5%

French fries. Lamb Weston is the largest producer of frozen potato products in North America and number two globally — ~$6.5B of revenue, roughly 80% foodservice (McDonald's is its largest customer at over 10% of sales), the rest retail (Alexia, Grown in Idaho, private label). Spun out of Conagra in 2016.

The pandemic-era fry boom led every producer to add capacity just as restaurant traffic softened in 2024; a botched ERP transition then cost Lamb Weston sales and inventory in the same year, and the stock fell by more than half. Activist Jana Partners arrived, the CEO was replaced, and management launched a cost program and capacity rationalization. It is a duopoly-like business (with McCain) that over-earned, over-built, and is now working through the hangover.

Shines when: Restaurant traffic (especially QSR) recovers, industry capacity additions slow, pricing holds, cost cuts hit, and the ERP problems are fully behind it.

Dangers: Restaurant-traffic weakness. Industry overcapacity and price competition. Potato crop and input costs. Customer concentration. GLP-1 effects on QSR.

Watch: Reports early Apr/Jul/Oct/Dec (fiscal year ends May). Volume and price/mix by segment, adjusted EBITDA margin, capacity utilization commentary, restaurant traffic data (Black Box, Placer.ai).

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is a volume-stabilization quarter or an activist-driven catalyst. If stuck, a duopoly business at a trough — comfortable if traffic recovers, painful if overcapacity persists.

POST Post Holdings

Sector: Packaged Food · Market cap: ~$6B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: none

Post cereals (Honey Bunches of Oats, Grape-Nuts, Fruity Pebbles), Weetabix (UK), Michael Foods (the largest egg processor for foodservice — liquid and precooked eggs — plus Bob Evans refrigerated sides), and pet food (Nutrish, Nature's Recipe, 9Lives, Kibbles 'n Bits, bought from Smucker in 2023) — ~$8B of revenue. Run by Rob Vitale as a serial acquirer and spinner: it bought and later spun off BellRing Brands (Premier Protein), sold a stake in 8th Avenue, and has bought dozens of brands at low multiples.

No dividend; aggressive buybacks (it has retired a large fraction of its shares). The cereal business shrinks; eggs swing with avian flu and egg prices; pet food and refrigerated retail grow. It is the packaged-food company for investors who trust capital allocation over category growth.

Shines when: Egg margins are strong, pet food integration delivers, buybacks compound EPS, an accretive acquisition lands, and cereal declines are managed for cash.

Dangers: Cereal decline. Avian-flu supply shocks in eggs. Leverage from acquisitions. Key-person risk on the CEO. A small-cap with limited following.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends September). Segment adjusted EBITDA (Foodservice, Post Consumer Brands), net leverage, buyback pace, acquisition announcements.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a deal announcement or an egg-margin beat. If stuck, a capital-allocation compounder at a mid-teens multiple — comfortable.

Grocery & discount retail

KR The Kroger Co.

Sector: Grocery · Market cap: ~$45B · P/E: ~14x · Yield: ~2%

The largest traditional supermarket chain in America — ~$150B of revenue from about 2,700 stores under Kroger, Ralphs, Fred Meyer, Harris Teeter, King Soopers, Smith's, and other banners, plus fuel centers, pharmacies, a large private-label business (Simple Truth, Private Selection), and a retail-media and data business (84.51°) that sells shopper insights to consumer-goods companies.

The $25B merger with Albertsons, announced in 2022, was blocked by a federal judge in December 2024 on antitrust grounds; Kroger responded with a $7.5B accelerated buyback. In March 2025 the long-time CEO resigned over personal conduct, and an interim CEO began closing underperforming stores and refocusing on fresh, digital, and alternative profit streams. Dividend raised annually since 2006.

Shines when: Identical-store sales grow ahead of inflation, private label and retail media expand margins, buybacks compound EPS, and Walmart's share gains slow.

Dangers: Thin margins (2–3% operating) with no cushion. Walmart, Costco, Amazon, and Aldi taking share. Labor costs and union negotiations. Leadership transition. Fuel-margin volatility.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends January). Identical sales excluding fuel, gross margin, digital sales growth, alternative profit (retail media), buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a same-store-sales beat or a defensive rotation. If stuck, a defensive cash generator with heavy buybacks — comfortable.

ACI Albertsons Companies

Sector: Grocery · Market cap: ~$11B · P/E: ~9x · Yield: ~3%

The second-largest traditional grocer — ~$80B of revenue from about 2,200 stores under Albertsons, Safeway, Vons, Jewel-Osco, Shaw's, Acme, Tom Thumb, and others — with a large pharmacy business and a growing retail-media and loyalty platform. Taken private by Cerberus in 2006, re-IPO'd in 2020, and then spent two years in limbo as the Kroger merger worked through the courts before being blocked in December 2024.

After the deal collapsed, Albertsons sued Kroger for breach, raised its dividend, launched a large buyback, and reset as a standalone company with a "Customers for Life" strategy focused on pharmacy, digital, and loyalty. It trades at a single-digit multiple because margins are thinner than Kroger's and the balance sheet carries more debt.

Shines when: Identical sales grow, pharmacy and digital drive traffic, retail media scales, cost cuts lift margins, and buybacks shrink the float.

Dangers: The same competitive pressures as Kroger with less scale and more leverage. Union labor costs. Pharmacy reimbursement pressure. Private-equity-era debt.

Watch: Reports mid Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends February). Identical sales excluding fuel, adjusted EBITDA, pharmacy growth, net leverage, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a same-store-sales inflection at a single-digit multiple. If stuck, cheap and defensive with a 3% yield, but leverage and margins make it the weaker of the two grocers.

CASY Casey's General Stores

Sector: Convenience Stores · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~0.5%

About 2,900 convenience stores across the Midwest and South, most in towns of fewer than 20,000 people where Casey's is the gas station, the grocery, and — the part that matters — the pizza place: it is one of the largest pizza chains in America by locations, with made-from-scratch dough, and prepared food carries margins no fuel or packaged-goods sale can match. Revenue is ~$16B, but inside-store gross profit is the number that drives the stock. The 2024 Fikes acquisition added nearly 200 stores in Texas.

Casey's has grown through acquisition of small-town chains that nobody else wants and through steadily increasing prepared-food attach. The dividend is small but raised for over 25 years; the stock has compounded quietly for two decades.

Shines when: Inside-store same-store sales grow, prepared-food margin expands, fuel margins hold (Casey's earns more when wholesale gasoline is volatile), and acquisitions add stores.

Dangers: Fuel-margin volatility. Rural-consumer weakness. Labor costs in a people-intensive model. A premium multiple for a convenience chain. EV adoption eventually reducing fuel traffic.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends April). Inside same-store sales, prepared food and dispensed beverage growth, fuel gross margin per gallon, store count.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a fuel-margin windfall quarter or a post-acquisition raise. If stuck, a durable regional monopoly — comfortable.

BJ BJ's Wholesale Club

Sector: Warehouse Club · Market cap: ~$14B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: none

The East Coast's Costco — about 250 warehouse clubs from Maine to Florida (expanding into Ohio, Indiana, and Tennessee), ~$21B of revenue, 7-plus million members, and a model that differs from Costco's in useful ways: smaller pack sizes, a broader grocery assortment, manufacturer coupons, and a larger share of revenue from its own gas stations. Membership fees (raised in 2025 for the first time in seven years) are nearly all profit and give it Costco-style earnings visibility.

Taken private in 2011 and re-IPO'd in 2018, BJ's has grown members and comparable sales consistently, expanded into new markets, and bought back stock rather than pay a dividend. It trades at a discount to Costco because it is smaller and regional, but the economics rhyme.

Shines when: Membership grows and renewal rates hold above 90%, comparable sales excluding gas grow, new clubs ramp, and the fee increase flows to earnings.

Dangers: Costco and Walmart's Sam's Club expanding into its markets. Gas-margin volatility. Regional concentration. Consumer trade-down helps, but a real recession hurts discretionary categories.

Watch: Reports mid Mar/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends January). Comparable club sales excluding gas, membership fee income, renewal rate, new club openings.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a comp beat or a membership-fee catalyst. If stuck, a recurring-fee model with a clean balance sheet — comfortable.

SFM Sprouts Farmers Market

Sector: Specialty Grocery · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: none

A natural and organic grocer with over 400 stores, mostly in the Sun Belt, built around produce and a "health enthusiast" customer — ~$9B of revenue, no meaningful private-label competition because its differentiated products (attribute-driven: keto, plant-based, gluten-free) don't overlap much with mainstream grocers. A 2020 strategy reset shrank the store format, exited weak markets, and refocused on its core shopper; the result was several years of double-digit earnings growth and a stock that rose fivefold before a 2025 pullback as growth normalized.

No dividend; buybacks. Sprouts is the rare grocer with pricing power and a growth runway (management targets 10% unit growth a year).

Shines when: Comparable sales grow mid-single digits or better, new stores ramp, gross margin holds, and the differentiated-products thesis keeps competition at bay.

Dangers: Valuation for a grocer. Comp deceleration after a strong run. Whole Foods (Amazon), Trader Joe's, and mainstream grocers expanding natural assortments. Produce cost volatility.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Comparable store sales, new store count, gross margin, e-commerce penetration, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15% — the most volatile grocer. The two-month setup is a post-pullback comp reacceleration. If stuck, a growth grocer with no debt; sideways is the likely stuck outcome.

DG Dollar General

Sector: Discount Retail · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~2%

Over 20,000 small-box stores, most in rural towns and small cities where it is the only general-merchandise retailer within miles — ~$42B of revenue, roughly 80% consumables (food, cleaning products, paper goods) sold to lower-income households at small pack sizes. The model is low rent, low labor, and proximity; a Dollar General is within five miles of three-quarters of Americans.

The stock collapsed in 2023–24 — from $250 to under $70 — as its core customer ran out of money, shrink (theft) surged, stores deteriorated, and the company cut guidance repeatedly. Former CEO Todd Vasos returned, slowed store growth, fixed store standards, closed the failed Popshelf concept, and by 2025 comps and margins were recovering and the stock had rebounded sharply. Dividend maintained through the trough; buybacks paused, then resumed.

Shines when: Low-income consumer health improves (wage growth, SNAP stability), shrink falls, same-store sales grow with traffic, and margins recover toward historical levels.

Dangers: Its customer is the most economically fragile in retail. Tariffs on imported general merchandise. Walmart's price investments and delivery expansion into rural areas. Labor and store-condition costs. Regulatory scrutiny of store safety.

Watch: Reports mid Mar/Jun/Aug/Dec (fiscal year ends January). Same-store sales (traffic vs. ticket), gross margin (shrink commentary), operating margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is a comp-recovery quarter or a trade-down thesis in a slowdown. If stuck, cheap and defensive with a dividend — but the 2023–24 collapse shows how far "boring" can fall when execution fails.

Beverages & alcohol

KDP Keurig Dr Pepper

Sector: Beverages · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: ~3%

Dr Pepper, Canada Dry, 7UP, Snapple, A&W, Sunkist, Core, Bai, plus the Keurig single-serve coffee system (brewers and K-Cup pods) and distribution deals with brands like Ghost energy, C4, Electrolit, and La Colombe — ~$16B of revenue. Dr Pepper overtook Pepsi as the number-two soda in America in 2024, which is the kind of thing that happens when a brand quietly grows for a decade.

In 2025 the company announced the acquisition of JDE Peet's (the Dutch coffee company behind Peet's, Douwe Egberts, and L'OR) for roughly $18B, with a plan to then split into two companies: a global coffee business and a North American beverage business. The stock fell on the announcement — leverage, and the market's dislike of coffee, a category with commodity-cost problems and slow growth. The split is the catalyst.

Shines when: The JDE Peet's deal closes and the separation is executed, U.S. refreshment beverages keep gaining share, energy-drink partnerships grow, and coffee costs ease.

Dangers: Leverage from a large acquisition. Coffee commodity costs and single-serve declines. Integration and split execution. Aluminum tariffs. Consumer weakness in beverages.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. U.S. Refreshment Beverages organic growth, Coffee segment volume and margin, deal and split timelines, net leverage.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%; deal headlines can be larger. The two-month setup is a split milestone. If stuck, a 3% yield and a growing soda franchise — comfortable, with coffee as the drag.

STZ Constellation Brands

Sector: Beer / Spirits · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~13x · Yield: ~2.5%

Modelo Especial (the number-one beer in America by dollar sales since 2023), Corona, Pacifico, and Victoria — the U.S. rights to Grupo Modelo's brands, acquired in 2013 — plus a wine and spirits business (Kim Crawford, Meiomi, High West, Casa Noble) that it has been shrinking by selling lower-end wine brands. Beer is ~85% of revenue and nearly all of the profit, brewed in Mexico and shipped north. Roughly $10B of revenue. Berkshire Hathaway took a stake in 2024–25.

For a decade Constellation was the best growth story in beverages as Modelo took share. Then 2025 brought a triple hit: Hispanic consumers — its core — pulled back sharply amid immigration enforcement and economic anxiety; aluminum tariffs raised can costs; and wine kept declining. The stock fell to multi-year lows and a 13x multiple.

Shines when: Hispanic consumer spending recovers, Modelo and Pacifico regain volume growth, tariff costs are absorbed, wine divestitures complete, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Hispanic consumer weakness and immigration policy. Tariffs on Mexican beer and aluminum. Beer category volume declines (younger drinkers drinking less). Wine deterioration. The Sands family's class-B control (recently collapsed into one class).

Watch: Reports early Apr/Jul/Oct/Jan (fiscal year ends February). Beer depletion growth, beer operating margin, Hispanic consumer commentary, wine divestiture progress, buybacks.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a depletion-recovery quarter or a tariff-relief headline. If stuck, the best beer franchise in America at a value multiple with Berkshire alongside — comfortable if the consumer recovers.

BF.B Brown-Forman

Sector: Spirits · Market cap: ~$14B · P/E: ~17x · Yield: ~3%

Jack Daniel's — the best-selling American whiskey in the world — plus Woodford Reserve, Old Forester, Herradura and el Jimador tequila, Gin Mare, Diplomático rum, and a ready-to-drink business (Jack & Coke with Coca-Cola). About $4B of revenue, roughly half international. The Brown family has controlled the company since 1870 through supervoting shares; the B shares are the widely traded non-voting class.

Spirits went through a post-pandemic hangover: consumers had stocked up, distributors overordered, and demand normalized down at the same time younger consumers drank less and GLP-1s reduced alcohol consumption at the margin. Brown-Forman's revenue fell for two years, it cut jobs, and the stock fell roughly 60% from its 2022 peak — the deepest drawdown in its history. Tariff retaliation from Europe and Canada on American whiskey was the final insult. Dividend raised for over 40 consecutive years.

Shines when: Distributor inventories normalize and depletions turn positive, tariff retaliation eases, tequila and premium whiskey grow, and the multiple re-rates from decade lows.

Dangers: Structural decline in alcohol consumption among younger consumers. Tariffs on American whiskey. Tequila competition. Family control limits strategic options. A long normalization.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Aug/Dec (fiscal year ends April). Organic net sales, Jack Daniel's depletions, distributor inventory days, gross margin, tariff commentary.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a depletion inflection or a tariff-truce headline. If stuck, a 150-year-old brand at its cheapest valuation in decades with a growing dividend — comfortable, though the recovery may be slow.

TAP Molson Coors Beverage Company

Sector: Beer · Market cap: ~$10B · P/E: ~9x · Yield: ~3.8%

Coors Light, Miller Lite, Coors Banquet, Blue Moon, Molson Canadian, Carling (UK), Staropramen, plus a growing non-alcohol and spirits portfolio (Fever-Tree U.S. distribution rights acquired in 2025, Blue Run bourbon) — ~$11.5B of revenue, mostly North America. The Coors and Molson families control the voting stock.

Molson Coors got a windfall in 2023 when Bud Light's marketing controversy sent drinkers to Coors Light and Miller Lite; the share gains were larger than anyone expected and mostly stuck, but by 2025 the beer category's volume declines reasserted themselves and the stock fell back to a single-digit multiple. A new CEO took over in late 2025. It is the cheapest beer stock in the world, for a reason: volumes shrink every year, and the offset is pricing, cost cuts, and buybacks.

Shines when: Share gains hold, pricing outruns volume decline, Fever-Tree and beyond-beer grow, cost savings hit, and buybacks shrink the float.

Dangers: Structural beer volume decline. Aluminum tariffs. Loss of the Bud Light windfall share. Weak international. A dual-class structure that discourages activism.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Brand volumes (Coors Light, Miller Lite), net sales per hectoliter, underlying EBITDA, U.S. share data (Circana, Nielsen), buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a share-data beat or a rotation into cheap defensives. If stuck, a 9x multiple and a near-4% yield — comfortable as long as you accept that the business shrinks slowly.

MNST Monster Beverage

Sector: Energy Drinks · Market cap: ~$60B · P/E: ~35x · Yield: none

Monster Energy, Reign, Bang (acquired out of bankruptcy in 2023), and a portfolio of energy and performance drinks — ~$8B of revenue, distributed through Coca-Cola's bottling system worldwide since Coke took a 20% stake in 2015. Energy drinks are one of the few beverage categories that still grow, and Monster shares the U.S. market with Red Bull and a rising Celsius. It also owns a small alcohol business (craft beer, hard seltzer) that has lost money.

Co-founders Rodney Sacks and Hilton Schlosberg ran it for over 30 years, turning a natural-soda company into one of the best-performing stocks of the century. No dividend; buybacks. The multiple has always been high because the category grows, margins are exceptional (gross margins in the mid-50s), and international runway remains.

Shines when: Energy-drink category growth stays high, international (EMEA, Asia) grows double digits, pricing sticks, aluminum costs ease, and the alcohol drag shrinks.

Dangers: Celsius and Red Bull taking share. Convenience-store traffic weakness (its main channel). Aluminum tariffs. A premium multiple. Founder transition. Regulatory scrutiny of caffeine.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Net sales growth, gross margin, U.S. share data (Nielsen), international growth, alcohol segment losses.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a share-stabilization quarter after a Celsius scare. If stuck, a category leader with no debt — comfortable, though the multiple leaves little margin.

Agriculture & protein

ADM Archer-Daniels-Midland

Sector: Agricultural Processing · Market cap: ~$28B · P/E: ~14x · Yield: ~3.5%

One of the "ABCD" grain giants — ~$85B of revenue buying, storing, transporting, and processing corn, soybeans, and wheat into meal, oil, ethanol, sweeteners, flour, and ingredients. Ag Services and Oilseeds (the largest — merchandising and crushing), Carbohydrate Solutions (corn milling: ethanol, sweeteners, starches), and Nutrition (flavors, specialty ingredients, animal nutrition — the segment that was supposed to be the growth story and stumbled). Founded in 1902; a dividend raised for over 50 consecutive years.

ADM's earnings track crush margins and ethanol spreads, which peaked in 2022 and fell back. Then in 2024 an accounting investigation into intersegment sales in Nutrition forced restatements and cost the CFO his job; the stock fell to multi-year lows. It trades on the recovery of crush margins and the credibility of a new finance team, with a policy tailwind from biofuel blending mandates and the 45Z clean-fuel credit that favor domestic soybean oil.

Shines when: Crush margins recover, biofuel policy (renewable diesel mandates, 45Z credits) supports soybean-oil demand, Nutrition margins improve, and cost cuts hit.

Dangers: Commodity-margin cyclicality. Trade-war disruptions to soybean exports (China). Accounting-credibility overhang. Weather and crop sizes. Nutrition underperformance.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Crush margins, ethanol margins, Nutrition operating profit, biofuel policy headlines, USDA crop reports, buybacks.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%; it also moves on policy and soybean prices. The two-month setup is a biofuel-policy catalyst or a crush-margin inflection. If stuck, a 50-year dividend grower at a value multiple — comfortable through a commodity trough.

BG Bunge Global

Sector: Agricultural Processing · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~12x · Yield: ~3.5%

ADM's closest peer and, after completing the merger with Glencore's Viterra in July 2025, a much larger one: combined revenue north of $70B from oilseed crushing (the world's largest soybean processor), grain merchandising and export (Viterra added Canada, Australia, and Argentina origination), refined and specialty oils, and milling. Relocated its domicile to Switzerland and its headquarters to St. Louis; founded in Amsterdam in 1818.

Same earnings drivers as ADM — crush margins, trade flows, biofuel policy — with more South American exposure and now a global grain-handling network that rivals Cargill's. The Viterra integration and debt paydown are the multi-year story. Dividend raised annually; buybacks.

Shines when: Viterra synergies land, crush margins recover, South American crops are large, biofuel demand supports oil prices, and leverage falls.

Dangers: Integration of a transformational merger. Commodity-margin cyclicality. Brazil and Argentina currency and policy. Trade-war disruption. Thin margins on huge revenue.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Agribusiness segment EBIT, crush margins by region, Viterra synergy progress, net leverage, full-year adjusted EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a synergy update or a crush-margin turn. If stuck, a 200-year-old commodity handler at a low multiple with a 3.5% yield — comfortable, with integration as the watch item.

TSN Tyson Foods

Sector: Protein / Packaged Meat · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: ~3.5%

The largest meat processor in America — ~$54B of revenue from Chicken (the profit engine, once it fixed its 2023 operational problems), Beef (the largest segment by revenue and, in the current cattle cycle, a loss-maker as tight cattle supplies squeeze packer margins), Pork, Prepared Foods (Jimmy Dean, Hillshire Farm, Ball Park), and International. The Tyson family controls the voting stock.

Tyson's earnings are the sum of three commodity cycles that rarely align: chicken is strong when feed costs are low and supply is disciplined; beef is weak when the U.S. cattle herd is at multi-decade lows (as now, with recovery years away); pork sits in between. Prepared Foods is the stabilizer. Dividend raised for over a decade; the yield near 3.5% reflects the beef trough.

Shines when: Chicken margins stay strong, the cattle cycle turns (herd rebuilding), Prepared Foods grows, feed costs fall, and beef losses narrow.

Dangers: The cattle cycle — beef could lose money for several more years. Avian flu. Feed-cost swings. Labor and plant-safety scrutiny. Export disruptions (China). Family control.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends September). Segment operating income (Chicken vs. Beef), USDA cattle inventory, corn and soybean-meal prices, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a chicken-margin beat or an early cattle-cycle signal. If stuck, a 3.5% yield from the largest protein company in the country — comfortable, with beef as the long wait.

Healthcare

Drug distributors, hospital operators, lab-instrument makers, diagnostics, orthopedics, animal health, and the pharmacy-insurance conglomerate. The Atlas has the drugmakers and the marquee devices (LLY, JNJ, MRK, ABBV, PFE, UNH, ISRG, TMO, DHR, ABT, MDT, SYK, BSX); this is the plumbing of the system — lower margins, lower multiples, and in several cases far more predictable earnings.
