Distribution, pharmacy & generics

CVS CVS Health

Sector: Pharmacy / Insurance / PBM · Market cap: ~$95B · P/E: ~12x · Yield: ~3.5%

The largest healthcare company in America by revenue — ~$390B — assembled from three businesses that were supposed to reinforce each other. Health Care Benefits is Aetna, a top-five health insurer with a large Medicare Advantage book. Health Services is Caremark, one of the three pharmacy benefit managers that negotiate drug prices for most insured Americans, plus Oak Street Health (primary-care clinics for seniors) and Signify (in-home health assessments). Pharmacy and Consumer Wellness is the ~9,000 retail stores, which fill a quarter of U.S. prescriptions and are being closed by the hundreds as front-of-store retail declines.

2024 was a disaster: Aetna badly mispriced Medicare Advantage, costs spiked, guidance was cut four times, and the CEO was replaced by David Joyner, the former Caremark head. 2025 was the recovery — Aetna repriced, exited unprofitable plans, and margins rebounded, and the stock roughly doubled from its lows. The overhang that remains is PBM reform: both parties want to change how Caremark makes money, and Congress has come close to acting several times.

Shines when: Aetna's Medicare Advantage margins normalize, PBM reform is milder than feared, store closures lift pharmacy profitability, and the dividend is maintained through the reset.

Dangers: PBM legislation and FTC action. Medicare Advantage rate and utilization surprises. Retail pharmacy decline (Walgreens went private for a reason). Leverage from the Aetna deal. Reimbursement pressure across every segment.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Aetna medical benefit ratio, Medicare Advantage star ratings and bids, Caremark client retention, pharmacy same-store scripts, PBM legislation headlines, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15% — huge for a company this size. The two-month setup is a Medicare Advantage rate announcement (April) or a post-guidance-raise drift. If stuck, a 3.5% yield and a business too large to fail, but the 2024 collapse shows how fast "boring" can turn.

MCK McKesson

Sector: Drug Distribution · Market cap: ~$95B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~0.4%

The largest pharmaceutical distributor in the U.S. — ~$360B of revenue delivering a third of the country's medicines from manufacturers to pharmacies, hospitals, and clinics, on operating margins around 1% that are somehow among the most reliable in healthcare. U.S. Pharmaceutical is the core, and it has grown faster than expected as GLP-1 drugs (high-priced, high-volume) flow through its warehouses. Prescription Technology Solutions (CoverMyMeds — prior-authorization and patient-access software) is the higher-margin growth piece. Medical-Surgical Solutions and International are smaller; Canada and Europe have been partly divested. McKesson has also built an oncology platform (a stake in Florida Cancer Specialists' management company, Core Ventures) that gives it exposure to specialty drug growth.

Founded in 1833, it survived the opioid settlements ($7B-plus over 18 years), Walgreens's retreat, and the Amazon-pharmacy scare, and it compounds through buybacks that retire 3–5% of shares a year. The dividend is nominal; capital return is almost entirely repurchases.

Shines when: Prescription volumes grow (GLP-1s, specialty, biosimilars), generics pricing stays rational, CoverMyMeds and oncology grow double digits, and buybacks compound EPS.

Dangers: Customer concentration (CVS is a large customer). Drug-pricing reform reducing the value of distributed product. Opioid litigation tail. A shift in GLP-1 distribution economics. Cyber risk on critical infrastructure (Change Healthcare's 2024 outage hit the whole sector).

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). U.S. Pharmaceutical segment operating profit, Prescription Technology growth, full-year EPS guidance revisions, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a guidance raise (they come often) or a sector-wide drug-pricing scare that has nothing to do with distribution economics. If stuck, one of the most predictable earnings streams in the market — comfortable.

COR Cencora

Sector: Drug Distribution · Market cap: ~$60B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~0.7%

The former AmerisourceBergen, renamed in 2023 — ~$310B of revenue as the number-two U.S. drug distributor and the leader in specialty distribution (oncology, rare disease, plasma) through its Alliance Healthcare (Europe), World Courier (cold-chain logistics), and MWI (animal health) businesses. It acquired Retina Consultants of America in 2025, extending its physician-practice management into retinal care, the highest-volume specialty-drug setting. Walgreens was its largest shareholder for a decade and sold down before going private.

Same economics as McKesson: thin margins, reliable volume growth, specialty mix improving profitability, and heavy buybacks. It trades at a slight discount to McKesson with a slightly higher dividend.

Shines when: Specialty and GLP-1 volumes grow, U.S. Healthcare Solutions margins expand, Retina Consultants integration adds physician-services growth, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Customer concentration (Walgreens, Express Scripts). Drug-pricing reform. Opioid litigation tail. European business softness. The same cyber and infrastructure risks as the sector.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends September). U.S. Healthcare Solutions operating income, specialty growth, full-year EPS guidance, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. Same setups as McKesson; the two are near-interchangeable and diverge on specialty mix. If stuck, comfortable.

CAH Cardinal Health

Sector: Drug Distribution / Medical Products · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~1.3%

The third of the big-three distributors — ~$225B of revenue — with a twist: alongside Pharmaceutical and Specialty Solutions (drug distribution, plus nuclear pharmacy and a growing specialty-practice business through acquisitions of ION oncology and GI Alliance), it owns Global Medical Products and Distribution, a manufacturer and distributor of gloves, syringes, surgical kits, and other medical-surgical supplies to hospitals. The medical business was a multi-year drag (cost inflation, lost volume) and is being turned around.

Cardinal lost its largest pharma customer (Optum Rx) in 2024 and absorbed it better than expected; the stock has re-rated as the medical turnaround progressed and specialty acquisitions grew. Dividend raised for over 35 consecutive years; buybacks. Ohio-based, with an activist (Elliott) on the board since 2022.

Shines when: Pharma segment profit grows on specialty mix, the medical segment's margin recovery continues, acquisitions (ION, GI Alliance, Solaris) add growth, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Customer losses (the Optum exit showed the risk). Medical products cost inflation and tariffs on imported supplies. Integration of specialty acquisitions. Drug-pricing reform. Opioid litigation tail.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). Pharma segment operating profit growth, GMPD segment profit, full-year EPS guidance, acquisition contribution.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a guidance raise as the medical turnaround lands. If stuck, a 35-year dividend grower — comfortable.

VTRS Viatris

Sector: Generic & Off-Patent Pharma · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~4x adjusted · Yield: ~5%

The 2020 merger of Mylan with Pfizer's Upjohn (Lipitor, Viagra, Lyrica, Norvasc off-patent brands) — ~$14B of revenue from generics, biosimilars, and mature brands sold in over 165 countries. It is what happens when two low-growth drug businesses combine: enormous cash flow, a shrinking revenue base as generics deflate and brands erode, and a stock market that values it at 4x adjusted earnings and a 5% yield.

The company has been divesting (biosimilars to Biocon, OTC and API businesses) to pay down debt and buying back stock. A 2024 FDA import alert on its Indore, India plant disrupted supply and cut 2025 guidance. Management has pointed to a pipeline of complex generics and a few novel products, but the equity story is capital return on a declining base.

Shines when: The Indore plant issues resolve, debt falls below targets, buybacks accelerate, new product launches offset erosion, and the market re-rates a 4x multiple even modestly.

Dangers: Structural revenue decline. Manufacturing and FDA compliance risk. Generic price deflation. Leverage. Emerging-market currency. A value trap by construction.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Adjusted EBITDA, free cash flow, net leverage, new product revenue, Indore remediation progress.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a manufacturing-resolution headline at a distressed multiple. If stuck, the yield is well covered by cash flow — but the stock has drifted down for years, and a stuck position here is a slow bleed offset by dividends.

Medical devices & supplies

BDX Becton, Dickinson and Company

Sector: Medical Technology · Market cap: ~$50B · P/E: ~13x · Yield: ~2.2%

Syringes, needles, IV catheters, infusion pumps (Alaris — returned to market in 2023 after a long recall), medication-management systems (Pyxis), surgical products, and urology and interventional devices — the consumables hospitals use billions of times a year, ~$21B of revenue. In 2025 BD agreed to separate its Biosciences and Diagnostic Solutions businesses (flow cytometry, microbiology, molecular diagnostics) and merge them with Waters in a Reverse Morris Trust, leaving a "New BD" of roughly $18B focused on medtech with higher growth and margins.

BD has raised its dividend for over 50 consecutive years and trades at one of the lowest multiples in large-cap medtech because growth has been mid-single digits, the Alaris recall dragged for years, and China volume-based procurement hurt. The Waters separation is the catalyst to re-rate what remains.

Shines when: The Waters transaction closes and New BD's growth and margin profile is re-rated, Alaris pump replacements ramp, the BD Excellence cost program delivers, and China stabilizes.

Dangers: Execution on the separation. Hospital capital-spending softness. China procurement and tariffs. Litigation (hernia mesh). Low growth in core consumables.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends September). Organic growth by segment, adjusted operating margin, Waters deal milestones, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a separation milestone or a re-rating quarter. If stuck, a dividend aristocrat at a value multiple — comfortable.

EW Edwards Lifesciences

Sector: Structural Heart Devices · Market cap: ~$45B · P/E: ~28x · Yield: none

The company that pioneered transcatheter aortic valve replacement — replacing a heart valve through a catheter instead of open-heart surgery — and still leads it with the Sapien platform, ~$6B of revenue. TAVR is ~65% of sales and has slowed as the procedure matures; the growth story is Transcatheter Mitral and Tricuspid Therapies (Evoque, the first approved transcatheter tricuspid valve; Pascal), which could be as large as TAVR over a decade. Surgical valves round it out. Edwards sold its Critical Care monitoring business to BD in 2024 to become a pure structural-heart company.

No dividend; buybacks. A 2024 guidance cut on TAVR growth sent the stock down 30% in a day; it has since recovered on TMTT momentum and a large trial (EARLY TAVR) showing benefit in asymptomatic patients, which expands the market.

Shines when: TAVR growth reaccelerates on asymptomatic-patient indication, TMTT scales toward a billion dollars, JenaValve (aortic regurgitation) integrates, and margins expand.

Dangers: TAVR competition from Medtronic, Abbott, and Boston Scientific. Hospital capacity constraints slowing procedures. Premium multiple. FTC scrutiny of acquisitions.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. TAVR underlying growth, TMTT revenue, gross margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is a TMTT-driven beat. If stuck, a pure-play leader with no debt — comfortable, though the multiple can compress.

ZBH Zimmer Biomet

Sector: Orthopedics · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~12x · Yield: ~1%

The largest pure-play orthopedics company — knees, hips, sports medicine, extremities, trauma, and the ROSA surgical robot — ~$8B of revenue. Knees and hips are mature, cash-generative, and slow-growing; the company has been buying growth in faster segments (Paragon 28 for foot and ankle in 2025, Monogram for robotics) and has spun off its spine and dental businesses (ZimVie, 2022) to focus.

Zimmer is the cheapest large medtech stock — around 12x earnings — because it has underperformed Stryker for a decade on growth and execution, and the market has stopped giving it credit. The bull case is a re-rating on portfolio moves and cost control; the bear case is that knees and hips are a commodity duopoly with Stryker winning. Small dividend; buybacks.

Shines when: Procedure volumes stay elevated (aging demographics, GLP-1s enabling surgery for obese patients), new products (Persona knee, Oxford) gain share, acquisitions add growth, and margins expand.

Dangers: Stryker's share gains and robotic lead. Pricing pressure in hips and knees. Hospital capacity and staffing. Integration of acquisitions. A history of self-inflicted problems (quality, supply).

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Organic growth by segment (U.S. knees and hips), adjusted operating margin, ROSA placements, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a clean quarter at a discount multiple. If stuck, a duopoly member at a value price — comfortable, if unexciting.

RMD ResMed

Sector: Sleep & Respiratory Devices · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~0.9%

The leader in CPAP devices and masks for sleep apnea — ~$5.2B of revenue, roughly 80% devices and masks (masks are the recurring, high-margin consumable) and 20% software (Brightree, MatrixCare — out-of-hospital care management). Philips's 2021 recall of its sleep devices handed ResMed years of share gains. Australian-founded, San Diego-based, with a razor-and-blade model that grows as sleep apnea diagnosis rises.

The 2023 GLP-1 scare — the theory that weight-loss drugs would cure sleep apnea and end CPAP demand — cut the stock by a third; the data since then showed the opposite (diagnosed patients on GLP-1s were more likely to stay on therapy), and the stock recovered to new highs. Dividend raised annually; buybacks. Gross margins expanded to the low 60s on cost and mix.

Shines when: Device and mask growth stays high single digits, gross margin expands, software grows, and the GLP-1 narrative continues to prove neutral-to-positive.

Dangers: Philips returning to the U.S. market. GLP-1 long-term effects on the patient pool. Reimbursement changes. Tariffs on Singapore and Australia manufacturing. A premium multiple.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends June). Device and mask revenue growth, gross margin, software revenue, full-year commentary.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a post-GLP-1-headline dip. If stuck, a razor-and-blade leader with a clean balance sheet — comfortable.

COO The Cooper Companies

Sector: Contact Lenses / Women's Health · Market cap: ~$14B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~0.1%

CooperVision is the number-two contact-lens maker (after Johnson & Johnson's Acuvue) — daily silicone-hydrogel lenses (MyDay, clariti), toric and multifocal specialty lenses, and MiSight, the first FDA-approved lens to slow myopia progression in children. CooperSurgical is a women's-health business: fertility products (IVF media, genetic testing, donor services) and surgical devices. About $4B of revenue, roughly 65% vision and 35% surgical.

Contact lenses are a four-company oligopoly with steady mid-single-digit growth and a shift to premium daily lenses; Cooper has taken share for a decade. The stock fell in 2025 as fertility softened (economic pressure on IVF cycles) and vision growth slowed from supply constraints, pushing it to a decade-low multiple. Token dividend; the company has said it will start buybacks.

Shines when: MyDay capacity expands and share gains resume, MiSight adoption grows, fertility recovers, and margins expand on mix.

Dangers: Fertility-cycle weakness. Lens supply constraints. Competition from J&J, Alcon, and Bausch. Tariffs on lenses made in Puerto Rico, the UK, and Costa Rica. Currency.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Aug/Dec (fiscal year ends October). CooperVision organic growth, CooperSurgical fertility growth, adjusted operating margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a de-rated oligopoly member reporting clean numbers. If stuck, a durable franchise at a historically low multiple — comfortable.

STE Steris

Sector: Sterilization / Infection Prevention · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~0.9%

Sterilizers, washers, surgical tables and lights, sterile-processing consumables, and — the unusual piece — a contract sterilization services network (Applied Sterilization Technologies) that sterilizes medical devices and pharmaceuticals for other manufacturers using gamma, ethylene oxide, and electron-beam facilities around the world, a service with no practical substitute. About $5.6B of revenue across Healthcare (~70%), AST (~20%), and Life Sciences. Irish-domiciled; sold its dental business in 2024.

Steris compounds on hospital procedure volumes and the outsourcing of sterilization, with margins in the mid-20s and a dividend raised for nearly 20 consecutive years. Ethylene-oxide regulation is the recurring worry — the EPA tightened rules and litigation followed — but the industry has no alternative for many devices.

Shines when: Procedure volumes grow, hospital capital spending recovers, AST volumes rise with device production, and pricing holds.

Dangers: Ethylene-oxide regulation and litigation. Hospital capex softness. A premium multiple. Currency.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Organic growth by segment, Healthcare capital equipment orders and backlog, AST growth, adjusted EBIT margin.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a capital-equipment backlog conversion quarter. If stuck, a near-monopoly service business — comfortable.

GEHC GE HealthCare Technologies

Sector: Medical Imaging · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~17x · Yield: ~0.2%

Spun out of General Electric in January 2023 — ~$20B of revenue from imaging (MRI, CT, X-ray, and molecular imaging; the largest segment), Advanced Visualization Solutions (ultrasound, surgical visualization), Patient Care Solutions (monitors, anesthesia, maternal-infant care), and Pharmaceutical Diagnostics (contrast agents and radiopharmaceuticals, including Flyrcado, a new PET agent for cardiac imaging with a large potential market). Roughly half of revenue is recurring service and consumables.

GE HealthCare is a three-way oligopoly with Siemens Healthineers and Philips; it grows with hospital capital budgets and imaging procedure volumes, and has been expanding margins as a standalone company. Tariffs on China-sourced components and China's own procurement policies hit 2025 guidance hard, which is why it trades at a discount to its peers. Small dividend; modest buybacks.

Shines when: Hospital imaging capex grows, orders and backlog build, tariff mitigation lands, China stabilizes, and Flyrcado and other PDx products ramp.

Dangers: Tariffs and China (volume-based procurement, anti-corruption campaigns). Hospital capex cyclicality. Competition from Siemens and Philips. Leverage from the spin.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic orders and revenue growth by segment, adjusted EBIT margin, tariff impact updates, China commentary, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a tariff-relief headline or an order-growth quarter. If stuck, an oligopoly at a discount multiple — comfortable.

SOLV Solventum

Sector: Medical Supplies / Health IT · Market cap: ~$13B · P/E: ~13x · Yield: none

3M's healthcare business, spun off in April 2024 — ~$8B of revenue from MedSurg (wound care including the Tegaderm and Prevena brands, sterilization indicators, surgical supplies), Dental Solutions (Filtek composites, Clarity aligners, Clinpro), Health Information Systems (clinical documentation and coding software used by most U.S. hospitals — the 3M 360 Encompass business), and Purification and Filtration, which it agreed to sell to Thermo Fisher for ~$4.1B in 2025 to pay down debt.

Solventum came out of 3M with too much debt and a portfolio that had been under-invested for years; the stock fell sharply after the spin, then attracted activist investor Trian, which pushed for the filtration sale and a focus on wound care and health IT. It trades at a low-teens multiple with no dividend as it delevers.

Shines when: The filtration sale closes and leverage falls, MedSurg organic growth improves, Health Information Systems grows on AI-driven coding products, and margins expand on cost separation from 3M.

Dangers: Execution on a complex separation. Low growth in dental. Health IT competition (Epic, Oracle Health). Leverage. Tariffs on imported supplies.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Organic growth by segment, adjusted operating margin, net leverage, filtration sale milestones, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a deleveraging or activist catalyst. If stuck, a cheap post-spin turnaround with real franchises — comfortable if patient.

BAX Baxter International

Sector: Hospital Products · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: ~0.5%

IV solutions and premixed drugs (Baxter makes most of the IV bags in American hospitals), infusion pumps (Novum IQ), nutrition, surgical products (Advanced Surgery), and the Hillrom hospital-bed and patient-monitoring business acquired in 2021 — ~$11B of revenue after selling its kidney-care business (Vantive) to Carlyle for ~$3.8B in January 2025 and its biopharma-solutions business in 2023.

Baxter is a cautionary tale: the $12B Hillrom deal loaded it with debt at the top of the cycle, Hurricane Helene shut its largest IV plant in 2024, the Novum pump had quality problems, and the dividend was cut. A new CEO took over in 2025. It trades at a single-digit multiple with a token dividend and a balance sheet still under repair — the cheapest name in medtech, with the most operational risk.

Shines when: Pump issues are resolved and placements resume, IV supply normalizes, Hillrom grows, cost cuts lift margins, and leverage falls.

Dangers: Leverage. Product-quality and manufacturing problems (the last two years have been a series of them). Hospital capex weakness. Competition from BD and ICU Medical. Further dividend risk.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic growth by segment, adjusted operating margin, free cash flow, net leverage, pump commentary.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is a turnaround-progress quarter at a distressed multiple. If stuck, the businesses are essential — hospitals cannot function without IV bags — but this is the riskiest name in the section.

Instruments

A Agilent Technologies

Sector: Life Science Instruments · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~22x · Yield: ~0.8%

The original Hewlett-Packard test-and-measurement company, spun out in 1999 (Keysight was later spun out of Agilent) — ~$6.8B of revenue from liquid chromatography and mass spectrometry instruments, consumables (columns, sample prep), pathology diagnostics (companion diagnostics for cancer drugs), genomics, and CrossLab, a services business that maintains instruments from any vendor and is the recurring-revenue engine. Customers are pharma, biotech, chemical, food, and environmental labs.

Instruments are cyclical with pharma and academic capital budgets, which were weak in 2023–25 (biotech funding, China stimulus delays, NIH cuts). Agilent's consumables and services (~60% of revenue) cushion the swings. Dividend raised annually; buybacks. It bought BioVectra (contract manufacturing) in 2024.

Shines when: Pharma and biotech capital spending recovers, China instrument demand returns, CrossLab grows high single digits, and margins expand.

Dangers: Capex cyclicality. China exposure (a large market) and tariffs. NIH and academic funding cuts. Competition from Thermo Fisher, Waters, and Danaher. Currency.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends October). Core organic growth by segment, instrument orders, China growth, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a capex-cycle inflection — instrument orders lead. If stuck, a consumables-and-services cushion makes it a comfortable hold through the trough.

WAT Waters Corporation

Sector: Analytical Instruments · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~28x · Yield: none

The purest play on liquid chromatography — ~$3B of revenue from LC and mass spectrometry instruments, columns and consumables, and TA Instruments (thermal analysis), sold mainly to pharma quality-control labs where its systems are written into regulatory filings and rarely replaced. Recurring revenue (consumables and service) is over half. Waters has some of the highest margins in instruments (low-30s operating) and buys back stock rather than paying a dividend.

The 2025 deal with Becton Dickinson changes its scale: Waters will combine with BD's Biosciences and Diagnostic Solutions businesses (flow cytometry, microbiology, molecular diagnostics) in a ~$17.5B Reverse Morris Trust, roughly doubling revenue to ~$6.5B and adding diagnostics and bioprocessing exposure. The market's initial reaction was skeptical — a focused company becoming a broader one — and the stock fell double digits on the announcement.

Shines when: Pharma instrument replacement cycles turn, the BD combination delivers synergies and re-rates, China recovers, and recurring revenue grows.

Dangers: Integration of a transformational deal. Instrument capex cyclicality. China and tariffs. Competition from Agilent, Thermo Fisher, and Shimadzu. Leverage from the transaction.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Organic growth (instruments vs. recurring), pharma end-market growth, BD deal milestones and synergy targets, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a deal-close re-rating or a replacement-cycle quarter. If stuck, a high-margin franchise mid-transformation — comfortable, with integration as the risk.

MTD Mettler-Toledo International

Sector: Precision Instruments · Market cap: ~$28B · P/E: ~28x · Yield: none

Laboratory balances (it invented the modern analytical balance), pipettes, titrators, and lab software; industrial scales and weighing systems for factories and logistics; product-inspection equipment (metal detection, X-ray) for food and pharma lines; and retail scales — ~$3.9B of revenue, about 55% lab, 40% industrial, 5% retail, sold globally through a direct sales force. Nobody thinks about who makes the scale, which is why Mettler owns the category.

The financial model is one of the most consistent in industrials: low-single-digit volume growth, steady pricing, margin expansion every year, and buybacks that have retired more than half the shares since 2000 — no dividend, no acquisitions of note, just repurchases. China (over 15% of sales) and pharma capex drive the cyclical swings.

Shines when: Lab and pharma capital spending recovers, China demand stabilizes, pricing holds, and margin expansion continues through cost programs.

Dangers: China exposure and tariffs. Capex cyclicality across lab and industrial. A premium multiple. Currency (Swiss operations, global sales).

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Local-currency sales growth by segment and region, adjusted operating margin, full-year EPS guidance, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a China-stabilization quarter. If stuck, a buyback compounder with a category monopoly — comfortable.

Labs, trials & data

IQV IQVIA Holdings

Sector: Clinical Research / Health Data · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: none

The 2016 merger of Quintiles (the largest contract research organization) and IMS Health (the dominant pharmaceutical data company) — ~$16B of revenue running clinical trials for drugmakers (Research and Development Solutions, ~55%), selling prescription and patient data and analytics (Technology and Analytics Solutions, ~40%), and contract sales and medical solutions. The combination is the moat: its data tells it which sites have the right patients, which makes its trials faster.

The CRO cycle depends on biotech funding and large-pharma R&D budgets, both of which softened in 2023–25 (higher rates, pharma cost cuts after patent-cliff planning, NIH turmoil); backlog stayed large but bookings slowed. The stock de-rated to a mid-teens multiple. No dividend; buybacks.

Shines when: Biotech funding recovers, large-pharma outsourcing grows, book-to-bill exceeds 1.2, data and analytics grow with AI-driven products, and margins expand.

Dangers: Biotech funding cycles. Pharma R&D cuts. Trial cancellations. Competition from ICON, Fortrea, Medpace. Leverage from the merger. Data-privacy regulation.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Jul/Nov. R&DS book-to-bill and backlog, TAS organic growth, adjusted EBITDA margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a book-to-bill inflection or a biotech-funding rebound. If stuck, a data-and-trials monopoly at a value multiple — comfortable.

LH Labcorp Holdings

Sector: Clinical Laboratories · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~16x · Yield: ~1.1%

One of the two national clinical laboratory chains — ~$14B of revenue running blood tests, pathology, and genetic testing for physicians, hospitals, and health plans (Diagnostics Laboratories, ~80%), plus Biopharma Laboratory Services (central-lab testing for clinical trials, ~20%). It spun off its clinical-trials business as Fortrea in 2023 to become a pure lab company, and has grown by buying hospital outreach labs (Ascension, Providence, and others) that hospitals no longer want to run.

Labs are a scale business: more volume through the same instruments means higher margins, and Labcorp and Quest have consolidated the industry for decades. Reimbursement pressure (Medicare's PAMA cuts) is the perennial headwind. Dividend since 2022; buybacks.

Shines when: Test volumes grow (aging population, esoteric and genetic tests), hospital-lab acquisitions add volume, PAMA cuts are delayed or softened, and Biopharma bookings recover.

Dangers: Reimbursement cuts. Hospital in-sourcing. Pricing pressure from health plans. Biopharma cyclicality. Labor costs in a people-intensive business.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Diagnostics organic volume and price/mix, adjusted operating margin, Biopharma book-to-bill, PAMA headlines, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a PAMA-delay headline or an acquisition-driven volume beat. If stuck, a duopoly at a mid-teens multiple — comfortable.

DGX Quest Diagnostics

Sector: Clinical Laboratories · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~1.8%

The other national lab — ~$10.5B of revenue, about 2,000 patient service centers, and a network of laboratories running a large share of America's routine blood tests, plus advanced diagnostics (oncology, genetics, cardiometabolic), consumer-initiated testing (order your own labs), and health-plan and employer services. It bought LifeLabs, Canada's largest lab, in 2024 for ~$1B, and has acquired hospital outreach labs and specialty labs (Haystack Oncology for cancer detection).

Same industry economics as Labcorp, with a slightly higher dividend and slightly less biopharma exposure. Dividend raised annually; buybacks. The stock has been a steady compounder as volumes grow with an aging population and the company takes share from smaller labs.

Shines when: Volumes grow, advanced diagnostics and consumer testing grow faster than routine, LifeLabs and hospital deals add revenue, and reimbursement is stable.

Dangers: The same reimbursement, in-sourcing, and labor risks as Labcorp. Health-plan network exclusions. Integration of acquisitions.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Base-business revenue growth, requisition volume, revenue per requisition, adjusted operating margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. Same setups as Labcorp; pick based on relative valuation. If stuck, comfortable.

Providers

HCA HCA Healthcare

Sector: Hospitals · Market cap: ~$95B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: ~0.7%

The largest hospital operator in America — about 190 hospitals and 2,400 outpatient sites in 20 states, concentrated in Texas, Florida, Tennessee, and other growth markets — ~$75B of revenue. HCA runs hospitals like an industrial company: scale purchasing, shared services, and a relentless focus on volume, mix (more commercial-insurance patients, more high-acuity procedures), and labor cost. It is the only hospital company that has consistently earned its cost of capital, and it has retired more than half its shares through buybacks since its 2011 re-IPO.

The policy backdrop is the risk: hospitals depend on Medicaid, Medicare, and ACA exchange plans, and the 2025 budget law cut Medicaid and let enhanced ACA subsidies expire at the end of 2025, which raises uncompensated care. HCA's markets and commercial mix make it the best-positioned hospital operator to absorb that, which is why it trades at a premium to peers. Small dividend; large buybacks.

Shines when: Admissions and surgeries grow, commercial mix improves, labor costs (contract nursing) stay contained, and policy cuts prove milder than feared.

Dangers: Medicaid and ACA subsidy cuts raising bad debt. Labor inflation. Reimbursement rate pressure from Medicare Advantage plans. Site-neutral payment reform. Leverage.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Same-facility admissions and equivalent admissions, revenue per equivalent admission, adjusted EBITDA margin, bad-debt trend, policy headlines.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%; policy headlines can be larger. The two-month setup is buying a Washington-driven selloff when volumes are intact. If stuck, the best-run hospital company at a mid-teens multiple with heavy buybacks — comfortable.

UHS Universal Health Services

Sector: Hospitals / Behavioral Health · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: ~0.4%

Two businesses in one: about 28 acute-care hospitals (mostly in Nevada, Texas, California, and Florida) and the largest behavioral-health network in the country, roughly 330 psychiatric and addiction-treatment facilities in the U.S. and UK — ~$16B of revenue, split roughly evenly. Founded and still controlled by the Miller family through supervoting shares. Behavioral health has structural demand growth (mental-health coverage mandates, addiction treatment) and less capital intensity than acute hospitals.

UHS trades at the lowest multiple in the sector — around 10x — because of the family control, its exposure to Medicaid (heavy in behavioral), periodic litigation (a large verdict in 2024 over patient care), and a lack of the buyback firepower HCA deploys. It has been a consistent earner regardless. Small dividend; buybacks.

Shines when: Behavioral admissions grow and staffing normalizes, acute-care volumes hold, Medicaid supplemental payments (state-directed payments) continue, and the multiple re-rates toward peers.

Dangers: Medicaid cuts. Litigation. Behavioral-health labor shortages. Family control limiting strategic options. Regulatory scrutiny of behavioral facilities.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Same-facility admissions and revenue by segment, behavioral EBITDA margin, Medicaid supplemental payment updates, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a policy-scare pullback at a single-digit multiple. If stuck, cheap and steady, but the discount to HCA is structural.

Animal health

ZTS Zoetis

Sector: Animal Health · Market cap: ~$65B · P/E: ~22x · Yield: ~1.3%

The largest animal-health company in the world, spun out of Pfizer in 2013 — ~$9.5B of revenue, about two-thirds companion animals (dogs and cats) and one-third livestock. The franchises: Apoquel and Cytopoint for canine dermatitis (billion-dollar brands), Simparica Trio (parasiticide), Librela and Solensia (monoclonal antibodies for osteoarthritis pain in dogs and cats — the first of their kind, launched 2023), plus vaccines, diagnostics, and livestock antibiotics and vaccines.

Zoetis compounded for a decade on the "humanization of pets" theme — owners spend on animals like family — and traded at 35–40x earnings. The stock fell in 2024–25 as Librela faced safety reports and FDA label changes, competitors launched rival dermatology products, vet-clinic visits softened, and livestock weakened. It now trades at its lowest multiple as a public company. Dividend raised annually; buybacks.

Shines when: Librela concerns fade and adoption grows, dermatology franchises hold share against generics and rivals, vet visits recover, and new products (renal, oncology) launch.

Dangers: Competition in dermatology (Elanco's Zenrelia, Merck). Librela safety and label risk. Vet-visit softness in a weak consumer. Livestock cyclicality. Generic erosion on older products.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Companion-animal organic growth, dermatology and parasiticide franchise growth, Librela U.S. sales, operational full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a de-rated quality name reporting clean numbers. If stuck, a category leader with pricing power at a historically cheap multiple — comfortable.

IDXX IDEXX Laboratories

Sector: Veterinary Diagnostics · Market cap: ~$45B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

The dominant supplier of diagnostics to veterinary clinics — ~$4.2B of revenue from in-clinic analyzers (Catalyst, ProCyte, the new inVue cytology system) and the consumables they run, reference-lab services, rapid tests (SNAP), and practice-management software. Roughly 90% of revenue is recurring, and every analyzer placed in a clinic generates years of consumable sales. Competitors (Zoetis, Heska/Mars, Antech) have never dented its share.

IDEXX compounded at 20%-plus for years on rising pet ownership and more diagnostics per visit; the multiple has always been high. 2023–25 brought softer clinic visits (a post-pandemic normalization plus a weaker consumer) and the stock stalled, but the installed base kept growing. No dividend; buybacks.

Shines when: U.S. vet visits recover, analyzer placements grow, new products (inVue, Cancer Dx) launch, and international expands.

Dangers: A 40x multiple. Vet-visit weakness in a soft consumer. Pricing pushback from clinic consolidators. Competition from Zoetis diagnostics. Currency.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Companion Animal Group organic growth, U.S. clinic visit trends, premium analyzer placements, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a visit-recovery quarter. If stuck, a near-monopoly with recurring revenue — but the multiple means sideways is the likely stuck outcome.

Financials

Super-regional banks, property-casualty insurers, life insurers, insurance brokers, asset managers, custodians, wealth platforms, exchanges, and card lenders. The Atlas has the money-center banks, the alternatives giants, the card networks, and the ratings and index companies; this is the layer beneath — where the dividends are higher, the multiples lower, and the rate cycle does most of the work.
