The regulated utility provides the base; the nuclear provides the kicker. Dividend raised annually for over a decade.

Shines when: PJM power prices and capacity auctions clear high, nuclear signs long-term contracts with data centers, New Jersey rate cases approve grid investment, and rates fall.

Dangers: Merchant nuclear exposure to power-price declines. New Jersey political risk on rates (affordability has become a campaign issue). Nuclear operating risk. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. PJM forward prices and capacity auction results, nuclear hedging and contract announcements, rate-case decisions, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%; power-price and data-center headlines can add more. The two-month setup is a nuclear-contract announcement or a PJM auction result. If stuck, a regulated base plus a nuclear option — comfortable.

WEC WEC Energy Group

Sector: Electric & Gas Utility (Wisconsin) · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~3.3%

Wisconsin's largest utility (We Energies, Wisconsin Public Service) plus gas utilities in Illinois (Peoples Gas), Michigan, and Minnesota — ~$10B of revenue and 4.7 million customers. Wisconsin has one of the most constructive regulatory environments in the country (high allowed returns, forward-looking rate cases), and WEC has used it to compound earnings at 6–8% a year for two decades with a dividend raised for over 20 consecutive years.

The growth catalyst arrived in 2024: Microsoft's multibillion-dollar data-center campus in Mount Pleasant (the former Foxconn site) and other large loads pushed WEC's capex plan to ~$28B over five years, its largest ever, for gas plants, renewables, and grid.

Shines when: Wisconsin regulators approve the data-center-driven buildout at existing returns, Microsoft's load ramps, Illinois gas rate cases are resolved constructively, and rates fall.

Dangers: Illinois regulatory risk (Peoples Gas pipeline-replacement disputes). Affordability pushback in Wisconsin. Data-center timing. Rate sensitivity. A premium multiple.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Rate-case outcomes, capex plan and data-center load updates, FFO-to-debt, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%. The two-month setup is a rate rotation or a large-load announcement. If stuck, the best-regulated utility in the Midwest — comfortable.

ETR Entergy

Sector: Electric Utility (Gulf South) · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~2.7%

The utility for Louisiana, Mississippi, Arkansas, and southeast Texas — ~$12B of revenue, 3 million customers — with a large nuclear fleet (Grand Gulf, River Bend, Waterford, Arkansas Nuclear One) and, since 2024, the most dramatic industrial-load story in the sector: Meta's Hyperion data center in northeast Louisiana, a multi-gigawatt campus for which Entergy is building three new gas plants, plus LNG terminals, petrochemical expansions, and other data centers along the Gulf. Industrial sales growth is running at rates no other utility can match.

Entergy split its stock 2-for-1 in 2024 and has raised its capex plan repeatedly; the dividend is growing again after years of flat payouts. Its regulators (Louisiana especially) have been supportive of growth-driven investment.

Shines when: Industrial and data-center load ramps as planned, regulators approve the new plants at fair returns, hurricane seasons are mild, and rates fall.

Dangers: Hurricanes — Gulf Coast storm costs are recurring. Concentration in a few large customers (Meta). Regulatory risk on affordability. Nuclear operating risk. A premium multiple for the growth.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Industrial sales growth, new-plant approvals, capex plan updates, storm-cost recovery, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%. The two-month setup is a large-load announcement or a post-hurricane-season relief rally. If stuck, the fastest-growing regulated utility — comfortable.

DTE DTE Energy

Sector: Electric & Gas Utility (Michigan) · Market cap: ~$28B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~3.2%

Detroit's utility — DTE Electric (2.3 million customers in southeast Michigan, including the Fermi 2 nuclear plant) and DTE Gas (1.3 million customers) — ~$13B of revenue, plus non-regulated businesses (DTE Vantage: industrial energy projects, renewable natural gas) and an energy-trading arm. Like CMS, it is retiring coal, building renewables and gas, and has a capex plan of ~$30B over five years; it has announced multiple data-center supply agreements with hyperscalers for campuses in Michigan.

DTE has raised its dividend annually for over 15 years and targets 6–8% EPS growth. Its regulatory relationship has been slightly more contentious than CMS's, and reliability complaints after storms have been a political issue.

Shines when: Data-center contracts convert to load and rate base, Michigan rate cases approve the buildout, reliability investment reduces storm outages, and rates fall.

Dangers: Michigan regulatory and political risk. Storm costs and reliability scrutiny. Nuclear operating risk. Energy-trading volatility. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Data-center agreements and load forecasts, rate-case outcomes, capex plan, FFO-to-debt, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%. The two-month setup is a data-center announcement or a rate rotation. If stuck, comfortable.

PPL PPL Corporation

Sector: Electric & Gas Utility (Pennsylvania / Kentucky / Rhode Island) · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~3.2%

PPL Electric Utilities in central and eastern Pennsylvania (a transmission-and-distribution utility in the heart of the data-center corridor emerging along the Susquehanna), Louisville Gas & Electric and Kentucky Utilities (vertically integrated with coal and gas generation), and Rhode Island Energy (bought from National Grid in 2022 after PPL sold its UK business). About $8.5B of revenue.

PPL reset itself in 2021–22 — selling the UK utility at a good price, buying Rhode Island, cutting the dividend to a sustainable level — and has since grown it again. Pennsylvania data-center interconnection requests are enormous (tens of gigawatts), and PPL has formed a joint venture with Blackstone to build gas generation for them, an unusual move for a wires utility.

Shines when: Pennsylvania data-center load converts to rate base and the Blackstone generation venture proceeds, Kentucky approves new gas plants, Rhode Island integration completes, and rates fall.

Dangers: Pennsylvania regulatory design (who pays for data-center grid costs). Kentucky coal-retirement politics. Rhode Island regulatory friction. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Data-center interconnection pipeline, generation-venture milestones, rate-case outcomes, capex plan, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%. The two-month setup is a data-center or generation-venture announcement. If stuck, comfortable.

CNP CenterPoint Energy

Sector: Electric & Gas Utility (Houston) · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~2.3%

Houston's electric transmission-and-distribution utility (2.9 million customers in the fastest-growing large metro in the country) plus natural-gas distribution in Texas, Indiana, Ohio, and Minnesota — ~$9B of revenue. CenterPoint has a large capex plan (~$50B over ten years) for Houston's grid resilience and growth, including data centers, LNG, and petrochemical load.

Hurricane Beryl in July 2024 left millions of Houston customers without power for days, and the political backlash — state investigations, threats to its franchise, executive departures — reset the company's priorities toward resilience. It sold its Louisiana and Mississippi gas utilities to fund the plan. Dividend raised annually; growth targets of 8% EPS.

Shines when: Houston load growth continues, resilience capex is approved and recovered, storm seasons are mild, the Beryl backlash fades, and rates fall.

Dangers: Hurricanes. Texas political and regulatory risk after Beryl. Execution on a very large capex plan. Leverage. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Houston load growth, resilience-plan approvals, storm-cost recovery, FFO-to-debt, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%; hurricane headlines can be larger. The two-month setup is a post-hurricane-season rally or a large-load announcement. If stuck, the utility for the fastest-growing big city in America — comfortable outside storm season.

Wires & steady compounders

EXC Exelon

Sector: Electric Transmission & Distribution · Market cap: ~$45B · P/E: ~17x · Yield: ~3.5%

After spinning off its power plants as Constellation in 2022, Exelon became the largest pure transmission-and-distribution utility in the country — no generation, just the wires and poles serving ~10 million customers through ComEd (Chicago), PECO (Philadelphia), BGE (Baltimore — your local utility), Pepco (Washington, D.C.), Delmarva, and Atlantic City Electric. About $23B of revenue.

Wires-only is the lowest-risk utility model: no fuel or commodity exposure, no plant-outage risk, and a rate base that grows with grid investment (reliability, resilience, data-center interconnection). Exelon's capex plan is ~$38B over five years, and it has been pushing regulators to make large load customers pay for their own grid upgrades. Dividend raised annually since the spin.

Shines when: Rate cases in Illinois, Pennsylvania, and Maryland approve grid investment at fair returns, data-center interconnection adds load, and rates fall.

Dangers: Regulatory risk in Illinois (a bribery scandal at ComEd produced a settlement and political backlash) and Maryland. Affordability pushback. Rate sensitivity. Storm costs.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Rate-case outcomes, capex plan and rate-base growth, FFO-to-debt, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%. The two-month setup is a rate rotation or a favorable rate-case order. If stuck, the cleanest utility model at a 3.5% yield — comfortable.

ED Consolidated Edison

Sector: Electric & Gas Utility (New York City) · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~3.3%

The utility for New York City and Westchester — electric, gas, and steam (it runs the largest district steam system in the world, heating Manhattan's buildings) — plus Orange & Rockland in the suburbs. About $15B of revenue. Con Ed sold its clean-energy generation business in 2023 to become a pure regulated wires-and-pipes company serving the densest, wealthiest service territory in the country.

It has raised its dividend for over 50 consecutive years — a dividend aristocrat and one of the longest streaks among utilities. Growth is steady rather than exciting (New York doesn't have data-center load growth), and the regulatory environment is demanding: New York's climate law forces enormous grid investment while the state's political climate resists rate increases.

Shines when: Rate cases approve multi-year plans at fair returns, electrification (heat pumps, EVs) drives load and rate base, rates fall, and the dividend streak continues.

Dangers: New York regulatory and political risk on rates. Gas-system restrictions under climate policy. Storm costs. Slow growth. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Rate-case outcomes (multi-year plans), capex plan, FFO-to-debt, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–4% — among the lowest in the market. Not a two-month mover. As a stuck-bucket holding: a 50-year dividend record from a monopoly on Manhattan — about as safe as equity gets.

AEE Ameren

Sector: Electric & Gas Utility (Missouri / Illinois) · Market cap: ~$27B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~2.9%

St. Louis-based, serving Missouri (electric generation and distribution, including the Callaway nuclear plant) and Illinois (electric and gas distribution, plus a large FERC-regulated transmission business) — ~$8B of revenue, 2.5 million electric and 900,000 gas customers. Ameren has one of the sector's longest records of steady 6–8% earnings growth, and a capex plan (~$26B over five years) for grid, renewables, and new gas generation, with data-center interest in Missouri rising.

Missouri passed constructive legislation in 2025 (allowing recovery of construction work in progress for new gas plants), improving the regulatory picture; Illinois has been more contentious. Dividend raised annually since 2014.

Shines when: Missouri approves new generation at fair returns, transmission investment grows, data-center load materializes, and rates fall.

Dangers: Illinois regulatory risk. Nuclear operating risk (Callaway). Affordability pushback. Rate sensitivity. A premium multiple.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Jul/Oct. Rate-case outcomes in both states, capex plan, FFO-to-debt, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%. Rate-rotation setups only. If stuck, a steady mid-single-digit grower — comfortable.

CMS CMS Energy

Sector: Electric & Gas Utility (Michigan) · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~19x · Yield: ~3%

Consumers Energy — Michigan's largest utility, serving 6.8 million people with electricity and natural gas, ~$8B of revenue — plus NorthStar Clean Energy, a small non-regulated generation business. CMS has delivered 6–8% annual EPS growth for over 20 consecutive years, one of the most consistent records in the sector, through a "self-funding" model of cost cuts offsetting rate increases. Michigan's regulatory environment has been constructive, and the state's clean-energy law mandates a large renewable buildout that becomes rate base.

Data-center load in Michigan (a large campus near Saline announced in 2025) and the retirement of coal plants drive the capex plan (~$20B over five years). Dividend raised for nearly 20 consecutive years.

Shines when: Michigan rate cases are constructive, the renewable buildout proceeds, data-center load arrives, cost programs offset rate pressure, and rates fall.

Dangers: Michigan political risk on rates. Storm costs (reliability has been a public issue). Coal-retirement execution. Rate sensitivity. A premium multiple.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Rate-case decisions, capex plan, reliability metrics, FFO-to-debt, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%. Rate-rotation setups only. If stuck, the most consistent grower in the sector — comfortable.

ES Eversource Energy

Sector: Electric & Gas Utility (New England) · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~14x · Yield: ~4.3%

New England's largest utility — electric and gas transmission and distribution in Connecticut, Massachusetts, and New Hampshire, ~$12B of revenue, 4.4 million customers. Eversource made a costly detour into offshore wind (partnering with Ørsted on three projects) and exited in 2023–24 with ~$2B of impairments, then sold its Aquarion water business in 2025 to repair the balance sheet. It is now a pure regulated wires-and-pipes company again.

The stock trades at the lowest multiple among large utilities because of the offshore-wind losses, a difficult regulatory relationship in Connecticut (the state's regulator has been openly hostile), and leverage. The bull case is that the mess is behind it and the yield is the highest in the group. Dividend raised annually.

Shines when: Connecticut regulation stabilizes, Massachusetts and New Hampshire rate cases are constructive, leverage falls after the Aquarion sale, and the multiple re-rates toward peers.

Dangers: Connecticut regulatory hostility. Leverage. Storm costs. Affordability politics in high-cost New England. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Jul/Nov. Connecticut rate-case and regulatory developments, FFO-to-debt, capex plan, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%. The two-month setup is a regulatory-relief headline at a 4.3% yield. If stuck, the cheapest large utility with the highest yield — comfortable if Connecticut cooperates.

Wildfire-exposed

XEL Xcel Energy

Sector: Electric & Gas Utility (Upper Midwest / Colorado) · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~19x · Yield: ~3%

Serves 3.9 million electric and 2.2 million gas customers across Minnesota, Colorado, Texas, New Mexico, and the Dakotas — ~$14B of revenue — and has been the most aggressive large utility in shifting to wind and solar, with one of the largest capex plans in the sector (~$45B over five years) for renewables, transmission, and gas-plant retirements. It also owns two nuclear plants in Minnesota.

Xcel's problem is wildfire: it settled claims over the 2021 Marshall Fire in Colorado and faced litigation over the 2024 Smokehouse Creek Fire in Texas, the largest in the state's history, which its equipment likely ignited. Wildfire liability is now the central risk for Western utilities, and Xcel's multiple carries a discount for it. Dividend raised for over 20 consecutive years.

Shines when: Wildfire settlements are contained and state liability reforms pass, rate cases approve the renewable and transmission buildout, data-center load lands in Minnesota and Colorado, and rates fall.

Dangers: Wildfire liability. Regulatory pushback on capex and affordability. Renewable-policy changes. Rate sensitivity. Nuclear operating risk.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Wildfire litigation and settlement updates, rate-case decisions, capex plan, FFO-to-debt, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%; wildfire headlines can be larger. The two-month setup is a wildfire-liability resolution or a rate rotation. If stuck, a 20-year dividend grower with wildfire as the tail risk — comfortable.

EIX Edison International

Sector: Electric Utility (Southern California) · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: ~6%

Southern California Edison — electric transmission and distribution for 15 million people across central and Southern California (not Los Angeles proper, which has a municipal utility), ~$18B of revenue, and the most wildfire-exposed utility in the country after PG&E. The January 2025 Eaton Fire in Altadena, which killed 17 people and destroyed thousands of homes, was likely ignited by SCE equipment; the liability is capped by California's Wildfire Fund (created after the PG&E bankruptcy) but the stock fell 40% and trades at a single-digit multiple with a 6% yield.

The bull case is the Wildfire Fund working as designed — the utility contributes, the fund pays, and shareholders are protected if the utility acted prudently — plus California's aggressive electrification and a rate base growing at high single digits. The bear case is that the fund is exhausted or the prudence standard fails. Dividend raised for over 20 years.

Shines when: Eaton Fire liability is resolved within the Wildfire Fund framework, California legislation replenishes the fund, rate cases approve grid-hardening investment, and the multiple re-rates.

Dangers: Wildfire liability beyond the fund. Prudence findings. California regulatory and political risk. Another fire. Leverage.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Eaton Fire investigation and claims, Wildfire Fund legislation, general rate case decisions, FFO-to-debt.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%; wildfire headlines can be ±10–20%. The two-month setup is a fund-legislation catalyst at a 6% yield. If stuck, the yield is high because the risk is real — this is the utility for investors who understand the Wildfire Fund mechanics.

PCG PG&E Corporation

Sector: Electric & Gas Utility (Northern California) · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: ~0.6%

Pacific Gas and Electric — Northern and Central California's utility, 16 million people, ~$25B of revenue — and the company whose equipment caused the 2018 Camp Fire that destroyed Paradise, killed 85 people, and sent it into bankruptcy. It emerged in 2020 with a new management team, a $20B-plus wildfire-mitigation program (undergrounding 10,000 miles of lines, sensors, public-safety shutoffs), and the protection of California's Wildfire Fund. It reinstated a small dividend in 2023.

The turnaround has been real: no catastrophic fires attributed to PG&E since 2021, earnings growing 9–10% a year on the largest capex plan in the sector (~$73B over five years), and data-center load growth in Silicon Valley. The multiple is the lowest in the group because the wildfire risk never fully goes away and the state's political relationship with PG&E is permanently strained.

Shines when: Fire seasons pass without a PG&E-caused catastrophe, undergrounding is approved in rate cases, data-center load grows, the dividend grows toward a normal payout, and the Wildfire Fund is replenished.

Dangers: A catastrophic fire — the tail risk is existential. California regulatory and political risk. Wildfire Fund exhaustion (the Eaton Fire drew on it). Leverage. A tiny dividend for a utility.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Fire-season outcomes, general rate case decisions, capex plan, dividend growth, Wildfire Fund legislation, FFO-to-debt.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%; fire headlines can be far larger. The two-month setup is a post-fire-season relief rally (November onward) or fund-replenishment legislation. If stuck, the cheapest utility in the group with the fastest EPS growth — comfortable only if you can sit through fire season.

Gas & water

ATO Atmos Energy

Sector: Natural Gas Distribution · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~2.3%

The largest pure natural-gas distribution utility in the country — 3.3 million customers across eight states, dominated by Texas (roughly 70% of rate base, including Dallas–Fort Worth), plus a Texas intrastate pipeline business (Atmos Pipeline–Texas). About $4.5B of revenue. Gas distribution has no fuel-margin risk (gas costs pass through to customers) and Atmos's growth comes from replacing aging pipe — a decades-long, regulator-approved capex program — and Texas population growth.

Atmos has raised its dividend for over 40 consecutive years and grows EPS 6–8% annually with almost no drama; Texas regulation allows annual rate adjustments that reduce lag. It is the boring-utility archetype, with a premium multiple to match.

Shines when: Pipe-replacement capex continues at approved returns, Texas customer growth adds volume, gas-policy sentiment improves, and rates fall.

Dangers: Electrification policy reducing long-term gas demand. Pipeline-safety incidents. Rate sensitivity. A premium multiple. Winter Storm Uri-type cost recoveries (already securitized).

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends September). Capex plan, rate-base growth, customer additions, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–4%. Not a mover. As a stuck-bucket holding: a 40-year dividend record with Texas growth — comfortable.

AWK American Water Works

Sector: Water Utility · Market cap: ~$28B · P/E: ~24x · Yield: ~2.3%

The largest regulated water and wastewater utility in the country — 14 million people served across 14 states (Pennsylvania, New Jersey, Missouri, Illinois, and others), ~$5B of revenue, growing by buying small municipal water systems that towns can no longer afford to maintain and by replacing century-old pipes. Water utilities have the most predictable growth in the sector: regulators approve pipe replacement, customers can't switch, and demand doesn't fall in recessions.

In October 2025 American Water agreed to merge with Essential Utilities (Aqua water plus Peoples natural gas in Pennsylvania) in an all-stock deal, creating a ~$60B combined company — the largest water deal ever, expected to close in 2027. Dividend raised annually since its 2008 IPO; the multiple is the highest in utilities because water is the scarcest regulated asset.

Shines when: Rate cases approve pipe-replacement capex, municipal acquisitions continue, the Essential merger closes with synergies, and rates fall.

Dangers: A 24x multiple with heavy rate sensitivity. Merger integration and regulatory approvals across many states. Affordability pushback on water rates. PFAS remediation costs. Adding gas exposure via Essential.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Rate-case outcomes, acquisition pipeline, Essential merger milestones, capex plan, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%. The two-month setup is a rate-cut rotation — water utilities are the most rate-sensitive stocks in the market. If stuck, a monopoly on water — comfortable, at a price.

REITs

Real estate investment trusts pay out most of their income as dividends and are valued on funds from operations (FFO) rather than earnings, so the multiples below are price-to-FFO. The Atlas has the giants (PLD, AMT, EQIX, O, SPG, DLR, CCI, PSA, WELL, AVB, EXR); this is the next tier — net-lease landlords, apartment owners, single-family rentals, and a few specialty names. Like utilities, they move with the ten-year yield more than with their own results.

Net leaseVICIWPCNNNADCResidentialMAACPTEQRESSINVHSpecialtyIRMSBACVTR

Net lease

VICI VICI Properties

Sector: Gaming & Experiential Net Lease · Market cap: ~$35B · P/FFO: ~13x · Yield: ~5.5%

Owns the real estate under the Las Vegas Strip — Caesars Palace, the Venetian, MGM Grand, Mandalay Bay, Bellagio (a stake), and dozens of regional casinos — leased back to the operators (Caesars, MGM, Penn, and others) under triple-net leases running 30-plus years with rent escalators, many of them tied to inflation. Spun out of Caesars' bankruptcy in 2017; now ~$4B of revenue and the largest experiential REIT, expanding into golf, bowling, and destination resorts through sale-leasebacks and development loans.

The leases are the moat: 100% occupancy, tenants who cannot move a casino, and rent coverage that survived the pandemic without a missed payment. Dividend raised every year since IPO. It trades at a discount to net-lease peers because of tenant concentration and "gaming" in the name.

Shines when: Las Vegas visitation holds, inflation escalators kick in, new sale-leasebacks and loans are accretive, and rates fall.

Dangers: Tenant concentration (Caesars and MGM are most of rent). Las Vegas tourism cycles. Rate sensitivity. Growth depends on external acquisitions in a competitive market.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. AFFO per share, acquisition and loan volume, tenant rent coverage, dividend increases (typically Q3).

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%. The two-month setup is a rate-cut rotation into yield. If stuck, a 5.5% yield from leases that outlast most careers — comfortable.

WPC W. P. Carey

Sector: Diversified Net Lease · Market cap: ~$14B · P/FFO: ~13x · Yield: ~5.5%

A net-lease REIT with ~1,600 properties — industrial, warehouse, retail, and self-storage — leased to corporate tenants in the U.S. and Europe under long-term leases with rent escalators (over half tied to CPI, unusual and valuable in inflationary years). In 2023 it spun off and sold its entire office portfolio, cutting the dividend for the first time in its history to reset the payout — a move the market punished then and has since rewarded as the portfolio quality improved.

Carey is Realty Income's smaller, more industrial, more European cousin; the dividend has grown every quarter since the reset. About half of rent comes from investment-grade or equivalent tenants.

Shines when: Acquisition volume grows at accretive spreads, CPI-linked escalators lift same-store rent, European exposure benefits from currency, and rates fall.

Dangers: Tenant credit events (a few large tenants have struggled). European economic weakness and currency. Rate sensitivity. The 2023 dividend cut in institutional memory.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. AFFO per share, investment volume and cap rates, same-store rent growth, tenant watch list, dividend increases.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%. Rate-rotation setups. If stuck, a 5.5% yield from inflation-linked leases — comfortable.

NNN NNN REIT

Sector: Retail Net Lease · Market cap: ~$8B · P/FFO: ~13x · Yield: ~5.5%

Formerly National Retail Properties — ~3,600 single-tenant retail properties (convenience stores, restaurants, auto service, fitness, car washes) leased under triple-net leases averaging 10 years, to tenants like 7-Eleven, Mister Car Wash, LA Fitness, and Dave & Buster's. It focuses on relationship-driven sale-leasebacks with mid-sized regional operators rather than investment-grade chains, which produces higher cap rates and a portfolio the big net-lease REITs don't compete for.

NNN has raised its dividend for over 35 consecutive years — one of only a handful of REITs with a record that long — through a conservative balance sheet, low leverage, and disciplined acquisitions of a few hundred million dollars a year. Occupancy has stayed above 98% for decades.

Shines when: Acquisition spreads are wide (cap rates above cost of capital), tenant credit holds, rent escalators lift revenue, and rates fall.

Dangers: Tenant bankruptcies in restaurants and fitness. Retail secular pressure on marginal tenants. Rate sensitivity. Small-cap liquidity. Slow growth by design.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Core FFO per share, acquisition volume and initial cap rate, occupancy, dividend increases (mid-year).

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–4%. Rate-rotation setups. If stuck, a 35-year dividend record at a 5.5% yield — among the safest REITs to hold.

ADC Agree Realty

Sector: Retail Net Lease · Market cap: ~$8B · P/FFO: ~17x · Yield: ~4%

A net-lease REIT that deliberately does the opposite of NNN: it targets investment-grade, recession-resistant national tenants — Walmart, Tractor Supply, Dollar General, TJX, Home Depot, Lowe's, CVS, Kroger — under long leases, accepting lower cap rates in exchange for tenant quality. About 2,500 properties, roughly two-thirds of rent from investment-grade tenants, plus a ground-lease portfolio (owning the land under stores, the safest real estate there is). Pays a monthly dividend.

Run by Joey Agree, the founder's son, with an unusually disciplined acquisition machine and a conservative balance sheet. It trades at a premium multiple to NNN because of the tenant quality and faster growth.

Shines when: Acquisition volume grows (it targets over a billion dollars a year), investment-grade tenants keep expanding stores, ground leases add value, and rates fall.

Dangers: Rate sensitivity in a premium-multiple name. Tenant concentration in a few large retailers. Cap-rate compression narrowing spreads. Growth dependence on equity issuance.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. AFFO per share, acquisition volume and cap rates, investment-grade percentage, monthly dividend increases.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%. Rate-rotation setups. If stuck, a monthly dividend from the highest-quality tenant roster in net lease — comfortable.

Residential

MAA Mid-America Apartment Communities

Sector: Sun Belt Apartments · Market cap: ~$18B · P/FFO: ~16x · Yield: ~4%

About 100,000 apartment units across the Sun Belt — Atlanta, Dallas, Austin, Nashville, Charlotte, Tampa, Orlando, Phoenix — the markets that received the most population migration in the 2020s and, as a result, the most new apartment construction. Memphis-based, ~$2.2B of revenue, low leverage, and a dividend raised annually for over 15 years.

MAA's problem in 2023–25 was supply: developers delivered record numbers of new units in exactly its markets, pushing rent growth to zero or negative and pressuring occupancy. The cycle is turning — construction starts collapsed in 2024 as financing dried up, so deliveries fall sharply from 2026 — which is the bull case: the same migration, far less new competition.

Shines when: New-supply deliveries fall and rent growth turns positive, Sun Belt job growth continues, occupancy rises, and rates fall.

Dangers: Supply overhang lasting longer than expected. Sun Belt job-growth slowdown. Property taxes and insurance costs. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Same-store revenue and NOI growth, blended lease rate growth (new and renewal), occupancy, supply-delivery forecasts by market.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%. The two-month setup is a lease-rate inflection as supply fades. If stuck, a 4% yield from Sun Belt apartments — comfortable.

CPT Camden Property Trust

Sector: Sun Belt Apartments · Market cap: ~$12B · P/FFO: ~15x · Yield: ~4%

About 58,000 apartment units in Sun Belt markets — Houston, Dallas, Austin, Atlanta, Phoenix, Denver, Southeast Florida, Charlotte, Raleigh, Nashville — with a reputation for the best operations and corporate culture in the sector (it has been on the "best companies to work for" lists for years). Houston-based; ~$1.6B of revenue; low leverage.

Same supply story as MAA: record deliveries in its markets crushed rent growth in 2023–25, and the collapse in new construction starts sets up a recovery as deliveries fall. Camden also develops its own communities, which adds value in a recovery and risk in a downturn. Dividend raised annually.

Shines when: Supply deliveries fall and rent growth turns positive, Sun Belt job growth holds, developments lease up, and rates fall.

Dangers: Supply overhang. Texas and Florida property-tax and insurance inflation. Houston energy-economy exposure. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Same-store revenue and NOI growth, blended lease rates, occupancy, development lease-up, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%. Same setup as MAA — a pair. If stuck, comfortable.

EQR Equity Residential

Sector: Coastal Apartments · Market cap: ~$25B · P/FFO: ~17x · Yield: ~4%

About 80,000 apartment units concentrated in coastal gateway cities — Boston, New York, Washington, Seattle, San Francisco, Southern California — with a growing push into Denver, Atlanta, Dallas, and Austin. Founded by Sam Zell; ~$3B of revenue. Coastal markets have the opposite dynamic from the Sun Belt: almost no new supply (zoning, cost, land) and high-income renters priced out of homeownership, so rent growth has held up while Sun Belt rents stalled.

EQR is the blue-chip apartment REIT: low leverage, an investment-grade balance sheet, and a dividend that tracks cash flow. Its urban West Coast exposure (San Francisco, Seattle) was a drag post-pandemic and has been recovering with the return to office and AI hiring.

Shines when: Coastal rent growth stays positive on limited supply, San Francisco and Seattle recover, expansion-market acquisitions are accretive, and rates fall.

Dangers: Rent-control and tenant-protection politics in New York, California, and Washington. Tech-employment cycles in the West Coast markets. Rate sensitivity. Slow growth.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Same-store revenue growth, blended lease rates by market, occupancy, bad-debt trend, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±2–5%. Rate-rotation setups. If stuck, the highest-quality apartment landlord — comfortable.

ESS Essex Property Trust

Sector: West Coast Apartments · Market cap: ~$18B · P/FFO: ~17x · Yield: ~3.5%

About 62,000 apartment units, all on the West Coast — Northern California (Silicon Valley, San Francisco, Oakland), Southern California (Los Angeles, Orange County, San Diego), and Seattle. The most concentrated bet on West Coast housing scarcity: these markets have the most restrictive zoning in the country, so supply stays low and rents follow tech employment. ~$1.8B of revenue.

Essex has raised its dividend for over 30 consecutive years — a dividend aristocrat, rare among REITs — and runs with modest leverage. The AI hiring boom in the Bay Area has been a tailwind after the post-pandemic exodus; Los Angeles has lagged.

Shines when: Bay Area and Seattle rent growth accelerates on tech hiring, Southern California stabilizes, supply stays constrained, and rates fall.

Dangers: California rent-control and tenant-protection legislation. Tech-employment cycles. Wildfire and earthquake exposure. Concentration risk. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Same-store revenue growth by region, blended lease rates, occupancy, Bay Area job data, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%. The two-month setup is a Bay Area rent-growth acceleration. If stuck, a 30-year dividend record — comfortable.

INVH Invitation Homes

Sector: Single-Family Rental · Market cap: ~$20B · P/FFO: ~16x · Yield: ~3.5%

The largest owner of single-family rental homes in the country — about 85,000 owned homes plus another 20,000-plus managed for third parties, concentrated in the Sun Belt and West (Atlanta, Dallas, Phoenix, Florida, Southern California, Seattle). Assembled by Blackstone after the 2008 housing crash, buying foreclosed homes by the thousand; public since 2017; ~$2.6B of revenue. It now grows through purchasing new homes from builders in bulk and through a third-party management business.

Single-family rental benefits from housing unaffordability — families who would buy but can't at current prices and rates rent instead — and has less new supply than apartments. The political risk is the flip side: institutional homeownership is a target for legislation in several states and in Washington.

Shines when: Home affordability stays stretched (renters stay renters), rent growth holds, builder partnerships add homes at good yields, and rates fall.

Dangers: Legislation restricting institutional single-family ownership. Home-price declines hitting asset values. Property-tax and insurance inflation (Florida, Texas). Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Same-store revenue and NOI growth, blended lease rate growth, occupancy, turnover, legislative headlines.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%. The two-month setup is a rate-cut rotation or a post-legislative-scare recovery. If stuck, a landlord to the priced-out — comfortable.

Specialty

IRM Iron Mountain

Sector: Records Storage / Data Centers · Market cap: ~$30B · P/FFO: ~22x · Yield: ~3%

The company that stores the world's paper — hundreds of millions of boxes of corporate records, medical files, and legal documents in warehouses and a former limestone mine in Pennsylvania — and has used that cash flow to build a data-center business (~1 gigawatt leased or under construction, in the U.S., Europe, and India) and a digital-transformation services arm (scanning, asset lifecycle management, IT-equipment disposition). About $6.5B of revenue; records storage still generates most of the profit.

The narrative shift from "melting ice cube of paper" to "data-center growth story" re-rated the stock several-fold; records storage turned out to be remarkably durable (boxes rarely leave, and storage prices rise every year), and the data-center pipeline rides the AI build-out. Dividend raised annually; leverage is elevated.

Shines when: Data-center leasing and development stay strong, storage pricing rises, asset-lifecycle management grows, and the multiple holds.

Dangers: Leverage. Data-center capex intensity and competition from Equinix, Digital Realty, and private developers. Records-storage decline eventually accelerating. A premium multiple. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. AFFO per share, data-center leasing (megawatts signed), storage organic revenue growth, net leverage, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a data-center leasing beat. If stuck, a durable cash business funding a growth business — comfortable, with leverage as the caveat.

SBAC SBA Communications

Sector: Wireless Towers · Market cap: ~$22B · P/FFO: ~17x · Yield: ~2%

The third of the three U.S. tower REITs (after the Atlas's American Tower and Crown Castle) — about 40,000 towers in the U.S., Central and South America, and (since 2025) Central Europe, leased to wireless carriers under long-term contracts with annual escalators. Towers are among the best business models in real estate: three or four tenants per tower, incremental tenants at near-100% margin, and carriers who cannot leave without rebuilding their network.

SBA is the most leveraged and most U.S.-concentrated of the three, with a small dividend (initiated 2019) and a history of aggressive buybacks. Carrier consolidation and the end of the 5G spending surge slowed leasing growth in 2023–25; the next catalyst is carriers densifying networks for data traffic and fixed wireless.

Shines when: Carrier leasing activity picks up (new spectrum deployments, network densification), international growth holds, buybacks continue, and rates fall.

Dangers: Carrier consolidation and churn (the Sprint–T-Mobile merger removed a tenant; EchoStar's spectrum sale removed another). Leverage. Rate sensitivity. Latin American currency.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. AFFO per share, domestic same-tower leasing growth, churn, net leverage, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%. The two-month setup is a carrier-capex reacceleration or a rate rotation. If stuck, an infrastructure toll road — comfortable.

VTR Ventas

Sector: Senior Housing / Healthcare Real Estate · Market cap: ~$30B · P/FFO: ~20x · Yield: ~2.8%

The second-largest healthcare REIT (after the Atlas's Welltower) — ~1,400 properties, with senior housing (independent and assisted living) as the growth engine and outpatient medical buildings and research/life-science facilities as the stabilizers. Senior housing operated through third-party managers (Ventas keeps the operating upside) has been the best-performing real-estate category of the decade: the oldest baby boomers are entering their 80s, new construction collapsed after the pandemic, and occupancy and rents have risen for several years running.

Ventas cut its dividend in 2020 and has been rebuilding it; it trades at a discount to Welltower because Welltower executed the senior-housing pivot earlier and better. The catch-up trade is the bull case.

Shines when: Senior-housing occupancy and rents keep rising on demographic demand and limited supply, acquisitions at high yields continue, outpatient medical stays stable, and rates fall.

Dangers: Senior-housing labor costs. Operator performance (Ventas depends on managers). Life-science real-estate weakness. A multiple that has already risen. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports early Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Senior-housing operating portfolio same-store NOI growth, occupancy, acquisition volume and yields, normalized FFO guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%. The two-month setup is an occupancy-driven guidance raise. If stuck, the demographic tailwind is the most reliable in real estate — comfortable.

Materials

Industrial gases, paint, packaging, aggregates, steel, and fertilizer. The Atlas has LIN, FCX, NEM, and NUE; this is the rest — a sector where the best businesses (gases, aggregates, paint) compound quietly and the worst (commodity chemicals) destroy capital on a schedule. The section is sorted roughly from most to least boring.

Gases, coatings & specialtyAPDSHWECLPPGDDPackagingIPPKGBALLAVYAggregates & building materialsVMCMLMCRHMetalsSTLDRSFertilizer & commodity chemicalsCFDOWLYB

Gases, coatings & specialty

APD Air Products and Chemicals

Sector: Industrial Gases · Market cap: ~$60B · P/E: ~22x · Yield: ~2.6%

The number-three industrial gas company (after Linde and Air Liquide) — ~$12B of revenue supplying oxygen, nitrogen, hydrogen, argon, and helium to refineries, chemical plants, steel mills, electronics fabs, and hospitals under 15–20-year take-or-pay contracts, often from plants built on the customer's site. Industrial gases are a three-company global oligopoly with pricing power, high returns, and a dividend Air Products has raised for over 40 consecutive years.

It lost its way in 2020–24 by betting tens of billions on green and blue hydrogen megaprojects (NEOM in Saudi Arabia, Louisiana blue hydrogen, others) with no committed customers; the stock lagged badly, activists arrived, and a new CEO (Eduardo Menezes, from Linde) took over in 2025 with a mandate to cancel uncommitted projects, cut costs, and return to the core. The turnaround — back toward Linde-style discipline — is the equity story.

Shines when: Core gases pricing and volumes grow, uncommitted projects are exited and capex falls, margins recover toward Linde's, and the NEOM project finds offtakers.

Dangers: Hydrogen-project write-downs and stranded capital. Execution on the turnaround. Industrial-production cyclicality. Helium price swings. A premium multiple already assumes recovery.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends September). Adjusted EBITDA margin, capex guidance and project exits, core gas volumes and pricing, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a project-cancellation or margin-progress catalyst. If stuck, an oligopoly member with a 40-year dividend record mid-turnaround — comfortable.

SHW The Sherwin-Williams Company

Sector: Paints & Coatings · Market cap: ~$85B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~0.9%

The largest paint company in the world — ~$23B of revenue — with a model no competitor has copied: the Paint Stores Group, ~4,800 company-owned stores selling directly to professional painters and contractors, who are the majority of paint volume and who value convenience and service over price. Consumer Brands (Valspar, Minwax, sold through Lowe's) and Performance Coatings (industrial, automotive refinish, packaging, protective coatings) round it out. It bought Valspar in 2017 and has taken share every year since.

Sherwin has raised its dividend for over 45 consecutive years and compounded earnings at double-digit rates through store growth, pricing, and buybacks. Its cycle risk is housing and remodeling: when existing-home sales fall, so does repaint volume — which is what happened in 2023–25, and the stock still held up on share gains and pricing.

Shines when: Existing-home sales and remodeling recover (rate cuts), raw-material costs (titanium dioxide, resins) ease, pro-painter share gains continue, and new stores ramp.

Dangers: Housing cyclicality. Raw-material inflation. A 30x multiple. Competition from PPG and Behr (Home Depot). Tariffs on inputs.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Paint Stores Group same-store sales, gross margin, net new stores, full-year EPS guidance, existing-home-sales data.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a rate-cut housing rotation. If stuck, one of the best-run companies in America — comfortable, at a premium.

ECL Ecolab

Sector: Water / Hygiene / Industrial Services · Market cap: ~$75B · P/E: ~35x · Yield: ~1%

The global leader in water treatment, cleaning and sanitizing, and pest elimination for businesses — ~$16B of revenue from restaurants, hotels, hospitals, food and beverage plants, refineries, paper mills, and increasingly data centers (cooling-water chemistry and reuse are a growth business as AI facilities consume enormous water). Institutional and Specialty (~35%), Industrial (~40%), Healthcare and Life Sciences, and Pest Elimination. Service-heavy: 25,000 field reps visit customers and sell consumables on subscription-like contracts.

Ecolab has raised its dividend for over 30 consecutive years and grows earnings at double-digit rates through pricing (its "value pricing" program) and margin expansion. Bill Gates's Cascade is a large holder. The multiple is among the highest in materials because the business behaves like a services company.

Shines when: Pricing outruns costs, data-center and semiconductor water demand grows, life sciences recovers, and operating margin expands toward the 20% target.

Dangers: A 35x multiple. Restaurant and hotel demand cyclicality. Raw-material and freight costs. Currency. Slower industrial activity.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic sales growth by segment, operating margin, pricing contribution, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%. Not a two-month mover. As a stuck-bucket holding: a 30-year dividend grower with pricing power — comfortable.

PPG PPG Industries

Sector: Coatings · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~14x · Yield: ~2.5%

The second-largest coatings company in the world — ~$16B of revenue from Performance Coatings (automotive refinish, aerospace coatings and sealants, protective and marine, architectural outside North America) and Industrial Coatings (auto OEM, industrial, packaging, specialty). PPG sold its U.S. and Canadian architectural business (the Glidden and PPG Paints retail brands) to a private-equity buyer in 2024 to exit the low-margin fight with Sherwin-Williams and focus on industrial and specialty coatings. Pittsburgh-based; a dividend raised for over 50 consecutive years.

Aerospace coatings (every Boeing and Airbus needs PPG's products, and the backlog stretches years) and auto refinish (collision repair, recession-resistant) are the crown jewels; auto OEM and general industrial are the cyclical drag. It trades at a discount to Sherwin because it is more industrial and less growth.

Shines when: Aerospace production ramps, auto refinish pricing holds, industrial volumes recover, raw-material costs ease, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Industrial and auto-production cyclicality. European exposure. Raw-material inflation. Currency. Slower growth than peers.

Watch: Reports mid Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic sales growth by segment, aerospace backlog, segment margins, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is an industrial-cycle inflection. If stuck, a 50-year dividend record at a value multiple — comfortable.

DD DuPont de Nemours

Sector: Specialty Materials · Market cap: ~$18B (post-spin) · P/E: ~17x · Yield: ~2.5%

The 220-year-old company has been taken apart and reassembled so many times that "DuPont" now means something specific: after the 2019 DowDuPont breakup and the November 2025 spin-off of its electronics business as Qnity (semiconductor materials, the growth piece), what remains is a ~$7B specialty-materials company focused on water (reverse-osmosis membranes, ion-exchange resins — the FilmTec and Amberlite businesses, a leader in desalination and industrial water), healthcare (medical packaging, Tyvek for sterile packaging, biopharma consumables), and protection (Kevlar, Nomex, Tyvek building wrap, Corian).

The strategy is to be a focused, higher-multiple specialty company rather than a conglomerate; the Qnity spin was the last big step. Executive chairman Ed Breen has run the portfolio surgery for a decade. Dividend reset with the spin; buybacks.

Shines when: Water and healthcare grow mid-to-high single digits, the post-spin multiple re-rates toward specialty peers, PFAS liabilities are settled within reserves, and margins expand.

Dangers: PFAS litigation (a large settlement is in place; more claims may come). Construction and industrial cyclicality in protection. Execution as a smaller company. Currency.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Organic growth by segment, adjusted EBITDA margin, PFAS developments, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a post-spin re-rating quarter. If stuck, a focused specialty company with real franchises — comfortable, with PFAS as the tail risk.

Packaging

IP International Paper

Sector: Packaging · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~4%

The largest containerboard and corrugated-box maker in North America and, after acquiring DS Smith (UK) in January 2025 for ~$7B, in Europe as well — ~$24B of revenue from the brown boxes that carry e-commerce, food, and industrial goods. It sold its global cellulose fibers business in 2025 to focus entirely on packaging. Memphis-based; a dividend that has been paid for decades but was cut in 2021 (with the Sylvamo spin) and has been flat since.

New CEO Andy Silvernail (from IDEX, an 80/20 disciple like ITW's) took over in 2024 and launched a sweeping overhaul — closing mills, cutting SKUs, exiting low-margin customers, and targeting $4B-plus of EBITDA — that the market re-rated on before the results arrived. The DS Smith integration doubles the challenge.

Shines when: Box demand recovers (e-commerce, industrial production), containerboard prices rise, the 80/20 program delivers margin, and DS Smith synergies land.

Dangers: Containerboard price cycles and overcapacity. Execution on a very large restructuring plus a very large acquisition at once. European weakness. Leverage. A dividend that could stay flat for years.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Adjusted EBITDA by segment, box shipments, containerboard pricing (Fastmarkets RISI), synergy and cost-out progress, free cash flow.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12% — large for a paper company, because the turnaround is priced in. The two-month setup is a margin-delivery quarter. If stuck, a 4% yield from an essential product — comfortable if the turnaround works, frustrating if it stalls.

PKG Packaging Corporation of America

Sector: Packaging · Market cap: ~$18B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~2.5%

The third-largest containerboard producer and the best-run — ~$9B of revenue from corrugated packaging (about 90%) and a small paper business (office paper, the former Boise). PCA runs fewer, larger, well-invested mills at higher utilization than peers, sells mostly to small and mid-sized box customers who value service, and has posted the highest margins in the industry for two decades. It bought Greif's containerboard business (~$1.8B) in 2025 to add capacity.

Dividend raised regularly; conservative balance sheet; no restructuring drama. It trades at a premium to International Paper because it never needed a turnaround.

Shines when: Box demand grows, containerboard prices rise, the Greif mills integrate, and mill utilization stays high.

Dangers: Containerboard price cycles. Industrial-production sensitivity. Input costs (fiber, energy, chemicals). A premium multiple in a cyclical industry.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Corrugated shipments per day, containerboard pricing, packaging segment EBITDA margin, quarterly guidance (PCA guides one quarter ahead).

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a containerboard price increase (announced in industry publications) or a box-demand inflection. If stuck, the quality name in packaging — comfortable.

BALL Ball Corporation

Sector: Aluminum Packaging · Market cap: ~$16B · P/E: ~16x · Yield: ~1.5%

The world's largest maker of aluminum beverage cans — ~$12B of revenue supplying Coca-Cola, PepsiCo, Anheuser-Busch, Molson Coors, and the energy-drink and sparkling-water brands that drove can demand for a decade. Ball sold its aerospace business to BAE Systems for $5.6B in 2024 to become a pure packaging company, and used the proceeds to cut debt and buy back stock. Aluminum cost passes through to customers, so Ball earns a conversion margin per can, not a commodity spread.

Can demand is steady (aluminum keeps winning share from glass and plastic on sustainability) but not fast-growing; Ball's earnings growth comes from cost, mix (specialty can sizes), and share repurchases. Dividend raised annually.

Shines when: Can volumes grow (energy drinks, hard seltzers, sparkling water), specialty-can mix rises, Europe and South America volumes recover, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Beverage-volume softness (beer decline). Customer concentration. Aluminum tariff pass-through friction. Capacity additions by competitors (Crown, Ardagh). Currency in Brazil and Argentina.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Global shipment growth by region, comparable EBITDA, free cash flow, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a volume-growth beat. If stuck, a duopoly-like business with steady cash return — comfortable.

AVY Avery Dennison

Sector: Labels / RFID · Market cap: ~$14B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~2%

The world's largest maker of pressure-sensitive label materials — the adhesive labels on every consumer product, shipping box, and pharmaceutical bottle — ~$8.7B of revenue from Materials Group (label stock, graphics films, industrial tapes) and Solutions Group (apparel labels and tags, and RFID inlays, where Avery is the dominant supplier as retailers like Walmart tag every item for inventory tracking). RFID is the growth story: billions of tags a year, expanding from apparel into food, logistics, and pharmaceuticals.

Avery has raised its dividend for over a decade and buys back stock consistently; its earnings track consumer-goods volumes and apparel retail, with RFID adding a secular layer.

Shines when: RFID adoption expands into new categories, apparel and general-retail volumes recover, label-material pricing holds, and margins expand.

Dangers: Consumer-goods and apparel cyclicality. Raw-material costs (paper, adhesives, films). RFID pricing competition. Currency.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic sales growth by segment, Intelligent Labels (RFID) growth, adjusted EBITDA margin, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is an RFID-adoption announcement or a retail-volume recovery. If stuck, a category leader with a secular growth line — comfortable.

Aggregates & building materials

VMC Vulcan Materials

Sector: Aggregates · Market cap: ~$37B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~0.7%

The largest producer of construction aggregates in the U.S. — crushed stone, sand, and gravel, roughly 230 million tons a year from ~400 quarries, mostly in the Sun Belt — plus asphalt and ready-mix concrete. Aggregates are the best business in materials: quarries cannot be permitted near cities anymore, so existing ones are local monopolies; the product is too heavy to ship far; and pricing has risen mid-to-high single digits every year for a decade regardless of volume.

Demand comes from highways (federal infrastructure funding), non-residential construction (data centers, factories), and housing. Vulcan's "unit margin" — profit per ton — has grown every year through pricing and cost control. Dividend raised annually; modest buybacks; the multiple is high because the pricing power is nearly unique.

Shines when: Infrastructure spending flows (state DOT lettings), non-residential construction grows, pricing stays mid-to-high single digits, and volumes recover from the housing trough.

Dangers: Volume sensitivity to construction cycles and weather. A 30x multiple. Diesel and energy costs. Infrastructure-funding politics. Mexico dispute (the Calica quarry was shut by the Mexican government).

Watch: Reports mid Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Aggregates shipments, freight-adjusted price per ton, cash gross profit per ton, full-year EBITDA guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is an infrastructure-funding catalyst or a weather-driven miss that the next quarter corrects. If stuck, a portfolio of local monopolies — comfortable, at a price.

MLM Martin Marietta Materials

Sector: Aggregates · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~28x · Yield: ~0.6%

Vulcan's rival and near-twin — ~200 million tons of aggregates a year from quarries across the Southeast, Texas, Colorado, and (via acquisitions) the Southwest and California, plus cement in Texas, and ready-mix and asphalt. Raleigh-based. Same local-monopoly economics, same pricing power, same infrastructure and non-residential drivers. Martin Marietta has been the more acquisitive of the two and in 2025 executed a large asset swap with Quikrete (cement and ready-mix out, aggregates in) to become even purer.

Dividend raised annually; consistent buybacks. The stock and Vulcan's move together; the differences are geographic mix (Martin has more Texas, Vulcan more Southeast and California) and Martin's cement exposure.

Shines when: Same as Vulcan — infrastructure, non-residential, pricing — plus Texas growth and acquisition accretion.

Dangers: Same as Vulcan, plus acquisition integration and Texas weather.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Aggregates shipments and pricing, gross profit per ton, full-year EBITDA guidance, acquisition announcements.

How to use it: Pair with Vulcan; pick on relative valuation. If stuck, comfortable.

CRH CRH plc

Sector: Building Materials · Market cap: ~$70B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~1.5%

The largest building-materials company in the world — ~$37B of revenue — and, since moving its primary listing from London to New York in 2023 and joining the S&P 500 in 2025, effectively an American company: about 75% of profit comes from North America, where it is the largest producer of aggregates, asphalt, and ready-mix (Oldcastle), a major cement producer (Ash Grove), and a large maker of building products (precast, water and drainage, outdoor living). Irish-domiciled; the rest is Europe.

CRH's model is "integrated solutions" — bundling materials, products, and paving for infrastructure and commercial projects — and disciplined acquisition. It trades at a discount to Vulcan and Martin Marietta because it is more diversified and less pure, which is the bull case: same infrastructure tailwind, lower multiple, higher yield, and heavy buybacks.

Shines when: U.S. infrastructure and non-residential spending grows, pricing holds across materials, European operations stabilize, acquisitions add, and the U.S.-listing re-rating continues.

Dangers: Construction cyclicality. European weakness. Weather. Acquisition integration. A more complex portfolio than the pure aggregates names.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Americas Materials Solutions organic growth and margin, pricing by product, full-year EBITDA guidance, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is an infrastructure catalyst or a re-rating toward U.S. peers. If stuck, the largest company in its industry at the lowest multiple — comfortable.

Metals

STLD Steel Dynamics

Sector: Steel · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: ~1.5%

The second-largest and best-run electric-arc-furnace steelmaker in the U.S. (after the Atlas's Nucor) — ~$18B of revenue from flat-rolled and long-product steel made from recycled scrap in mini-mills, plus steel fabrication (joists and decking for construction), metals recycling (OmniSource), and, since 2025, a new aluminum flat-rolled mill in Mississippi aimed at beverage-can and automotive sheet — a diversification away from steel's cycles. Founded in 1993 by Nucor alumni; Fort Wayne-based.

Steel is cyclical, but EAF producers are lower-cost and more flexible than the old integrated mills, and U.S. tariffs (raised to 50% on imported steel in 2025) protect domestic pricing. Steel Dynamics has raised its dividend annually for over a decade and buys back stock aggressively at cycle troughs.

Shines when: Steel prices rise (tariffs, infrastructure and manufacturing demand), the aluminum mill ramps profitably, fabrication backlog holds, and scrap spreads widen.

Dangers: The steel price cycle — earnings can halve in a downturn. Aluminum mill start-up costs and execution. Tariff policy reversals. Auto and construction demand. Scrap-cost inflation.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Steel shipments and average selling price, metal spread, fabrication backlog, aluminum segment losses/profits, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%; moves with hot-rolled coil prices. The two-month setup is a steel-price rally or a tariff catalyst. If stuck, the best operator in a cyclical industry — comfortable at mid-cycle, painful at the top.

RS Reliance, Inc.

Sector: Metals Service Centers · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~1.6%

The largest metals service center in North America — ~$14B of revenue buying steel, aluminum, and other metals from mills, then cutting, shaping, and delivering small quantities to over 125,000 customers (fabricators, machine shops, construction, aerospace) within 24 hours. It is a distribution business with value-added processing: Reliance earns a spread that is more stable than the mills' because it holds little inventory and prices with the market.

Built through 75-plus acquisitions of family-owned service centers, run decentrally, and known for conservative finance — low debt, steady dividend growth, and buybacks. It is the quietest way to own U.S. industrial activity without taking mill-level commodity risk.

Shines when: Industrial demand and non-residential construction grow, metal prices rise modestly (inventory gains), acquisitions add, and value-added processing mix increases.

Dangers: Industrial-activity sensitivity. Metal-price deflation (inventory losses). Aerospace and semiconductor end-market cycles. Acquisition integration.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Tons sold, average selling price, gross margin, quarterly guidance (Reliance guides one quarter ahead), buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is an industrial-cycle inflection. If stuck, a conservatively financed distributor — comfortable.

Fertilizer & commodity chemicals

CF CF Industries Holdings

Sector: Nitrogen Fertilizer · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: ~12x · Yield: ~2.3%

The largest nitrogen fertilizer producer in the world — ammonia, urea, UAN, and ammonium nitrate from plants in Louisiana, Oklahoma, Iowa, and the UK, ~$6.5B of revenue. Nitrogen is made from natural gas, and CF's plants sit on the cheapest gas in the world, which gives it a structural cost advantage over European and Asian producers whose gas costs several times more. Its earnings are a spread: nitrogen prices (set globally by corn demand and marginal producers) minus U.S. gas costs.

The growth angle is low-carbon ammonia: CF is building a blue-ammonia plant (Blue Point, with JERA and Mitsui) in Louisiana with carbon capture, aimed at Japanese and Korean power producers using ammonia as fuel. Dividend steady; enormous buybacks — the share count has fallen by a third in a decade.

Shines when: Nitrogen prices rise (corn acreage, European plant curtailments, geopolitical supply shocks), U.S. gas stays cheap, blue ammonia finds offtakers, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Nitrogen price cycles. U.S. gas price spikes. Corn-price weakness reducing fertilizer demand. Blue-ammonia project cost and demand risk. Tariffs and trade disruptions on fertilizer flows.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Realized nitrogen prices, gross margin per ton, Henry Hub gas, Blue Point milestones, buyback pace, USDA planting intentions (March).

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%; moves with urea and corn prices. The two-month setup is a spring-planting demand rally or a European gas spike. If stuck, a low-cost producer buying back stock through the cycle — comfortable.

DOW Dow Inc.

Sector: Commodity Chemicals · Market cap: ~$18B · P/E: n/m (trough) · Yield: ~4% after a 2025 dividend cut

The largest commodity-chemical company in the U.S. — ~$42B of revenue from polyethylene (plastics for packaging), polyurethanes, silicones, and industrial chemicals — spun out of DowDuPont in 2019. Dow's economics are the ethane-to-polyethylene spread: cheap U.S. natural-gas liquids as feedstock, global plastic prices as output. When the world overbuilds capacity (as China did through 2025), the spread collapses.

2024–25 was a trough: polyethylene oversupply, weak European and Chinese demand, tariffs disrupting trade flows, and Dow cut its dividend in half in July 2025 — the first cut in its history as a standalone — while closing European assets and delaying its Canadian expansion. It is the least boring business in this section, included because it is a real company at a cycle low with a franchise that recovers every cycle.

Shines when: Polyethylene margins recover (global capacity rationalization, demand growth), European closures reduce losses, U.S. feedstock advantage widens, and the dividend is stabilized.

Dangers: A prolonged oversupply cycle. Further dividend risk. Leverage. Tariffs and trade disruption. European energy costs. Plastic-regulation politics.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Polyethylene margins and volumes, operating EBITDA by segment, free cash flow, net leverage, capacity-closure announcements.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a cycle-bottom signal (capacity closures, margin inflection). If stuck, you own a cyclical at a trough with a halved dividend — tolerable if the cycle turns within a year, a slow grind if not.

LYB LyondellBasell Industries

Sector: Commodity Chemicals · Market cap: ~$18B · P/E: ~20x (trough) · Yield: ~9% (the market is pricing a cut)

Dow's closest peer — ~$30B of revenue from polyethylene and polypropylene, propylene oxide, and polymer-technology licensing (its Spheripol process is used worldwide), plus a Houston refinery it has been trying to exit. Netherlands-domiciled with a large U.S. Gulf Coast footprint, so it shares Dow's feedstock advantage and Dow's overcapacity problem. It has been closing and selling European assets and cutting costs while holding a dividend that now yields near 9% — a number that says the market expects the same cut Dow made.

The bull case is the same as Dow's: a trough in a cyclical industry, a low-cost U.S. position, and a management committed to the dividend. The bear case is that the cycle is structurally longer this time because of Chinese capacity.

Shines when: Polyolefin margins recover, European exits reduce losses, the refinery is sold or closed, the dividend is maintained through the trough, and cost cuts hit.

Dangers: A dividend cut. Prolonged oversupply. Leverage. European energy and demand. Refinery exit costs.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Oct. EBITDA by segment, free cash flow vs. dividend, net leverage, European asset-sale progress, polyethylene margin trends.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a dividend-affirmed clean quarter or a margin inflection. If stuck, the 9% yield is only comforting if it survives — the riskiest name in the section.

Consumer Discretionary

Auto parts, farm-and-ranch retail, homebuilders, hotels, pest control, pool supplies, casual dining, travel, beauty, luxury, and the two Japanese carmakers. The Atlas has HD, LOW, MCD, SBUX, NKE, TJX, ROST, BKNG, CMG, YUM, DIS, GM, and F; this is the second tier, where the best businesses are as durable as any staple and the worst are as cyclical as any industrial.

Auto parts & specialty retailAZOORLYGPCLKQTSCOBBYULTAHomebuildersDHILENPHMNVRHotels & travelMARHLTEXPERestaurantsDRITXRHQSRHome servicesROLPOOLApparel & luxuryRLTPRAutomakersTMHMC

Auto parts & specialty retail

AZO AutoZone

Sector: Auto Parts Retail · Market cap: ~$65B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: none

About 7,500 auto-parts stores in the U.S., Mexico, and Brazil — ~$19B of revenue — selling batteries, brakes, filters, and fluids to do-it-yourself customers (about 70%) and, increasingly, to professional repair shops (the commercial business, growing double digits as AutoZone builds hub and mega-hub stores with deep inventory). The category is recession-resistant: when people can't afford new cars they fix old ones, and the average U.S. vehicle is over 12 years old.

AutoZone is the most famous buyback story in retail: it has repurchased over 90% of its shares since 1998 and never paid a dividend, which is why the stock price runs into the thousands of dollars. Margins are the highest in the category (gross margins above 50%), and same-store sales have been positive for most of three decades.

Shines when: Commercial share gains continue, the aging vehicle fleet drives DIY demand, Mexico and Brazil grow, weather (hot summers, cold winters) drives failures, and buybacks compound EPS.

Dangers: Competition from O'Reilly in commercial. Tariffs on imported parts. EV adoption eventually reducing parts demand (a very long-tail risk). Currency in Mexico and Brazil. Inventory investment compressing free cash flow.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends August). Domestic same-store sales (DIY vs. commercial), gross margin, commercial sales per store, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a comp acceleration or a recession-fear rotation into defensives. If stuck, one of the most reliable compounders in retail — comfortable.

ORLY O'Reilly Automotive

Sector: Auto Parts Retail · Market cap: ~$85B · P/E: ~33x · Yield: none

AutoZone's rival and, by execution, the leader — about 6,400 stores across the U.S., Mexico, and Canada, ~$17B of revenue, with a roughly even split between DIY and professional customers and a distribution network (hub stores, regional distribution centers with same-day delivery) that professional shops rate best in class. O'Reilly has posted positive same-store sales for over 30 consecutive years and split its stock 15-for-1 in 2025.

Same buyback model as AutoZone — no dividend, relentless repurchases — with a higher multiple because its commercial business is more mature and its growth more consistent. Family-influenced (the O'Reillys of Springfield, Missouri) and famously disciplined.

Shines when: Professional share gains continue, the aging fleet supports demand, distribution expansion (new DCs) lifts service levels, Mexico and Canada grow, and buybacks compound.

Dangers: A 33x multiple. Tariffs on parts. Competition from AutoZone, Advance, and Genuine Parts in commercial. EV adoption long term.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Comparable-store sales, gross margin, professional vs. DIY growth, full-year guidance, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. Same setups as AutoZone; pair on relative valuation. If stuck, the best-run retailer in America by many measures — comfortable, at a premium.

GPC Genuine Parts Company

Sector: Auto & Industrial Parts Distribution · Market cap: ~$18B · P/E: ~16x · Yield: ~3.2%

NAPA Auto Parts (about 6,000 stores in North America, plus Europe and Australia) and Motion Industries (the largest industrial parts distributor — bearings, belts, power transmission — for factories) — ~$24B of revenue, roughly 60% automotive and 40% industrial. Atlanta-based; a dividend raised for nearly 70 consecutive years, the longest streak of any company in this guide.

NAPA is more wholesale and commercial than AutoZone or O'Reilly (it sells to independent repair shops and through independently owned stores), with lower margins; Motion adds industrial cyclicality. The stock has lagged the pure-play retailers for years and trades at half their multiple with a 3% yield; a restructuring program and a new CEO in 2025 are the catalysts.

Shines when: Industrial activity recovers (Motion), NAPA comps improve on commercial share, Europe stabilizes, restructuring savings hit, and the multiple re-rates.

Dangers: Industrial cyclicality. Lower margins than pure-play retailers. European exposure. Tariffs. A long underperformance record.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Automotive and Industrial comparable sales, segment margins, restructuring progress, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is an industrial-cycle inflection at a value multiple. If stuck, a 70-year dividend record at a 3% yield — comfortable.

LKQ LKQ Corporation

Sector: Alternative Auto Parts · Market cap: ~$8B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: ~4%

The largest distributor of alternative collision and mechanical auto parts — recycled parts from salvage vehicles, aftermarket replacement parts, remanufactured engines and transmissions — sold to body shops and repairers as cheaper alternatives to OEM parts, ~$14B of revenue across North America, Europe (the largest segment by revenue after a decade of acquisitions), and specialty (RV and truck accessories). Insurers push shops toward LKQ's parts to hold down claim costs.

The stock fell to decade lows in 2024–25 as European operations underperformed, collision volumes fell (fewer claims as repair costs made insurers total more cars), and an activist arrived. It trades at a single-digit multiple with a 4% yield; management is cutting costs and reviewing the European portfolio.

Shines when: Collision repair volumes recover, Europe margins improve or assets are sold, insurer adoption of alternative parts rises, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: European execution. Declining collision volumes (advanced driver-assistance systems reduce accidents; totaling reduces repairs). Scrap and precious-metal price swings. Leverage. Tariffs on imported parts.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic parts and services revenue by segment, segment EBITDA margin, free cash flow, European restructuring progress.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is a European-fix catalyst at a distressed multiple. If stuck, a 4% yield from an essential-but-unloved business — tolerable, with Europe as the wait.

TSCO Tractor Supply Company

Sector: Farm & Ranch Retail · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~26x · Yield: ~1.6%

The largest rural-lifestyle retailer in America — about 2,300 stores selling livestock and pet feed, fencing, tools, workwear, and seasonal goods to hobby farmers, ranchers, and rural homeowners — ~$15B of revenue, plus the Petsense pet-store chain. Its customer is the "out here" lifestyle: land, animals, and self-reliance, a demographic that grew during the pandemic exodus to exurbs and has stayed.

Tractor Supply has grown store count and comps for decades with almost no direct competition (no other national chain serves the segment), a loyalty program with over 40 million members, and consistent buybacks and dividend growth. It split 5-for-1 in 2024. Roughly half of sales are consumable, usable, and edible products (feed, fuel, pet food), which recur regardless of the economy.

Shines when: Rural consumer spending holds, new stores ramp, the consumable mix drives traffic, pet and animal ownership grows, and weather cooperates (cold winters and dry summers sell product).

Dangers: Big-ticket discretionary (riding mowers, safes) softness. Weather. Tariffs on imported hard goods. A premium multiple. Deflation in commodity-linked categories (feed).

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Comparable-store sales (transactions vs. ticket), gross margin, new-store count, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a comp acceleration or a rural-consumer rotation. If stuck, a category monopoly — comfortable.

BBY Best Buy

Sector: Consumer Electronics Retail · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: ~12x · Yield: ~5%

The last national consumer-electronics chain standing — about 1,000 stores and a large online business, ~$41B of revenue — after Circuit City, RadioShack, and the rest failed. Best Buy survived Amazon by price-matching, using stores as showrooms and pickup points, building the Geek Squad service business, and becoming the physical channel for Apple, Samsung, and Sony. Revenue peaked in the pandemic electronics boom and has drifted down since as the replacement cycle lengthened.

The stock is a yield-and-buyback story: a dividend above 5%, raised annually for two decades, and consistent repurchases, on a business that earns steady mid-single-digit margins. The hoped-for catalyst is a replacement cycle — AI PCs, new gaming consoles, TVs — that lifts comps.

Shines when: A product cycle (AI PCs, consoles, phones) lifts comparable sales, services and membership grow, tariffs are absorbed, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Tariffs — most electronics are imported. Long replacement cycles. Amazon, Walmart, and Costco. Low margins with limited cushion. Category deflation.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends January). Comparable sales, gross margin, membership growth, full-year guidance, tariff commentary.
