How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a product-cycle quarter or a tariff-relief headline. If stuck, a 5% yield from a survivor — comfortable if you accept flat growth.

ULTA Ulta Beauty

Sector: Beauty Retail · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: none

The largest beauty retailer in the U.S. — about 1,450 stores selling prestige and mass cosmetics, skincare, haircare, and fragrance under one roof, with salons inside, ~$12B of revenue. The model is the mix: Chanel next to Maybelline, which no department store or drugstore offers. A loyalty program with 45 million members drives nearly all sales. It has partnerships with Target (shop-in-shops) and bought Space NK (UK) in 2025 for international expansion.

Growth slowed in 2024 as Sephora (inside Kohl's) and Amazon took share and the beauty category normalized after a post-pandemic boom; a new CEO (Kecia Steelman) took over in 2025 with a plan to reaccelerate through exclusives, wellness, and international. No dividend; heavy buybacks. Berkshire briefly held a stake in 2024.

Shines when: Comparable sales reaccelerate, beauty category growth holds, exclusive brands and wellness expand the basket, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Sephora and Amazon competition. Beauty-category slowdowns. Shrink. Consumer trade-down. A new-CEO transition.

Watch: Reports early Mar/late May/Aug/Dec (fiscal year ends January). Comparable sales, gross margin, loyalty member growth, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15%. The two-month setup is a comp-reacceleration quarter. If stuck, a category leader with no debt — comfortable.

Homebuilders

DHI D.R. Horton

Sector: Homebuilding · Market cap: ~$45B · P/E: ~13x · Yield: ~1%

The largest homebuilder in America by volume — about 90,000 homes a year, ~$36B of revenue — focused on entry-level and first-move-up buyers across the Sun Belt and beyond, plus rental communities (single-family build-to-rent), lot development (through its majority-owned Forestar), and mortgage and title services. Horton builds fast, sells spec homes at accessible prices, and uses mortgage-rate buydowns to keep buyers qualified when rates are high.

Homebuilders traded at low multiples for a decade because the market treats them as cyclicals, but the structural housing shortage (underbuilding since 2008) and the "lock-in effect" (existing homeowners won't sell their 3% mortgages, so buyers turn to new homes) changed the picture. Dividend raised annually; large buybacks.

Shines when: Mortgage rates fall (demand and margins recover as incentives shrink), the existing-home shortage persists, lot costs stabilize, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Mortgage-rate spikes. Incentive costs compressing gross margin (they rose sharply in 2024–25). Land and labor inflation. Tariffs on lumber and materials. A demand slowdown in the Sun Belt.

Watch: Reports mid Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends September). Net orders, closings, gross margin, incentive levels, cancellation rate, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%; the stock moves with mortgage rates. The two-month setup is a rate-cut or ten-year-yield decline. If stuck, the volume leader at a low multiple — comfortable outside a housing recession.

LEN Lennar Corporation

Sector: Homebuilding · Market cap: ~$32B · P/E: ~14x · Yield: ~1.5%

The second-largest builder — about 80,000 homes a year, ~$34B of revenue — and the one that has pushed hardest toward an "asset-light" model: in 2025 it spun off much of its land into Millrose Properties, a REIT that buys and develops lots and sells them back to Lennar just in time, freeing Lennar's balance sheet from years of land inventory. Lennar also runs an "even-flow" production strategy — building at a steady pace and adjusting price to sell — which maximizes volume at the cost of margin when demand is weak.

The Miller family (Stuart Miller is executive chairman) has run it for decades. Dividend raised annually; heavy buybacks. Margins fell in 2024–25 as Lennar cut prices and added incentives to keep volume up, which the market debated.

Shines when: Rates fall and pricing power returns, the asset-light model lifts returns on equity, land costs through Millrose stay predictable, and volumes hold.

Dangers: Margin compression from the even-flow strategy in weak demand. Mortgage rates. Millrose dependence. Land and labor costs. Tariffs.

Watch: Reports mid Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends November). New orders, deliveries, gross margin and incentive levels, average selling price, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. Same rate-driven setups as Horton. If stuck, comfortable outside a housing recession.

PHM PulteGroup

Sector: Homebuilding · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: ~0.8%

The third-largest builder — about 30,000 homes a year, ~$18B of revenue — with a deliberately diversified buyer mix: roughly a quarter entry-level (Centex), 40% move-up (Pulte), and a third active-adult (Del Webb, the 55-plus communities), which makes it less rate-sensitive than Horton or Lennar because active-adult buyers often pay cash from home-sale proceeds. Pulte has the highest gross margins among the large builders, a disciplined land strategy (options rather than ownership where possible), and heavy buybacks.

It trades at the lowest multiple of the three because it grows slower and the market remembers its 2008-era problems; the bull case is that margin quality and the Del Webb franchise deserve a premium.

Shines when: Rates fall, active-adult demand stays resilient, gross margins hold above peers, and buybacks compound.

Dangers: Mortgage rates. Move-up buyer weakness. Land and labor costs. Tariffs. Slower volume growth than peers.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Net new orders by buyer segment, closings, gross margin, cancellation rate, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. Same rate setups; the cash-buyer mix makes it the steadier of the three. If stuck, the highest-margin builder at the lowest multiple — comfortable.

NVR NVR, Inc.

Sector: Homebuilding · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~17x · Yield: none

The builder that never buys land. NVR (Ryan Homes, NVHomes, Heartland Homes) builds about 22,000 homes a year, ~$10B of revenue, concentrated in the Mid-Atlantic (Maryland and Virginia are its heartland), Southeast, and Midwest — and it acquires every lot through options with developers, paying a deposit and taking title only when a home is sold. The result is the highest return on equity in homebuilding, no land-impairment risk, and a stock price above $7,000 because it has never split and has bought back more than 80% of its shares.

NVR pays no dividend, gives no guidance, holds no earnings calls, and has outperformed every other builder over 30 years. It is the closest thing to a boring homebuilder that exists.

Shines when: Rates fall, Mid-Atlantic and Southeast demand grows, lot-option deposits stay small relative to land values, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Mortgage rates. Regional concentration (a Washington, D.C.-area slowdown from federal cuts is a specific risk). Developers raising option prices. A stock price that limits liquidity for small accounts.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. New orders, settlements, gross margin, backlog, buyback pace. No guidance, so results speak for themselves.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. Same rate setups as the sector. If stuck, the safest balance sheet in homebuilding — comfortable.

Hotels & travel

MAR Marriott International

Sector: Hotels (Asset-Light) · Market cap: ~$70B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~1%

The largest hotel company in the world — about 9,500 properties and 1.7 million rooms across 30 brands (Marriott, Ritz-Carlton, St. Regis, Sheraton, Westin, Courtyard, and others) — that owns almost none of them. Marriott franchises and manages hotels that others own, earning fees on room revenue; the Bonvoy loyalty program (over 200 million members) and the co-branded credit cards generate a large, high-margin revenue stream on top. Revenue is ~$26B including reimbursed costs; fee revenue is the number that matters.

Asset-light means Marriott's earnings grow with global room count (it adds 5–6% a year, mostly in Asia and midscale brands) and revenue per available room (RevPAR), with almost no capital required — so free cash flow funds enormous buybacks and a growing dividend. The Marriott family remains a large holder.

Shines when: RevPAR grows (business and group travel recovery, international), the development pipeline converts to rooms, credit-card fees grow, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Travel demand cycles (recession, geopolitical shocks). China and international softness. A premium multiple. Competition from Hilton and short-term rentals. Owner financing constraints slowing new hotels.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Global RevPAR growth, net rooms growth, fee revenue, pipeline signings, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a travel-demand catalyst or a post-scare recovery. If stuck, a fee stream on the world's largest hotel system — comfortable.

HLT Hilton Worldwide Holdings

Sector: Hotels (Asset-Light) · Market cap: ~$60B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~0.2%

Marriott's closest rival — about 8,800 properties and 1.3 million rooms across 24 brands (Hilton, Waldorf Astoria, Conrad, DoubleTree, Hampton, Hilton Garden Inn, Tru, and others) — and arguably the better-run of the two: higher fee margins, a faster net-unit-growth rate (6–7%), and a development pipeline weighted toward the high-return limited-service brands (Hampton, Tru, Spark). Hilton Honors has over 200 million members. Spun off its owned hotels (Park Hotels) and timeshare (Hilton Grand Vacations) in 2017 to become pure fee-based.

CEO Chris Nassetta has run it since 2007; buybacks are the primary capital return (the dividend is nominal). The multiple is higher than Marriott's because growth and margins are slightly better.

Shines when: RevPAR grows, net unit growth stays above 6%, new brands ramp, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Same travel-cycle risks as Marriott. A 30x multiple. Owner financing constraints. Short-term rental competition.

Watch: Reports early Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. System-wide RevPAR, net unit growth, pipeline, adjusted EBITDA, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. Pair with Marriott on relative valuation. If stuck, comfortable.

EXPE Expedia Group

Sector: Online Travel · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: ~0.8%

Expedia, Hotels.com, Vrbo (vacation rentals), Orbitz, Travelocity, and a large B2B business that powers travel booking for airlines, banks, and other companies' loyalty programs — ~$14B of revenue on ~$110B of gross bookings. It is the number-two online travel agency after the Atlas's Booking Holdings, with a heavier U.S. mix and a history of execution problems that a 2023–24 platform consolidation (one loyalty program, one tech stack) was meant to fix.

A new CEO (Ariane Gorin) took over in 2024; B2B has grown double digits while the consumer brands were rebuilt; buybacks have been heavy and a dividend was initiated in 2025. The multiple is roughly half of Booking's — the value play in travel.

Shines when: Consumer-brand bookings reaccelerate on the unified loyalty program, B2B keeps growing double digits, Vrbo recovers share from Airbnb, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Travel-demand cycles. Google and AI search changing how travel is booked. Competition from Booking and Airbnb. Marketing-cost intensity. Execution history.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Gross bookings growth (B2C vs. B2B), room nights, adjusted EBITDA margin, buyback pace.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±8–15% — the most volatile name in this section. The two-month setup is a B2C reacceleration quarter. If stuck, a cheap cash generator with buybacks — comfortable, though the AI-search risk is real.

Restaurants

DRI Darden Restaurants

Sector: Casual Dining · Market cap: ~$23B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~3%

Olive Garden (the largest casual-dining chain in America, about half of profit), LongHorn Steakhouse, the fine-dining group (Ruth's Chris, The Capital Grille, Eddie V's), and other brands (Cheddar's, Yard House, Chuy's — acquired in 2024) — about 2,100 company-owned restaurants and ~$12B of revenue. Darden owns rather than franchises, which means it captures full margins and bears full costs, and it runs the industry's best supply chain and labor model.

Casual dining has been shrinking for years, but Darden takes share within it: Olive Garden's value positioning (unlimited breadsticks, never-ending pasta) holds traffic when competitors lose it. Dividend raised annually since the pandemic cut; buybacks.

Shines when: Same-restaurant traffic holds or grows, commodity and labor inflation ease, Olive Garden's delivery partnership (Uber) adds sales, and fine dining recovers.

Dangers: Consumer traffic declines in a slowdown. Beef and labor costs. GLP-1 effects on restaurant demand. Fine-dining exposure to corporate spending. Casual-dining secular decline.

Watch: Reports late Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends May). Same-restaurant sales and traffic by brand, restaurant-level margin, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a traffic-beat quarter. If stuck, a 3% yield from the best operator in its category — comfortable.

TXRH Texas Roadhouse

Sector: Casual Dining · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~26x · Yield: ~1.5%

About 800 restaurants (Texas Roadhouse, Bubba's 33, Jaggers), ~$5.5B of revenue, and the best traffic record in casual dining — positive same-store traffic nearly every quarter for a decade while the category shrank, on a formula of hand-cut steaks, fresh-baked rolls, and prices deliberately kept below what the market would bear. Mostly company-owned; managing partners invest in their restaurants and share profits, which produces unusually low turnover.

Founded by Kent Taylor (who died in 2021); the culture survived him. Dividend raised annually; buybacks. The multiple is high for a restaurant because the traffic growth is so rare.

Shines when: Traffic growth continues, beef costs ease, new-unit growth stays on plan, and pricing modestly lifts margins.

Dangers: Beef inflation (its largest cost; the cattle cycle is at a tight point). Consumer weakness. A premium multiple. Labor costs.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Same-restaurant sales and traffic, restaurant-level margin, beef-cost commentary, new-unit openings.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±5–10%. The two-month setup is a beef-cost relief or a traffic beat. If stuck, the best-run restaurant chain in the country — comfortable.

QSR Restaurant Brands International

Sector: Quick-Service Franchisor · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~3.5%

Burger King, Tim Hortons, Popeyes, and Firehouse Subs — about 32,000 restaurants worldwide, ~$9B of revenue, nearly all franchised, so RBI earns royalties and rent rather than operating restaurants. Toronto-based, created by 3G Capital's 2014 merger of Burger King and Tim Hortons; Popeyes (2017) and Firehouse (2021) followed. Tim Hortons dominates Canadian coffee; Burger King is a distant number-two in U.S. burgers and has been mid-remodel under a "Reclaim the Flame" investment plan; Popeyes is the growth brand.

RBI bought Carrols, Burger King's largest franchisee, in 2024 to accelerate remodels and refranchise later. Dividend raised annually; a 3.5% yield that is high for a franchisor. It trades at a discount to McDonald's and Yum because Burger King's U.S. turnaround is unfinished.

Shines when: Burger King U.S. comps improve as remodels complete, Tim Hortons holds share, Popeyes grows units internationally, and the Carrols restaurants are refranchised at a gain.

Dangers: Burger King's U.S. position (McDonald's, Wendy's, and fast-casual competition). Canadian consumer weakness. Franchisee financial health. Leverage. Currency.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Comparable sales by brand, net restaurant growth, franchisee profitability, adjusted EBITDA, remodel progress.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. The two-month setup is a Burger King comp inflection. If stuck, a 3.5% yield from a franchise royalty stream — comfortable.

Home services

ROL Rollins, Inc.

Sector: Pest Control · Market cap: ~$28B · P/E: ~45x · Yield: ~1.2%

Orkin — the largest pest-control company in the world — plus dozens of regional brands acquired over decades (HomeTeam, Northwest, Fox, and others), ~$3.6B of revenue from residential (~45%), commercial (~35%), and termite services (~20%), nearly all recurring: customers sign up for monthly or quarterly treatments and stay for years. The Rollins family controls the company; it has raised its dividend for over 20 consecutive years and pays specials.

Pest control is the archetypal boring compounder: demand is non-discretionary (nobody stops treating termites in a recession), pricing rises every year, and the industry is fragmented, so Rollins buys small operators every quarter. Growth of 8–12% a year with expanding margins has earned it a multiple more common in software.

Shines when: Organic growth stays high single digits, pricing holds, acquisitions add, margins expand, and warm, wet weather drives pest activity.

Dangers: A 45x multiple — a de-rating to 30x is a 33% drawdown with nothing wrong. Labor costs in a people-intensive business. Competition from Rentokil-Terminix. Weather.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Organic revenue growth by segment, adjusted EBITDA margin, acquisition contribution, pricing.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. Not a two-month mover. As a stuck-bucket holding: nearly perfect business, imperfect price.

POOL Pool Corporation

Sector: Pool Supplies Distribution · Market cap: ~$11B · P/E: ~26x · Yield: ~1.7%

The largest distributor of swimming-pool supplies in the world — chemicals, equipment (pumps, filters, heaters from Pentair, Hayward, and Fluidra), parts, and outdoor-living products — sold through about 450 sales centers to pool builders, service technicians, and retailers. About $5.3B of revenue. Roughly 60% of sales are maintenance and repair (chemicals and replacement parts for the installed base of over 5 million U.S. pools), which recur every year regardless of new construction.

Pool Corp compounded for two decades on pool ownership growth, acquisitions of regional distributors, and pricing; the pandemic pool boom pulled demand forward, and 2023–25 was the hangover as new-pool construction fell by half. Berkshire Hathaway took a stake in 2024. Dividend raised annually; buybacks.

Shines when: New-pool construction recovers (rates fall, housing turns), maintenance demand holds, weather is hot and long, and acquisitions add.

Dangers: Housing and discretionary sensitivity. Weather. Chemical price deflation. A premium multiple for a distributor. Sun Belt concentration.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Base-business sales growth, gross margin, new-pool construction commentary, full-year EPS guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a housing-rate catalyst ahead of pool season. If stuck, a distribution monopoly with a maintenance annuity — comfortable.

Apparel & luxury

RL Ralph Lauren Corporation

Sector: Apparel / Luxury Lifestyle · Market cap: ~$18B · P/E: ~22x · Yield: ~1.3%

The polo-shirt company that became a case study in brand elevation — ~$7.5B of revenue across North America, Europe, and Asia (the fastest-growing region, especially China), with direct-to-consumer (its own stores and website) now the majority of sales and wholesale (department stores) deliberately shrunk. Under CEO Patrice Louvet since 2017, Ralph Lauren cut discounting, closed weak doors, raised average unit prices every year, and reached younger consumers — and the stock more than tripled from its 2020 lows.

Ralph Lauren himself remains executive chairman and controls the vote. Dividend raised annually; buybacks. It is the rare American apparel brand with luxury-like pricing power.

Shines when: Average unit retail keeps rising, Asia and Europe grow double digits, direct-to-consumer mix expands margins, and tariffs are absorbed through pricing.

Dangers: Consumer discretionary weakness. Tariffs on Asian-made apparel. China exposure. Department-store decline in the remaining wholesale business. Founder succession.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Comparable sales by region, average unit retail growth, gross margin, direct-to-consumer mix, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a strong holiday quarter or a tariff-relief headline. If stuck, a brand with pricing power and a clean balance sheet — comfortable.

TPR Tapestry, Inc.

Sector: Accessible Luxury · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.5%

Coach and Kate Spade — ~$7B of revenue, about 80% of it Coach, whose handbags at $300–500 sit in the "accessible luxury" tier between mass brands and European houses. Coach has been the turnaround of the decade in fashion: new creative direction, a focus on Gen Z, tight inventory, and full-price selling drove several years of growth and margin expansion. Tapestry tried to buy Capri (Michael Kors, Versace) for $8.5B in 2023; the FTC blocked it in 2024, and the stock rose on the news. It sold Stuart Weitzman in 2025.

Dividend raised annually; large buybacks funded by the cash it would have spent on Capri. It trades at a discount to European luxury because it is American and mid-priced — and it has outgrown most of them recently.

Shines when: Coach keeps growing with younger consumers, Kate Spade stabilizes, gross margin expands on full-price selling, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Fashion risk — brand heat fades. Consumer weakness. Tariffs on Asian production. Kate Spade underperformance. China.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). Coach revenue growth, gross margin, average unit retail, new-customer acquisition, full-year guidance.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±6–12%. The two-month setup is a Coach-driven beat. If stuck, a cash-generative brand at a value multiple — comfortable while Coach stays hot.

Automakers

TM Toyota Motor Corporation (ADR)

Sector: Automaker · Market cap: ~$250B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: ~3%

The largest automaker in the world by volume (about 10 million vehicles a year) and by a wide margin the most profitable — ~$300B of revenue, an operating margin no other mass-market carmaker approaches, and a net cash position that lets it self-fund everything. Toyota bet on hybrids while competitors bet on pure EVs, and won: hybrid demand surged in 2023–25 while EV adoption slowed, and Toyota could not build enough RAV4 and Camry hybrids. Also owns Lexus, Daihatsu, Hino, and stakes in Subaru, Mazda, and Suzuki.

The ADR gives a U.S. investor the world's best-run car company at 10x earnings with a 3% yield. The risks are Japanese: the yen (a weaker yen inflates translated profits; a stronger one deflates them), and U.S. tariffs on imported vehicles and parts, which hit Toyota's earnings directly in 2025. It is also the largest company in the group's cross-shareholding unwinding, which has produced large buybacks.

Shines when: Hybrid demand stays strong, tariff costs are absorbed or reduced, the yen is stable-to-weak, U.S. production expands, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: U.S. tariffs. Yen strength. Chinese EV competition in Asia and Europe. Certification scandals (Daihatsu, Hino). The auto cycle. EV transition risk if the market shifts back.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Operating profit and full-year guidance, hybrid mix, U.S. sales and incentives, yen assumptions, tariff impact commentary.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±3–6%; the ADR moves with the yen and tariff headlines. The two-month setup is a tariff-relief or yen-weakening catalyst. If stuck, the best automaker in the world at a value multiple — comfortable.

HMC Honda Motor Co. (ADR)

Sector: Automaker / Motorcycles · Market cap: ~$45B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: ~4%

The world's largest motorcycle maker (about 20 million a year, dominant across Southeast Asia and India — and by far its most profitable business) and a top-ten automaker (Civic, Accord, CR-V, Acura), ~$140B of revenue, plus power equipment and the HondaJet. Honda's cars are profitable in the U.S. and loss-making in China, where local EV makers have taken its share; it has been restructuring China and slowing its EV investment to focus on hybrids.

Merger talks with Nissan in late 2024 collapsed in early 2025 when Nissan refused to become a subsidiary; Honda walked away with its balance sheet intact and launched a large buyback. It trades at 10x earnings with a 4% yield — cheaper than Toyota because it is smaller, less profitable in autos, and more exposed to China.

Shines when: Motorcycle demand in Asia grows, U.S. hybrid sales strengthen, China losses narrow through restructuring, tariff costs ease, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: U.S. tariffs. China auto losses. Yen. EV strategy uncertainty. Nissan-style consolidation pressure returning. The auto cycle.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Motorcycle segment profit, automobile segment profit by region, full-year guidance, buyback pace, yen assumptions.

How to use it: Earnings reactions ±4–8%. Same yen and tariff setups as Toyota with more China risk. If stuck, a 4% yield backed by the motorcycle franchise — comfortable.

For the Curious: Popular Names Outside the Flywheel

The stocks that dominate retail flows, options volume, and financial media — and don't appear in the Atlas or anywhere else in this guide. Between the Atlas and the Ledger, this closes the gap: the names left out after this are small-caps, penny stocks, and the ones that won't exist in a year. Most fail the hold-it-for-a-year test; that's the point of reading about them. Each entry adds a line on why the stock is popular and ends with a flywheel verdict, usually pointing to the boring version elsewhere in the guide.

Fintech, brokerage & crypto

HOOD Robinhood Markets

Sector: Retail Brokerage / Fintech · Market cap: ~$100B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

Commission-free brokerage for stocks, options, and crypto with roughly 26–27 million funded customers and $3.5–4B-plus of revenue. It makes money three ways: transaction revenue (payment for order flow on options and equities, and crypto trading spreads — the largest and most volatile line), net interest income (on customer cash, margin loans, and securities lending — steady and rate-sensitive), and subscriptions (Robinhood Gold, several million members paying for higher cash yields, margin discounts, and a credit card). It has expanded aggressively: retirement accounts with matching, a credit card, futures, prediction markets (event contracts on elections and sports), tokenized stocks in Europe, wealth management, and the Bitstamp acquisition for institutional crypto. Added to the S&P 500 in 2025.

Why it's popular: It is the retail-trading generation's own stock — its customers are its shareholders — and its earnings are a leveraged bet on trading activity and crypto prices. The stock went from single digits in 2023 to over $100 in 2025.

Shines when: Retail trading volumes and options activity are high, crypto rallies, rates stay elevated for interest income, and new products add recurring revenue.

Dangers: Activity-driven revenue collapses in bear markets — revenue fell ~25% in 2022. Regulatory risk on payment for order flow, crypto, and prediction markets. Competition from Schwab, Coinbase, and banks moving into trading. Valuation.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct, plus monthly operating metrics (funded customers, net deposits, trading volumes by asset). Options and crypto volumes, Gold subscriber count, net interest income.

Flywheel verdict: Not a candidate — earnings reactions ±10–20% and a beta to retail sentiment and crypto that no boring stock has. Schwab (Atlas) and Interactive Brokers (financials section) are the hold-forever versions of the same business.

SOFI SoFi Technologies

Sector: Digital Bank · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~50x · Yield: none

A one-stop digital bank with ~12 million members and a national bank charter (2022) that lets it fund loans with deposits. Three segments: Lending (personal, student, and home loans — the majority of profit, with personal loans the core), Technology Platform (Galileo and Technisys, the card-processing and core-banking infrastructure it sells to other fintechs), and Financial Services (checking and savings, invest, credit card, and the "loan platform business" — originating loans on behalf of third-party buyers for a fee, a capital-light line that has become a major growth driver). GAAP profitable since late 2023.

Why it's popular: A retail-investor favorite since its 2021 SPAC, with member growth of 30%-plus and a narrative of becoming both a top-ten bank and the infrastructure layer for fintech.

Shines when: Member and product growth stay high, personal-loan credit holds, the loan-platform business scales, deposits grow cheaply, and rate cuts steepen the curve.

Dangers: Credit risk on unsecured personal loans in a downturn. A valuation many times book value for a bank. Rate sensitivity. Regulation of a bank that is also a fintech. Fair-value accounting on its loan book.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Member and product adds, net interest margin, personal-loan charge-offs and delinquencies, fee-based revenue share, tangible book value.

Flywheel verdict: Not boring — earnings reactions ±10–15% and a stock that trades on narrative. The super-regional banks in the financials section are the sleep-well versions.

NU Nu Holdings

Sector: Latin American Digital Bank · Market cap: ~$75B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: none

Nubank — the largest digital bank in Latin America, with over 120 million customers in Brazil, Mexico, and Colombia, offering credit cards, checking, personal loans, investments, and insurance through an app, at a cost per customer a fraction of incumbent banks'. Founded by David Vélez in 2013; Berkshire Hathaway was an early investor and later sold. Revenue ~$14B growing 30–40%, high-teens return on equity, profitable since 2023.

Why it's popular: A genuine hypergrowth bank with emerging-market demographics behind it — the stock most often cited as "the next great fintech."

Shines when: Customer growth continues, revenue per customer rises as products deepen, Mexico and Colombia scale, credit quality holds, and Brazilian rates fall.

Dangers: Brazilian macro, currency, and political risk. Credit cycles in unsecured lending. Regulatory changes. Competition from incumbents and other neobanks. Valuation for a bank.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Customer adds, monthly average revenue per active customer, net interest margin, 90-day delinquency, Mexico deposits.

Flywheel verdict: No — but a name worth understanding if you ever build an international sleeve.

TOST Toast

Sector: Restaurant Software & Payments · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

A point-of-sale and operations platform built specifically for restaurants: ordering, kitchen display, payroll, online ordering, loyalty, and payments processing, sold as a bundle. Around 150,000 locations, revenue ~$6B (most of it pass-through payments volume; gross profit is the number to watch), GAAP profitable since 2024. Expanding into retail, enterprise chains, and international.

Why it's popular: The "software eats restaurants" story with visible adoption — you see the terminals everywhere — and consistent location growth.

Shines when: Location adds stay high, take rate and software attach grow, enterprise wins land, and margins scale.

Dangers: Restaurant cyclicality and closures. Square, Clover (Fiserv), Lightspeed, and Shift4. Take-rate pressure. Valuation.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Net location adds, annual recurring run-rate, gross payment volume, adjusted EBITDA.

Flywheel verdict: No — Fiserv (boring tech) is its main rival at a value multiple.

AFRM Affirm

Sector: Buy Now, Pay Later · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~60x · Yield: none

Point-of-sale installment lending — "pay in four" and longer interest-bearing loans — embedded at Amazon, Shopify, Apple Pay, Walmart, and thousands of merchants, plus the Affirm Card. Founded by Max Levchin (PayPal co-founder). Gross merchandise volume $40B-plus annually, revenue $3B-plus, GAAP profitable since 2025. It funds loans through bank partnerships, forward-flow agreements with asset managers, and securitizations.

Why it's popular: The BNPL category leader with brand-name partnerships, high growth, and a stock that moves violently on rate expectations and funding-market news.

Shines when: GMV grows, merchant partnerships expand, funding costs fall, credit performance holds, and the card gains adoption.

Dangers: Credit risk on unsecured consumer loans. Funding-market dependence. Regulation of BNPL as credit. Klarna, Afterpay (Block), PayPal, and Apple. Rate sensitivity.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). GMV growth, revenue less transaction costs, delinquencies, funding capacity, active consumers.

Flywheel verdict: No.

MSTR Strategy (MicroStrategy)

Sector: Bitcoin Treasury · Market cap: ~$100B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none (its preferreds yield 8–10%)

Once a mid-sized business-intelligence software company, now the world's largest corporate holder of bitcoin — well over 600,000 coins — funded by selling common stock at the market, convertible notes, and a family of perpetual preferred stocks (STRK, STRF, STRD, STRC) that pay high fixed dividends. The software business still exists but is a rounding error. Michael Saylor's thesis: the company can raise capital at a premium to its bitcoin net asset value, buy more bitcoin, and increase "bitcoin per share." The stock trades as a leveraged, premium-priced bitcoin proxy.

Why it's popular: Bitcoin with extra beta, buyable in any brokerage account with enormous options volume, and Saylor is a relentless promoter. Dozens of copycat "treasury companies" followed.

Shines when: Bitcoin rises, the premium to net asset value expands, and capital markets stay open for equity and preferred issuance.

Dangers: A bitcoin drawdown hits the stock harder than the coin. The premium can collapse toward or below net asset value. Preferred dividends are a fixed cash obligation with no operating cash flow behind them. Dilution. Copycats competing for the same capital.

Watch: Bitcoin price, mNAV (market cap divided by bitcoin holdings), weekly purchase filings, preferred issuance. Reports early Feb/May/Aug/late Oct.

Flywheel verdict: Not a stock in the usual sense — a bitcoin derivative wrapped in a corporate structure. A spot bitcoin ETF is the clean way to own the coin.

AI infrastructure

SMCI Super Micro Computer

Sector: AI Servers · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: none

An AI server assembler that competes with Dell and HPE, building GPU servers and liquid-cooled racks in San Jose, Taiwan, and Malaysia — $22–25B of revenue, most from a handful of AI customers. It became a 2024 retail obsession as the fastest way to own Nvidia demand, then a cautionary tale: a short-seller report, its auditor EY resigning over governance concerns, delayed 10-K filings, a near-delisting, and a Justice Department inquiry. It eventually caught up on filings and kept its listing, but the stock fell 80% from its peak and remains a hyper-volatile vehicle.

Why it's popular: Pure AI-server exposure, wild swings, and options volume. It behaves like a leveraged Nvidia tracker with its own governance drama.

Shines when: AI server demand outruns supply, gross margins hold above 10%, new GPU platforms ramp, and no new accounting surprises appear.

Dangers: Governance — an auditor resignation is not a normal event. Gross margins in the single digits to low teens. Customer concentration. Dell, HPE, and Taiwanese ODMs going direct. Guidance whiplash.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). Gross margin, guidance revisions, filing timeliness, customer concentration.

Flywheel verdict: No. Dell in the boring-tech section is the version with the same demand and an audited balance sheet.

CRWV CoreWeave

Sector: AI Cloud Infrastructure · Market cap: ~$60B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

A GPU cloud: it borrows billions, buys Nvidia GPUs, builds or leases data centers, and rents the clusters to AI labs and hyperscalers under multi-year contracts — Microsoft, OpenAI, Meta, and others, with a contracted backlog in the tens of billions. IPO'd in March 2025 at $40, ran past $180, then fell by half as lockups expired and debt costs drew scrutiny. Revenue growing several-fold year over year, with heavy losses driven by depreciation and interest. It started as an Ethereum mining operation.

Why it's popular: The purest public play on AI compute demand, with a business model that is essentially "borrow to buy GPUs." Enormous options volume and 10% days are routine.

Shines when: AI compute stays supply-constrained, contracts convert to revenue on schedule, debt refinances cheaply, and customer concentration diversifies.

Dangers: Leverage — tens of billions of debt secured against GPUs that depreciate fast. Customer concentration in Microsoft and OpenAI. GPU pricing falling as supply catches up. Lockup and secondary-sale overhang. Nvidia is both supplier and shareholder.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Backlog, capex, interest expense, customer concentration, contract renewals, debt raises.

Flywheel verdict: No — the most leveraged bet on AI capex in the market.

VRT Vertiv

Sector: Data Center Power & Cooling · Market cap: ~$70B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: ~0.1%

Makes the equipment that keeps data centers running: uninterruptible power supplies, power distribution units, switchgear, precision air cooling, and — critically for AI — liquid cooling for GPU racks that air can't cool. About $10B of revenue. It was Emerson's Network Power division, sold to Platinum Equity in 2016 and taken public via SPAC in 2020, and became one of the defining AI-infrastructure stocks of 2023–25, rising more than tenfold. Backlog exceeds a year of revenue; margins have expanded from single digits toward 20%.

Why it's popular: The picks-and-shovels play on AI data centers that isn't a chipmaker — every GPU needs power and cooling regardless of which chip wins.

Shines when: Data-center capex grows, liquid-cooling adoption accelerates, orders and backlog rise, and operating margin keeps climbing.

Dangers: Hyperscaler capex cyclicality — orders can pause abruptly. A valuation that assumes years of growth. Schneider Electric, Eaton, and Asian cooling vendors. Tariff and supply-chain exposure.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Orders growth, backlog, adjusted operating margin, liquid-cooling commentary, hyperscaler capex guidance.

Flywheel verdict: The closest thing in this section to a flywheel name — real earnings, real cash flow, a genuine industrial. But earnings reactions ±10–15% and a 40x multiple mean the stuck scenario is a 40% drawdown. Eaton (Atlas) is the boring version.

ANET Arista Networks

Sector: Data Center Networking · Market cap: ~$160B · P/E: ~45x · Yield: none

Arista builds high-speed Ethernet switches and the EOS network operating system that hyperscalers use to connect servers — Meta and Microsoft have each been 15–20% of revenue — and has become the leading merchant vendor for AI back-end networks, where clusters of tens of thousands of GPUs need lossless, ultra-low-latency connections. $9–10B of revenue growing 20–30%, gross margins in the 60s, no debt, and a founder (Andy Bechtolsheim) and CEO (Jayshree Ullal) who took share from Cisco for fifteen years. Expanding into campus and enterprise networking.

Why it's popular: The AI networking pure play — the debate over Ethernet versus Nvidia's InfiniBand for AI clusters made Arista the Ethernet champion.

Shines when: Hyperscaler capex grows, AI back-end networking hits its multibillion-dollar targets, gross margin holds, and campus wins broaden the customer base.

Dangers: Customer concentration in Meta and Microsoft. Nvidia's Spectrum-X Ethernet push and white-box switching from ODMs. Gross-margin pressure from hyperscaler pricing. Valuation.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/late Jul/Nov. Cloud-titan revenue, AI networking revenue, gross margin, deferred revenue, campus growth.

Flywheel verdict: A high-quality business at a growth multiple; earnings reactions ±8–15%. Cisco (Atlas) is the boring version; HPE post-Juniper is the cheap version.

Power & nuclear

GEV GE Vernova

Sector: Power Equipment · Market cap: ~$170B · P/E: ~50x · Yield: ~0.2%

General Electric's energy business, spun off in April 2024. Three segments: Power (gas turbines — sold out for years as data centers and utilities scramble for new generation; also nuclear services and a small modular reactor design), Electrification (transformers, switchgear, grid software — equipment for a grid that hasn't been upgraded in decades), and Wind (onshore turbines that make money, offshore turbines that lost billions). $35–40B of revenue with a backlog over $120B. The stock rose several-fold from the spin as gas-turbine demand exploded on AI power needs.

Why it's popular: The "AI needs electricity" trade in one ticker, with gas turbines as the scarcest asset in the energy build-out.

Shines when: Gas turbine orders and slot reservations keep filling, Electrification margins expand, offshore wind losses end, and pricing power holds.

Dangers: Valuation — 50x earnings for an industrial. Wind write-downs. Execution risk expanding turbine capacity. Policy shifts on renewables. A capex cycle that could peak.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Gas turbine orders and backlog, Electrification orders and margin, wind segment losses, free cash flow.

Flywheel verdict: A real, cash-generating industrial with a momentum-stock multiple; earnings reactions ±8–15%. Emerson and Eaton (Atlas) are the boring versions — there is no sane-multiple way to own gas turbines.

CEG Constellation Energy

Sector: Nuclear Power Generation · Market cap: ~$100B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~0.5%

The largest nuclear operator in the U.S. — about 20 reactors across Illinois, Pennsylvania, New York, and Maryland (Calvert Cliffs is the local one) — spun out of Exelon in 2022. It sells carbon-free electricity into wholesale markets and directly to large customers; the 2024 deal to restart Three Mile Island Unit 1 for Microsoft under a 20-year contract made it the face of the "AI needs nuclear" trade. It acquired Calpine (the largest gas and geothermal fleet in the U.S.) in a ~$27B deal to become the country's largest clean-and-reliable power producer. Federal nuclear production tax credits set a floor under its revenue.

Why it's popular: Nuclear was a dead sector until data-center demand and federal support revived it; Constellation is the only large-scale way to own it, and the stock rose fivefold.

Shines when: Power prices rise, hyperscaler contracts are signed at premium rates, Calpine integration delivers, and uprates and restarts add capacity.

Dangers: Power-price sensitivity in deregulated markets (PJM capacity auctions swing results). Political backlash on data centers driving up consumer bills. Calpine integration and leverage. A multiple that assumes contract growth continues.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Forward power prices, PJM capacity auction results, new hyperscaler contracts, Calpine synergy progress.

Flywheel verdict: A utility-adjacent business with a growth multiple and ±8–12% earnings reactions. Duke and Southern (Atlas) and Dominion (utilities section) are the boring versions.

VST Vistra

Sector: Independent Power Producer · Market cap: ~$70B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: ~0.5%

Texas-based owner of gas, coal, nuclear (Comanche Peak, plus four reactors bought from Energy Harbor in 2024), and battery-storage plants, selling power into deregulated markets — the largest competitive generator in the U.S. by capacity. Unlike a utility, its profits rise when power prices rise, which is exactly what data-center demand has done. It signed a 20-year nuclear supply deal with Meta and is pursuing data-center co-location at its plants. Heavy buybacks.

Why it's popular: One of the best-performing S&P 500 stocks of 2024, up more than 250%, as the market realized who would sell electricity to the AI build-out.

Shines when: Power prices and forward curves rise, capacity auctions clear high, co-location deals are signed, and the nuclear fleet captures federal credits.

Dangers: Power-price sensitivity — the leverage cuts both ways. Regulatory risk on co-location (FERC). Texas grid politics. Coal exposure. A run that may have priced the story.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Forward power prices (ERCOT, PJM), hedged margin, data-center deal announcements, buybacks.

Flywheel verdict: Same as Constellation — real cash flow, momentum-stock behavior.

OKLO Oklo

Sector: Advanced Nuclear (pre-revenue) · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

A developer of small "fast" fission reactors (the Aurora powerhouse, ~75 MW) intended to be built quickly and sold as power-purchase agreements to data centers and industrial users, with a first unit targeted at the Idaho National Laboratory in the late 2020s. Went public via a SPAC backed by Sam Altman (who chaired the board until stepping down in 2025) and has essentially no revenue — its value is the licensing pathway, a large pipeline of non-binding customer letters of intent, and federal enthusiasm for nuclear.

Why it's popular: Nuclear plus AI plus Altman; the stock has moved 20% in a day on executive orders and Department of Energy announcements.

Shines when: NRC licensing milestones land, DOE pilot programs advance, binding customer agreements replace letters of intent, and the first reactor's construction starts.

Dangers: No revenue, no licensed design, a decade of regulatory and construction risk, and heavy dilution ahead. The valuation is entirely narrative.

Watch: NRC and DOE announcements, capital raises, INL construction timeline. It reports quarterly, but the numbers are immaterial.

Flywheel verdict: No. Constellation is the version with reactors that exist.

Space, quantum & moonshots

RKLB Rocket Lab

Sector: Space Launch & Systems · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: n/m (unprofitable) · Yield: none

The closest thing to a public SpaceX. Two businesses: Launch (Electron, the most frequently launched small rocket outside SpaceX at roughly $8M per mission; and Neutron, a reusable medium-lift rocket designed to compete for the constellation and national-security launches SpaceX dominates, with first flight targeted around 2026) and Space Systems (~70% of revenue — satellite components like solar cells, reaction wheels, and star trackers sold to other manufacturers, plus complete spacecraft built for customers like the Space Development Agency). Acquisitions of Mynaric (laser communications) and Geost (payloads) push it further into defense. Revenue roughly $600–800M, losing money as Neutron development consumes cash.

Why it's popular: Space is a retail passion, Peter Beck is a credible founder, and Neutron's milestones give the stock scheduled catalysts. It rose roughly tenfold from its 2024 lows.

Shines when: Neutron flies successfully, launch cadence rises, Space Systems wins large constellation and defense contracts, and margins improve.

Dangers: Neutron delays or a failure — rockets explode. Competing with SpaceX on price. Regular dilution. A valuation on revenue, not earnings. Both segments are lumpy.

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Neutron milestones, backlog, launch count, Space Systems bookings, cash burn.

Flywheel verdict: The reason this section exists — it's the exact opposite of the flywheel brief. Learn it, watch Neutron, don't put sleeve money in it.

IONQ IonQ

Sector: Quantum Computing · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

The most prominent public quantum-computing company, building trapped-ion quantum computers sold through cloud platforms (AWS, Azure, Google Cloud) and directly to governments and research institutions. Revenue is small (well under $500M) and growing partly through acquisitions — Oxford Ionics (2025, for ion-trap chip technology), ID Quantique (quantum networking and security), Lightsynq, and others — funded by frequent equity raises. Losses are large. The roadmap targets fault-tolerant machines around 2030.

Why it's popular: Quantum is the next "next big thing," Nvidia's CEO moved the stock 30% by commenting on timelines, and there are few ways to own the theme.

Shines when: Technical milestones (qubit count, error rates) beat the roadmap, government contracts land, and the sector re-rates on any large-company quantum announcement.

Dangers: Commercial quantum advantage is years away; revenue doesn't support the valuation; dilution is constant; Google, IBM, and Microsoft are competitors with far deeper pockets.

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Bookings, technical roadmap updates, cash position and raises, acquisitions.

Flywheel verdict: No.

ASTS AST SpaceMobile

Sector: Satellite Direct-to-Cell · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

Building a constellation of very large satellites (BlueBird, with antennas the size of a tennis court) designed to connect directly to ordinary smartphones, filling coverage gaps for carrier partners — AT&T, Verizon, Vodafone, Rakuten, and others — who share revenue rather than sell handsets. Five satellites launched in 2024; the next-generation, much larger BlueBird satellites are the ones needed for continuous service. Pre-revenue in any meaningful sense, with large capital needs.

Why it's popular: The "space cell tower" story, carrier partnerships that lend credibility, and a stock that has moved 50% in weeks on launch news.

Shines when: Launches succeed, carrier agreements convert to revenue, spectrum deals close, and coverage becomes commercial.

Dangers: Launch and satellite failures. Competition from SpaceX's Starlink direct-to-cell service with T-Mobile. Enormous capital needs and dilution. Regulatory timelines.

Watch: Launch schedule, satellite commissioning, carrier contract announcements, cash and raises. Reports mid Mar/May/Aug/Nov.

Flywheel verdict: No.

Consumer & internet

APP AppLovin

Sector: Ad Tech · Market cap: ~$170B · P/E: ~45x · Yield: none

An advertising engine for mobile games — its AXON machine-learning system decides which ads to show to which players in which apps and takes a cut of every install it generates. It sold its own game studios in 2025 to become a pure ad-tech company, then began extending the engine to e-commerce advertisers with striking early results. Revenue $5–6B growing 40%-plus, EBITDA margins above 70%, and enormous free cash flow — arguably the most profitable ad-tech company ever built. Founded by Adam Foroughi; added to the S&P 500 in 2025.

Why it's popular: The best-performing large-cap stock of 2024 (up roughly 700%), a momentum favorite, and the subject of multiple short-seller reports alleging aggressive data collection and attribution practices — which it denied and the stock shrugged off.

Shines when: Ad revenue growth stays above 40%, e-commerce advertisers scale, margins hold, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Short-seller allegations and any regulatory follow-through. Platform dependence on Apple and Google policy. Concentration in mobile gaming. A valuation that prices continued hypergrowth.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Advertising revenue growth, e-commerce ad revenue, adjusted EBITDA margin, free cash flow, buybacks.

Flywheel verdict: Not boring — earnings reactions ±15–25%. Learn it as a case study in how a niche ad engine became a mega-cap.

RDDT Reddit

Sector: Social Media / Advertising · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~50x · Yield: none

The forum of everything, public since March 2024. Revenue ~$2B growing 50%-plus, split between advertising (the majority — ads placed in communities with intent-rich context, plus a growing self-serve business) and data licensing (deals with Google and OpenAI to train models on Reddit content, which also drove the surge of Reddit results in Google Search). GAAP profitable since 2024. Daily active users over 100 million, with much of the growth coming from logged-out search traffic.

Why it's popular: A household brand at a small size relative to Meta, a data-licensing angle unique among social platforms, and a stock that doubled and halved repeatedly in its first two years.

Shines when: User growth continues, ad revenue per user rises toward peers, international expands via machine translation, and data licensing renews at higher rates.

Dangers: Dependence on Google search traffic — algorithm changes have hit it before. AI answer engines reducing clicks to forums. Advertiser concentration. Valuation.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Daily active uniques (logged-in vs. logged-out), ad revenue growth, ARPU, data-licensing revenue.

Flywheel verdict: No — earnings reactions ±15–25%.

DASH DoorDash

Sector: Delivery / Local Commerce · Market cap: ~$110B · P/E: ~60x · Yield: none

The dominant U.S. food-delivery platform (roughly two-thirds of the market), expanding into grocery, convenience, retail, and advertising, and internationally through Wolt (Europe) and the 2025 acquisition of Deliveroo (UK). Over $80B of annual gross order value, revenue $12B-plus, GAAP profitable since 2024, with DashPass subscriptions and a fast-growing ads business improving margins. Also bought SevenRooms (restaurant reservations) and Symbiosys (ad tech).

Why it's popular: A category winner with a long runway into new verticals, and a stock that compounded straight through the post-pandemic "delivery is dead" period.

Shines when: Order frequency rises, grocery and new verticals scale, ad revenue grows, international breaks even, and take rate holds.

Dangers: Gig-worker regulation and labor costs. Uber Eats and Instacart. Restaurant fee pushback. A valuation on future margin, not current.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Total orders, gross order value, contribution margin, adjusted EBITDA, DashPass growth.

Flywheel verdict: No — but among the most legitimate businesses in this section.

HIMS Hims & Hers Health

Sector: Telehealth · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

A direct-to-consumer telehealth platform selling subscription treatments for hair loss, erectile dysfunction, mental health, dermatology, and — the business that made it a market obsession — weight loss. Over 2 million subscribers and $2B-plus of revenue. In 2024–25 it sold compounded GLP-1 injections at a fraction of branded prices during the FDA shortage, then had to unwind that when the shortage ended; a partnership with Novo Nordisk to sell branded Wegovy collapsed within weeks amid accusations over compounding. It continues to sell personalized compounded formulations and has expanded into hormone therapy, testosterone, and lab testing.

Why it's popular: The weight-loss gold rush, a subscription model retail investors understand, and a stock that regularly moves 20–30% on FDA and Novo headlines.

Shines when: Subscriber growth stays high, non-weight-loss categories grow, personalized compounding survives regulatory scrutiny, and margins expand.

Dangers: Regulatory — the FDA and drugmakers dispute the legality of mass-compounded GLP-1s. Novo and Lilly's own direct-to-consumer channels. Customer acquisition costs. Headline volatility.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Subscriber count, revenue excluding GLP-1s, gross margin, marketing spend, regulatory news.

Flywheel verdict: No.

CVNA Carvana

Sector: Online Used-Car Retail · Market cap: ~$70B · P/E: ~50x · Yield: none

Sells used cars online with home delivery and the car-vending-machine gimmick; also runs a wholesale auction business (ADESA, bought in 2022) and finances most of its sales, selling the loans to investors. It nearly went bankrupt in 2022 — over-expansion, ADESA debt, and a collapsing used-car market took the stock to $4 — then restructured its debt, cut costs, and became the fastest-growing, most profitable used-car retailer, with units growing 30–40% and industry-leading unit economics. The Garcia family controls it; a 2025 Hindenburg report questioned loan sales to affiliated parties.

Why it's popular: One of the great stock comebacks of the decade (from $4 to over $400), and a permanent battleground between believers in its logistics network and skeptics of its accounting and leverage.

Shines when: Unit growth stays high, gross profit per unit expands, financing markets stay open for its loan sales, and debt refinances cheaply.

Dangers: Leverage. Used-car price swings. Credit quality of a subprime-heavy loan book. Related-party questions. A valuation that assumes it becomes the largest auto retailer in America.

Watch: Reports late Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Retail units, gross profit per unit, adjusted EBITDA margin, loan-sale terms, leverage.

Flywheel verdict: No. AutoZone and O'Reilly in the consumer section are the boring auto-retail versions.

BABA Alibaba

Sector: Chinese E-commerce & Cloud · Market cap: ~$350B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~1%

China's largest e-commerce company (Taobao, Tmall), largest cloud provider (Alibaba Cloud, whose Qwen family of open-source AI models became among the most used in the world), plus international commerce (AliExpress, Lazada, Trendyol), logistics (Cainiao), local services, and a stake in Ant Group. Revenue $140B-plus. After the 2020–23 regulatory crackdown crushed the stock from $300 to $60, Beijing's stance softened, the company restructured, and a 2025 AI re-rating (Qwen, a huge cloud capex plan) revived it. Massive buybacks and a dividend since 2024.

Why it's popular: The cheapest large-cap AI-and-cloud story in the world by multiple, and the proxy for "China is investable again."

Shines when: Chinese consumption recovers, cloud growth reaccelerates on AI demand, Qwen adoption builds ecosystem value, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: China regulatory and geopolitical risk — delisting threats, tariffs, the VIE structure. Domestic competition from PDD, JD, and Douyin. Consumer weakness. Capex burden. Currency.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Customer-management revenue (Taobao/Tmall ads), cloud growth, international losses, buyback pace, China policy.

Flywheel verdict: Not boring — but at 18x earnings with buybacks it is the closest thing to a value hold in this section. MCHI and KWEB (Atlas) are the diversified versions.

AXON Axon Enterprise

Sector: Public Safety Technology · Market cap: ~$55B · P/E: ~90x · Yield: none

The company formerly known as Taser: conducted-energy weapons, body cameras, in-car video, drones, and — the real business — Axon Cloud, the evidence-management, records, and AI software that police departments subscribe to for the life of the hardware. $2.5–3B of revenue growing 25–30%, with a subscription base that makes it look like a software company. Draft One, an AI report-writing tool, was adopted unusually fast. Expanding into enterprise security, federal, and international.

Why it's popular: A near-monopoly in body cameras with a software-like model, consistent beats, and an aggressive stock-based CEO pay plan that Wall Street tolerates because the stock keeps going up.

Shines when: Cloud ARR grows, new products (drones, Draft One, counter-drone) adopt, international and federal expand, and margins expand.

Dangers: Valuation — 90x earnings. Stock-based compensation dilution. Government budget cycles. Motorola Solutions in software and video. Taser controversy.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Annual recurring revenue, net revenue retention, bookings, adjusted EBITDA margin.

Flywheel verdict: No — Motorola Solutions (boring tech) is the version at 30x.

AI infrastructure — optics, interconnect & the picks and shovels

LITE Lumentum Holdings

Sector: Optical Components · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

Makes the lasers and optical components inside the transceivers that connect GPUs to each other and to the network — the EML and CW lasers that every 800-gigabit and 1.6-terabit link needs — plus optical circuit switches (a new product hyperscalers use to reconfigure AI clusters), telecom transport, and industrial lasers. Spun out of JDSU in 2015; bought Oclaro, NeoPhotonics, and Cloud Light. Revenue ~$2B and growing fast after years of telecom-driven stagnation.

Why it's popular: AI clusters are bottlenecked by optical bandwidth, laser supply is tight, and Lumentum's stock rose several-fold in 2025 as it sold every laser it could make.

Shines when: Hyperscaler capex grows, laser capacity expansions sell out, optical circuit switches ramp, and telecom recovers.

Dangers: Hyperscaler capex cycles. Competition from Coherent, Broadcom, and Chinese laser makers. Customer concentration. Technology transitions (co-packaged optics could change the supply chain). A valuation built on a supply squeeze.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). Cloud and networking revenue, laser capacity, gross margin, hyperscaler design wins.

Flywheel verdict: No — a capex-cycle stock with ±15–25% earnings reactions. Corning (boring tech) is the boring version of the optical story.

COHR Coherent Corp.

Sector: Optical Components / Lasers · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: none

The largest maker of the optical transceivers that plug into AI switches and servers (it competes with Innolight and Eoptolink), plus indium-phosphide lasers, silicon-carbide substrates for power electronics, and industrial and precision lasers — the 2022 merger of II-VI and Coherent. Revenue ~$6B. A new CEO (Jim Anderson, from Lattice) arrived in 2024, sold the aerospace and defense unit, and refocused on data-center optics and margin.

Why it's popular: The transceiver volume leader in the AI build-out, with a turnaround narrative on top.

Shines when: 800G and 1.6T transceiver volumes grow, margins expand toward targets, silicon carbide recovers, and debt from the merger is paid down.

Dangers: Chinese transceiver competitors and tariffs. Hyperscaler capex cycles. Leverage. Technology shifts (co-packaged optics). Execution on the portfolio simplification.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). Datacom revenue growth, gross margin, net leverage, 1.6T ramp commentary.

Flywheel verdict: No.

CLS Celestica

Sector: Electronics Manufacturing / Networking · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: none

A Toronto-based contract manufacturer that reinvented itself as the builder of hyperscalers' own-design networking switches (800-gigabit Ethernet switches for Google, Amazon, Meta) and AI servers, alongside legacy work in aerospace, industrial, and healthcare electronics. Revenue ~$12B. The Connectivity and Cloud Solutions segment went from a sideline to the whole story as hyperscalers moved away from Cisco and Arista toward white-box switches Celestica builds.

Why it's popular: The stock rose more than tenfold from 2023 — the best-performing EMS company in history — on hyperscaler switch volumes.

Shines when: Hyperscaler networking capex grows, 1.6T switch programs ramp, AI server wins add, and margins hold.

Dangers: Customer concentration (a few hyperscalers). Thin EMS margins. Capex cycles. Competition from Taiwanese ODMs. A multiple that assumes continued hypergrowth.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. CCS segment revenue and margin, hyperscaler concentration, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No — Jabil and Flex (boring tech) are the versions with diversified end markets.

ALAB Astera Labs

Sector: AI Connectivity Semiconductors · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~80x · Yield: none

Designs the connectivity chips inside AI servers — PCIe and CXL retimers (which keep high-speed signals clean over longer distances), Scorpio fabric switches for connecting GPUs within a rack, and Ethernet-based scale-out products — sold to Nvidia-based and custom-silicon rack builders. IPO'd in March 2024 and became a standard-bearer for the AI semiconductor trade. Revenue under $1B, growing over 100% a year, profitable.

Why it's popular: A pure play on AI-rack connectivity with hypergrowth and a fabless model.

Shines when: AI server volumes grow, Scorpio switches ramp into rack-scale designs, and it wins slots in next-generation GPU platforms.

Dangers: Nvidia or Broadcom integrating its functions. Customer concentration. Design-win cycles. Extreme valuation.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Revenue growth, product mix (Scorpio vs. retimers), gross margin, guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No.

CRDO Credo Technology Group

Sector: AI Connectivity · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~60x · Yield: none

Makes active electrical cables (AECs) — copper cables with built-in signal-boosting chips that connect servers and switches inside an AI data center more cheaply and reliably than optics over short distances — plus optical DSPs and SerDes IP. Cayman-domiciled, based in San Jose. Revenue grew several-fold in fiscal 2025 as Amazon, Microsoft, and xAI standardized on AECs for rack-to-rack links.

Why it's popular: One of the fastest-growing chip companies in the AI build-out, with the stock rising tenfold from 2024.

Shines when: AEC adoption spreads to more hyperscalers, new rack architectures use more cables per GPU, and optical DSPs add a second growth line.

Dangers: Customer concentration (Amazon was over half of revenue). Competition from Marvell, Broadcom, and cable makers. Technology substitution. Valuation.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends April). Revenue growth, customer diversification, gross margin, guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No.

SNDK Sandisk Corporation

Sector: NAND Flash Memory · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~15x forward · Yield: none

The flash-memory business Western Digital spun off in February 2025 — one of the four large NAND producers (with Samsung, SK Hynix/Solidigm, and Micron; Kioxia is its manufacturing partner through a joint venture in Japan). It sells SSDs and flash for phones, PCs, data centers, and consumer devices; enterprise SSDs for AI servers are the growth line. NAND is brutally cyclical: prices halve and double on multi-year supply cycles.

Why it's popular: A textbook cycle trade — the stock rose from around $30 at the spin to several hundred dollars within the year as NAND prices turned up on AI storage demand and producer capex discipline.

Shines when: NAND prices rise, enterprise SSD mix grows, the Kioxia joint venture keeps costs low, and supply stays disciplined.

Dangers: The NAND cycle — the next downturn will be as violent as the upturn. Chinese producers (YMTC) adding supply. Capex intensity. Customer concentration in consumer devices.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). NAND contract pricing, bit shipments, gross margin, enterprise SSD revenue, capex guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No — even less boring than Seagate and Western Digital, which at least have a duopoly.

NBIS Nebius Group

Sector: AI Cloud Infrastructure · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

What remained of Yandex after the Russian assets were sold in 2024: a Dutch-domiciled AI cloud company building GPU data centers in Finland, Paris, Iceland, New Jersey, and Kansas City, renting capacity to AI labs and enterprises — plus stakes in ClickHouse and Toloka and the Avride autonomous-vehicle unit. Nvidia is an investor. A September 2025 contract with Microsoft worth up to ~$17.4B over five years turned it from a curiosity into a CoreWeave rival overnight.

Why it's popular: A "second CoreWeave" with a cleaner balance sheet and a Microsoft anchor, trading at a fraction of CoreWeave's size.

Shines when: Capacity comes online on schedule, new hyperscaler contracts land, GPU utilization stays high, and financing stays available.

Dangers: Capital intensity and dilution (it raises equity and converts regularly). Customer concentration. GPU depreciation. Competition from hyperscalers and CoreWeave. Execution on rapid buildouts.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Contracted capacity (megawatts), ARR guidance, capex, cash and financing, Microsoft ramp.

Flywheel verdict: No.

IREN IREN Limited

Sector: Bitcoin Mining / AI Cloud · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

An Australian bitcoin miner (formerly Iris Energy) with renewable-powered sites in British Columbia and Texas that pivoted, like several miners, to renting its power and data-center capacity for AI: GPU cloud services and, in November 2025, a ~$9.7B five-year contract to supply Microsoft with Nvidia GPU capacity from its Childress, Texas campus. The pivot re-rated the stock from a crypto derivative to an AI-infrastructure name. Peers making the same move include Cipher, TeraWulf, Applied Digital, and Core Scientific.

Why it's popular: The miner-to-AI pivot is the highest-beta version of the data-center trade, and IREN landed the biggest contract in the group.

Shines when: AI contracts ramp on schedule, more capacity is signed, bitcoin stays high enough that mining funds the transition, and financing costs fall.

Dangers: Execution on a business it has never run at scale. Convertible-debt and equity dilution. Bitcoin price. Customer concentration. GPU depreciation.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends June). AI cloud revenue and contracted capacity, bitcoin mined and cost per coin, capex, financing.

Flywheel verdict: No.

FIX Comfort Systems USA

Sector: Mechanical & Electrical Contracting · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~0.2%

The contractor that installs the HVAC, plumbing, and electrical systems in data centers, semiconductor fabs, and industrial plants — ~$8B of revenue, a backlog of over $8B, and a modular-construction business that prefabricates mechanical systems off-site. A Houston-based roll-up of regional contractors that spent two decades as a sleepy small-cap, then became one of the best-performing industrial stocks of the AI era as data-center construction exploded. EMCOR is its larger peer.

Why it's popular: The purest way to own data-center construction without owning a chip — and the stock rose roughly tenfold from 2022.

Shines when: Data-center and industrial construction grows, backlog converts at high margins, modular capacity expands, and acquisitions add.

Dangers: Construction cyclicality. Data-center capex pauses. Labor availability. Project execution risk on fixed-price work. A multiple that assumes the boom continues.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Backlog, same-store revenue growth, gross margin, technology-sector revenue share.

Flywheel verdict: Closer than most in this section — it is a real, profitable business — but the multiple and the capex sensitivity make it a momentum stock. Trane and Johnson Controls (industrials) are the boring versions.

Power & nuclear — the second wave

TLN Talen Energy

Sector: Independent Power Producer · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: ~25x · Yield: none

A Pennsylvania power producer that emerged from bankruptcy in 2023 owning the Susquehanna nuclear plant (2.5 gigawatts) and a fleet of gas plants in PJM — and then signed the deal that started the data-center power trade: a 2024 agreement to sell nuclear power directly to Amazon's data center built on its own site, expanded in 2025 to nearly 2 gigawatts. It bought two more gas plants in 2025 to add capacity.

Why it's popular: The original "sell nuclear to a hyperscaler" story, with a stock up roughly tenfold since relisting.

Shines when: PJM power prices and capacity auctions rise, the Amazon contract ramps, more co-location deals are signed, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Power-price sensitivity. FERC and state rulings on co-location. Nuclear operating risk on a single plant. Leverage. A small float with a large run behind it.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. PJM forward prices and capacity auction results, hedged margin, data-center contract updates, buybacks.

Flywheel verdict: No — same logic as Constellation and Vistra, with more concentration.

NRG NRG Energy

Sector: Power Generation / Retail Energy · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1%

A Houston-based power company with two halves: a large retail electricity and natural-gas business (NRG, Reliant, Direct Energy, Green Mountain — serving millions of homes in Texas and the Northeast) and a generation fleet that it expanded sharply in 2025 by buying ~13 gigawatts of gas plants from LS Power for ~$12B, becoming one of the largest gas generators in PJM and Texas. It also owns Vivint Smart Home (acquired 2023, a bet on the "connected home" that the market never loved).

Why it's popular: The gas-generation version of the power trade — as data centers need firm power, NRG's plants and its Texas retail franchise both benefit — and the stock more than doubled in 2025.

Shines when: Power prices and capacity auctions rise, the LS Power plants integrate, retail margins hold, data-center supply deals are signed, and buybacks continue.

Dangers: Power-price sensitivity. Leverage from the LS Power deal. Texas grid politics. Retail margin compression. Vivint underperformance.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Adjusted EBITDA by segment, forward power prices, integration progress, free cash flow, buybacks.

Flywheel verdict: No.

BE Bloom Energy

Sector: Fuel Cells / On-Site Power · Market cap: ~$20B · P/E: ~100x · Yield: none

Makes solid-oxide fuel cells — refrigerator-sized boxes that convert natural gas (or hydrogen) into electricity on-site without combustion — sold to data centers, hospitals, and factories that can't wait years for a grid connection. After a decade as a money-losing clean-tech story, Bloom found its market in 2024–25: AEP ordered a gigawatt for data centers, Oracle and Equinix signed deployments, and Brookfield committed up to $5B to fund Bloom-powered AI facilities. Revenue ~$1.5–2B, recently profitable.

Why it's popular: "Behind-the-meter" power is the fastest way to energize a data center, and Bloom is the only scaled fuel-cell supplier. The stock rose roughly tenfold from its 2024 lows.

Shines when: Data-center on-site power orders grow, manufacturing capacity expands, margins improve on scale, and policy supports fuel cells.

Dangers: Competition from gas turbines and reciprocating engines. Product-cost and reliability history. Customer concentration. Dilution history. A valuation far ahead of earnings.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Revenue growth, gross margin, backlog and order announcements, capacity expansion.

Flywheel verdict: No.

SMR NuScale Power

Sector: Small Modular Reactors (pre-revenue) · Market cap: ~$10B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

The only small-modular-reactor design with U.S. NRC certification — a 77-megawatt light-water module approved in 2025 (its earlier 50-megawatt design was certified in 2023) — intended to be built in factories and installed in clusters of six or twelve. Its first customer project (a Utah utility consortium) was canceled in 2023 on cost; the pipeline now runs through ENTRA1, a development partner that announced a 6-gigawatt agreement with the Tennessee Valley Authority in 2025. Majority-owned by Fluor. Essentially no revenue.

Why it's popular: The most "real" SMR — a certified design — in a sector where most competitors have only slide decks.

Shines when: A first commercial order is placed with a firm construction timeline, the TVA agreement moves from framework to contract, and federal nuclear support accelerates.

Dangers: No orders, no revenue, and a history of a canceled flagship project. Cost per megawatt uncompetitive without subsidy. Dilution. Competing designs (GE Vernova's BWRX-300, X-energy, TerraPower).

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Order announcements, ENTRA1 and TVA milestones, cash and raises.

Flywheel verdict: No.

CCJ Cameco Corporation

Sector: Uranium / Nuclear Fuel · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~60x · Yield: ~0.2%

The largest publicly traded uranium miner in the West — McArthur River and Cigar Lake in Saskatchewan, the two highest-grade uranium mines on earth, plus a stake in Kazakhstan's Inkai — and, through a 49% stake in Westinghouse Electric (bought with Brookfield in 2023), a piece of the company that designs reactors and supplies fuel to half the world's nuclear fleet. Uranium prices roughly tripled from 2020 to 2024 as utilities restocked, Russia's supply became politically toxic, and new demand from data centers arrived.

Why it's popular: The blue-chip way to own the uranium bull market, with Westinghouse as an option on new reactor construction.

Shines when: Uranium spot and term prices rise, long-term contracting at higher prices grows, Westinghouse wins reactor orders (Poland, Bulgaria, U.S.), and mine production hits plan.

Dangers: Uranium price cycles. Mine operating issues (McArthur River has had them). Kazakhstan geopolitics. A multiple that prices years of higher uranium. Reactor construction timelines measured in decades.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Realized uranium price, production, long-term contract book, Westinghouse EBITDA and order news.

Flywheel verdict: No — a commodity stock, however strategic. If you want nuclear exposure with earnings, Constellation.

Defense tech, space & aerospace

HWM Howmet Aerospace

Sector: Aerospace Components · Market cap: ~$75B · P/E: ~45x · Yield: ~0.3%

The engine-parts company inside every jet: turbine airfoils and structural castings (the most demanding parts in an engine, made by only a handful of suppliers), aerospace fasteners, titanium and aluminum forgings, and forged aluminum truck wheels — ~$8B of revenue, spun out of Arconic (itself spun out of Alcoa) in 2020. CEO John Plant turned a mediocre industrial into the best-performing large-cap aerospace stock: margins doubled, the aftermarket (spare parts for engines flying longer than planned) grew, and the stock rose roughly fivefold.

Why it's popular: The pure play on the commercial-aerospace upcycle — Boeing and Airbus backlogs, engine spare-parts demand — run by a CEO the market treats as a legend.

Shines when: Aircraft production ramps, engine aftermarket demand stays high, margins keep expanding, and defense (F-35 parts) grows.

Dangers: A 45x multiple for a parts supplier. Boeing production problems. CEO succession. Commercial-aerospace cyclicality. Truck-wheel weakness.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Engine Products revenue and margin, commercial aerospace growth, full-year guidance, buybacks.

Flywheel verdict: The business is boring in the best sense — it belongs with TransDigm and Parker in quality — but at 45x it's a momentum stock. Parker-Hannifin (industrials) is the version at 26x.

KTOS Kratos Defense & Security Solutions

Sector: Defense Technology · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: n/m (minimal earnings) · Yield: none

A mid-sized defense contractor that makes the things the Pentagon says it wants next: jet-powered target and combat drones (the XQ-58 Valkyrie, a candidate for the Air Force's "loyal wingman" programs), hypersonic test vehicles and rocket motors, satellite ground systems, microwave electronics, and turbine engines for cruise missiles and drones. Revenue ~$1.3B, growing double digits, with thin margins because it invests ahead of programs.
