Why it's popular: The "new defense" trade — drones, hypersonics, cheap attritable systems — in one small-cap that the primes might buy. The stock rose severalfold in 2025.

Shines when: Drone and hypersonic programs move to production, rocket-motor and engine capacity expands, and defense budgets prioritize unmanned systems.

Dangers: Program timing and Pentagon budget politics. Thin margins. Dilution (it raises equity for capacity). Competition from Anduril, Shield AI, and the primes. A valuation on hope more than earnings.

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Organic revenue growth, backlog and book-to-bill, program awards (Valkyrie, hypersonics), adjusted EBITDA margin.

Flywheel verdict: No — L3Harris and General Dynamics (industrials) are the boring defense versions.

AVAV AeroVironment

Sector: Drones & Loitering Munitions · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~60x · Yield: none

The largest maker of small military drones (the Puma and Raven reconnaissance aircraft) and the Switchblade loitering munition — the "kamikaze drone" that became a symbol of the Ukraine war and is now in the Army's procurement plan at scale. In 2025 it bought BlueHalo (~$4.1B) to add space, counter-drone, and directed-energy businesses, roughly doubling revenue to ~$2B. Arlington, Virginia-based.

Why it's popular: Drones and counter-drones are the fastest-growing line in the defense budget, and AeroVironment is the American pure play.

Shines when: Switchblade production ramps under Army programs, allied orders grow, BlueHalo integrates, and counter-drone demand rises.

Dangers: Program lumpiness and budget timing. Integration of a large acquisition. Competition from Anduril and cheaper foreign drones. A premium multiple. Leverage.

Watch: Reports late Jun/Sep/Dec/Mar (fiscal year ends April). Backlog, Loitering Munitions revenue, BlueHalo contribution, adjusted EBITDA, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No.

JOBY Joby Aviation

Sector: Electric Air Taxis (pre-revenue) · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

The leading developer of electric vertical-takeoff-and-landing aircraft — a four-passenger air taxi designed to fly 100-plus miles at 200 mph — working through FAA type certification (the final stages of a five-stage process) with a launch planned in Dubai and then U.S. cities. Toyota has invested nearly $900M and is its manufacturing partner; Delta is a launch airline partner; it acquired Blade's passenger business in 2025 for a ready-made helicopter-charter network. Revenue is negligible; cash burn is several hundred million dollars a year.

Why it's popular: Flying cars — the most literal science-fiction stock on the market, with credible backers. Archer Aviation is the rival.

Shines when: FAA certification milestones land, Dubai commercial service launches, manufacturing scales, and government (defense) contracts add revenue.

Dangers: Certification delays. Cash burn and dilution. Unit economics of air taxis are unproven. Safety incidents. Competition from Archer and Chinese eVTOL makers.

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Certification stage progress, aircraft built, cash position, Dubai launch timing.

Flywheel verdict: No.

LUNR Intuitive Machines

Sector: Lunar Services (early revenue) · Market cap: ~$2B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

The company that landed the first commercial spacecraft on the Moon (Odysseus, February 2024 — it tipped over on landing but transmitted data) and then a second (Athena, March 2025 — also tipped), under NASA's commercial lunar payload program. It also won a NASA contract to build lunar communications and navigation infrastructure worth up to $4.8B over a decade, and provides lunar-terrain vehicles and data services. Houston-based; revenue ~$250M, losses.

Why it's popular: The Moon. Each landing attempt produces a week of extreme trading, and the NASA contracts give it a real backlog.

Shines when: A landing succeeds upright, NASA contracts convert to revenue, the lunar-network program advances, and Artemis funding holds.

Dangers: Mission failures. NASA budget cuts and Artemis restructuring. Dilution. A small company with binary outcomes.

Watch: Reports mid Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Mission schedule, backlog, NASA award news, cash.

Flywheel verdict: No.

Crypto, fintech & the 2025 IPO class

CRCL Circle Internet Group

Sector: Stablecoin Issuer · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~100x · Yield: none

The issuer of USDC, the second-largest dollar stablecoin (~$70B in circulation), which earns nearly all its revenue from interest on the Treasury bills and cash backing the coins — a business that is, in effect, a money-market fund whose customers don't collect the interest. It shares a large portion of that revenue with Coinbase under a distribution agreement. IPO'd in June 2025 at $31, rose past $290 within weeks as the GENIUS Act (the first U.S. stablecoin law) passed, then fell by more than half.

Why it's popular: The first pure-play public stablecoin company, arriving the month stablecoins became legal infrastructure.

Shines when: USDC circulation grows (payments adoption, tokenized assets), rates stay elevated (reserve income), and the Coinbase revenue share is renegotiated favorably.

Dangers: Falling rates cut revenue directly. Tether's dominance and bank-issued stablecoins entering. The Coinbase revenue split. Regulatory changes. Lockup expirations and a valuation far ahead of earnings.

Watch: USDC circulation (daily public data), reserve income, distribution costs, quarterly reports (mid Aug/Nov/Mar/May).

Flywheel verdict: No — though as a rate-sensitive fee business it is more analyzable than most crypto names.

FIG Figma

Sector: Design Software · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: n/m (GAAP losses) · Yield: none

The collaborative design tool that most product teams use to build software interfaces — browser-based, multiplayer, and the default for designers the way Google Docs is for writers — with ~$1B of revenue growing 40%-plus, high gross margins, and expansion into whiteboarding, slides, and AI-generated design. Adobe tried to buy it for $20B in 2022 and regulators blocked the deal in 2023, leaving Figma with a $1B breakup fee and its independence. IPO'd in July 2025 at $33, opened above $85, touched $140, and then fell back below $50.

Why it's popular: The most anticipated software IPO in years, with a product designers love and a "what AI does to design tools" debate attached.

Shines when: Net dollar retention stays above 130%, AI features drive seat expansion rather than compression, enterprise adoption grows, and stock-based compensation normalizes.

Dangers: AI design generation reducing seats. Competition from Adobe, Canva, and AI-native tools. Lockup expirations. Heavy stock-based compensation. A valuation that already fell hard once.

Watch: Reports early Sep/Nov/Mar/May (fiscal year ends January). Revenue growth, net dollar retention, customers over $100K, non-GAAP operating margin.

Flywheel verdict: No.

UPST Upstart Holdings

Sector: AI Consumer Lending · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

A lending platform that uses machine-learning models instead of FICO scores to approve personal loans, auto loans, and home-equity lines, originating through bank and credit-union partners and selling most loans to institutional investors. Revenue ~$1B. Upstart was a pandemic darling (the stock went from $20 to $400), collapsed 95% when rates rose and its funding partners fled in 2022, and has since rebuilt with committed capital partners and better models.

Why it's popular: A rate-sensitive, AI-branded lender with enormous volatility — the retail trader's favorite way to bet on rate cuts.

Shines when: Rates fall (approval rates and loan demand rise), funding partners commit capital, credit performance holds, and new products (auto, HELOC) scale.

Dangers: Funding-market dependence — the 2022 collapse can repeat. Credit losses in a downturn. Rate sensitivity. Competition from banks and fintechs. Model risk.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Origination volume, conversion rate, contribution margin, committed funding capacity, loan performance.

Flywheel verdict: No.

OPEN Opendoor Technologies

Sector: iBuying / Real Estate Technology · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

The company that buys homes directly from sellers with an algorithmic offer, makes light repairs, and resells them — "iBuying," a model that lost billions when home prices turned in 2022 and that Zillow abandoned. Opendoor survived, shrank, and became a 2025 meme stock: a hedge-fund manager's public campaign, a retail surge that took it from under $1 to over $10 in weeks, a new CEO (Kaz Nejatian, from Shopify) with a "founder mode" rebrand, and a plan to reduce inventory risk with agent partnerships. Revenue ~$4–5B, losses.

Why it's popular: The 2025 meme revival — retail traders adopted it as the housing-recovery lottery ticket.

Shines when: Home-price appreciation resumes, transaction volumes recover, the asset-light model reduces losses, and rates fall.

Dangers: Home-price declines hitting inventory. Losses and dilution. A business model that has never been consistently profitable. Meme-driven volatility in both directions.

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Homes purchased and sold, contribution margin, adjusted EBITDA, inventory, cash.

Flywheel verdict: No.

MARA MARA Holdings

Sector: Bitcoin Mining / Treasury · Market cap: ~$6B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

The largest publicly traded bitcoin miner by hash rate — data centers in Texas, North Dakota, Nebraska, and abroad running mining rigs — and, since 2024, also a bitcoin-treasury company in the Strategy mold: it holds roughly 50,000 coins, funded partly by convertible notes, and "HODLs" its production rather than selling it. Formerly Marathon Digital. It has also moved into owning its own power (wind farms, gas-to-power at wellheads) to lower costs, and dabbles in AI-compute hosting.

Why it's popular: The highest-beta bitcoin proxy in the U.S. market — mining leverage plus treasury leverage. Riot, CleanSpark, and Bitfarms are the peers.

Shines when: Bitcoin rises, network difficulty growth slows, power costs fall, and the treasury premium expands.

Dangers: Bitcoin price (a 30% drop is a 50%-plus drop here). Halving-driven margin compression. Convertible-debt dilution. Power costs. Regulatory risk.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov, plus monthly production updates. Bitcoin held, hash rate, cost per coin, energy cost, debt.

Flywheel verdict: No.

BMNR Bitmine Immersion Technologies

Sector: Ether Treasury · Market cap: ~$10B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

A tiny bitcoin-mining company that, in mid-2025, reinvented itself as the Strategy of ether: Wall Street strategist Tom Lee became chairman, the company raised billions through at-the-market stock sales and private placements (with backers including Peter Thiel's Founders Fund and ARK), and it accumulated the largest corporate ether treasury in the world — millions of ETH within months — with a stated goal of owning 5% of all ether. The stock went from a few dollars to over $100 and traded billions of dollars a day.

Why it's popular: The ether version of the treasury-company trade, promoted by a widely followed strategist, arriving as ether rallied.

Shines when: Ether rises, the premium to net asset value holds, staking income adds yield, and capital markets stay open for issuance.

Dangers: Ether drawdowns. Premium collapse. Dilution (its model requires constant issuance). Copycats (SharpLink, Bit Digital, and others). Regulatory scrutiny. A company whose only asset is a token.

Watch: Ether price, ETH held (weekly updates), mNAV, issuance pace, staking yield.

Flywheel verdict: No — an ether derivative, like Strategy is a bitcoin derivative.

Biotech & health favorites

MRNA Moderna

Sector: mRNA Therapeutics · Market cap: ~$10B · P/E: n/m (losses) · Yield: none

The company that went from obscurity to a $190B valuation on its COVID vaccine and back to ~$10B as COVID revenue collapsed — from $18B in 2022 to under $3B — while the pipeline (RSV vaccine Mresvia, a combined flu-COVID shot, a personalized cancer vaccine with Merck, rare-disease programs) has been slower and smaller than promised. It cut R&D spending sharply, laid off staff, and faced a hostile U.S. vaccine policy environment in 2025 as federal health leadership questioned mRNA and canceled contracts. Cash of several billion dollars is the floor.

Why it's popular: A fallen giant with a cult following, a real technology platform, and binary catalysts (cancer vaccine data, flu approvals) that move it 20% in a day.

Shines when: The Merck cancer-vaccine program reads out positively, flu and combination vaccines are approved, COVID revenue stabilizes, and cash burn falls toward break-even.

Dangers: Continued revenue decline. U.S. vaccine policy hostility. Pipeline failures. Cash burn. Competition from Pfizer/BioNTech and Novavax.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Product revenue, cash burn and runway, cancer-vaccine (intismeran) data timing, regulatory decisions.

Flywheel verdict: No.

CRSP CRISPR Therapeutics

Sector: Gene Editing · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

Co-developer (with Vertex, which leads commercialization) of Casgevy, the first approved CRISPR gene-editing therapy — a one-time cure for sickle-cell disease and beta-thalassemia, approved in late 2023, with a slow launch because the treatment requires months of preparation and a bone-marrow transplant. It also runs in-vivo editing programs for cardiovascular disease (cholesterol, lipoprotein(a)) and cell therapies for cancer. Swiss-domiciled; cash of over $1.5B; losses.

Why it's popular: The name is the technology — it is how retail investors own CRISPR — and every editing headline moves it. ARK is a large holder.

Shines when: Casgevy patient starts accelerate, in-vivo cardiovascular programs produce strong data, and the sector re-rates on gene-editing progress.

Dangers: A slow launch for a very expensive therapy. Pipeline binary risk. Competition from Intellia, Beam, and Vertex's own programs. Cash burn. Sector sentiment swings.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Casgevy patient initiations (reported by Vertex), pipeline data readouts, cash runway.

Flywheel verdict: No.

TEM Tempus AI

Sector: Precision Medicine / Health Data · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: n/m (approaching profitability) · Yield: none

Founded by Eric Lefkofsky (Groupon) after his wife's cancer diagnosis, Tempus sequences tumors and other samples for oncologists (genomic testing, its largest revenue line), and licenses the resulting de-identified clinical and molecular data to drugmakers for trial design and AI models. IPO'd in June 2024; revenue ~$1.3B, growing 30%-plus, with adjusted EBITDA turning positive. It bought Ambry Genetics (hereditary testing) in 2025.

Why it's popular: "AI plus healthcare data" in one ticker, with a founder story and Nancy Pelosi's disclosed options trades making it a retail favorite.

Shines when: Genomics test volumes grow, data-licensing contracts with pharma expand, reimbursement improves, and profitability arrives.

Dangers: Reimbursement and pricing pressure on tests. Competition from Guardant, Foundation Medicine (Roche), and Exact Sciences. Losses and dilution. Data-privacy regulation. Insider selling.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Genomics revenue growth, data and services revenue, adjusted EBITDA, remaining contract value.

Flywheel verdict: No.

OSCR Oscar Health

Sector: Health Insurance (ACA Marketplace) · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: ~15x (volatile) · Yield: none

A technology-first health insurer that sells almost entirely on the Affordable Care Act exchanges — about 2 million members, ~$12B of revenue — co-founded by Josh Kushner in 2012 and run by Mark Bertolini (former Aetna CEO) since 2023. It became profitable in 2024 as ACA enrollment hit records on enhanced subsidies, then fell sharply in 2025 as those subsidies were set to expire at the end of the year and medical costs ran hot.

Why it's popular: The only pure-play ACA insurer, and a leveraged bet on health-policy outcomes in Washington — the stock moves 20% on subsidy headlines.

Shines when: ACA subsidies are extended or the market stabilizes without them, medical-loss ratios normalize, and membership holds.

Dangers: Subsidy expiration shrinking the market. Medical-cost trend. Risk-adjustment payments. Concentration in a single product. Policy volatility.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Membership, medical loss ratio, subsidy legislation, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No — CVS/Aetna (healthcare section) is the diversified version.

VKTX Viking Therapeutics

Sector: Obesity Biotech (pre-revenue) · Market cap: ~$4B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

A San Diego biotech developing VK2735, a GLP-1/GIP dual agonist for obesity — the same mechanism as Eli Lilly's Zepbound — in both an injectable form (Phase 3) and an oral pill (Phase 2), with early data that produced weight loss comparable to the market leaders. It also has a liver-disease program. No revenue, a cash balance near $1B, and a valuation that rises and falls entirely on obesity-drug news and takeover speculation.

Why it's popular: The most-cited independent obesity biotech — every rumor that Pfizer or another large drugmaker wants an obesity asset sends it up 20%.

Shines when: Phase 3 data confirm efficacy and tolerability, the oral pill advances, a partnership or acquisition is announced, and the obesity market keeps growing.

Dangers: Trial failure or tolerability problems. Being late to a market Lilly and Novo dominate with pricing power. Dilution. Manufacturing scale-up. A valuation that assumes a buyout.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Trial readout timing, cash runway, partnership headlines, competitor data (Lilly's orforglipron, Novo's oral Wegovy).

Flywheel verdict: No.

China, emerging markets & EVs

PDD PDD Holdings

Sector: E-commerce (China / Temu) · Market cap: ~$150B · P/E: ~12x · Yield: none

Pinduoduo — the Chinese e-commerce platform that overtook JD and challenged Alibaba with group-buying, extreme low prices, and gamified shopping — and Temu, its international arm, which became one of the most downloaded apps in the world by shipping ultra-cheap goods directly from Chinese factories to Western consumers. Revenue ~$55B, net margins in the high 20s, and a founder (Colin Huang) who briefly became China's richest man. Famously opaque: minimal disclosure, no buybacks, no dividend, and a cash pile of over $50B.

Why it's popular: The cheapest large-cap growth company in the world by multiple, and the Temu story is a live experiment in whether Chinese cross-border commerce survives tariffs.

Shines when: Chinese consumption holds, Temu adapts to tariffs and de minimis changes with local warehousing, margins stay high, and management starts returning cash.

Dangers: U.S. and EU tariffs and de minimis elimination hitting Temu. Chinese regulatory and geopolitical risk. Opacity and governance. Domestic competition from Alibaba, JD, and Douyin. Delisting risk.

Watch: Reports late Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Revenue growth, operating margin, Temu commentary (it discloses little), cash balance, China policy.

Flywheel verdict: No — though the multiple makes it the value trade in this group. Alibaba is the version with buybacks and a dividend.

SE Sea Limited

Sector: Southeast Asian Internet · Market cap: ~$100B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

Singapore's tech champion: Shopee (the largest e-commerce platform in Southeast Asia and Brazil), Garena (the game publisher behind Free Fire, one of the most-played mobile games on earth), and SeaMoney (digital banking and consumer lending, the fastest-growing profit line). Revenue ~$20B. Sea went from $350 to $40 in 2021–22 as growth-at-all-costs collapsed, then rebuilt itself around profitability — Shopee turned cash-positive, Free Fire revived, and the lending book grew — and the stock recovered fivefold.

Why it's popular: The "Amazon plus Tencent of Southeast Asia," with a demographic runway of 700 million people and a proven turnaround.

Shines when: Shopee's take rate and margins expand, Free Fire holds its user base, SeaMoney credit performs, and Brazil scales.

Dangers: Competition from TikTok Shop, Lazada (Alibaba), and Temu. Credit risk in consumer lending. Game-franchise dependence on Free Fire. Currency and emerging-market volatility. A premium multiple.

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Shopee GMV and adjusted EBITDA, Garena bookings, SeaMoney loan book and non-performing loans, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No.

BYDDY BYD Company (ADR)

Sector: Electric Vehicles / Batteries · Market cap: ~$120B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~1%

The largest EV maker in the world — over 4 million vehicles a year (battery-electric and plug-in hybrid), more than Tesla — and one of the largest battery makers (the Blade lithium-iron-phosphate battery), vertically integrated from chips to semiconductors to ships that carry its cars to Europe and Latin America. Founded by Wang Chuanfu as a battery company in 1995; Berkshire Hathaway invested in 2008 and has been selling down. Its 2025 "5-minute charging" platform and aggressive price cuts triggered a brutal Chinese price war. The ADR trades over the counter.

Why it's popular: The company that actually won the EV race, at a multiple a fraction of Tesla's.

Shines when: Export growth (Europe, Southeast Asia, Latin America) offsets Chinese price competition, battery technology leads hold, margins stabilize, and tariff barriers ease.

Dangers: The Chinese price war compressing margins. EU and U.S. tariffs blocking exports. Beijing's intervention in the industry. OTC ADR liquidity. Geopolitical risk.

Watch: Reports late Mar/Apr/Aug/Oct, plus monthly sales releases. Vehicle deliveries by region, gross margin, overseas sales, price actions.

Flywheel verdict: No — Toyota (consumer discretionary) is the boring automaker.

NIO NIO Inc.

Sector: Chinese EVs · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: n/m (losses) · Yield: none

A premium Chinese EV maker known for battery-swap stations (thousands across China, letting drivers exchange a depleted battery in minutes) and for never having made money — cumulative losses run into the tens of billions of dollars, funded by Chinese state-linked investors and repeated share issuance. It launched two cheaper brands (Onvo and Firefly) in 2024–25 to reach volume, and delivers ~300,000-plus vehicles a year. XPeng and Li Auto are the peers; Li is the one that has been profitable.

Why it's popular: The "Chinese Tesla" of retail-trading lore — a high-beta way to bet on Chinese EVs recovering, with meme-like trading around delivery numbers.

Shines when: Deliveries scale on the new brands, gross margin rises above 15%, losses narrow toward break-even, and China stimulus supports EV demand.

Dangers: Losses and dilution. The price war with BYD and Tesla. Capital-intensive battery-swap network. Delisting and geopolitical risk. Competition from Xiaomi and others entering.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Sep/Nov, plus monthly deliveries. Vehicle margin, net loss, cash balance, new-brand volumes.

Flywheel verdict: No.

RIVN Rivian Automotive

Sector: Electric Vehicles · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: n/m (losses) · Yield: none

The maker of the R1T pickup and R1S SUV — premium, well-reviewed electric trucks built in Normal, Illinois — and of electric delivery vans for Amazon, its largest shareholder. Its future rides on the R2, a ~$45,000 midsize SUV launching in 2026 from the Normal plant and later a new Georgia factory, which needs to sell in volume for Rivian to survive. Volkswagen agreed in 2024 to invest up to $5.8B in a software joint venture, validating Rivian's vehicle-software architecture and funding its runway. Revenue ~$5B; gross profit turned positive in late 2024; net losses remain large.

Why it's popular: The most credible American EV startup after Tesla, with an Amazon and VW backstop and a make-or-break product launch on the calendar.

Shines when: R2 launches on time and sells, gross margin expands, the VW joint venture pays milestones, and EV demand recovers after federal credits ended.

Dangers: R2 execution — a delayed or flawed launch is existential. Cash burn and dilution. The end of federal EV tax credits. Tariffs on components. Competition from Tesla, GM, Ford, and Hyundai.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov, plus quarterly production and deliveries. Gross margin, cash and runway, R2 timeline, VW milestone payments.

Flywheel verdict: No.

LCID Lucid Group

Sector: Electric Vehicles · Market cap: ~$6B · P/E: n/m (losses) · Yield: none

The maker of the Lucid Air, a luxury sedan with the longest range of any EV, and the Gravity SUV launched in 2025 — engineered by a team from Tesla's early days, funded overwhelmingly by Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, which owns about 60% and has kept it alive through years of losses far exceeding revenue. It did a 1-for-10 reverse split in 2025, signed a deal to supply vehicles for Uber's robotaxi program (with Nuro's autonomy), and plans a cheaper midsize platform for 2026–27. Revenue under $1B; losses of ~$3B a year.

Why it's popular: A meme-era EV name that never died because a sovereign wealth fund wouldn't let it — with the Uber robotaxi angle adding a new narrative.

Shines when: Gravity deliveries scale, the Uber program advances, the midsize platform launches on budget, and the Saudi funding continues.

Dangers: Losses and dilution (the Saudi fund keeps funding at lower prices). Low volumes for a capital-intensive business. Competition. Dependence on a single shareholder's patience.

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov, plus quarterly deliveries. Gravity production, gross margin, cash runway, PIF funding announcements.

Flywheel verdict: No.

Software, solar & media

MDB MongoDB

Sector: Database Software · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~60x · Yield: none

The leading "document" database — a flexible alternative to the relational databases Oracle and Microsoft sell — used by developers to build modern applications, with most revenue now from Atlas, its cloud service that runs on AWS, Azure, and Google Cloud and bills by consumption. Revenue ~$2.3B, growing high teens to 20%. The stock lived through the full software cycle: a $600 peak in 2021, a collapse to $150, a 2025 slump on fears that AI would let developers skip databases like it, and a rebound as AI applications turned out to need somewhere to store their data (including vector search, which MongoDB added).

Why it's popular: The developer-favorite database and a bellwether for whether AI helps or hurts software companies — it moves ±20% on earnings.

Shines when: Atlas consumption growth accelerates, AI application workloads land on the platform, large-enterprise deals grow, and margins expand.

Dangers: Consumption-revenue volatility. Competition from the cloud providers' own databases and Postgres. AI compressing developer seats. A high multiple. Stock-based compensation.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends January). Atlas revenue growth, customer count over $100K, non-GAAP operating margin, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No — Oracle and IBM are the boring database companies.

SOUN SoundHound AI

Sector: Voice AI · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: n/m (losses) · Yield: none

A voice-recognition and conversational-AI company that sells its platform to automakers (Hyundai, Stellantis, Kia), restaurant chains (drive-thru ordering for Chipotle, White Castle, and others), and enterprises through the Amelia customer-service acquisition. Revenue ~$200M, growing fast from a small base, with large losses. It became a retail phenomenon in 2024 when Nvidia disclosed a small stake — the stock rose tenfold — and fell when Nvidia sold it in 2025.

Why it's popular: A small-cap "pure play on AI" that retail traders adopted, with the Nvidia association doing most of the work.

Shines when: Restaurant and automotive deployments scale, the Amelia enterprise business grows, gross margin recovers from acquisition mix, and losses narrow.

Dangers: Competition from Google, Amazon, OpenAI, and every large model provider offering voice for free. Losses and dilution. Customer concentration. A valuation with no earnings support.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Revenue growth, gross margin, cumulative bookings backlog, adjusted EBITDA, cash.

Flywheel verdict: No.

FSLR First Solar

Sector: Solar Manufacturing · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: none

The only large American solar-panel maker — and the only major producer using cadmium-telluride thin-film technology rather than Chinese-dominated crystalline silicon — with factories in Ohio, Alabama, and Louisiana, plus India and Malaysia, producing ~20 gigawatts a year for U.S. utility-scale projects. Revenue ~$5B, a backlog of over 60 gigawatts, and net cash. It is the primary beneficiary of federal manufacturing credits (45X) and of tariffs on Chinese and Southeast Asian panels, which is why its earnings rose as competitors' collapsed.

Why it's popular: The solar stock that works when solar policy is protectionist — and the one that moves 10–20% on every Washington headline about credits and tariffs.

Shines when: Manufacturing credits survive, tariffs on imports hold, utility-scale demand (data centers need power) grows, and new factories ramp.

Dangers: Policy reversal on credits or tariffs. Panel price deflation. Product-quality issues (a 2024 warranty charge). Customer project delays. Chinese competition finding routes around tariffs.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Backlog and bookings, module ASPs, 45X credit recognition, full-year guidance, policy headlines.

Flywheel verdict: No — a policy stock with real earnings; Enphase is the residential cousin.

ENPH Enphase Energy

Sector: Residential Solar Electronics · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: ~15x · Yield: none

Makes microinverters — the electronics that convert each rooftop solar panel's output to household current — and home batteries, sold through installers in the U.S. and Europe. It was one of the best-performing stocks of the 2010s (from $1 to $300) as it took the U.S. residential market from SolarEdge, then fell 80%-plus as rising rates crushed home-solar financing, California cut its net-metering payments, Europe destocked, and the federal residential solar credit (25D) was eliminated at the end of 2025. Revenue fell from ~$2.3B to ~$1.4B; it stayed profitable.

Why it's popular: A former high-flier at a decade-low valuation, with a loyal following waiting for the residential-solar cycle to turn.

Shines when: Rates fall and home-solar financing recovers, battery attach rates rise, Europe restocks, and third-party-owned (leased) solar — which keeps a tax credit — grows.

Dangers: The end of the residential tax credit. Rate sensitivity. Competition from Tesla and Chinese inverter makers. Tariffs on components. A structurally smaller market.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Revenue by region, microinverter and battery shipments, gross margin, quarterly guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No.

WBD Warner Bros. Discovery

Sector: Media · Market cap: ~$55B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

HBO, HBO Max, Warner Bros. film and television studios, CNN, Discovery, TNT, TBS, and the DC Comics library — the 2022 merger of WarnerMedia and Discovery, loaded with debt and dragged by declining cable networks. In 2025 it announced a split into two companies (Warner Bros., with the studios and streaming, and Discovery Global, with the cable networks and the debt), and then became the subject of a bidding war: Paramount Skydance made repeated offers for the whole company, and Netflix agreed to acquire the Warner Bros. studios-and-streaming business. The outcome, regulatory review, and final structure were still being contested into 2026.

Why it's popular: The biggest media takeover fight in a generation, with the stock trading on deal headlines rather than fundamentals.

Shines when: A deal closes at a premium, or the split creates a streaming-and-studio company the market values like Netflix.

Dangers: Deal collapse or regulatory block. Cable-network decline. Leverage. Streaming competition. Political scrutiny of media consolidation.

Watch: Deal announcements and regulatory filings above all. Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Streaming subscribers, studio results, net leverage.

Flywheel verdict: No — a merger-arbitrage situation, not an investment. Comcast (Atlas) is the boring media name.

Consumer brands, restaurants & retail favorites

CELH Celsius Holdings

Sector: Energy Drinks · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

The "fitness" energy drink that took the category's third spot behind Red Bull and Monster in five years, distributed by PepsiCo since 2022 (Pepsi owns a stake). Revenue ~$2B after acquiring Alani Nu (a female-focused energy brand) for ~$1.8B in 2025. The stock rose from $5 to $100 (split-adjusted) in three years, fell by two-thirds in 2024 when Pepsi destocked inventory and growth stalled, and recovered on Alani Nu and renewed share gains.

Why it's popular: The rare consumer brand with hypergrowth, and a case study in how distributor inventory can whipsaw a stock.

Shines when: Retail scan data shows share gains, Alani Nu integrates, international expands, and Pepsi's distribution holds.

Dangers: Monster and Red Bull competition. Category slowdown. Pepsi-relationship risk. Distributor inventory swings. A premium multiple.

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Revenue growth, U.S. share (Nielsen/Circana), gross margin, Alani Nu contribution.

Flywheel verdict: No — Monster (staples) is the boring version.

ELF e.l.f. Beauty

Sector: Cosmetics · Market cap: ~$7B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

A mass-market cosmetics brand — $5 dupes of prestige products, marketed through TikTok — that took share from Maybelline and L'Oréal for 25 consecutive quarters, with revenue growing from ~$300M to ~$1.5B in five years. In 2025 it bought Rhode, Hailey Bieber's skincare brand, for ~$1B. Its problem is manufacturing: most production was in China, and 2025 tariffs forced price increases and sourcing shifts.

Why it's popular: The growth stock of the beauty aisle, a Gen Z brand story, and a tariff bellwether.

Shines when: Share gains continue, Rhode scales, international grows, tariff costs are absorbed through price and sourcing, and margins hold.

Dangers: Tariffs on Chinese production. Category slowdown. Competition copying the dupe model. Rhode integration and celebrity-brand risk. A high multiple.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Net sales growth, U.S. share gains, gross margin and tariff impact, Rhode revenue.

Flywheel verdict: No — Church & Dwight (staples) is the boring beauty-and-personal-care version.

CAVA CAVA Group

Sector: Fast-Casual Restaurants · Market cap: ~$8B · P/E: ~90x · Yield: none

A Mediterranean fast-casual chain — bowls and pitas with a Chipotle-style assembly line — with about 450 restaurants, growing 15–20% a year in units, and same-store sales that were the best in the industry until they slowed in 2025. IPO'd in June 2023 at $22, rose to $170 in 2024 on "the next Chipotle" enthusiasm, and fell by half as comps decelerated. Profitable, with restaurant-level margins in the mid-20s.

Why it's popular: The best new restaurant concept since Chipotle, and the market treats every comp print as a referendum on whether it can reach thousands of units.

Shines when: Same-store sales reaccelerate, new markets (Midwest, South Florida) open strongly, and restaurant margins hold through expansion.

Dangers: Comp deceleration. A 90x multiple. Consumer weakness. Execution risk on rapid expansion. Competition from Sweetgreen and regional chains.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Same-restaurant sales and traffic, new-unit openings, restaurant-level margin, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No — Texas Roadhouse (consumer discretionary) is the boring growth restaurant.

BROS Dutch Bros

Sector: Drive-Thru Coffee · Market cap: ~$10B · P/E: ~90x · Yield: none

An Oregon-born drive-thru coffee chain — sugary energy drinks and lattes served by upbeat "broistas" — with over 1,100 shops, mostly in the West and Texas, adding 150-plus a year toward a stated goal of 7,000. Revenue ~$1.5B. It IPO'd in 2021, stumbled in 2022–23 on expansion costs and a CEO change, then became one of the best-performing restaurant stocks of 2024–25 as comps turned positive, mobile ordering rolled out, and food was added to the menu.

Why it's popular: A unit-growth story with a cult customer base — the coffee version of the CAVA trade.

Shines when: Same-shop sales grow, mobile order and food attach lift tickets, new shops in new markets ramp, and margins expand.

Dangers: A 90x multiple. Coffee and dairy costs. Consumer weakness on a discretionary treat. Starbucks's turnaround. Expansion execution.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Same-shop sales and transactions, new shop count, company-operated shop margin, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No.

WING Wingstop

Sector: Franchised Restaurants · Market cap: ~$8B · P/E: ~60x · Yield: ~0.3%

About 2,800 chicken-wing restaurants, nearly all franchised, with the highest unit-growth rate in the industry (over 15% a year), a digital-order mix above 70%, and a decade of positive same-store sales that ended in 2025 when comps went negative after a run of 20%-plus growth. Franchisees earn some of the best returns in restaurants (a unit costs ~$500K and pays back in two years), which keeps the pipeline full. Wingstop pays a regular dividend plus large special dividends funded by securitized debt.

Why it's popular: The asset-light growth compounder that traded at 100x earnings for years — and the 2025 comp reversal made it a battleground.

Shines when: Comps turn positive again, unit growth continues, digital and delivery mix rise, chicken-wing costs stay stable, and international expands.

Dangers: Comp deceleration after a lapping problem. Chicken-wing commodity costs. A premium multiple. Franchisee economics if traffic falls. Leverage from the securitization.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Domestic same-store sales, net new units, system-wide sales, bone-in wing costs, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No — Restaurant Brands (consumer discretionary) is the boring franchisor.

CROX Crocs

Sector: Footwear · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: ~7x · Yield: none

The foam clog that went from punchline to $3B brand — with 40%-plus operating margins on the Crocs brand, collaborations, and Jibbitz charms — and then the deal that undid the stock: the 2022 purchase of HEYDUDE (casual canvas shoes) for $2.5B, which grew briefly and then declined for three years, producing impairments. Revenue ~$4B. The Crocs brand still earns extraordinary margins; the stock trades at a single-digit multiple because the market has lost faith in management's capital allocation and fears the brand's fashion cycle is peaking.

Why it's popular: A meme-adjacent brand at a value-stock multiple — deep value or value trap, argued daily.

Shines when: HEYDUDE stabilizes, the Crocs brand holds its international growth, tariff costs are absorbed, and buybacks continue at a 7x multiple.

Dangers: Fashion risk on Crocs. HEYDUDE continued decline. Tariffs on Vietnamese and Chinese production. Leverage from the HEYDUDE deal. Consumer weakness.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Crocs brand revenue growth (international vs. North America), HEYDUDE revenue, gross margin, buyback pace.

Flywheel verdict: Not boring — but at 7x with buybacks it is the value trade of this group. Deckers (Atlas) is the higher-quality footwear name.

ONON On Holding

Sector: Athletic Footwear & Apparel · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

A Swiss running-shoe brand founded in 2010 by a former triathlete, known for its cloud-shaped cushioning pods and a Roger Federer partnership, that has grown 30%-plus a year to ~$3B of revenue by taking share from Nike among premium runners and fashion buyers. Direct-to-consumer and Asia are the growth vectors; apparel is the next expansion. It IPO'd in 2021 and, unlike most 2021 IPOs, kept growing.

Why it's popular: The growth brand in footwear while Nike stumbles — the "Hoka and On" duo (Hoka is owned by Deckers) that dominates running-store shelves.

Shines when: Growth stays above 25%, direct-to-consumer mix and gross margin rise, Asia scales, and apparel gains traction.

Dangers: Fashion cycles — running brands come and go. Tariffs on Vietnamese production. Nike fighting back. A premium multiple. Swiss franc reporting.

Watch: Reports mid Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Net sales growth by channel and region, gross margin, adjusted EBITDA margin, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No.

DUOL Duolingo

Sector: Language-Learning App · Market cap: ~$10B · P/E: ~60x · Yield: none

The owl. The most-downloaded education app in the world — over 130 million monthly users, 10 million-plus paying subscribers — using gamification (streaks, leagues, guilt-tripping notifications) to make language practice a daily habit, and expanding into math, music, and chess. Revenue ~$1B, growing 40%, GAAP profitable. A 2025 "AI-first" memo from the CEO (saying contractors would be replaced by AI) drew a public backlash, and the stock later fell on user-growth deceleration fears — then recovered.

Why it's popular: A consumer subscription business with a beloved brand and a real profit engine, and the market's live test of whether AI helps or replaces education apps.

Shines when: Daily active users keep growing 40%-plus, paid conversion rises (the Max tier with AI features), new subjects scale, and margins expand.

Dangers: User-growth deceleration. AI chatbots as a free substitute. App-store fee changes. A premium multiple. Brand risk from marketing missteps.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Daily active users, paid subscribers, bookings growth, adjusted EBITDA margin.

Flywheel verdict: No.

CHWY Chewy

Sector: Pet E-commerce · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: none

The online pet store — food, supplies, prescriptions, and now vet clinics — with ~$12B of revenue, 20 million active customers, and the highest-retention model in e-commerce: over 80% of sales come from Autoship subscriptions. Founded by Ryan Cohen, bought by PetSmart in 2017, IPO'd in 2019, and now independent after private-equity owner BC Partners sold down. It became profitable on a GAAP basis in 2024 and started buying back stock.

Why it's popular: A pandemic winner that survived the hangover, with a subscription model retail investors understand and the Ryan Cohen origin story.

Shines when: Active customers grow again after two flat years, net sales per customer rise (pharmacy, vet care, sponsored ads), and margins expand.

Dangers: Amazon and Walmart in pet. Slow customer growth. Pet-adoption normalization. Thin margins on food. A premium multiple for a retailer.

Watch: Reports late Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends January). Active customers, net sales per active customer, Autoship share, adjusted EBITDA margin.

Flywheel verdict: No — Tractor Supply (consumer discretionary) is the boring pet-adjacent retailer.

TTWO Take-Two Interactive Software

Sector: Video Games · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: n/m (GAAP losses on amortization) · Yield: none

Grand Theft Auto, Red Dead Redemption, NBA 2K, Borderlands, Civilization, and the mobile games from Zynga (acquired 2022) — ~$6B of revenue. Everything about the stock is Grand Theft Auto VI: the most anticipated entertainment product in history, delayed from 2025 to May 2026 and then to November 2026, expected to sell tens of millions of copies at $70-plus and generate years of online revenue. GTA V, released in 2013, has sold over 200 million copies and still earns.

Why it's popular: One product launch that could produce the largest single-year revenue jump of any media company ever — the stock trades on the GTA VI calendar.

Shines when: GTA VI ships on schedule and sells to expectations, NBA 2K holds, mobile stabilizes, and margins expand on the launch.

Dangers: Another delay. A launch that underwhelms relative to enormous expectations. Zynga mobile weakness. Console-cycle timing. Heavy amortization producing GAAP losses.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Net bookings guidance, GTA VI date confirmations, recurrent consumer spending, NBA 2K performance.

Flywheel verdict: No — a single-catalyst stock. Electronic Arts went private; there is no boring version.

RBLX Roblox Corporation

Sector: Gaming Platform · Market cap: ~$70B · P/E: n/m (GAAP losses) · Yield: none

A platform where users make and play games built by other users — over 100 million daily active users, most under 25, spending virtual currency (Robux) that Roblox sells and splits with creators. Bookings ~$6B growing 50%-plus in 2025 after a viral hit (Grow a Garden) drew record engagement, expanding into older users, advertising, and licensed brand experiences. GAAP losses come from stock compensation and developer payouts; free cash flow is strongly positive.

Why it's popular: The metaverse that actually exists, with a user base that makes every other media company jealous — and a stock that tripled in 2025.

Shines when: Daily active users and hours engaged grow, bookings per user rise with age diversification, advertising scales, and free cash flow margin expands.

Dangers: Child-safety lawsuits and regulation (states and plaintiffs' firms have sued over predators on the platform). Engagement dependence on viral hits. App-store fees. Valuation. Stock-based compensation.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Daily active users, hours engaged, bookings, free cash flow, safety-related headlines.

Flywheel verdict: No.

DKNG DraftKings

Sector: Online Sports Betting & iGaming · Market cap: ~$18B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: none

One of the two dominant U.S. online sportsbooks (with FanDuel, owned by Flutter), operating in over 25 states, plus iGaming (online casino) in the handful of states that allow it, daily fantasy sports where it started, and a lottery-courier business (Jackpocket, acquired 2024). Revenue ~$6B, adjusted-EBITDA positive since 2024 after a decade of losses. It is expanding into prediction markets (event contracts on sports and other outcomes) to compete with Kalshi and Polymarket, which operate nationally under federal derivatives rules rather than state gaming licenses.

Why it's popular: The pure play on legalized sports betting, with a retail base that uses the product — and now a live fight over whether prediction markets eat its business.

Shines when: Handle and hold rate grow, new states legalize (California and Texas would be transformative), promotional spending falls, iGaming expands, and prediction markets become an opportunity rather than a threat.

Dangers: State tax increases (Illinois, others raised rates sharply). Prediction-market competition operating outside state regulation. Customer-friendly sports outcomes hitting hold. Regulatory backlash on problem gambling. Competition from FanDuel, Fanatics, and ESPN Bet.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Handle, structural hold rate, monthly unique payers, adjusted EBITDA guidance, state legislation and tax headlines.

Flywheel verdict: No — Cboe (financials) is the boring way to own event-driven trading volume.

RCL Royal Caribbean Group

Sector: Cruise Lines · Market cap: ~$80B · P/E: ~18x · Yield: ~1%

The most profitable cruise company — Royal Caribbean International, Celebrity, and Silversea, ~$17B of revenue — and the industry's product leader: the Icon of the Seas class (the largest ships ever built, essentially floating resorts), private destinations (Perfect Day at CocoCay, the Bahamas beach club, and more coming), and a yield-management machine that fills ships at rising prices. It nearly failed in the pandemic, took on billions in debt, and then produced the best recovery in travel: record bookings, record margins, investment-grade credit restored, and a dividend reinstated in 2024.

Why it's popular: The best-performing large-cap travel stock of the recovery — up roughly tenfold from 2022 lows — as cruising won share from land vacations on value.

Shines when: Net yields grow on pricing, new ships and private destinations lift onboard spending, fuel costs are stable, and deleveraging continues.

Dangers: Travel-demand recessions. Fuel prices. Geopolitical and health shocks (the industry's history). Leverage. Overcapacity from a full industry order book. Carnival and Norwegian competing on price.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Net yield growth, load factor, booked position and pricing commentary, adjusted EPS guidance, net leverage.

Flywheel verdict: The closest thing to a flywheel name in the consumer group here — real earnings, a leadership position, a restored dividend — but travel cyclicality and ±8–12% earnings reactions put it in the trading bucket. Marriott and Hilton (consumer discretionary) are the boring travel names.

DAL Delta Air Lines

Sector: Airlines · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~9x · Yield: ~1%

The most profitable U.S. airline — ~$62B of revenue — and the one that broke the industry's commodity model: premium cabins, the American Express co-brand credit card (which pays Delta billions a year for SkyMiles), a loyalty program worth more than the airline, and a hub network (Atlanta, Detroit, Minneapolis, Salt Lake, New York, Los Angeles) with pricing power. Over half of revenue now comes from premium products and non-ticket sources, which is why its margins run double the industry's. United is the closest peer on the same strategy.

Why it's popular: The airline that retail investors buy when they want "the good one," and a favorite recovery trade after every shock.

Shines when: Premium and international demand hold, fuel prices fall, capacity discipline keeps fares firm, the Amex agreement grows, and buybacks resume.

Dangers: Airlines are still airlines — recessions, fuel spikes, weather, labor, and accidents. Government-shutdown and air-traffic-control disruptions. Leverage from the pandemic. Capacity additions by competitors.

Watch: Reports mid Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Total revenue per available seat mile, premium revenue growth, fuel price per gallon, adjusted EPS guidance, free cash flow.

Flywheel verdict: No — even the best airline is a cyclical with a 9x multiple for a reason.

GME GameStop Corp.

Sector: Video-Game Retail / Cash & Bitcoin · Market cap: ~$10B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

The original meme stock — the 2021 short squeeze that made retail trading a cultural event — is now a strange hybrid: a shrinking chain of about 3,000 game stores (revenue down by more than half from its peak as games went digital), a trading-card business (Pokémon, sports cards) that became its growth line, and a treasury of over $9B in cash and Treasuries plus several thousand bitcoin, raised through at-the-market stock sales into meme rallies. Chairman and CEO Ryan Cohen took no salary and has hinted at acquisitions and a Berkshire-style holding company; the company is profitable mostly on interest income.

Why it's popular: The flagship of retail-trading culture, with a loyal shareholder base that treats it as a movement; any Roaring Kitty post still moves it 20%.

Shines when: Cohen deploys the cash into something the market likes, trading cards grow, bitcoin rises, and the meme base rallies.

Dangers: The core business is in structural decline. The stock trades at a large premium to its cash. Dilution into every rally. Bitcoin exposure. No stated strategy for the treasury.

Watch: Reports early Mar/Jun/Sep/Dec (fiscal year ends January). Cash and bitcoin holdings, collectibles revenue, store count, share count, Cohen commentary.

Flywheel verdict: No.

Aerospace quality, data-center cooling & the strategic-materials trade

TDG TransDigm Group

Sector: Aerospace Components · Market cap: ~$75B · P/E: ~35x · Yield: irregular special dividends

A collection of hundreds of small aerospace-parts businesses — actuators, pumps, valves, latches, cockpit displays, ignition systems — each of which is the sole source for a part on an aircraft that flies for 30 years, giving TransDigm pricing power that borders on the notorious (a Pentagon inspector general has documented it). Revenue ~$8.5B, with EBITDA margins above 50% — the highest of any industrial company — driven by aftermarket spare parts. Built by Nick Howley through a private-equity playbook applied to a public company: buy sole-source parts businesses, raise prices, cut costs, borrow against the cash flow, pay special dividends.

Leverage is high by design (5–7x EBITDA) and the special dividends are funded by debt. It compounds book value faster than almost any industrial and trades at a multiple that reflects it.

Why it's popular: The most successful compounder in aerospace and a favorite of quality-growth investors; the Berkshire of airplane parts.

Shines when: Commercial aftermarket grows (aircraft flying more hours, older fleets), acquisitions add sole-source parts, pricing holds, and special dividends continue.

Dangers: Leverage. Pricing scrutiny from the Pentagon and airlines. Commercial-aerospace cyclicality. Succession (Howley stepped back). A premium multiple.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends September). Commercial aftermarket bookings and revenue, EBITDA margin, net leverage, acquisition and special-dividend announcements.

Flywheel verdict: A real candidate if you accept the leverage — it is arguably the best business in the guide. Parker-Hannifin and Howmet are the less-levered comparisons.

GNRC Generac Holdings

Sector: Backup Power · Market cap: ~$10B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: none

The dominant maker of home standby generators (about 75% U.S. share) — the natural-gas units that switch on when the grid fails — plus portable generators, commercial and industrial generators, and a clean-energy business (home batteries, EV chargers) that has lost money since it was assembled. Revenue ~$4.5B. Demand spikes with hurricanes, wildfires, and grid outages, and Generac's earnings boomed in the pandemic (home improvement plus outage anxiety), then fell by half as dealer inventory unwound.

The new angle is data centers: Generac has entered the large-generator market for backup power at AI facilities, competing with Caterpillar and Cummins, with early orders announced in 2025. Milwaukee-based; buybacks, no dividend.

Why it's popular: A weather-and-grid-anxiety stock that becomes an AI-infrastructure stock whenever a data-center order lands.

Shines when: Major outage events drive home-standby demand, data-center generator orders scale, the clean-energy losses narrow, and margins recover.

Dangers: Weather-dependent demand. Dealer inventory cycles. Clean-energy losses. Competition in data-center generators from far larger players. Tariffs on components.

Watch: Reports late Feb/Apr/Jul/Oct. Residential product sales, commercial and industrial sales, data-center order commentary, gross margin, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No — Cummins (industrials) is the boring generator name.

AAON AAON, Inc.

Sector: Custom HVAC / Data-Center Cooling · Market cap: ~$8B · P/E: ~50x · Yield: ~0.3%

A Tulsa maker of premium, semi-custom rooftop HVAC units for commercial buildings that stumbled into the AI boom: its BASX subsidiary builds the large liquid-cooling and air-handling systems for data centers, and orders from hyperscalers turned a $1B-revenue niche company into a growth stock with a backlog above $1B and a new Memphis factory. Revenue ~$1.3B. Founder-influenced, conservatively financed, and historically a slow compounder.

Why it's popular: The small-cap way to own data-center cooling alongside Vertiv — with the volatility of a small cap.

Shines when: Data-center orders grow, the Memphis plant ramps, commercial HVAC (schools, hospitals) holds, and margins expand on mix.

Dangers: A 50x multiple. Customer concentration in a few hyperscalers. Execution on rapid capacity expansion. Commercial construction cyclicality. Competition from Vertiv, Trane, and Johnson Controls.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Backlog, BASX segment growth, gross margin, capacity ramp commentary.

Flywheel verdict: No — Trane (industrials) is the boring version.

MOD Modine Manufacturing

Sector: Thermal Management · Market cap: ~$7B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: none

A 110-year-old Wisconsin radiator maker that reinvented itself: after decades of low-margin automotive and truck heat exchangers, Modine shifted under CEO Neil Brinker (2020) to Climate Solutions — data-center cooling (the Airedale brand's chillers and computer-room air handlers, sold to hyperscalers and colocation operators), HVAC, and heat-transfer products — and exited or sold its worst vehicle businesses. Revenue ~$2.7B; data-center revenue grew from nothing to over $1B in a few years, and the stock rose roughly tenfold.

Why it's popular: The turnaround-plus-AI-cooling story — an old-economy name that found the hottest end market on earth.

Shines when: Data-center cooling orders grow, capacity additions (U.S. and Europe) ramp, the 80/20 portfolio pruning lifts margins, and acquisitions add.

Dangers: Hyperscaler capex cycles. Customer concentration. Competition from Vertiv, Schneider, and Johnson Controls. Remaining vehicle-business cyclicality. A premium multiple for a former radiator maker.

Watch: Reports late Jan/May/Jul/Oct (fiscal year ends March). Climate Solutions revenue and data-center revenue, adjusted EBITDA margin, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No.

BWXT BWX Technologies

Sector: Nuclear Components · Market cap: ~$15B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: ~0.7%

The only manufacturer of the nuclear reactors that power U.S. Navy submarines and aircraft carriers — a sole-source, decades-long franchise that is most of its ~$3B of revenue — plus nuclear fuel, components for commercial reactors, medical isotopes, and a growing role in small modular reactors and microreactors (it is building the Pele microreactor for the Army and supplying components for several SMR developers). Lynchburg, Virginia-based; spun out of Babcock & Wilcox in 2015.

Why it's popular: The "picks and shovels" of the nuclear revival with a naval monopoly underneath — the one nuclear stock with real, defense-backed earnings.

Shines when: Naval submarine production ramps (Columbia and Virginia class), SMR and microreactor programs move to production, commercial-nuclear restarts and uprates need components, and medical isotopes scale.

Dangers: A 40x multiple for a government contractor. Program timing. Concentration in naval contracts. SMR commercialization is years away. Capacity constraints.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Government Operations backlog and revenue, Commercial Operations growth, adjusted EBITDA, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: The business is boring — a naval monopoly — but the multiple prices the SMR dream. Huntington Ingalls (industrials) is the version at 18x.

MP MP Materials

Sector: Rare Earths · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

The only rare-earth mine and processing operation in the Western Hemisphere — Mountain Pass, California — and, since 2025, a de facto national-security company: the Department of Defense took a ~$400M equity stake, guaranteed a floor price for its neodymium-praseodymium output (the material in permanent magnets for motors, drones, and missiles), and committed to buy from a new magnet plant; Apple then signed a $500M supply deal. Revenue is small (~$300M) and lumpy; the value is strategic.

Why it's popular: China's export controls on rare earths in 2025 made MP the only American answer, and the government backstop turned a commodity miner into a policy stock — the shares tripled.

Shines when: The magnet plant ramps, the DoD price floor holds, more domestic customers sign, and China restrictions tighten.

Dangers: Rare-earth price collapses if China floods the market (the floor helps but doesn't eliminate risk). Execution on magnet manufacturing. Dilution. A valuation on strategy rather than earnings.

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. NdPr production and realized price, magnet-plant milestones, DoD and customer agreements, cash.

Flywheel verdict: No.

ALB Albemarle Corporation

Sector: Lithium · Market cap: ~$10B · P/E: n/m (trough) · Yield: ~1.5%

The largest lithium producer in the world — brine in Chile and Nevada, spodumene from Australia (Greenbushes, Wodgina), and conversion plants in China and the U.S. — plus bromine (flame retardants) and a small catalysts business. Revenue ~$5B, down from ~$10B at the 2022 peak. Lithium prices rose tenfold from 2020 to 2022 on EV demand, then fell 80%-plus as Chinese supply flooded the market and EV growth slowed; Albemarle's earnings went from record to losses, it cut capex and jobs, and the stock fell from $330 to $60. The dividend, raised for 30 years, was held flat.

Why it's popular: The lithium bull market's flagship and its bust's cautionary tale — and now the cycle-bottom bet.

Shines when: Lithium prices recover (supply curtailments in China and Australia, EV and grid-storage demand growth), costs fall, and the balance sheet holds.

Dangers: A longer lithium oversupply. Chinese competition. Chile's regulatory stance on lithium. Leverage during a trough. EV demand disappointments.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Jul/Nov. Lithium realized prices and volumes, adjusted EBITDA, free cash flow, net leverage, industry supply-curtailment news.

Flywheel verdict: No — a commodity cyclical, however important the commodity.

AEM Agnico Eagle Mines

Sector: Gold Mining · Market cap: ~$70B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.2%

The largest gold miner by market value — mines in Canada (Detour Lake, Canadian Malartic, Nunavut), Finland, Australia, and Mexico — and the one institutional investors trust most: low political risk (mostly Canada), consistent guidance, and disciplined capital allocation. Production ~3.4 million ounces a year at some of the industry's lowest all-in costs. Gold's run to record highs in 2024–25 (central-bank buying, dollar diversification, geopolitics) sent its free cash flow to records, funding debt paydown, buybacks, and a rising dividend.

Why it's popular: The gold miner you buy when you want gold with operating leverage but without the jurisdiction risk — Newmont (Atlas) is the larger but messier alternative.

Shines when: Gold prices rise, production hits guidance, costs stay contained, and capital return grows.

Dangers: Gold price declines. Cost inflation (labor, energy). Mine-life and permitting. Currency (Canadian dollar). A miner's multiple compresses fast when gold falls.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/late Apr/Jul/Oct. Gold production and all-in sustaining cost, free cash flow, net cash position, dividend and buyback.

Flywheel verdict: No — though as gold miners go, it is the boring one. GLD (Atlas) is the pure gold exposure.

TPL Texas Pacific Land Corporation

Sector: Land & Royalties · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: ~45x · Yield: ~0.5%

The strangest large company in the S&P 500: a trust created in 1888 from a bankrupt railroad's land grants that now owns about 870,000 acres of West Texas — most of it in the Permian Basin — and earns royalties on the oil and gas produced beneath it, easement and surface fees from pipelines and roads crossing it, and revenue from selling and treating water for fracking. It has almost no employees, no debt, 85%-plus margins, and pays dividends plus specials. Converted from a trust to a corporation in 2021 and joined the S&P 500 in 2024. Horizon Kinetics is the long-time large holder.

Why it's popular: A royalty company with land as a permanent asset and the best margins in the index — and a cult following that has debated its valuation for a decade.

Shines when: Permian production and drilling grow on its acreage, water revenue expands, oil prices rise, and new uses for the land (data centers, solar, bitcoin mining) emerge.

Dangers: Oil price and Permian activity. A 45x multiple for royalty income. Water-business capital intensity. Concentration in one basin.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Royalty production, water sales volumes, easement revenue, cash balance, special dividends.

Flywheel verdict: A real candidate if you can stomach the multiple — no debt, no employees, and a business that has existed since 1888. Otherwise, the midstream names (energy) are the fee-based alternative.

VG Venture Global

Sector: LNG Export · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: nominal

The fastest-growing U.S. LNG exporter — Calcasieu Pass (operating) and Plaquemines (ramping) in Louisiana, with CP2 and further projects under construction — built on a modular design that gets terminals running faster than the industry norm. Founded by Mike Sabel and Bob Pender; IPO'd in January 2025 at $25, well below its initial target, and fell further as investors digested a fight with customers: Shell, BP, and others accused it of selling cargoes on the spot market at high prices during 2022–24 instead of delivering under their long-term contracts, and arbitration rulings went against it in 2025.

Why it's popular: The pure play on U.S. LNG growth — Cheniere (Atlas) is the established one — with a founder-led, aggressive, controversial reputation that makes it a battleground.

Shines when: Plaquemines reaches full output, CP2 secures financing and offtake, arbitration liabilities are bounded, and global LNG demand supports spot margins.

Dangers: Arbitration awards to customers. Leverage on a massive construction program. LNG price and spread cycles. Governance (founders control the vote). Permitting and construction risk.

Watch: Reports mid Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Cargoes exported, adjusted EBITDA, project milestones, arbitration developments, financing.

Flywheel verdict: No — Cheniere (Atlas) is the version with the settled contracts.

LEU Centrus Energy

Sector: Uranium Enrichment · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: ~50x · Yield: none

The only U.S.-owned uranium-enrichment company — the former U.S. Enrichment Corporation — and the only Western producer of high-assay low-enriched uranium (HALEU), the fuel most advanced small reactors need and which, until 2025, only Russia made at scale. It operates a demonstration cascade in Piketon, Ohio, under Department of Energy contracts, and brokers enriched uranium from Russia and Europe for utilities (the LEU segment, most of its ~$450M revenue) — a business squeezed by the U.S. ban on Russian enriched uranium imports.

Why it's popular: The bottleneck of the nuclear revival: without domestic enrichment, none of the SMR stories work, and Centrus is the only American name.

Shines when: DOE funds a full-scale HALEU plant, utilities sign long-term enrichment contracts, Russian-supply bans tighten the market, and SMR developers commit to fuel purchases.

Dangers: Dependence on federal funding decisions. Russian-supply dependence in the brokerage business. Dilution to fund expansion. Competition from Urenco and Orano expanding U.S. capacity. Tiny scale.

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. DOE contract awards, HALEU production, LEU segment backlog, cash and raises.

Flywheel verdict: No.

CLF Cleveland-Cliffs

Sector: Integrated Steel · Market cap: ~$5B · P/E: n/m (losses) · Yield: none

The largest flat-rolled steelmaker in North America — blast furnaces and finishing mills across Ohio, Michigan, Indiana, and Pennsylvania, plus iron-ore mines in Minnesota and Michigan — assembled by CEO Lourenco Goncalves through the 2020 acquisitions of AK Steel and ArcelorMittal USA and the 2024 purchase of Stelco (Canada). Revenue ~$19B. The largest supplier of steel to the U.S. auto industry, which is both its franchise and its problem: auto production has been soft, its blast-furnace cost structure is higher than the mini-mills' (Nucor, Steel Dynamics), and it has lost money through the downturn.

Goncalves is the industry's loudest voice for tariffs (which he got — 50% on imports in 2025) and lost the fight for U.S. Steel to Nippon Steel. It idled plants, cut costs, and is betting on tariffs and a manufacturing revival.

Why it's popular: The tariff trade in one stock, with a CEO who makes headlines, and a leveraged bet on U.S. steel prices.

Shines when: Steel prices rise on tariffs, auto production recovers, idled capacity restarts profitably, and leverage falls.

Dangers: Losses and leverage. Blast-furnace cost disadvantage. Auto-demand weakness. Tariff policy reversals. Pension and legacy liabilities.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Steel shipments and average selling price, adjusted EBITDA, free cash flow, net debt, hot-rolled coil prices.

Flywheel verdict: No — Steel Dynamics (materials) and Nucor (Atlas) are the boring steel names.

More favorites — health, alternatives, travel, retail & media

PODD Insulet Corporation

Sector: Insulin Pumps · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~50x · Yield: none

The Omnipod — a tubeless, disposable insulin pump worn as a patch, controlled by a phone, and sold through pharmacies like a prescription rather than through the durable-medical-equipment channel — which took Insulet from a niche device maker to the fastest-growing diabetes company. Revenue ~$2.8B, growing 20%-plus, with the Omnipod 5 automated-delivery system (it pairs with a glucose sensor to dose automatically) expanding into type 2 diabetes, a market several times larger than type 1. A new CEO in 2025.

Why it's popular: A medical-device growth story with a consumer-like product, and the counterweight to fears that GLP-1 drugs shrink the diabetes market.

Shines when: Type 2 adoption grows, international expands, new-customer starts stay high, and gross margin expands on manufacturing scale.

Dangers: A 50x multiple. Competition from Tandem and Medtronic's patch pumps. GLP-1 effects on insulin use. Reimbursement. A leadership transition.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. U.S. Omnipod revenue growth, new customer starts, type 2 mix, gross margin, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: No — ResMed and Becton Dickinson (healthcare) are the boring device names.

NTRA Natera

Sector: Genetic Testing · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: n/m (approaching profitability) · Yield: none

The leader in cell-free DNA testing — Panorama (non-invasive prenatal screening, its original franchise), Signatera (a personalized blood test that detects residual cancer after treatment, the fastest-growing product and a category it essentially created), and Prospera (transplant rejection monitoring). Revenue ~$2B, growing 30%-plus, with Signatera volumes compounding as oncologists adopt it and Medicare coverage expands across cancer types. Losses narrowed to near break-even.

Why it's popular: The growth stock of diagnostics — Signatera is the product every liquid-biopsy investor watches — and a stock that rose fivefold in three years.

Shines when: Signatera volumes and reimbursement grow, new cancer indications win coverage, gross margin expands, and profitability arrives.

Dangers: Reimbursement and pricing risk. Competition from Guardant, Exact Sciences (now Abbott), and Tempus. Patent litigation (constant). Valuation.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Signatera test volumes, oncology revenue, gross margin, cash flow, coverage decisions.

Flywheel verdict: No — Quest and Labcorp (healthcare) are the boring testing names.

INSM Insmed Incorporated

Sector: Biotech (Rare Respiratory Disease) · Market cap: ~$25B · P/E: n/m · Yield: none

A biotech that graduated from one product to a franchise: Arikayce (an inhaled antibiotic for a rare lung infection, ~$400M a year) and brensocatib, approved in August 2025 as the first treatment for bronchiectasis — a chronic lung disease affecting hundreds of thousands of Americans with no prior approved drug — and expected to become a multibillion-dollar product. Pipeline programs in pulmonary hypertension and gene therapy follow. Losses will continue until brensocatib scales.

Why it's popular: The biggest biotech launch of 2025, and a company that many expect to be acquired by a large pharma.

Shines when: The brensocatib launch beats expectations, label expansion to other indications advances, Arikayce grows, and takeover interest emerges.

Dangers: Launch execution. Pricing and reimbursement. Pipeline binary risk. Dilution. A valuation that already assumes a large launch.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Brensocatib prescriptions and revenue, Arikayce revenue, cash runway, pipeline readouts.

Flywheel verdict: No.

ARES Ares Management Corporation

Sector: Alternative Asset Management · Market cap: ~$50B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~2.5%

The leader in private credit — direct lending to mid-sized companies, a business that has taken share from banks for fifteen years — with ~$600B of assets under management across credit, real estate, private equity, infrastructure, and secondaries (GCP International, acquired 2025, added Asian real estate). Revenue is management fees on a base that is over 90% locked up for years, plus performance fees. Los Angeles-based; founded in 1997 by Tony Ressler and partners.

Why it's popular: The pure play on private credit, the fastest-growing corner of asset management, at a time when insurers, wealth clients, and retirement plans are all being sold alternatives. Blackstone, KKR, and Apollo (Atlas) are the larger comparisons.

Shines when: Fundraising stays strong (wealth-channel products especially), deployment grows with M&A, fee-related earnings compound, and credit performs.

Dangers: A credit cycle — private-credit defaults have never been tested at scale. Fee compression. Regulatory scrutiny of retail alternatives. A premium multiple. Key-person risk.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Fee-related earnings, fundraising, fee-paying AUM, realized performance income, dividend growth.

Flywheel verdict: No — though the fee stream is durable. Blackstone (Atlas) is the established version; T. Rowe (financials) the cheap traditional-manager version.

BN Brookfield Corporation

Sector: Alternative Assets / Holding Company · Market cap: ~$100B · P/E: ~20x on distributable earnings · Yield: ~0.6%

A Toronto conglomerate that owns a controlling stake in Brookfield Asset Management (the ~$1 trillion alternative manager it partially spun out in 2022), a wealth-solutions business (Brookfield Wealth Solutions — annuities and insurance, the fastest-growing piece), and directly held operating businesses in real estate (office towers, malls, multifamily), renewable power, infrastructure, and private equity — an empire built by Bruce Flatt over 20-plus years. It is the "capital" side of the Brookfield complex; BAM is the "fee" side.

Why it's popular: A Berkshire-like compounder for investors who want alternative assets, real estate, and infrastructure in one ticker, with a valuation debate (the sum of the parts is worth more than the stock) that has run for years.

Shines when: Wealth Solutions scales, BAM fundraising grows, real-estate monetizations close at good prices, and rates fall (real assets re-rate).

Dangers: Complexity and opacity. Real-estate (office) exposure. Leverage at the operating-company level. Rate sensitivity. Key-person risk.

Watch: Reports mid Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Distributable earnings per share, Wealth Solutions growth, BAM fee-related earnings, real-estate monetization activity.

Flywheel verdict: A plausible long-term hold, but the complexity keeps it out of the flywheel — you can't easily tell what moved it.

ALLY Ally Financial

Sector: Auto Lending / Digital Bank · Market cap: ~$12B · P/E: ~10x · Yield: ~3%

The former GMAC — the largest all-digital bank in the U.S. (~$140B of deposits, no branches) and one of the largest auto lenders, financing cars through dealers (many of them still GM and Stellantis dealers) and funding the loans with online savings accounts. It sold its credit-card business and exited mortgages in 2025 to focus on auto, corporate finance, and deposits. Berkshire Hathaway is a large holder. Revenue ~$8B.

Why it's popular: A cheap, simple bank with a Berkshire endorsement, a 3% yield, and a stock that trades on used-car prices and credit.

Shines when: Net interest margin expands as deposit costs fall, auto credit losses normalize, used-car prices hold, and buybacks resume.

Dangers: Auto credit losses in a consumer downturn. Used-car price declines. Deposit competition. Rate sensitivity. Dealer concentration.

Watch: Reports mid Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Net interest margin, retail auto net charge-offs, originations and yields, CET1, buybacks.

Flywheel verdict: Closer to the flywheel than most here — it belongs with Capital One and Synchrony (financials) as a cheap consumer lender.

CPNG Coupang

Sector: E-commerce (South Korea) · Market cap: ~$50B · P/E: ~50x · Yield: none

The Amazon of South Korea — over 20 million active customers in a country of 52 million, same-day and dawn delivery through its own logistics network, plus Coupang Eats (food delivery), Coupang Play (streaming), fintech, and the luxury platform Farfetch (bought out of distress in 2024). Revenue ~$33B, growing 15–20%, profitable since 2023. Founded by Bom Kim; incorporated in Delaware, listed in New York, operating in Korea and expanding into Taiwan.

Why it's popular: A rare profitable e-commerce growth story outside the U.S. and China, with SoftBank as a large (selling) holder and a developing-market expansion narrative.

Shines when: Korean market share grows, Taiwan scales, Farfetch stabilizes, and margins expand as logistics investments mature.

Dangers: Korean regulatory scrutiny (antitrust fines, labor). Developing-offerings losses (Taiwan, Farfetch). Korean consumer and currency. A premium multiple.

Watch: Reports early Mar/May/Aug/Nov. Product commerce revenue and active customers, developing-offerings losses, adjusted EBITDA margin.

Flywheel verdict: No.

LVS Las Vegas Sands

Sector: Casinos & Resorts (Asia) · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~2%

Despite the name, it owns nothing in Las Vegas anymore (it sold the Venetian in 2022): its business is Macau (five properties including the Venetian Macao and the Londoner) and Singapore (Marina Bay Sands, the most profitable casino in the world, mid-expansion). Revenue ~$12B. Macau's recovery from the pandemic and China's crackdown on junket VIP gambling has been uneven; Singapore has been a record-setter. The Adelson family controls the company and also owns the Dallas Mavericks.

Why it's popular: The cleanest way to bet on the Chinese consumer traveling and gambling again — every Macau visitation number moves it.

Shines when: Macau mass-market gaming revenue grows, Singapore's expansion opens, Chinese travel recovers, and buybacks and dividends continue.

Dangers: Chinese policy toward Macau and capital controls. Macau concession terms. Competition from Wynn and MGM in Macau. Geopolitical risk. A New York or Texas casino license that never comes.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Macau property EBITDA and mass-market share, Marina Bay Sands EBITDA, Macau monthly gross gaming revenue, capital return.

Flywheel verdict: No.

UAL United Airlines Holdings

Sector: Airlines · Market cap: ~$30B · P/E: ~8x · Yield: none

The second of the two premium U.S. airlines (with Delta) — the largest international network of any U.S. carrier, hubs at Newark, Chicago, Denver, Houston, San Francisco, and Washington Dulles, and a strategy under CEO Scott Kirby of adding premium seats, bigger planes, and international capacity while the discount carriers struggle.
