Revenue ~$58B. It moved most of its Newark operations through the 2025 air-traffic-control crisis and a government shutdown, and still expanded margins.

Why it's popular: The airline with the most operating leverage to the premium and international travel boom, at a single-digit multiple — the higher-beta version of Delta.

Shines when: International and premium demand hold, fuel falls, capacity discipline keeps fares firm, and Newark and ATC problems ease.

Dangers: All the usual airline risks — recession, fuel, labor, accidents — plus Newark's operational fragility and a large aircraft order book.

Watch: Reports mid Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Total revenue per available seat mile, premium and international revenue, fuel cost, adjusted EPS guidance, free cash flow.

Flywheel verdict: No.

WSM Williams-Sonoma

Sector: Home Furnishings Retail · Market cap: ~$22B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~1.5%

Williams Sonoma, Pottery Barn, West Elm, Rejuvenation, and Mark and Graham — the largest digital-first home retailer in the U.S. (about two-thirds of ~$7.7B of revenue online) and the rare furniture company with pricing power: it designs its own products, refuses to discount, and has held operating margins near 18% through a housing slump that crushed Wayfair and RH. CEO Laura Alber has run it since 2010. Dividend raised annually; heavy buybacks; the stock split 2-for-1 in 2024.

Why it's popular: The best-run home retailer, a beneficiary of any housing recovery, and a stock that quietly quadrupled while the category fell apart.

Shines when: Housing turnover recovers (furniture follows moves), comparable sales turn positive, tariff costs are absorbed, and margins hold.

Dangers: Housing and discretionary weakness. Tariffs on Asian-made furniture. Category deflation. A premium multiple for a furniture retailer.

Watch: Reports mid Mar/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends January). Comparable brand revenue by banner, operating margin, tariff commentary, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: A genuine candidate — Ralph Lauren-style pricing power in furniture, a growing dividend, and a clean balance sheet. It would fit in the consumer-discretionary section if you wanted to move it.

SIRI Sirius XM Holdings

Sector: Satellite Radio / Audio · Market cap: ~$8B · P/E: ~8x · Yield: ~5%

Satellite radio (about 33 million subscribers, mostly through new-car trials that convert to subscriptions) and Pandora (streaming, advertising-supported) — ~$8.5B of revenue, high margins, and a subscriber base that has been slowly shrinking as younger drivers use phones instead. In 2024 it merged with Liberty Media's tracking stock to simplify its structure and did a 1-for-10 reverse split; Berkshire Hathaway then built a stake of over 35%, which is the main reason anyone talks about it.

Why it's popular: Berkshire's most puzzling recent purchase — a cash-rich, slowly declining business at a single-digit multiple with a 5% yield. Value investors argue about it constantly.

Shines when: Subscriber declines stabilize, pricing holds, in-car integration (the new 360L platform) improves retention, and free cash flow funds buybacks and the dividend.

Dangers: Structural subscriber decline. Competition from Spotify, Apple, and free streaming in the car. Leverage. Advertising weakness at Pandora.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Self-pay subscriber net adds, churn, ARPU, free cash flow, net leverage.

Flywheel verdict: Arguably belongs in the guide — cash flow, yield, Berkshire — but the subscriber decline makes it a value bet, not a hold-through name.

LYV Live Nation Entertainment

Sector: Live Events / Ticketing · Market cap: ~$35B · P/E: ~40x · Yield: none

The largest concert promoter in the world (it promotes ~50,000 events a year), the owner or operator of hundreds of venues, and — through Ticketmaster — the dominant ticketing platform for major venues in the U.S. Revenue ~$25B. The live-music boom after the pandemic (Taylor Swift, Beyoncé, stadium tours at record prices) drove record results; the Justice Department's antitrust lawsuit seeking to break up Live Nation and Ticketmaster, filed in 2024 and heading to trial in 2026, is the overhang.

Why it's popular: The pure play on the "experiences economy," with a monopoly debate that makes headlines every time a ticket presale crashes.

Shines when: Concert attendance and pricing grow, venue expansion adds high-margin revenue, sponsorship grows, and the antitrust case is settled without a breakup.

Dangers: A breakup order or structural remedies. Consumer pullback on discretionary spending. Artist and fan backlash over fees. Leverage. Regulatory scrutiny in Europe too.

Watch: Reports late Feb/May/Aug/Nov. Concerts attendance and revenue, Ticketmaster fee-bearing tickets, adjusted operating income, antitrust case developments.

Flywheel verdict: No.

CHTR Charter Communications

Sector: Cable Broadband · Market cap: ~$40B · P/E: ~7x · Yield: none

Spectrum — the second-largest U.S. cable operator (about 30 million broadband customers across 41 states), plus video (declining), mobile (growing fast as a bundled add-on through a Verizon wholesale deal), and business services. Revenue ~$55B. In 2025 it agreed to merge with Cox Communications (~$34.5B including debt), the largest private cable company, creating a broadband giant. The stock has fallen by more than half from its peak as broadband subscriber growth turned negative — fiber overbuilders and fixed-wireless from the phone companies took share — and the market decided cable's monopoly era was ending.

Why it's popular: The most-debated value stock in telecom — 7x earnings, enormous free cash flow, relentless buybacks (the share count has fallen by more than half in a decade), and a subscriber trend that keeps disappointing. Berkshire was a holder for years.

Shines when: Broadband losses stabilize, mobile bundling reduces churn, the Cox merger delivers synergies, capex falls after network upgrades, and buybacks compound.

Dangers: Continued broadband subscriber losses. Leverage (over 4x EBITDA). Cox integration. Video decline. Pricing pressure from fiber and fixed wireless.

Watch: Reports late Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct. Internet net adds, mobile lines, adjusted EBITDA, free cash flow, buyback pace, Cox merger milestones.

Flywheel verdict: Not boring in behavior, but it is the kind of leveraged cash machine a patient investor holds. Comcast (Atlas) is the steadier cable name.

NTDOY Nintendo Co. (ADR)

Sector: Video Games · Market cap: ~$100B · P/E: ~30x · Yield: ~1%

Mario, Zelda, Pokémon (a one-third owner of the Pokémon Company), Animal Crossing, and the hardware they run on — the Switch, which sold over 150 million units and became the third-best-selling console in history, and the Switch 2, launched in June 2025 and the fastest-selling console ever in its first months. Revenue ~$12B in a normal year and far more in a launch year. Kyoto-based, debt-free, with tens of billions in cash and a growing licensing business (the Super Mario movies, theme parks with Universal). The ADR trades over the counter.

Why it's popular: The strongest character franchise outside Disney, a console cycle just beginning, and a stock that doubled into the Switch 2 launch.

Shines when: Switch 2 hardware and software sales beat forecasts, first-party titles (a new Mario Kart, Zelda, Pokémon) drive attach rates, and licensing grows.

Dangers: Console cycles — the stock has always fallen between generations. Tariffs on Vietnamese and Chinese manufacturing. Yen. OTC liquidity. A premium multiple at the cycle's start.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Switch 2 hardware and software units, full-year forecast revisions, digital sales share, yen assumptions.

Flywheel verdict: No — a cycle stock with a cash pile. Sony is the diversified version.

SONY Sony Group Corporation (ADR)

Sector: Entertainment & Electronics · Market cap: ~$150B · P/E: ~20x · Yield: ~0.5%

PlayStation (the leading console business, over 80 million PS5s sold, plus a growing software and subscription business), Sony Music (one of the three global majors), Sony Pictures (Spider-Man, Crunchyroll anime), image sensors (the dominant supplier of camera sensors for smartphones including iPhones), and consumer electronics. Revenue ~$85B. It spun off its financial-services arm (Sony Financial Group) in October 2025 to become a pure entertainment-and-technology company — the culmination of a decade-long transformation under Kenichiro Yoshida from a struggling electronics maker into a content-and-platform company.

Why it's popular: A media conglomerate with the best gaming platform and a monopoly-like sensor business, at a multiple below Netflix or Nintendo, and a Japanese-stock re-rating story.

Shines when: PlayStation software and services grow (higher margin than hardware), music streaming royalties rise, anime scales globally, image-sensor demand holds, and the post-spin simplification re-rates the stock.

Dangers: Console cycle maturity. Smartphone sensor cyclicality (Apple concentration). Film-studio volatility. Tariffs. Yen.

Watch: Reports early Feb/May/Aug/Nov (fiscal year ends March). Game & Network Services operating income, PS5 units and monthly active users, music segment growth, image-sensor revenue, full-year guidance.

Flywheel verdict: The most flywheel-eligible name in this batch — diversified, profitable, buying back stock — held out only because it is an ADR with yen exposure. Would sit comfortably in an international section.
